# Changelog

All notable changes to this project will be documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/),
and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

## [Unreleased]

### Fixed

- **The #851 disclosure could not be printed on the console it was written for** ([#863](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/863)). `presets/_untrusted.py` discloses a control character in tracker text as the Control Pictures glyph that names it, and the whole security property of #851 rests on a reader *seeing* that glyph. Not one character it emits is ASCII: `␛` and its 32 siblings, the `⟨ ⟩` fence markers, the `…` and the em dash in `banner()` and `flat_note()`, the em dash in the `NEUTRALISED` replacement. Measured: `"\\u241b".encode(cp)` raises `UnicodeEncodeError` for `cp437`, `cp850`, `cp852` and `cp1252` — every Windows console codepage — and so does `"\\u27e8"`.

  **The marker fails before the glyph does, and that is the half the issue did not name.** The first `UnicodeEncodeError` in the reproduction is `'\\u27e8' in position 0`: the *opening fence marker*, on a render whose body had not been reached. A fence whose edges cannot be printed is not a fence — #693 through a third door, after the typed delimiter and the erasing escape sequence.

  **Under a strict stream that is a crash; under a replace-configured one it is a `?`.** Nothing in this repo sets `errors="replace"`, so the reachable failure today is the loud one — but `real line?FORGED` is the outcome the design has to exclude on principle, because it is this repo's own defect class landing inside the fix for that class: an absence produced by the tool, read by the reader as an absence in the world.

  **Three states, two of which say so.** The vocabulary is now chosen per render from what `sys.stdout` declares. A stream that carries it renders **exactly as before** — that is the documented default, and a clause on every banner of every op is the wallpaper [#854](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/854) removed. A stream that says it cannot gets ASCII markers (`<|remote a1b2c3d4|>`), ASCII prose, and the `[U+XXXX]` spelling the C1 fallback already used — with the banner naming the encoding and the spelling: `this stream is cp1252 and cannot carry the control-picture glyphs, so a control character reads as [U+001B] here`. A stream that will not declare an encoding gets the same fallback and a different sentence, because "cannot" and "could not tell" are different facts (`docs/validators.md` §"Declining instead of guessing"). `scrub()` neutralises whichever marker shape the render actually printed, so the ASCII fence cannot be closed from inside either.

  **What it costs, stated rather than hidden.** `[U+001B]` is eight characters for one, so a degraded render can be inflated by a hostile field where a picture render cannot. That is the same bound C1 has always carried, it is bounded by the call site's own cap times eight rather than by anything in the helper, and it is the price of a marker the reader can see. `<|`/`|>` rather than `[[`/`]]` or `<<`/`>>` because an ASCII marker is a shape a body can actually type, and those two are typed constantly — wiki links, TOML array-of-tables headers, git conflict markers — where this pair is not.

  **Not `use_utf8_stdout()`, deliberately.** Reusing `presets/git/_git_common.py`'s reconfiguration was the smaller change. On a console that is genuinely cp437 it writes UTF-8 bytes to a decoder that is not one, so the marker becomes mojibake instead of a marker — a marker-shaped absence reached by a different road — and it would change the stream for every op that prints remote text. Detection reads what the stream is; reconfiguration overwrites the answer. `./supertool` already pins `PYTHONIOENCODING=utf-8` for every preset it launches, so the common route is unaffected either way; this covers the routes that are not it.

  **Method.** TDD, red before green. RED: 13 tests, **12 failing** — and the one that passed is the pin that the UTF-8 render is byte-for-byte what #854 left. Assertions are on the rendered string being `.encode()`-able with `errors="strict"` at the three codepages, not on which branch ran, so a half-fix that switched the glyphs and left the markers still fails. GREEN: 13 passing.

  **Not verified on a real Windows console.** The encode failures and the absent reconfiguration are measured; nobody on this change had a `cmd.exe`. The design is the one that holds under *both* unresolved outcomes — raise or `?` — which is why it did not wait for the observation.
- **`git-resolve:both` on a Keep a Changelog file produced a structurally wrong document and reported `markers: clean`** ([#839](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/839)). Every branch older than one merge conflicts on `CHANGELOG.md` here, so `git-resolve:::both:::all` is the most-run resolution in the repo — three times in one afternoon on the day this was filed. When one side has had a release section cut above the entry both branches touched, the release heading sits *inside* the hunk, once on each side, and a line-level union emits it twice. The document then carries two `## [0.23.0] - 2026-08-05` sections, and every `[Unreleased]` line between the two copies is reparented under the first: unreleased work reads as shipped. One occurrence silently reverted [#829](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/829), a fix that existed precisely to move an entry *out* of a released section, four hours after it merged.

  **`markers: clean` is the [#744](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/744) defect one file type over** — a check answering a narrower question than the reader believes it answered. It means "no conflict markers remain". It reads as "the file is correct". The union itself is not wrong; it mirrors git's own `merge=union` driver line for line. What is wrong is applying a line-level strategy to a document whose meaning comes from repeating structural headings and then reporting only that it completed.

  **`both` is now refused per file when the union would duplicate a heading**, on the same terms #744 set for source text: the file is left conflicted, so git itself refuses `rebase --continue`, which is the only signal that does not depend on reading a receipt. The refusal names what it saw — `⊘ CHANGELOG.md: structured document — union would duplicate 1 heading(s) present on both sides: '## [0.23.0] - 2026-08-05', reparenting the lines between the two copies under the first; refused` — because a refusal that does not say what it found is a refusal you override blind. `force` unions anyway and the tally discloses it (`Resolved: 1 (1 file(s) with duplicated heading(s) — verify section structure)`), since the `validate: ok` beside it is true and useless. Refusal is per file: `both:all` over a broken `CHANGELOG.md` plus a clean `docs/notes.md` still resolves the notes.

  **The detector is narrow on purpose, and it never merges anything.** A heading counts only when the *same* heading line appears on *both* sides of one hunk — precisely the line the union would concatenate into two copies. The ordinary changelog conflict, two bullets under a shared `### Fixed`, has no heading inside the hunk at all and is untouched, which is what keeps this from firing on every conflict and training the override into a reflex. Scoped to Markdown extensions, since the `#` heading grammar is Markdown's; diff3 `|||||||` base content is skipped because `_union_file` drops it.

  **Rejected: warning instead of refusing**, which is the issue's own first-choice option. A warning is one line in a receipt whose previous line already says `validate: ok`, printed at the moment the caller is mid-rebase and reading for the word "Next" — and it leaves the wrong document staged. #744 rejected the same option for the same reason and that reasoning did not weaken by moving from `.py` to `.md`. **Rejected: a format-aware `sections` merge mode.** Teaching the resolver Keep a Changelog means knowing which headings are equivalent, how to interleave entries under a merged one, and what to do when the two sides order sections differently — a content-aware guess about a file the op is meant to treat as lines, and the issue is right that guessing wrong in a changelog is worse than a conflict. Detection is cheap and honest; repair is a product.

  **`merge=union` in `.gitattributes` does not bypass this guard**, unlike the source-text guard it sits beside. The attribute answers "union this file", which is a different question from "this union came out sound"; only `force` gets through. In practice a path with the attribute is auto-resolved by git and never reaches `git-resolve` at all.
- **A payload that meant to end a literal block with a quote — refused, and the premise checked first** ([#834](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/834)). `new = '''    kind = 'mr\''''` was written to disk without complaint, `py-syntax` agreed the file parsed, and Python then died at runtime on a string that was never closed. Two round-trips went into establishing that the mistake was in the payload syntax and not in the code being written — the tool's own defect class, an absence it produced (the content that was meant) read as a presence in the world (a file that parses as something else).

  **The issue proposed refusing content that ends in `'`, and that guard would have refused its own fix.** A TOML multi-line literal ends with one or two apostrophes perfectly well: a closing run may be four or five quotes and the surplus belongs to the content, so `'''    kind = 'mr''''` *is* the correct spelling of the payload in the issue, and its value ends in `'`. Firing on the trailing quote would have made the right payload unwritable while leaving the actual mistake untouched.

  **The actual mistake is the backslash.** Inside a literal block a backslash is content and never an escape, so the escape reflex every other language rewards is inert here and silently becomes part of the line. A `'''` block whose content ends with a backslash immediately before its closing run is now **refused at parse time**, before anything is written, and the message spells the caller's own line back both ways — without the backslash, and as a `"""basic"""` block where a genuinely wanted backslash doubles.

  **Refusing rather than warning is a decision about what stays writable, not about how certain the guard is.** Both readings of that backslash have another spelling, so a refusal strands nothing; where a refusal *would* strand a legitimate intent, the honest severity is a warning. That rule is now written down in `docs/validators.md` §"Declining instead of guessing", next to the three states it sits beside. It is deliberately not applied to the `\\`-at-end-of-line warning in shell payloads ([#835](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/835)), where a doubled backslash is sometimes exactly what was meant and blocking needs an opt-out to exist first.

  **And the recommended spelling had to work on every interpreter first.** `_mini_toml_loads`, the fallback parser used below Python 3.11, closed a multi-line string at the first three quotes and choked on the surplus — so `'''kind = 'mr''''` parsed under stdlib `tomllib` and failed under the fallback, and a refusal message naming it would have been advice that fails on a third of the support matrix. Both parsers now agree about closing runs, for `'''` and `"""` alike, held to a table read off `tomllib` the way [#684](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/684)'s escape table is.
- **`claude-channel` — a burst of individually-legal events could still spend the context window** ([#605](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/605), the axis [#608](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/608) named and left open). The per-attribute (2,048) and per-event (8,192) caps bound one event against itself and nothing else. Forty events of 8,192 chars are each perfect by every one of them: measured on `235b377` through a real socket at a real server, forty legal events left the bridge at **315,460 bytes** — a third of a 200K-token window, delivered as forty flawless notifications. The window does not care whether it was spent by one event or by forty, so a per-event cap alone is a cap on the wrong noun.

  **#608's reason for declining constrains the fix rather than being overruled by it.** It argued that *dropping event #41 for the sins of #1–40 refuses an event on grounds unrelated to its own content, and a limiter that silently eats a `pipeline_failed` is a worse radar than a chatty one.* That is an argument against **dropping**, not against **bounding**, and the two separate here exactly as they separated per-event — because an event is two things glued together. Its **routing** (`watcher_source`/`id`/`event`) is ~60 chars and is the entire product of this bridge; its **payload** is all of the bulk and is context the session can fetch for itself. So a burst does not stop events; it stops payload. Past `SUPERTOOL_CHANNEL_WINDOW_MAX` (65,536 chars in a rolling 60 s) an event keeps its routing, loses its payload **whole**, and carries a `burst` attribute plus a body line naming the budget that took it. Forty red pipelines are still forty notifications; the same forty events now cost ~78 KB. Nothing is dropped for what its neighbours did, and an event that had nothing to lose is delivered unreduced and says nothing.

  **The hard limit is the one real loss, so it is the one thing most worth saying.** Reduction alone is a ~50x discount rather than a bound — a routing-only event is ~120 chars, so a producer in a spin loop still spends the window, just slower, and the issue's framing as a soft denial-of-service surface is only narrowed by it. Past `SUPERTOOL_CHANNEL_WINDOW_HARD` (262,144 chars, ~1,700 routing-only events in a minute, which is not a radar but a loop) events are suppressed. Each writes a `drop()` line through the existing loud path, and the **count rides the next event that gets through**, as a `suppressed` attribute and a body line: `4 events were suppressed over the last 7s — past the burst hard limit of 262144 chars/60s`. stderr is not readable from inside a session; the next delivery is. Without that, a suppressed burst would be [#554](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/554)'s invisible delivery gap with a budget attached. The disclosure is deliberately not re-charged against the budget: an event that goes over by confessing a gap is correct, one that stays under by hiding it is the defect.

  **The numbers come from the traffic, and the window is rolling.** Across ten live watchers the largest complete payload was 488 chars and pollers emit only on state change, so 65,536 chars/60 s is ~8% of a 200K-token window per minute and ~130x the busiest real minute — a fleet spawn, where every watcher emits one `first_tick` at once, is ten events and ~5 KB. A fixed bucket would let a burst spend a full budget either side of a boundary and report it as compliant. The budget is one budget for the **server**, not one per connection: ten watchers are ten connections, and per-connection accounting would reopen the axis through the bookkeeping. `WINDOW_HARD` must exceed `WINDOW_MAX` or the server refuses to start (exit `4`) — at or below it the reduce-and-disclose stage is unreachable and every over-budget event is suppressed instead, a strictly louder failure than the operator configured, arrived at in silence.

  **Considered and rejected: cap-and-spill-to-a-file.** A path the reader can open is strictly better than a byte count *when the machinery exists*, and here it does not — supertool's oversize handling is cap-and-disclose (`MAX_READ_BYTES`, `_probe_minified`) and `gitlab-mr`'s `+N more`-inside-the-value; nothing spills to a path, so this would be a new mechanism rather than a reuse. It is also in the wrong process (a `bun` server with no cleanup owner, so a hostile producer's DoS relocates from RAM to disk), and worst, a path pointing at 400 KB in an agent's context is an invitation: the window cost the cap exists to prevent arrives one turn later with the cap's own blessing.

  **Drive-by, on the line above the new refusal:** `capFromEnv`'s own refusal message ended in a literal `\n` rather than a newline, so the one line an operator sees when a cap override is unreadable printed mangled and unterminated.

  **Tests.** `tests/test_notifiers_claude_channel_605_burst.py`, 5 tests, on #554's live-server harness — a real `channel.ts` under `bun`, real events over a real UDS socket, assertions on what the consumer receives. Written and run red first: **4 failed, 1 passed**, the headline being `forty legal events cost 315460 bytes of the window`. **Green: 5 passed**, and 18 with #605's and #554's suites. The post-condition asserted is three-part and would not be satisfied by a silent limiter: a burst is bounded, **and** all forty ids still arrive (a limiter that drops events fails here), **and** the reduction says so in both the attributes and the body. Size and disclosure are separate assertions, because a cap that reduces without disclosing satisfies every byte assertion and is a worse bug than the one being fixed. The one test that passes in both states is `test_normal_traffic_is_delivered_whole_and_says_nothing` — the over-reach guard, so a patch that reduced everything or annotated every event fails there instead of reading as progress. `Channel` gained an additive `env` kwarg so a test can pin small thresholds; the socket path stays non-overridable.
### Added

- **`git-worktrees` — "is an agent working in this worktree?", answered in three states** ([#860](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/860)). Filed from damage, not from a wishlist. To decide whether a worktree was free, the maintainer ran `ps aux | grep -c "st-wt/804"`, got `0`, and reported the agent dead — *verified*. It was alive. **A worktree path is not in that process's argv; the process is `chdir`'d there.** The zero was a fact about the pattern. A second agent went into the occupied tree and for two minutes both wrote through one index: files changing between one agent's own calls, a `reset` in the reflog nobody issued, a `batch` anchor 93 lines from where it had been minutes earlier. It reverted rather than merging someone else's half-work. Nothing was lost, and that was luck.

  **The third state is the whole feature.** `occupied`, `idle`, and **`cannot tell`** — the repo's standing contract (`docs/validators.md` §"Declining instead of guessing") arriving at the one surface where delegation decisions are made. Liveness is undecidable in the general case, so the tempting shape is a heuristic that answers yes/no; that shape *is* the bug, because "no evidence of an agent" is exactly what the `ps` grep already said. `idle` is therefore not a default but an earned verdict: every probe must have answered — the tree stat'd and quiet **and** the process table read and holding nobody. Anything else declines and names which probe went silent. An occupied tree reported free costs a contaminated run; a `cannot tell` costs one question to a human.

  **Every verdict names its evidence**, because a bare verdict is what misled the reporter: `occupied` cites `index.lock present in the git dir (2s old)`, `HEAD moved 40s ago`, or `pid 51234 (claude) has cwd inside this worktree`; `cannot tell` cites `lsof did not answer within 10s — the process table was not read, so nothing is claimed about it`. The signals are weighed rather than trusted individually — `index.lock` is proof when present and absent between any two calls of a busy agent, mtimes catch an agent that is writing and miss one that is thinking, and the cwd scan is the one that answers the question the `ps` grep asked wrong.

  **Inference rather than announcement, deliberately.** A claim file written by the occupant on entry looks stronger and was rejected: nothing already running writes one. Five agents were live in sibling worktrees while this was built and announced nothing, so a claim-based checker would have reported every one of them as unclaimed — the same false all-clear in new packaging. A claim also fails towards `occupied` forever once its writer dies, so it needs an age *and* a liveness cross-check, which is this op again underneath it. Where an announcement already exists it is read rather than reinvented: `git worktree lock` is git's own, and a locked tree is `occupied` with git's own reason quoted.

  **`idle` needs two windows, and the second one came from the live fleet.** The first run against real worktrees found two that had been edited 7 and 12 minutes earlier and in which **no process had its cwd inside** — an agent's parent process need not be `chdir`'d into the tree it is editing. That is the licence for `idle` weakening under observation: on its own it would have called a working tree free at the sixteenth minute. So `occupied` uses the short window (`SUPERTOOL_WORKTREE_ACTIVE_WINDOW`, 15m — *is somebody writing now*) and `idle` a long one (`SUPERTOOL_WORKTREE_IDLE_QUIET`, 1h — *has this been abandoned*), with `cannot tell` between them, saying so: *quiet for less than 1h, and an empty cwd scan does not prove absence*.

  **Platform divergence is disclosed, not smoothed over.** The cwd scan is `/proc/<pid>/cwd` on Linux and `lsof -a -d cwd` elsewhere; Windows has neither, and there the op declines by name (`no way to read process cwds on this platform (win32) … occupancy undecidable`) instead of degrading to `idle`. An `lsof` that times out or prints nothing is `unknown` too — "the tool did not speak" and "nobody is there" are the two sentences this entire issue is about.

  **Inspection only.** Nothing is removed, pruned, unlocked or written, and no `git worktree remove` is ever printed: a destructive suggestion under an ambiguous verdict is how an occupied tree gets removed. The listing still answers the adjacent chore that motivated it — branch, path, `merged` and `prunable` markers in one call, instead of `git -C ~/Documents/st-wt/NNN` twenty times with the branch-to-path map living in someone's head. With a `PATH` the exit code carries the same three states: `0` for `idle` and only `idle`, `1` for `occupied`, `2` for `cannot tell`, so a caller testing `== 0` gets the safe reading of an undecided answer.
- **Every remaining test call site decoded a child's output with the locale codec** ([#862](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/862)). #856 pinned one file while four Windows legs held up the v0.24.0 tag; the same hazard sat in the rest of `tests/`, where any call that ever printed a Control Picture, a box-drawing character or an accented name would red the Windows legs again — and would look like a different bug each time, because the failure surfaces downstream of its cause as a `TypeError` about `None`.

  **The filed count was 163 across 71 files; the real one is 169 across 79.** The file count in the issue is exactly right for the class it describes, and the call count is not: an AST walk for the #856 shape — a `subprocess` runner in text mode with no `encoding=` — finds **151** sites in those same 71 files, not 163. A textual scan finds 191 `text=True` lines, of which 171 carry no `encoding=` on the same line; that is what a grep sees, and it counts prose, `tempfile.mkstemp(text=True)`, and the wrapper signatures `def _run(cmd, capture_output=True, text=True, **kwargs)` as call sites. Neither number is 163, so the figure is not reproducible from the tree by any predicate worth enforcing. The number that governs is the repo's own rule, already written for shipped code in `test_encoding_seam.py`: a call is a violation when it provably decodes and leaves **either** `encoding=` **or** `errors=` to the default. That finds **168** violations in **78** files — the 151 bare ones, plus **17** that pin `encoding="utf-8"` and leave the error handler strict. Those 17 are a real defect and not a technicality: strict decoding is what killed `gl-mr` in #498 on the first byte of a conflicted PNG.

  **The guard was widened, not copied.** #856 answered its own file with a file-local AST walk, which is the right size for a release-blocking fix and the wrong size for 78 files — 78 copies of a guard is 78 things to drift. The scan already existed one layer up: `subprocess_encoding_violations()` in `tests/test_encoding_seam.py` has enumerated this exact property for `supertool.py`, `presets/`, `hooks/`, `validators/`, `formatters/` and `notifiers/` since #501, and its docstring named `tests/` as out of scope pending an issue. This is that issue, so `tests/` now goes through the same function, with the same three states — clean, violation, and **undecidable** for a call whose kwargs are not literals the parser can read.

  **Widening the scope populated a blind spot the scan had declared empty.** Its docstring listed an aliased runner — `_REAL_POPEN = subprocess.Popen`, `from subprocess import run as _r` — as something it could not see, with the note "no occurrences today". True of shipped code, and false the moment `tests/` came into scope: there are **21** such bindings there, and the scan walked straight past a genuine unpinned `Popen(..., text=True)` behind one of them. That is the failure this file's own read-half argues against — "a scan with silent false negatives that looks complete is worse than a blunt one" — so the rule now resolves aliases and judges the call under the runner's real name. It brings the total to **169**. The trampolines are deliberately not flagged: `def fake(*a, **kw): return real_run(*a, **kw)` is the monkeypatch idiom, it originates no kwargs of its own, and the call it stands in for is judged where that call is written. Flagging them would have bought 18 waivers for one defect, and a guard carrying 18 waivers is a guard somebody switches off.

  **The one undecidable was #856's own helper.** `_run_utf8(argv, *, check=False, **kwargs)` forwards blind, and a forwarded `**kwargs` can carry `text=True`, so the rule declines to judge it rather than passing it. Its four callers pass only `cwd`, `env`, `timeout` and `check`, so the signature now spells them out and the rule can read it. A shared helper for all 168 sites would have had the same problem at 78× the scale — every call site would have become invisible to the guard, which is why the sweep pins kwargs at each site instead of hiding them behind one function.

  **The lock fails on this machine, which is the whole point.** Every behavioural assertion in these files passes on a UTF-8 platform whether or not decoding is pinned, so a regression can be written on a Mac and go green on every leg its author can run. Two tests answer that. The enumerator reads source rather than behaviour and so fails wherever it runs; it also asserts it looked at more than 50 subprocess calls, so a scan that has drifted into matching nothing fails loudly instead of passing silently. And the hazard itself is reproduced here rather than assumed: under `LC_ALL=C` with PEP 538 and PEP 540 disabled, `locale.getpreferredencoding()` is ASCII, a bare `text=True` parent dies with `UnicodeDecodeError` on the first byte of `␛`, and the pinned spelling returns the glyph intact. The codec differs from the runners' cp1252; the defect does not.

  **Method.** TDD, red before green. RED: the two new enumerators fail, listing all 168 sites then visible and the one undecidable. GREEN: `tests/test_encoding_seam.py` 15 passed, full suite **6799 passed / 74 skipped**. The sweep was applied by a codemod driven by the guard's own rule rather than by hand, so the two cannot disagree about what a violation is, and the applied count was verified against the intended one — 168 of 168, none skipped, re-scan clean; the 169th was the aliased call the rule could not yet see. Two mutation checks confirm the lock is not vacuous: reverting a single site to `text=True` turns the enumerator red and names the file and line, and doing the same to the aliased `Popen` proves the new alias path is load-bearing rather than decorative.

## [0.24.0] - 2026-08-05

### Added

- **`gh-branch` — "is this branch green?", answered per workflow rather than per recency** ([#615](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/615)). `gh-pr:N` answers for a pull request. After a squash merge the ref that matters is the default branch, which no longer has one — `gh-pr:master:status` returns `ERROR: no PR found for branch 'master'` — so the post-merge check was hand-rolled every time: `gh run list --branch master --limit 1`, a second call for the run's jobs, and a jq `group_by` retyped from memory. That is the exact reflex [#454](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/454) exists to prevent, and it stayed invisible because the fallback works every time, so the tool never appears to fail. Both default branches sat red for hours on the night the issue was filed while the board read clean; the failure was that nobody looked, and nobody looked because looking cost three calls and a remembered incantation.

  **`--limit 1` selects a workflow, not the run you meant.** A repo with more than one `push` workflow has several runs per commit, and `--limit 1` returns whichever started most recently — routinely CodeQL. It reported `completed success` for a commit whose `tests` matrix had not started, and the right answer arrived by luck twice. So this op selects the newest run **of each workflow on the head SHA** (highest `databaseId` within a workflow, so a re-run supersedes what it replaces while a different workflow supersedes nothing) and its summary is **conjunctive**: green only when every workflow on the SHA concluded *and* every leg of every one of them passed. One workflow's conclusion never stands in for the commit's. The issue's own body proposes the broken line; it is not what shipped.

  **Four states, and no pair of them renders alike.** `GREEN`. `NOT GREEN`, covering both "a leg failed" and "a leg has not finished" — worded differently, because those are opposite next actions. `NO RUN`, which is zero runs on this SHA *with the reason*: inside the ~15min creation window measured in [#585](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/585) a run is still expected; past it the cause is declined rather than guessed, because a path filter and a workflow that never fired look identical from here, and inferring it from `.github/workflows/*` would be inferring it from files that need not be the ones on this ref. And `UNKNOWN`, when a job list did not come back — an unread leg count is never rendered as zero passing legs (`docs/validators.md` §"Declining instead of guessing"). "No `tests` run exists for this commit" and "`tests` passed" were the two sentences that had to stop rendering alike; a workflow present on the previous head and absent on this one is reported as its own observation, with its cause stated as UNKNOWN rather than as a verdict.

  **Every leg passing is not the same as green.** Observed live on `master` while the op was being written: 17 of 17 legs `success` and the `tests` run still `in_progress`. The tally structurally cannot see a leg GitHub has not created yet — a `needs:`-gated job appears only once its dependency finishes — so an unconcluded run blocks green on its own, the same reading [#789](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/789) gave the run-level field in `gh-run`.

  **A sibling op, not a second key into `gh-pr`'s renderer.** The tally *is* shared — `presets/_checks.py`, the same module `gh-pr` and `gh-run` render through, which is the issue's own argument for the op existing: one place where a `CANCELLED` can be mis-tallied, and terms that sum back to the leg count. The *render* is not, because the two are different shapes: `gh-pr` is handed a flat rollup on one commit, and this is a set of runs grouped by workflow with a conjunction over them, which would have arrived as a second data model inside one function.

  **A concluded run is unmistakably distinct from one still moving.** Every row states its phase in a word this module owns — `concluded`, `running`, `unestablished` — because the issue's first comment is a worked case of a bare elapsed-time column (`[time]`) being read as a possible `TIMED_OUT` and costing a second call to disambiguate. The head SHA is named, abbreviated and in full, because "the branch is green" is a claim about a commit.

  **With no argument it answers for the repo's default branch** — the shape used right after a merge, which is when it matters. It honours `repo:OWNER/NAME` ([#677](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/677)) on both of its `gh` routes, verified against `claude-remember` as well as this repo. The head SHA is resolved from the *ref*, never from the run list: deriving it from the newest run would make "no run exists for this commit" structurally unreachable, since the answer would always be a SHA that had a run.

  **The exit code is 0 for every established verdict, green or not.** Nonzero is reserved for "this op could not answer" — the family's convention (`gh-run` exits 0 on a run that concluded `failure`), and load-bearing here: supertool renders a nonzero exit as `FAIL`, which would have printed the same banner for a branch whose tests are still running as for a `gh` that is not authenticated. Two different things rendering alike is what the op exists to remove; it does not get to reintroduce it in its own exit code.

  **No `gl-` sibling yet.** The same gap presumably exists for `gl-pipeline` against a branch rather than a number, but nobody has hit it, and this shipped for the case that drew blood rather than being widened on speculation.

- **`gh-check` — the second id namespace, and the annotation the finding actually lives in** ([#793](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/793)). `gh-pr:792:status` named the failing leg (`failed: CodeQL`) and the natural next call died: `gh-job:92205186236:fail` → *"the job endpoint returned 404 for this ID too, so no such job exists in this repo. Check the ID."* The id existed. GitHub hands out bare integers from two namespaces — Actions jobs (`actions/jobs/<id>`) and check runs (`check-runs/<id>`) — and every GitHub App that is not Actions writes to the second: CodeQL default setup, Dependabot, code-scanning uploads, external CI. `gh-job` reads only the first, so its 404 was an absence *by one route* published as an absence from the world, which is this repo's own defect class ([#672](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/672)) arriving in its own error path. Getting the finding took three hand-written `gh api` calls, two of which returned nothing useful.

  **A sibling op, not an implicit fallback inside `gh-job`.** The two ids are syntactically identical, so "recognise a check-run id" can only mean *probe* — and an op whose header says `# Job #N` that turns out to have read a check run is a surface that misleads about which API answered. So each op probes the other namespace **only to improve its own 404 message, never to render content**, and both still exit 1 having read nothing. The probe fires on one path only — the one where the job endpoint *and* the log endpoint have both already 404'd — so it costs no extra request on any path that was working, the same trade [#723](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/723) made for the metadata call it reused.

  **Three states where there were two**, per `docs/validators.md` §"Declining instead of guessing". *Found in the other namespace*: `No Actions job #N — but a check run with that id does exist (CodeQL) … ./supertool 'gh-check:N'`. *Absent from both*: `both returned 404 … Check the ID` — the only case where blaming the id is true, and it now says so with two endpoints behind it instead of one. *Probe inconclusive* (auth, 5xx, timeout): declined outright — `whether it is a check run instead is UNKNOWN … this op is not guessing between them`. `gh-check` mirrors all three in the other direction and points back at `gh-job`.

  **What it prints is the annotation triple** — `path:line`, title, message — because for a scanning check that is the entire finding: `presets/github/issue.py:125 — Incomplete URL substring sanitization`. Capped at `GH_CHECK_ANNOTATION_CAP` (default `_checks.NAMED_CAP`, 5) with `+N more` in the **header and the footer**, because a reader cut off by a downstream budget never reaches a footer ([#605](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/605)/[#622](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/622)). A page that comes back full at `per_page=100` is disclosed as a floor rather than a total. **Zero annotations is never an all-clear**: on a non-passing check it prints the conclusion and says the detail may be in the output summary or in a system this op does not read; on a passing one it says plainly that nothing was flagged. A failed annotations fetch renders as `UNKNOWN`, never as an empty list — "the fetch failed" and "there are none" are the two sentences this whole issue is about.

  **`gh-check:pr:N` exists because the id was unreachable.** The check-run id rides on `detailsUrl` only for Actions legs (`_checks.github_job_id`), so `gh-pr:N:status` names `CodeQL` with no id beside it — an id-only op would have left the merge gate exactly where it was. This form reads the PR's head SHA and lists `commits/<sha>/check-runs` with ids and conclusions. An empty list says *0 check runs are attached to the head commit `<sha>`* and names the merge ref as a thing it did not read, rather than "no checks".

  **It does not touch the code-scanning API, deliberately.** `code-scanning/alerts?ref=refs/pull/N/merge` returned **empty** in the incident while the finding sat in an annotation — an emptiness that means "not the endpoint that knows" and reads as "no alerts on this PR". Nothing here can render it, and the test suite enforces that by raising on any unstubbed `gh` call.

  **Method.** TDD, red before green. RED against unmodified `master`: **20 failed / 2 passed**, the 2 being deliberate fences (the happy path makes no `check-runs` call; `gh-job` renders no check-run content). GREEN: **6459 passed / 72 skipped** across the suite. Twenty-three hand-written mutants over the changed logic — every branch of both 404 resolvers forced in each direction, the annotation cap removed, both cap disclosures deleted in turn, the page-floor boundary moved off `>=`, the zero-annotation warning forced on and off, the provenance line deleted, `PER_PAGE` and the cap default changed, and the old message restored — **23 killed, 0 survived**, over the pre-live design.

  **Verified live on 2026-08-05, and the live run changed the code twice.** Check run 92205186236 — the one from the incident — was read end to end: `gh-check` prints its CodeQL header, its output summary and the annotation `presets/github/issue.py:125 [failure] Incomplete URL substring sanitization`, the exact line #793 had to make three hand-written `gh api` calls to reach, and `gh-job` on the same id now names the check run instead of denying the id exists. Two things the fixtures had wrong surfaced only there. **A running check carries `status: in_progress` with an empty conclusion and zero annotations** — annotations are written *while* a check runs — and the first draft rendered that with the vocabulary of a finished clean check, which is this issue's own all-clear one state further back; it now says the count was taken mid-flight. And **the two id spaces are not disjoint**: GitHub creates a check run per Actions job sharing the integer, so an Actions job id resolves at `check-runs/<id>` too, and the overlap runs one way only. That makes `gh-check`'s point-back-at-`gh-job` branch unreachable against today's GitHub; it stays, documented as guarding an observation rather than a contract. Still fixture-only: the 404 body shapes, `raw_details`, a full 100-annotation page, and every transport-failure path.

### Changed

- **`gh-job` and `gh-pr` answer for a check run too — GitHub's two id namespaces are its seam, not yours** ([#827](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/827)). [#821](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/821) shipped `gh-check` and was right about the capability — nothing else could read a check run's annotations, which is where a scanning check keeps its actual finding. It was wrong about the routing, and Florian said so while reviewing it: you have a red check on a PR, and you should not have to know that GitHub keeps its results in two internal lists and pick a different command depending on which one it landed in. **GitLab needs no equivalent op** — one pipeline, one hierarchy, one id space — so the split is GitHub's history leaking into our command surface, and absorbing exactly that is what the `gh-*`/`gl-*` parity in [#628](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/628) is for.

  **`gh-job:ID` now takes either kind of id.** Hand it a check-run id and it renders the check run — status, output summary, annotations — under `# Check run #N`, with a `Routed:` line saying you called `gh-job` and this op read the checks API instead. `gh-check` is unchanged and stays as the explicit form; **nobody has to learn it**.

  **Answering was never the problem — answering *quietly* was.** #821 recognised the case and declined to use it, because rendering a check run from `gh-job` would be "a probe that silently changes which API answered". That reasoning is sound about *silently* and not about *answering*. A render whose header names the namespace it read is labelled, not silent. The invariant it was protecting is intact and still pinned by a test: a `# Job #N` header above a check run's body remains forbidden, and the routed output is the **check** shape rather than a job's log template with a check's content poured into it. A job renders as a log; a check run renders as status + output + annotations, and forcing either into the other is how the render starts lying.

  **A bare integer is not actually ambiguous, given an order — and the live API is what settled it.** Verified on 2026-08-05 against this repo: an Actions job's id **is** its check run's id. The job object publishes `check_run_url: …/check-runs/<the same integer>`, so for an Actions leg the two namespaces are one leg in two projections, not two things colliding; an App's check run 404s in the Actions namespace. Asking Actions first is therefore a total order rather than a coin-flip: an answer there is definitive and the checks API is never consulted. **Routing costs zero extra requests on every path that already worked**, including the happy one.

  **The residual uncertainty still declines, because it is a different question.** It is not "which of two things is this" but "one of the two routes did not answer", and that keeps its own state (`docs/validators.md` §"Declining instead of guessing"): a checks call that 404s falls through to `both returned 404 … Check the ID`, and a checks call that fails for any other reason exits 1 with `whether it is a check run instead is UNKNOWN — the checks API did not answer: <error>`. Neither renders an empty check. "I guessed the namespace and found nothing" arriving as "there is nothing" would have been a new instance of this repo's most-filed defect class, in the merge path.

  **A requested log mode is declined by name, not dropped.** `:fail`, `:raw` and `:grep` all slice a job log and a check run has no log. The routed render says which mode was asked for and that it was not applied, beside the routing line rather than below the output — both sentences answer "why does this not look like what I asked for", and a reader who has started reading Output has stopped asking.

  **`gh-pr` names a non-Actions leg's id, and this half is where the live run found a defect.** `gh-pr:N` and `gh-pr:N:status` now print `CodeQL (check #92264897684)` beside `pytest (ubuntu-latest, 3.9) (job #92264786336)` — the namespace is part of the label, not decoration, because the two spaces mint from one integer sequence and a `check #` labelled `job #` sends the reader to a 404 that reads as an absence. #793 recorded that "the check-run id rides on `detailsUrl` only for Actions legs", and `docs/presets/github.md` said so. Reading the real rollup for #827 showed that is not true: a check run's `detailsUrl` is its own page, `https://<host>/<owner>/<repo>/runs/<check-run-id>` — a second URL shape carrying the id, in a field `gh-pr` already fetches, that nothing parsed. So this half **costs no request at all**; it is a regex, not a call. The Actions shape is tested first on purpose, because an Actions URL contains `/runs/<run-id>` too and a looser parser would print the *run* id wearing a check run's label, manufacturing the exact defect the issue is about.

  **What did not change, and why.** `gh-check` stays. Removing a shipped public op is a scope decision, `gh-check:pr:N` has no equivalent elsewhere (it lists *all* check runs on a head commit, passing ones included), and an explicit escape hatch costs nothing now that no one is forced through it. `gh-pr` does not fetch a failing check's annotations inline: that is one extra API call per non-Actions red leg on the hot merge-gate path, and naming the id already makes the finding one op away. `gh-run` is untouched — it lists an Actions run's jobs, and a check run belongs to no run, so there is nothing there to route.

  **Method.** TDD, red before green. RED against unmodified `master`: **15 failed / 4 passed**, the 4 being regression fences on behaviour that was already correct (both-namespaces-404 blames the id; an unanswered probe declines; the happy path makes no `check-runs` call; an Actions leg's label never carries the run id). GREEN on the two new suites plus [#619](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/619), [#723](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/723) and [#793](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/793): 71 passed; **6650 passed / 74 skipped** across the whole suite.

  **Twenty-one hand-written mutants over the changed logic: 19 killed, 2 survived, and the survivors were worth more than the kills.** Both real holes it found were the *cheap* half of the change rather than the loud half. The first: routing keyed on the log 404 alone instead of on the job endpoint's 404 — which would have rendered the check-run projection of a **still-running Actions job** in place of #723's "the log is not written yet", because a running job answers at the job endpoint and 404s at the log. The second: `_missing_log_message` re-asking the checks API instead of using the probe already threaded to it, doubling a request on the both-404 path with every printed line identical. Both now have a test. The two survivors are equivalent mutants and are documented as such: swapping the order of the two `detailsUrl` patterns changes nothing, because the two-segment anchor is what keeps an Actions URL out of the check-run branch — so `github_check_ref`'s docstring no longer claims the order is what does the work, since a mutation run proved it is not.

  **Two #793 tests were rewritten rather than left passing, and the diff says why.** They asserted `rc == 1` on a recognised check-run id — they pinned the declining-to-answer decision that this issue overturns. Their surviving invariant (never render check-run content under a job header) is what they assert now, and the file carries a "Superseded in part by #827" note so the reversal is legible to whoever reads the old issue first. `_checks.github_named_states` grew from a `(name, state, job_id)` triple to a `(name, state, kind, id)` quad, so #619's helper tests were updated to pass the kind; every rendered string they assert is unchanged. A tolerant reader accepting both arities was rejected on the grounds that the lenient default would have to be `"job"`, which is a wrong provenance label manufactured silently — this issue's own defect, in the fix for it.

### Security

- **A check run could forge its own verdict: `gh-check` / `gh-job` / `gh-branch` printed remote text unfenced, and the fence itself did not stop a cursor** ([#851](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/851)). Found by the release-gating audit on the diff since 0.23.0, and it blocked 0.24.0. Fourteen preset files had adopted the `_untrusted` boundary from [#694](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/694); the two newest CI-reading ones, added in [#793](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/793)/[#821](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/821)/[#827](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/827) and [#615](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/615), had zero references to it. Everything a check run puts on the screen — `name`, `output.title`, `output.summary`, every annotation's `path` and `title` — is written by whoever owns the check run: **any GitHub App with `checks:write`**, and by extension anything whose finding text a PR author can steer.

  **What that bought.** A three-line `name` put a forged `Status: completed / success` *above* the real `failure`, on the block a reader uses to decide a merge; `_clip()` bounded length and nothing else, so interior newlines and control characters passed through whole. A failing security check could be made to read as passing, on a merge gate — this repo's defining defect class, arriving through a security surface. The `summary` could forge a whole `## Annotations (0)` section and a `NEXT STEP` line for the agent that supertool exists to serve. Reachable from `gh-check:ID`, from `gh-check:pr:N`, and — new in this cycle — from **`gh-job:ID`**, which routes an id the Actions namespace disowned into the same renderer (#827), so a caller reached it without ever naming `gh-check`.

  **The fix is in the helper, not only at the two call sites, and that is the part worth reading.** `_untrusted.flat()` split on `\n` and replaced `\r`, and the module said that kept a field to one line. It did not, and had not for two years: `\x0b` and `\x0c` move the cursor down on every terminal in normal use, `\x85` is a line break in the C1 set, and `\x1b[2K\x1b[1A` is worse than adding a line — it *erases* one the tool already wrote, which also means a fenced body could rub out its own closing marker, the [#693](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/693) defect through a different door. Adopting the helper unchanged at two sites would have closed the forged-line half and left the cursor half open. So `flat()` and `scrub()` now neutralise every C0, DEL and C1 character, which changes the behaviour of **all sixteen callers** — deliberately, and it is the better fix.

  **Disclosed, never stripped.** Control characters are replaced by the Unicode Control Pictures glyph that names them — `\x1b` renders as `␛` — because suppressing them silently converts *this text was hostile* into *this text was different*, which is this repo's own defect class wearing a fix's clothing. **Corrected in [#854](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/854):** this paragraph originally claimed "one code point in, one out, so a hostile field cannot inflate a render", and that is true only of C0 and DEL, which have a Control Pictures glyph each. C1 (`0x80`–`0x9F`) has none and falls back to naming the code point — `[U+0085]`, **eight characters for one**. The module docstring always said so; this entry did not, and a false claim in a security entry is worse than no claim. The inflation is bounded, but by the call site's own `_clip()` — which runs *before* the render — rather than by anything in the helper, so a field capped at `SUMMARY_MAX` can still render eight times that. The one exception is `flat()`'s newline, which stays a space: that is pinned by #694, no tracker permits a newline in a title or a login, and the `banner()` / `flat_note()` line above the render already says those fields are content.

  **What the renders look like now.** `gh-check` and the `gh-job` route print `_untrusted.banner()` first — first, because the check's `name` is *in* the header line, so a disclosure printed after it would arrive after the content it describes — and the `output.summary` is now a **fenced block** under a bare `Summary:` line rather than a one-line field, since a summary is where an app that publishes no annotations puts the entire finding. `gh-check:pr:N` and `gh-branch` fence nothing and so print `flat_note` instead, per [#819](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/819): a banner promising markers a render never prints teaches a reader to skip the next one.

  **Method.** TDD, red before green. RED: 11 new tests, 11 failing. GREEN: 11 passing, and the full suite at **6775 passed / 74 skipped**. Every assertion is on the **rendered bytes** — no raw control byte anywhere in the output, exactly one `Status:` line at column 0 and it reads `failure`, the forged section inside the markers and the tool's own sections outside them — rather than on the fencing function having been called, which a half-implementation would pass. One test drives the `gh-job` → `render_check` route specifically, because a fix applied only to `gh-check`'s entry point passes every other test in the file. Two existing tests were amended with the reason inline: #793's "the header is the first line" pinned an enforcement mechanism rather than its rule (the rule is that the header names the API that answered, and a banner is not a header), and #694's `flat("a\nb") == "a b"` is kept exactly as it was.

  **Fixture-only, and said so.** No App has been observed publishing a check run containing control characters. The attacker model is the API contract — these are free-text fields with no documented character restriction — not an incident.

- **The #851 disclosure fired on line endings and indentation, which is how a disclosure stops being read** ([#854](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/854)). `\r` and `\t` are C0, so the widening above disclosed them with the rest, and every body written in either tracker's web editor came back with `␍` on the end of every line: `Looks like it was missing from PR 58909␍`, `␍`, `Refs: 58909␍`, and `␉indented code` for every tab-indented fence. Measured on the first eight open issues per repo — **1210** carriage returns in `nodejs/node`, **1221** in `python/cpython`, **231** plus **159** tabs in `microsoft/vscode`. The blast radius is every fenced block in `gh-issue`, `gh-pr`, `gl-issue` and `gl-mr` and every `check.output.summary`. It held 0.24.0 rather than waiting for 0.24.1 because it is a security regression and not a cosmetic one: the module's own argument is that a convention read as noise is a convention that gets abandoned, and a glyph a reader meets on every line of every body spends on line endings the credibility `␛` needs to work.

  **The rule is about the pair, not about `\r`, and that distinction is the whole fix.** Adding `\r` to `keep` was the obvious move and would have reopened #851: alone, a carriage return returns the cursor to column 0, so `real line\rFORGED` overwrites what the tool already wrote — the same class, in one code point instead of five. So `\r\n` is normalised to a bare newline *before* disclosure, and any `\r` no `\n` followed is still `␍`. `\r\r\n` is therefore one line ending and one cursor command, which is what it is. `visible()` itself was not widened — it still only knows characters; the callers normalise the pair, because what counts as a line ending is a fact about lines and not about `\r`.

  **`scrub()` keeps tabs and `flat()` does not, and the asymmetry is the surface rather than the character.** A tab advances the cursor; it cannot reach a column it has passed and it cannot make a line. Inside a fence — text that spans lines already, with the markers owning its edges — it is the author's indentation, and `␉` down the left margin of every code block is exactly the wallpaper this fixes. A flattened field is interpolated into a line the tool owns, and `_board.render_row` flattens every cell of a **column-aligned** board ([#819](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/819)), where a tab is the one C0 character that can imitate the board's own structure without ever making a line. So it stays disclosed there.

  **Method.** TDD, red before green. RED: 12 new tests, **6 failing** — and the 6 that passed unchanged are the point, because they are the #851 pins (a lone `\r`, `\x0b`, `\x0c`, `\x85`, `\x1b`, DEL, in both functions) that the fix had to leave standing. GREEN: 12 passing, full suite **6793 passed / 74 skipped**. Every assertion is on rendered output, not on which branch ran. `test_a_lone_carriage_return_is_still_neutralised_in_a_block` is the one a naive `keep="\r"` fails, and `tests/test_untrusted_crlf_tab_render_854.py` carries the suite's first **CRLF fixture** — a body with CRLF endings, a blank CRLF line and a tab-indented fenced block, rendered end to end through `gh-issue`. Its absence is why this shipped: no fixture in 6775 tests used the line ending most of the web writes.

### Fixed

- **The Windows legs decoded a child's output with the Windows locale codec** ([#856](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/856)). All four Windows legs of `master` went red on `4c38be6` with `TypeError: unsupported operand type(s) for +: 'NoneType' and 'str'`, one failed against 6,309 passed; ubuntu and macOS were green. The `TypeError` and the `UnicodeDecodeError: 'charmap' codec can't decode byte 0x90` reported alongside it in a thread are **one failure, not two**: `test_env_knob_parsing_654.py`'s subprocess helpers ran with `text=True` and no `encoding=`, so `subprocess` decoded with the *locale's* preferred codec — cp1252 on the runners. The decode raised inside `subprocess`'s reader thread, `communicate()` returned `None` for stdout, and the test's `proc.stdout + proc.stderr` produced the `TypeError`. The traceback naming the real cause was demoted to a warning; the exception that failed the test named nothing.

  **The product was never disarmed.** Every one of `supertool.py`'s own 20 text-mode `subprocess` calls already pins `encoding="utf-8"`; the fault was entirely in the test's helpers. The 0x90 arrived from #853 as predicted but not by the predicted route: it is the second byte of a UTF-8 Control Picture (`U+2400`-`U+243F`, so `\xe2\x90\x9b` for `␛`), and it reached stdout because the checkout at that commit is `--depth 1` and the *commit message* of #853 quotes `␛` — which `git-trail` then printed. No file reader was involved, and the test does not touch `presets/_untrusted.py`.

  **The helpers now capture bytes and decode them here**, `utf-8` with `errors="replace"`, which removes the locale from the question rather than naming a better default for it. Reproduced on macOS before the fix by forcing the codepath — `LC_ALL=C PYTHONCOERCECLOCALE=0 PYTHONUTF8=0` against a depth-1 clone of the failing commit — so the repair is pinned by a run that fails on any platform rather than by a platform-gated skip.

  **The regression lock reads the AST, not the text.** Every behavioural assertion here passes on a UTF-8 platform whether or not the decoding is pinned, so `text=True` could be reintroduced from a Mac and go green on every leg its author can run; a guard walks this file's `subprocess` calls and fails on any text-mode call without an `encoding`. It parses rather than greps because the first version of it read the new helper's own docstring — which names the banned construct — as a violation.

  **Not swept beyond this file.** 163 further `text=True`-without-`encoding` call sites remain across 71 other test files. None of them failed on these legs and none is fixed here; widening the diff to them would have put an untested sweep in a release-blocking fix.

- **`gh-branch` guards its ref like every other ref site, instead of relying on call order** ([#852](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/852)). Surfaced by the same audit as #851 and filed separately because it is a different class: **there is no exploit here**. `main()` took `args[0]` unvalidated and handed it to `gh run list --branch <ref>`, where `--output` or `-b` is a flag and not a branch — but `_head_commit(ref)` runs first, puts the ref in a URL path where a leading dash is not a flag, 404s because git refs cannot begin with one, and returns 1. Nothing reached the vulnerable argv.

  **What was worth fixing is that the safety was a property of the call order**: invisible at the sink, undocumented, and removable by any refactor that hoisted the run list or made the head lookup lazy — none of which would have looked security-relevant to whoever made it. That exact shape (a correct, undocumented, untested ordering holding up a security property) has cost this repo before. The four other ref sites — `git/checkout.py`, `git/merge.py`, `_git_common.py`, `gitlab/mr.py`'s `_ORDINARY_REF` — all carry the guard `fix/818-git-arg-injection` established; a new file dropped it.

  **The guard, rather than a test pinning the ordering.** Pinning would make "`_head_commit` runs first" a documented requirement of the security model, which is a heavier thing to owe a future refactor than four lines of validation, and an invariant enforced at every site is one a reader can check without tracing a call graph. Bare `-` is refused too, unlike in `checkout.py` where it means "the previous branch" — there is no previous branch here. The test asserts on the argv `gh` was handed, not on the exit code, so it fails on a removed guard *and* on a reordering that would make the guard the only defence.

- **`gh-issues` — a row no longer leads with a foreign `#N`** ([#842](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/842)). Filed from reading the board, not from reasoning about it: `~ #231 mention +6  6d  4c  #226  [stale]` reads left to right as issue `#231`, and `#231` is `release: 0.10.0`, a merged pull request — the open issue is `#226`, one column later. The row's own number was always correct, in `_row()`'s `ident` slot; the defect was `_linked_cell()` spelling a *second*, unrelated `#N` earlier on the line, in the exact shape the board otherwise reserves for the row's own subject. The next move after scanning a triage board is `gh-issue:<the number just read>`, and that number belonged to a different object — a plausible, well-formed answer about the wrong thing, with nothing in it saying so.

  **Content survives; only the sigil moves.** `mention +6` and the closer's state (`merged`, `open`, …) are unchanged and still lead the cell — genuinely useful, and #782's point still holds: a mention renders as `~`, never as a link. What changed is that the referenced PR number no longer borrows `#`, the board's one sigil for "this row's subject": `~ #231 mention +6` is now `~ PR 231 mention +6`, and `✓ #761 merged +1` is now `✓ PR 761 merged +1`. `#` never precedes anything but the issue's own `ident` on this board, in either cell, so the first `#N` a reader's eye lands on is always the right one to paste into `gh-issue:`.

  **Method.** TDD. RED against unmodified code: 3 failing (`test_gh_issues_row_id_842.py`), asserting positionally on the *first* `#N` token in the rendered row rather than merely that the issue's own number appears somewhere in it — the weaker assertion passes on the broken render, since the number was always present, just not first. GREEN after re-keying `_linked_cell()`; 569 GitHub-preset tests pass, including the pre-existing `#782`/`#780` suites over the same function. Verified live against both `claude-supertool` and `claude-remember` — the exact `#231`/`#226` reproduction from the issue now renders `PR 231`/`#226`.
- **`gh-run`, `gh-pr:status` and `gh-branch` reconcile their leg counts against what the runs declare** ([#804](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/804), [#837](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/837)). One defect at three scopes: a tally over whatever the jobs API handed back, rendered as the whole matrix. [#724](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/724) fixed it for the PR path; the mechanism did not reach the other two, and on the PR path it was reconciling against the wrong source.

  **The premise was checked before anything was designed, and it holds.** `gh run view <id> --json jobs` requests `repos/{o}/{r}/actions/runs/{id}/jobs?per_page=100` with **no filter** — read off `GH_DEBUG=api`, not inferred — and GitHub defaults that endpoint to `filter=latest`. It is the same source #724 caught dipping, not a similar one. Caught in the act on this repo, re-running one failed leg of run 30997282630 while sampling every ~2s:

  ```
  15:57:31  run_view=0   latest=0   all_distinct=14
  15:57:39  run_view=9   latest=9   all_distinct=14
  15:57:49  run_view=14  latest=14  all_distinct=14
  ```

  For ~18s `gh-run` printed `9 total: 5 passed, 0 failed, 4 pending` against a fourteen-leg matrix — internally consistent, externally short — and at the bottom of the dip it printed the worse line: `completed failure, and zero legs ran — GitHub created no job for this run, so nothing was tested`, asserted as established fact about a run that had executed fourteen legs seconds earlier. That sentence now only prints when the second source does not contradict it.

  **`gh-pr:status` had #724's mechanism and it was silent anyway, and that is the more interesting half.** Reported live from the merge gate: PR #822 rendered `checks: 4 total: 4 passed, 0 failed, 0 pending` with no `⚠ NOT ALL GREEN`, on a repo whose matrix is 18. The reconciliation was not broken — it was **circular**. `_declared_legs` summed over the run ids parsed out of the rollup it was checking, so a run *entirely absent* from the rollup contributes nothing to either side and cancels out. Read against the real API, #822's head commit carries two Actions runs declaring 3 + 14 legs; in the window observed only the CodeQL run's legs had reached the rollup, so the declared count was computed over that run alone and reconciled at 3 of 3. A second source read through the first one is not a second source. The run ids now come from the commit (`actions/runs?head_sha=`).

  **Disclosure, not correction, and the three states are kept apart.** None of these ops can invent the missing legs, and padding a tally up to the declared count would be the same defect wearing the opposite costume — so the header still counts what was *read* (`9 total`, never `14 total`) and the gap is stated beside it: `⚠ INCOMPLETE — 9 of 14 legs read`, with the absent legs named, capped at 5 then `+N more` ([#605](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/605)). A second source that cannot be read prints `⚠ TALLY UNVERIFIED` rather than either silence or a number, per `docs/validators.md` §"Declining instead of guessing". On `gh-branch` an unreconciled tally that is otherwise all-green now renders `UNKNOWN`, not `GREEN`: "every leg I managed to read passed" is not "every leg passed", and that op exists precisely to be the authoritative check after a squash merge.

  **The residual is disclosed rather than omitted, because an omitted field reads as "nothing to report".** A run whose jobs GitHub has not created yet declares nothing, so it subtracts nothing and no arithmetic can see it — the exact shape of #822's just-pushed window. `gh-pr` names those runs in words: `not covered: tests — that run on this commit has no job yet, so how many legs it declares is UNKNOWN and none of them are in this tally`.

  **One shared helper, three ops, one PR.** `presets/_declared_legs.py` holds the whole mechanism: distinct job names under `filter=all` (never `total_count`, which counts every row of every attempt — 28 across two attempts of a fourteen-leg matrix, and would report a complete tally as a 14-leg shortfall), `None` on every failure rather than a fallback floor, and the attempt gate. Leaving one op behind would have made it the surface everyone reads.

  **The cost question, answered rather than assumed.** On attempt 1 `filter=all` and `filter=latest` are the same rows, so the second source is bought only where it can differ — measured on three attempt-1 runs of this repo (`latest=14, all_rows=14, all_distinct=14`). `gh-run` and `gh-branch` therefore cost **nothing extra on ordinary renders** and one `gh api` per re-run workflow. `gh-pr:status` pays one unconditional extra call to list the commit's runs; it sits in the merge gate, and a request is cheaper than a merge decided on four green CodeQL legs. `gh-prs` and `git-status` remain unreconciled — a board paying that per PR is a different trade, and neither is a merge gate.

  **#724's pins moved with the code rather than being dropped**: its unit-level tests now target `_declared_legs.legs_for_runs`, and its render-level pins stub one function further out, so everything they assert about how a declared count is treated is still exercised for real.

- **`gl-runners` — the fleet table's `<! silent` no longer lands on a row GitLab calls `online`** ([#814](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/814)). The row marker is a three-way chain, and [#805](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/805) added its `<! UNKNOWN` branch — but that branch is keyed on `stuck`/`unproven`, which count **stranded work**, not liveness. With an empty queue both are `0`, so every unresponsive row fell straight past it into `<! silent` whatever the evidence behind it. The marker that was given a third state kept publishing two, and the row it published wrongly is the [#750](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/750) shape: `21  docker-db-on-disk  project  online  idle  35m  0  0  0  …  <! silent` — `online` and `silent` on one row, with nothing telling the reader which half to believe. The runner had failed `_is_responsive` on `contacted_at` age alone, the throttled field that predicate consults last and loosely for exactly this reason, which is the state `_liveness_unknown` exists to name.

  **The last branch is now keyed on the evidence rather than on the negation.** `<! silent` fires only where `_demonstrably_down` holds — `paused`, `offline`, `stale`, `never_contacted` — so its STATUS column always agrees with it, and the gap goes to the `<! UNKNOWN` the same chain already prints one line above. `<! STARVED` and the queue's own `<! UNKNOWN` keep outranking it unchanged; `unproven` stays in the disjunction because a *down* runner with a live co-candidate is a queue claim, not a liveness one, and it survived mutation until a test was written for it.

  **Nothing was deleted and no threshold was widened.** That was the risk in the fix rather than in the bug: either would have converted "this row is confusing" into "this row tells you nothing", and a fleet check that never flags anything is one nobody reads. Every row that carried a marker before carries one now — a re-key, not a suppression — and `tests/test_gl_runners_silent_marker_814.py` pins the partition in both directions so neither marker can grow into the other's rows.

  **The case for leaving it, and why it did not hold.** [#806](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/806) declined this deliberately and offered the ticket instead: *"silent" states an observation — the heartbeat is stale — rather than asserting a verdict*, unlike the `check host uptime` caveat it was narrowing. That distinction is genuine, and it is why the two are separate contracts. It does not survive this row, because the heartbeat age is **already printed three columns to the left, in `SEEN`**. As an observation the marker is redundant with the table it sits in; the only work it does is verdict work, and beside `online` it is read as one.

- **`gl-mr` — #720's third state reaches the rest of the dashboard** ([#812](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/812)). [#720](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/720) gave the approvals line one line in all three states — an answer, a verified `none`, and a stated `UNKNOWN` naming why — and the sibling blocks in the same file were never brought along. Four endpoints down, the render used to say `Pipeline: none`, `## Comments (0)`, and nothing whatsoever where the threads line and the file list belonged. Every one of those is an absence produced by the tool being read as an absence in the world, which is this repo's most-filed defect class.

  **Two shapes, not one, and the fix for each is different.** A block that prints a *wrong but plausible* value (`Pipeline: none`, `## Comments (0)`) is worse than one that vanishes: a missing line is at least visibly missing, while a wrong number is indistinguishable from a right one. `Pipeline:` and the slim `pipeline:` now print `UNKNOWN — <why>` when the lookup could not complete and no payload fallback exists; `## Comments` prints `(UNKNOWN)` with the reason under it rather than a count it did not earn. `Unresolved threads:`, `Failed jobs:`, `## Files` and `:status`'s job list now print a line whatever happens. The rule applied uniformly: **a section that was asked for prints a line; a section prints nothing only when it was never asked for** — so there is still no `## Files` on an MR that changes nothing, and still no `Failed jobs:` on a pipeline that did not fail.

  **The issue said four vanishing sections; it is seven sites, and one of the four was the other shape.** `:status` carries its own copy of both defects — the slim pipeline line and its named-job list — and it is the poll-loop render, the one read most often and looked at least closely. `## Comments` never vanished at all: its heading is unconditional, so a failed notes fetch had been rendering as a verified zero.

  **Timeouts and unparseable bodies keep separate sentences**, because a timeout is a retry, an unparseable body is a bug report, a non-zero exit is usually auth or permissions, and a missing binary is an install. One `_fetch_json`/`_fetch_array` pair now carries all of them, and `_approvals_line` was refactored onto it with its #720 wording preserved word for word and pinned by a test — those messages are the contract this generalises, not a detail free to be reworded on the way past.

  **The `except` was not widened.** The failure worth preserving is the silent `pass`; catching more things more quietly would make it worse. `_fetch_json` names exactly `TimeoutExpired`, `JSONDecodeError` and `OSError` and maps each to its own printed sentence — there is no bare `except Exception`, so an `AttributeError` in supertool's own logic still surfaces as a traceback instead of a tidy decline. `OSError` is the one genuinely new catch: every sibling block caught the first two only, so an errno mid-render was an uncaught crash — the same thing [#507](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/507) found hiding inside a silent decline.

  **Degraded answers say they are degraded.** A live pipeline lookup that declines while the MR payload still carries a `head_pipeline` prints the real status followed by `! live pipeline lookup declined (…) — status above comes from the MR payload and can be stale`. A paginated file list whose later page failed reports the shortfall as a fetch failure instead of re-blaming the display cap, because `use gl-mr:N:full` cannot retrieve files the tool never received.

  32 tests, driving `main()` end to end with `subprocess.run` stubbed at the `glab` boundary — the half of the defect that matters is what the rest of the dashboard does, and a test scoped to one block would pass on a version that killed the render below it. 25 targeted mutants over the changed rendering logic, all killed.

- **`test_rebase_timeout_names_the_paused_worktree_and_the_way_out` no longer races the merge driver it exists to catch** ([#828](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/828)). The test reddened `master` on `pytest (windows-latest, 3.11)` with `AssertionError: the merge driver never spawned — this test would prove nothing`, on a merge that touched `README.md` and nothing else. `6056 passed` on the same leg: the suite was not disarmed, one fixture was, and the failure was the fixture declining to assert against a hazard it had not managed to set up — the three-state contract (`docs/validators.md` §"Declining instead of guessing") working from inside a test. Nothing was wrong with `presets/git/push.py`, and nothing in it changed here.

  **The defect is that a wall-clock budget cannot express "expire after the helper spawns".** The fixture stalls a real `git rebase` with a real custom low-level merge driver that sleeps, and pins the timeout by monkeypatching `_RECOVER_TIMEOUT` to 5 seconds. `subprocess.run(timeout=…)` starts that clock when *git* starts, so what the test actually asserted was "git reaches the merge driver in under 5s" — true on every macOS and Linux leg, measured here at **0.12s**, and on one loaded Windows runner not true. Widening the number would have bought margin on the same race; the assertion would still have been a statement about runner speed.

  **So the budget is now a barrier rather than a number.** `_BudgetAfterSpawn` is read by `subprocess.run` exactly once, as `_time() + budget`, and that addition happens *after* the child is already running — so blocking there until the helper's sentinel file exists sets the deadline from the moment the helper announced itself. `budget` seconds of real stall are then observed on any runner at any speed. `stage` names which use of the recovery budget the sentinel belongs to (the fetch is 1, the rebase that follows it is 2); earlier uses are ordinary git work with no helper to wait for. Both #640 timeout tests use it, because they are the same fixture family and #777's lesson is precisely that a hazard acknowledged on one test and left on its neighbour is how the neighbour walks into it.

  **The precondition assertion is untouched, deliberately.** Deleting it is the obvious way to make this green and it converts a loud honest failure into a silent vacuous pass — the exact defect class this tracker files more than any other, landing inside the thing that detects it. If the helper never announces itself the barrier gives up after `cap` (30s) and the test fails on its own precondition exactly as it does today. This removes the race, not the assertion. No platform-conditional skip was added either: there is no evidence of a Windows *capability* gap — the driver spawns fine on that platform, it was late once — and a skip would have deleted real Windows coverage of a real hazard to fix a timing bug.
