The source branch is named by whoever opened the merge request, and git refnames permit `;`, backtick, `$`, `&`, quotes, parentheses and spaces.

  Supertool does not execute this block — it prints it for a human or an agent to paste — so this is hardening rather than a fix for a command injection. It is not nothing: the block exists precisely because the reader is expected to run it, and the reader most likely to paste it without reading is the one the op is written for.

  A name matching `^[A-Za-z0-9._/-]+$` still prints bare, so the ordinary block is byte-identical to before; quoting every branch to defend against the rare one would make the common case harder to read, and a command block that is tedious to read is one that gets skimmed instead of checked. Anything else is `shlex.quote`d and a `⚠` line above the block names how many and says to read before running.

  Option-shaped names are the honest limit: `git checkout '-B'` still reads `-B` as a flag, so quoting cannot make it merely a ref. Those are quoted so they look wrong and flagged in the warning rather than passed silently — git itself refuses to create such a refname, so one arriving from the API is worth stopping over. The test asserts what is delivered (`'-B'` plus a warning), not a safety quoting does not provide.

  The issue cited `presets/gitlab/mr.py:822-825` and `presets/github/pr.py:67`. Both were stale: the gitlab lines had moved, and **`presets/github/pr.py` prints no shell block at all** — `gh-pr` has no conflicts-resolution section, so there was one site, not two.

- **Credential headers followed redirects to any host, and the hop was never disclosed** ([#691](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/691), theme T6). All four credentialed preset clients — Hashnode, dev.to REST, the dev.to session-cookie path and Bluesky — called `urllib.request.urlopen` on the default global opener. Its `HTTPRedirectHandler` rebuilds a redirected request stripping exactly two headers, `content-length` and `content-type`, and carries **everything else** to whatever host the `Location` names; `http_error_302` additionally permits an `https` -> `http` downgrade, for up to ten hops. Reproduced on two loopback origins with fake tokens: a server answering `302 Location: http://other-origin/` received the Hashnode `Authorization` header, the dev.to `api-key`, the Bluesky Bearer JWT, and the **full logged-in dev.to session cookie**.

  **The second half was worse than the first.** The redirect is followed transparently, so the attacker's `{"ok": true}` came back to the caller as the API's own answer. Nothing in the output said a redirect had happened at all — a publish op would report success on a post that was never published, to a host that now holds the credential.

  **All HTTP now routes through one opener, `presets/_http.py`.** It follows a redirect only within the same origin — `(scheme, host, port)`, default ports normalised, host compared case-insensitively — with a single asymmetry: an `http` -> `https` upgrade on the default ports is allowed, because it moves the credential onto a *more* protected channel and is what a public API edge does to a caller that typed the scheme wrong. A different host, **a different port on the same host**, an `https` -> `http` downgrade even on the same host, a non-HTTP scheme, and an unparseable port are all refused. The port is part of the origin deliberately: `example.com:8443` may be a dev server, a container, or a tunnel, and none of these four APIs redirects across ports.

  **The refusal is loud and names the destination.** `RedirectRefused` deliberately does *not* subclass `OSError`, so no existing `except urllib.error.URLError` or `except OSError` can absorb a credential-exfiltration attempt into a generic "network error"; every call site catches it explicitly and prints the origin, the status code, the attempted destination and the reason. Two helpers whose contract is "returns `None` on any error" — `gql_safe` and `refresh_session` — now exit instead, as a documented carve-out: a `None` from `refresh_session` falls back to a fresh login and would have hidden the whole event. Same-origin redirects are still followed, and a test asserts it, so the guard is not "no redirects".

  **A permitted redirect is disclosed as well.** Refusing the off-origin hop fixes the credential leak but not the other half of the defect, which is that a different URL answered the question and nothing said so. `_http.urlopen` now prints `NOTE: the request was redirected before it was answered: <asked> -> <answered>` to stderr whenever the final URL differs from the requested one. This is not a hypothetical hop: dev.to answers `/settings` with a same-origin 302 to `/enter` the moment the session cookie expires, and the op reported `authenticity_token not found in /settings HTML — Dev.to layout may have changed` — the HTML was `/enter`, the layout was fine, and the cookie was dead, so the one available message sent the reader after a dev.to redesign that never happened. Both URLs are printed with `repr`, and so are the two in the refusal message, because a `Location` header is remote-controlled text arriving at a terminal and `repr` escapes the control characters that would let it rewrite the lines around it.

  **Measured against the live APIs before choosing to refuse rather than strip-and-follow.** Under the exact request shape these presets send, Hashnode, the dev.to REST API and Bluesky perform no redirect at all, and the only redirect in the set — dev.to `/settings` -> `/enter` — is same-origin and still works. One caveat worth recording: `gql.hashnode.com` *does* answer a cross-origin 301 to `hashnode.com` for some client fingerprints (curl, or an absent `User-Agent`), though not for `Python-urllib`. The edge's cross-origin redirect machinery is live and fingerprint-dependent, so "no redirect today" is not "no redirect tomorrow" — which is the argument for the guard rather than against it.

  **A test fails the build on any bare `urllib.request.urlopen(` left under `presets/`.** A guard that exists but is not wired at every call site is the most frequently repeated defect in this codebase's own review; this one has to be inherited by a fifth integration rather than re-earned. Ten call sites across five files were rewired, including two uncredentialed dev.to resolver hops which keep their best-effort `None` but now name the destination they refused.

  **Not fixed here, filed separately:** `resp.read()` is still unbounded, `timeout=` is still a per-`recv` socket timeout rather than a deadline (a drip server at one byte per 0.5s stretched a `timeout=1` call to 4.7 seconds), and `http.client.IncompleteRead` is an `HTTPException` rather than an `OSError`, so `gql_safe`'s "never raises" contract remains false. Three separate defects that happen to live in the same files; `presets/_http.py` is where they will land.

### Fixed
- **Every C# file yielded zero symbols from `map:`/`between:` — a grammar-name mismatch swallowed by a bare `except`** ([#790](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/790)). `_TS_LANG_MAP` mapped `.cs` to `"c_sharp"`, the name the older `tree-sitter-languages` package used. The installed `tree-sitter-language-pack` calls it `"csharp"` and raises `LookupError` for the other spelling, and `_ts_extract`/`_ts_find_node` caught it with `except (ImportError, Exception)` — effectively bare — and returned `[]`/`None`. Output was byte-identical to a file with no definitions: found while measuring #670's abstract-read corpus, where C# was 0 symbols on 20 of 20 real files against 28-122 for every other language.

  **Not a one-character swap.** `"c_sharp"` -> `"csharp"` fixes `tree-sitter-language-pack` and breaks `tree-sitter-languages` identically. `_ts_get_parser` now tries the requested name and, on `LookupError`, its counterpart in a new `_TS_LANG_ALIASES` table (currently one entry, `c_sharp` <-> `csharp`) — whichever package is installed resolves either spelling.

  **C# was the only mismatch.** Every other `_TS_LANG_MAP` entry was checked against `tree-sitter-language-pack`'s supported-language list by hand, and is now checked on every run by `test_every_ts_lang_map_entry_resolves`, so a future extension added with the wrong spelling fails CI instead of shipping as a silent zero.

  **The bare `except` is narrowed at the two `get_parser()` call sites, not rewritten into a three-state result type across the whole tree-sitter subsystem.** `except (ImportError, Exception)` — the second member makes the first redundant, it catches everything — is now `except LookupError`, the exception `get_parser()` documents raising for an unresolved language name. This is a scoped fix: the two `try` blocks it replaces wrap only the parser-resolution call, not tree parsing or the definition walk, which were already unguarded and already crash loudly on a real bug. Widening the fix to a `skipped`/`finding`/`ok` result type for symbol extraction generally (as `docs/validators.md` §"Declining instead of guessing" does for validators) is a larger design change than this defect calls for and is not done here.

  **What the maintainer asked for regardless of that scope call still had to ship: a grammar that failed to load must not read like a file with no definitions.** A new `_TS_GRAMMAR_FAILED` cache (lang_name -> reason) is populated the one time a grammar fails to resolve under either spelling. `between:` on such a file now returns `ERROR: tree-sitter grammar for '.ext' failed to load (<reason>)` instead of the misleading `symbol 'X' not found`. `map:` — which already falls back tree-sitter -> ctags -> regex — now says `(tree-sitter grammar unavailable for .ext: <reason> — no symbols from any tier)` instead of the bare `(no symbols)` when every tier comes up empty and tree-sitter never actually ran.

  10 new tests in `tests/test_csharp_grammar_790.py`: real C# extraction via `map`/`between:`/`_ts_extract` (skipped without tree-sitter installed), the alias-retry and failure-caching behavior of `_ts_get_parser` (mocked, no tree-sitter required), and the "distinguishable from empty" behavior of `op_map`/`op_between_symbol`.
- **`git-push` could not make a branch's first push after `git worktree add -b`, and reported it as a remote rejection against a target nobody chose** ([#787](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/787)). `git worktree add -b <branch> <path> origin/master` — the normal way an agent starts a fresh branch — leaves `@{upstream}` resolving to `origin/master` via `branch.autoSetupMerge`, which tracks the *start point*, not the new branch. A branch that has never been pushed anywhere under its own name therefore reads as `has_upstream = True`, so `git-push` ran a bare `git push` and let `push.default` pick the target. Git refuses outright on a name mismatch (`push.default=simple`, the modern default), and the receipt rendered that as `NOT PUSHED - REJECTED  branch -> origin/master` — `REJECTED` implying the remote had acted, when the push was never attempted, and `origin/master` naming a target that came from `push.default`, not from anything the caller asked for.

  `has_upstream and remote_ref != branch` is the exact precondition for that fatal, decidable before `git push` is ever invoked — no string-matching git's own wording. `git-push` now declines in the three-state shape before printing a push line at all: names the real upstream it found, says nothing was pushed, and gives both of git's own remedies as one-liners (`git push -u origin HEAD` to push under the branch's own name — the usual first push — or `git push origin HEAD:master` to push onto the tracked branch on purpose). Picking one silently was rejected for the same reason `_refuse_unresolved_remote` (#656) declines rather than guesses: retargeting a tracking config the caller may have set up on purpose, or creating a branch nobody named, are both worse than an extra retype.

  Same root cause — treating a resolvable `@{upstream}` as proof of a same-named remote copy — also sits behind `git-commit`'s post-commit hint (`Next: git push (updates {existing})`), which is misleading on the identical phantom-upstream branch. Not fixed here: `git-commit`'s hint still points at `./supertool 'git-push'` on this path, which now handles the state correctly on its own, so the hint is imprecise rather than wrong.

  New hermetic test (`tests/test_git_push_mismatched_upstream_787.py`) reproduces the trigger with a real bare remote and a real `git worktree add`, red before the fix (`PUSH REJECTED`, `-> origin/master` both present), green after (neither string present, the decline names the branch, the phantom upstream, and both remedies, and nothing reaches the remote).

- **The issue board ranked on *has anyone mentioned this*, not *is anyone fixing it*, so untouched issues sank below claimed ones** ([#782](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/782)). `_linked` was derived from `timelineItems`, which merges two different facts. Found on the board's own first real run: four of 33 rows showed a **merged** PR against a still-open issue — #735/#736, #738/#739, #746/#747, #754/#755 — and not one of those PRs closes the issue it was displayed against. Each merely mentions the number while closing something else.

  **The first fix proposed for this was wrong, and measurement is what caught it.** The obvious move is to split `ConnectedEvent` (a real link) from `CrossReferencedEvent` (a mention). Against the live API both cases are cross-references — `#735 <- #736` (mention) and `#778 <- #781` (a genuine `Closes` that did close it) render identically, because a `Closes #N` body line produces a cross-reference like any other mention and `ConnectedEvent` only comes from the Development sidebar. Splitting on `__typename` would have marked #778 *unlinked*: dropping true links while keeping false ones, strictly worse than the defect.

  **`closedByPullRequestsReferences` is the relationship GitHub itself computes**, verified 4-for-4 (#735 none, #778 -> PR#781 MERGED, #746 none, #782 none). `includeClosedPrs: true` is load-bearing rather than decorative — without it a *merged* closer vanishes and a shipped fix renders as unclaimed, which recreates this bug pointing the other way.

  **Why this outranks a wrong cell.** `_linked` is a rank tier. A merged PR that name-dropped an issue pushed that issue *down* the board, so work nobody had started sorted below work someone had — the board answering "has anyone referenced this" while claiming to answer "is anyone on it". Cross-references are still shown, because they are usually where the adjacent work happened, but as `~ #N mention` and never as a link. On today's board that moved four rows from `✓ merged` to `~ mention` and the unlinked count from 16 to 33 — the second number being the honest one, since every open PR here says `Part of` rather than `Closes`.

  Two tests from #769 changed with it, deliberately: one asserted the timeline *was* the source and now asserts the closer is, and one fixture omitted the new field — which under the three-state contract now means **unknown** rather than empty, so it had to carry the `{"nodes": []}` a real GraphQL response always returns.

- **`radar` spawned and healed and never stopped anything, so every session restart added an emitter** ([#749](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/749)). Nothing in the tool removed a poller. Over a ~28h session that reached **36 live processes against 18 tracked**, each survivor emitting independently: one `mr_opened` arrived 13 times, with the rate ranging 2–14 copies per event. `radar` now reaps before it respawns — before the tiers spawn, not after, since a reap that ran last would be judging this run's own new pollers.

  **The bound is the fix, not the killing.** An over-eager reap is strictly worse than the duplicates it prevents, and this repo has the receipt: in [#511](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/511) three `ps` rows were *inferred* to be duplicate feed pollers and two were killed — they were the watchers for two different MRs, one of them the MR that most needed watching. So the reap acts on one provable fact and no inference: a poller labelled by [#511](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/511)'s `exec` names its own slot in its own argv as whole tokens, so **two pollers naming one slot are duplicates of each other**, and stopping all but one leaves that slot demonstrably covered. It removes a copy; it can never remove coverage.

  **What it deliberately does not touch.** A slot with one poller, tracked or orphan — a lone orphan is the only thing polling its slot, and killing it trades a duplicate nobody has for a blind spot ([#513](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/513)'s trade, backwards). A poller spawned before the labelling landed — it wears its parent's argv and nothing can tell it from the process that forked it, so `pkill -f 'presets/watch/'` stays an operator's call. And anything at all when `ps` could not be read.

  **The decline is the part that keeps this from becoming the defect it fixes.** A clean fleet is silent, a reap names every PID it stopped and the one still polling the slot, and a scan that could not run says `radar: reap skipped — the process scan was unavailable …`. A reaper that cannot see the fleet and renders a healthy board is `docs/validators.md` §"Declining instead of guessing" with a body count. A PID that refuses to die is named with the `unwatch` that reaches it rather than swallowed, and does not cost the rest of the sweep.

  **"Could not this time" and "can never here" are different, and the first draft conflated them.** The scan shells out to `ps`, which on the Windows legs cannot answer at all, so `scan_ok` is `False` there on every run forever — and four pre-existing exact-output tests in `tests/test_watch_radar.py` went red on all four Windows legs of the PR, which is what caught it. Editing those four to accept the line would have absorbed a real defect: a decline that prints unconditionally on a whole platform is not disclosure but furniture, a reader learns to skim it, and the skimming costs them the day it means something on a machine where `ps` *was* there and genuinely did not answer. **And "is there a `ps`" was the wrong question — the second draft asked it and went red on the same four legs.** GitHub's `windows-latest` carries a Git Bash / MSYS2 `ps` on `PATH`: it is found, it runs, and it does not accept `-axww -o`. So a presence check answered "supported", the loud branch fired on every run, and the legs stayed red exactly as before. The question is not whether a binary named `ps` exists but whether **this machine's `ps` can answer our invocation**.

  `transport.ps_scan_supported()` now probes, on exit status and spawnability only — never by matching a failure message, which would be brittle in precisely the way this bug was. No `ps` on `PATH`: permanent, nothing spawned. Otherwise run `_SCAN_PS_ARGV`, the same constant `_ps_rows` uses so the probe cannot drift to a question nothing asks; exit 0 means supported. If it failed, run a bare `ps` as a control — if *that* exits 0, this machine's `ps` works and is refusing our invocation specifically, which is evidenced, repeatable tomorrow, and therefore permanent. Anything else — a spawn error, a timeout, a `ps` that fails both probes — is unclassifiable and stays **loud**, because a machine where scanning normally works and is broken right now must not be written off as a platform limit. The verdict is reached once per process, since it describes the machine and the failure path should not pay two subprocesses per tick to re-learn it.

  The four tests are untouched, and were verified against a `ps` shim built to the runner's actual shape — exits 0 bare, exits 1 for the scan's flags. Under it, all four pass and `ps_scan_supported()` is `False`. Under a `ps` that fails *everything*, the verdict is `True` and all four fail again: the loud path still working, and proof the four were not made to pass by weakening them.

  **Not a new op, and not wider than radar.** The mechanism sits in `dispatcher.py`, which already owns spawning and killing; the policy is radar's alone. A `reap` op would be a second destructive command next to `unwatch`, and reaping from `watch:SOURCE:ID` would let a single-slot invocation signal processes the operator never named. Radar is the fleet-level reconcile that runs at session start, which is the moment the accumulation happens.

  **Also documented: a batched `kill $PID_LIST` against these processes silently no-ops** — exit 0, every process still alive, `-9` included, while a per-PID loop stops them fine. The mechanism is undiagnosed; the consequence is that the batched form looks like it worked. `unwatch` always signalled one PID at a time, the reaper does too, and `test_reap_stops_one_pid_per_call` pins it so neither can quietly grow a batched form.

  **18 tests, none of which may signal a real process** — the scan is a dict, liveness is a set, `_stop_pid` is a recorder. Nine of them fail against a reaper that kills the wrong survivor, keeps a non-deterministic one, kills a lone orphan, kills through an unavailable scan, or swallows a refused kill.

- **A flaky Windows gofmt decline reddened `master` twice in 35 minutes, and the fix is to make the deficiency visible rather than to skip it** ([#777](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/777)). `test_gofmt_check_unformatted_file_is_hard_error` demands the error code `formatting`; on Windows the adapter intermittently declines with `adapter` instead. The split rules out the obvious explanations: the toolchain is present (the test is guarded by `skipif(not shutil.which("gofmt"))`, so running at all proves gofmt was there), and it is not a platform rule either — on one commit, one runner image, py3.9 and py3.12 passed while py3.10 and py3.11 declined, and on the run before that only 3.11 did.

  **What is actually wrong is one layer down.** The sibling test has skipped win32 since it was written, with the reason `"gofmt adapter has encoding issues on Windows"` — a real product defect recorded in a skip reason and never diagnosed. Every Windows user of the `gofmt` validator still has it, and its unguarded neighbour walks into the same thing, which is how it reached `master`.

  **The change here does not skip, deliberately.** Skipping would make CI quiet and leave Windows users with a broken validator and no record of it. Instead the Windows-and-declined case `xfail`s with a message naming #777, so the row stays visible in the report; POSIX still asserts `formatting` strictly, so a genuine regression fails loudly. **#777 stays open for the adapter fix** — this only stops a red board training everyone to discount that file, which is the standing exception that later hides a real failure in the same place.

  Verified on macOS, where the Windows branch cannot execute: 6303 passed, 0 failed, coverage 89.39%. That run proves the POSIX path is unchanged and nothing more — the behaviour being accommodated is by definition not reproducible on the platform this was written on.
- **`gh-issue` printed nothing when it could not find out whether any PR was linked, and nothing reads as "there are none"** ([#780](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/780), item 1). Two silent paths: a non-zero `gh pr list` fell out of an `if returncode == 0` with no `else`, and `except (TimeoutExpired, JSONDecodeError): pass` swallowed the rest. Either way the reader sees no `Linked PRs` line.

  **The reading has an action attached, which is what makes it expensive.** "No linked PR" is the signal that an issue is unclaimed, so the next step it invites is delegating the work. That has already happened on this tracker — work re-delegated onto an already-merged fix because a list did not distinguish *none* from *could not ask*. Same class as #414, #445/#454, #459, #477/#482, #487, #486, and the framework has had the vocabulary for it since #406: three states, not two.

  The section now renders `Linked PRs: unknown — could not query (<reason>)`, carrying gh's own first stderr line where there is one. `none` survives untouched for a lookup that succeeded and genuinely found nothing, which is a real answer and must stay distinguishable from the new one.

  **Two further defects on the same lines are deliberately *not* fixed here**, both re-scoped on the issue after measurement, because they change what the op means rather than whether it admits ignorance. The lookup uses a full-text `--search`, so a PR whose body merely mentions the number counts as a link — `gh-issue:770` reported #774 as linked, and #774 closes #760. And `gh pr list --search` defaults to **open** PRs, so a merged closer is invisible: `gh-issue:778` reports `none` while #781 merged and closed it. False positive and false negative on one call. Both want `closedByPullRequestsReferences` with `includeClosedPrs: true` — verified 4-for-4 on this repo, and the flag is not optional, since omitting it recreates the false negative exactly.

  `tests/test_github_issue_linked_unknown_780.py` pins all three silent paths, plus two controls that pass against the broken code — a genuine empty result must still say `none`, and a populated one must still list the PRs — so the fix cannot satisfy the tests by simply never saying `none` again. The lookup was extracted to `_print_linked_prs` first, unchanged, so the RED run failed on the assertions rather than on a missing attribute.
- **`gh-issue`'s "Linked PRs" was wrong in the two directions item 1 had deliberately left alone** ([#780](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/780), items 2/3). `gh pr list --search N` matched the number anywhere in a PR's title or body, so a PR that merely *mentioned* the issue counted the same as one that closed it — live: `gh-issue:770` reported `#774` as linked, when `#774` closes `#760` and only name-drops `#770` in prose. In the other direction, `gh pr list` defaults to open PRs, so a **merged** closer never showed up at all — live: `gh-issue:778` reported `none` while `#781` (MERGED) is the PR that actually closed it, the more dangerous of the two since it reads as "nobody is on this" about an issue someone already fixed.

  **The maintainer's own first fix for item 2 — read the issue timeline — was proposed on the issue and then measured wrong**, before any code shipped: a `Closes #N` line in a PR body produces the identical `CrossReferencedEvent` a plain mention does, so the timeline conflates the two exactly like `--search` does, just differently. That measurement is what re-scoped items 2 and 3 onto one fix.

  **`closedByPullRequestsReferences(includeClosedPrs: true)`** is the field that discriminates, verified 4-for-4 on this repo and already trusted by `gh-issues`' board ranking (#782). `gh-issue` now asks GraphQL the same question `gh-issues` does, so the two ops agree instead of answering it two different ways (#628's theme, sitting in the op right next to the one it names). `includeClosedPrs: true` is not optional — omit it and a merged closer vanishes, which is exactly the false negative this fixes, wearing a different name.

  **Switching to GraphQL introduced one new way to fail, and it gets the same three-state treatment as the rest of the section.** `gh pr list` resolved its repo implicitly from the cwd's remote via `--repo`; `gh api graphql` has no such flag, so owner/name has to be embedded in the query text. When neither a repo target nor the issue's own URL can supply one, the section prints `unknown` rather than silently reporting `none`.

  **The cap is 20, not the 5 `gh-issues` uses for its board rows**, and hitting it says so. A board multiplies its per-row cap across the whole queue in one call; this op is answering about the single issue a reader named and most likely wants the complete list, so the cap here is generous and a truncated result is disclosed (`showing the first 20 — there may be more`) rather than silently cut — a silently truncated list being this repo's recurring defect.

  `tests/test_github_issue_linked_prs_search_wrong_780_items_2_3.py` stubs `_gh` to prove both the false positive and the false negative against the new query, plus the new owner/repo failure mode and the cap disclosure; `test_github_issue_linked_unknown_780.py`'s two payload-shaped tests were updated from the old `pr list --json` array to the GraphQL envelope the new lookup actually returns — the three-state contract they pin did not change, only the shape of the data underneath it.

- **The three `claude-log` ops advertised a usage pattern without `:raw`, while their own docs page showed one with it** ([#778](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/778)). `docs/presets/claude-log.md` documents `claude-log-tail:UUID[:N][:raw]`; the preset shipped `claude-log-tail:UUID[:N]`. Both are rendered to a reader — the docs table and the `ops` listing — and they disagreed about how to call the same op.

  **Why this particular flag.** `:raw` is the opt-out from #760's redact-by-default, which changed what all three ops print. The one flag a reader most needs after a breaking output change was the one missing from the string they would copy. The `description` did mention it, so it was not undiscoverable — but a usage pattern that omits the escape hatch is a surface that appears to answer the question and does not.

  **The general rule this does not adopt, and why.** The tempting invariant — every flag a description names must appear in its syntax — was checked against every shipped preset before being written, and it is wrong: `git-status` documents `:porcelain` as an alias of `:full`, and `gh-job`/`gl-job` document `:errors` as an alias of `:fail`. In all three the syntax deliberately carries the canonical spelling while the description names the alias. That is a convention, not a defect, and a test asserting the general form would have failed three correct ops. `:raw` is not an alias; it is the only spelling of a real flag.

  `tests/test_claude_log_raw_syntax_778.py` pins both surfaces — that the shipped syntax advertises `:raw`, and that it matches the docs table cell exactly — plus a guard on the fixture's own premise, so that a change to the table's formatting fails as "this test no longer reads the table" rather than silently asserting over an empty parse. Verified that the `@payload` route registry is unaffected: none of these syntax strings carries the `:::` separator, so `_fields_from_syntax` returned an empty field list before and after, and #770's pin stays green.

- **`git-investigate` blamed a path without a `--` separator, so a file whose name begins with `-` reported "Blame: unavailable"** ([#759](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/759)). Found by the sibling sweep in [#756](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/756) and filed rather than fixed there, so that a read-only defect got its own review instead of riding inside a data-loss fix. The other three git calls in the same file — `log`, `diff HEAD`, `diff --cached` — all pass the path after an explicit `--`, and `presets/git/blame.py`, whose whole job is this operation, does too. This one call did not, so `git blame --line-porcelain -foo.txt` handed `-foo.txt` to git as an option.

  Nothing is lost — `blame` only reads. What is lost is the answer, in the shape this repository keeps filing: the section renders `## Blame: unavailable`, which reads as *this file has no blame history* when what happened is that the argument was never treated as a file. The `os.path.exists` check upstream makes such a path less likely to arrive, not impossible; a file named `-foo.txt` exists happily.

  `tests/test_git_investigate_separator_759.py` runs a real git over a real repo holding a real `-dash.txt` and asserts the blame section renders, with an ordinary-path control that passes against the broken code so the fixture cannot pass vacuously. A third test pins the invariant across all four calls — every git invocation carrying the path must place it after a `--` — so a future edit to any of them is named rather than silently producing one empty section.

- **A `syntax`-string edit for readability could silently delete an op's entire `@payload` route — now pinned by a test, and diagnosable when it happens** ([#770](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/770)). Found as a near-miss while writing the #751 entry below: appending a clarifying parenthetical to `git-commit`'s `syntax` string removes the op's `@payload` route entirely, because `_fields_from_syntax` derives the field registry by parsing `syntax`, and its identifier guard correctly refuses to register a field name that isn't a clean `[a-z][a-z0-9_]*` — a syntax string carrying prose or punctuation (like git-resolve's `(SIDE: ours|theirs|both)`) must not register a route no payload key could ever match. That guard is right. What was missing is that its `[]` is silent toward the one audience it actually affects: the caller who types a routeless op's `:@-` gets a normal unknown-route error, but the editor who just broke the route gets nothing — no error, no warning, a suite that stays green, and an `ops` listing and README that still describe a payload route that no longer exists.

  **Two changes, doing different jobs.** `tests/test_at_file_route.py::TestPayloadRoutePin` builds the real `@file` registry from this repo's actual `.supertool.json` + shipped presets and pins it against a frozen `EXPECTED_PAYLOAD_ROUTES` map; any future syntax edit that adds, removes, or reshapes a route now fails at test time, naming the op — the exact signal the #751 near-miss didn't have, and the cheapest of the mitigations the issue considered. Its failure message also reports `_AT_FILE_DROPPED_ROUTES`, a new diagnostic table populated the moment the guard fires on a `':::'`-bearing syntax string (as opposed to a syntax string with no `':::'` at all, i.e. an ordinary read-only op — the common and correct empty case, and deliberately kept out of this table so it doesn't drown the signal in noise). Nothing reads `_AT_FILE_DROPPED_ROUTES` at runtime and no warning fires on ordinary invocation; it exists purely so a failing pin test — the one moment someone actually needs it — can say "this op's syntax carries `':::'` but its fields were discarded" instead of leaving the editor to rediscover `_fields_from_syntax`'s docstring from scratch. `tests/test_at_file_route.py::TestDroppedRouteDiagnostic` reproduces the #751 scenario directly: takes git-commit's real syntax string, appends the exact kind of parenthetical that incident added, and asserts the route disappears *and* is now recorded as dropped.

  **Rejected:** making `_fields_from_syntax` raise, or warning on every dispatch. Both convert a silent route loss into a louder failure for every user of the op, not just its editor — the second one specifically would fire on every process that builds the registry for as long as `git-resolve`'s intentionally-prose syntax exists, which is exactly the false-positive noise the issue warned a naive warning would produce. Documented at the point of editing too: `docs/contributing.md`'s custom-op schema table now states that `syntax` is parsed, not just displayed, for any op using `':::'`.

- **A Conventional Commits message no longer ends up in `git add` as a pathspec** ([#751](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/751)). Reproduced on master before anything was changed: `git-commit:fix(rector): link the CSV importer to its new test` died with `ERROR: git add failed: fatal: pathspec ' link the CSV importer to its new test' did not match any files`. supertool's single-colon CLI splits on **every** `:`, so the subject arrived as `MESSAGE='fix(rector)'` plus a PATH of `' link the CSV importer to its new test'`. Every `feat:` / `fix:` / `chore:` subject has that shape, which makes this the common case failing, and the error named neither the message nor the tokenizer — it pointed at staging, which was never involved.

  **This is a refusal, not a re-parse.** The issue offered folding the trailing segments back into the message when they do not resolve to real paths. That discriminator cannot be trusted, and the repo already contains the counterexample: [#324](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/324) added handling for a PATH that is *gone from disk* because it is an already-staged deletion. `git-commit:::chore: drop a:::a.txt` after a `git rm` is a correct call whose path does not exist, and an existence test folds it into the message. The two wrong guesses are not symmetric either: guessing *path* when it was prose fails loudly at `git add`, which is the bug being fixed; guessing *message* when it was a path commits whatever happened to be staged, under a mangled subject, and prints a success receipt with a HEAD-after sha for it. A fix that silently commits a different fileset than the caller intended is worse than the bug, so nothing is folded back.

  **What fires, and how narrow it is.** Before anything is staged, each PATH is checked against two questions: is it path-shaped (no whitespace, no quotes — glob magic like `src/*.py` still counts as a path), and does git know it (on disk, tracked, or an already-staged deletion). Only a PATH failing *both* is treated as spilled message text, and then the call is refused with nothing staged and nothing committed. A path-shaped path git does not know — a typo, a file not created yet — still gets git's own pathspec error, because supertool has no basis for deciding it was really prose.

  The refusal does the work the fold-back would have: it prints the reconstructed message and the two routes that carry a `:` intact, `git-commit:::MESSAGE[:::PATHS...]` and the `git-commit:@-` payload heredoc, with any genuine trailing paths kept as paths in both. One round-trip, and the caller pastes a command that is already correct.

  **What did not change**: the documented triple-colon form, the `@payload` file/stdin/JSON routes, a colon-free message with paths on the single-colon route, `--no-edit`, the staged-deletion path, and the pathspec error for an unknown path-shaped argument. Each is pinned end-to-end through `supertool.py` itself in `tests/test_git_commit_colon_message_751.py` — through the tokenizer, because a test that hands `commit.py` a pre-split argv cannot tell the two routes apart and would have stayed green either way.

  One thing nearly broke while documenting this: the op's `syntax` string is not decoration — `_fields_from_syntax` parses it to build the `@file` field registry, and a parenthetical appended after `[:::PATHS...]` makes the derived field name fail its identifier check, which silently *removes* `git-commit`'s payload route. The guidance went in the op `description` instead; the syntax string is byte-identical to before.

- **`around:PATTERN:PATH` misread as a line number now says so, instead of blaming the cwd** ([#734](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/734)). `around` is `PATTERN:PATH[:N]`; its sibling `around_line` is `PATH:LINE[:N]` — same op-name family, opposite argument order. Type one when meaning the other and PATH resolves to a digit string that is never a real path, and the failure printed `wrong CWD? Prefix the call with cwd:PATH` — the one piece of advice that cannot help, because the cwd was never wrong. Following it just produces a second failure shaped like the first.

  When the unresolved PATH is all-digits, the error now names the actual mistake and the fix: `` `around` takes PATTERN:PATH[:N] — '681' was read as the path. Did you mean: around_line:PATTERN:681[:N] ``. The generic `wrong CWD?` hint still fires for every other not-found case — including a path that is genuinely missing and non-numeric, and a file that happens to be named with digits and exists (which reads normally, unaffected). The heuristic is a suggestion only: it never changes what runs, and the call still fails loudly.

  Checked whether the same shape reaches other pattern-first ops (`grep`, `grep_around`): neither has a sibling with reversed argument order, so there is nothing to suggest — `around`/`around_line` is the only pair with this exact confusion.

- **`git-resolve:both` applied a union to source files and reported only that it completed** ([#744](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/744)). A rebase left four conflicted `.py` renderers; `git-resolve:both:all` — the reflex on a repo where the conflicting file is nearly always `CHANGELOG.md` — answered `Resolved: 4 | Failed: 0`, with `markers: clean | validate: ok` on every one. All four were wrong. Three of the four hunks had one side's whole `if comments:` block sitting next to the other side's replacement of it, so the union rendered every comment **twice**. That is not a syntax error, so it passed `ast.parse` and it passed the receipt's own validator digest, and the next step in the workflow is `git rebase --continue`.

  **`_union_file` is not the defect and is unchanged.** It mirrors git's `merge=union` driver exactly as documented: drop the markers, keep both blocks, drop the diff3 base. The defect was applying a strategy whose correctness depends entirely on the file type, and then reporting only completion. On a changelog a union is two entries and correct; on a function body it is the statement run twice, or two `def`s of the same name where the last silently wins.

  **`both` is now refused per file on known source extensions**, and the file is left conflicted — not merely warned about. A warning is one line in a receipt whose previous line already said `validate: ok`; leaving the markers in place means git itself refuses `rebase --continue`, which is the only signal that does not depend on reading. Refusal is **per file, never per set**: `git-resolve:both:all` on one `CHANGELOG.md` plus four `.py` still resolves the changelog and holds back the four, because refusing the whole set would only train the override. The receipt gains a `Refused: N` segment, a `⊘` line per file, and the three ways forward (`git-conflicts`, a side pick, or `force`).

  **Two ways through, and both are deliberate.** `git-resolve:::both:::PATH:::force` unions anyway — the point of the guard is not that a union of source is impossible but that it cannot be reached *by habit*: `force` is a token you can only type after reading the refusal. When it is used, the tally says so — `Resolved: 5 (4 source file(s) unioned — 'both' concatenates; verify manually)` — because the syntax digest above it will still say `validate: ok` about a file whose statements now run twice. The second way needs no flag at all: a path that `.gitattributes` declares as `merge=union` unions silently, since a repo that set the attribute has already answered this question. `git check-attr` is asked once for the whole batch, and any failure to ask it refuses rather than unions.

  **The extension list is a heuristic and is wrong in both directions.** It covers mainstream program text (`.py .js .ts .php .rb .go .rs .java .sh .sql`, ~32 in all) and misses an extensionless `bin/deploy`; it also catches a `.sql` file that is nothing but INSERT rows, where a union would have been fine — that case costs one token, the miss costs a silently doubled loop. Structured-data formats are deliberately **out**: a unioned `.json`/`.xml` usually fails the syntax validator that already runs, so it is not the silent class this guards. `.yaml` is a genuine gap, being both a data format and silently duplicable. The authoritative discriminator, where a repo states one, remains git's own `merge=union` attribute.
- **`git-checkout:PATHSPEC` destroyed uncommitted work unrecoverably, then reported `Working tree: clean`** ([#756](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/756)). If you lost work to this, here is what happened. `git checkout <arg>` is two operations sharing one name: given a ref it switches branches, given a pathspec it restores those paths from the index. `checkout.py` validated that the argument did not begin with `-` ([#150](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/150), on the grounds that a prompt-influenced `REF` is reachable) and then handed the string to git, which chose between the two readings by what the string happened to name. A pathspec needs no flag and no special characters, so the `-` guard could not see it — it is the *absence* of anything suspicious that made it work.

  **This is the only unrecoverable operation in the surface.** The restore writes no reflog entry, no stash and no object, so unlike every other mistake supertool can make there is no artifact anywhere to recover from. `git-checkout:work.txt` overwrote `work.txt` with its indexed content and exited 0.

  **The guard is two layers, and the second is the one that guarantees it.** An argument git resolves as a path but not as a commit is now refused before anything runs, naming what was passed and pointing at `git checkout -- <path>` for anyone who genuinely wanted the restore. Independently, every switch is issued as **`git checkout <ref> --`**, which forbids the pathspec reading outright — so an argument the first check did not anticipate (a glob, say) fails loudly instead of restoring files. The refusal exists for the message; the `--` exists for the guarantee.

  **There is deliberately no pathspec-restore op.** Restoring a file is a legitimate thing to want, but discarding uncommitted work is not a *mode* of a branch-switch command selected by string shape, and adding a new destructive op inside a data-loss fix would give it less review than a new destructive op deserves. If it is wanted it should arrive named, documented as discarding, and reviewed on its own.

  **An argument that is both a ref and a path now resolves to the ref, always.** A `docs` branch can sit beside a `docs/` directory; "whatever git picks" is how the original bug reads to a caller. The `--` pins it, and the receipt says `also names a path here; taken as a ref` rather than leaving the choice invisible.

  **The receipt was a second defect and is fixed too.** `Working tree: clean` was true only because the op had just cleaned it, and the identical before/after shas — the single available signal that no branch change occurred — were printed unflagged directly above it. Identical shas now print `# no branch change occurred — already on <branch>@<sha>`, whatever else happened.

  **Not one of the nine existing tests ever passed a file path**, which is why this shipped (the issue says six; the two checkout test files hold nine test functions, fourteen with the locale parametrisation — the count differs, the point does not); the regression suite (`tests/test_git_checkout_pathspec_756.py`) runs a real git against a real working tree with real uncommitted content and asserts on **the bytes on disk afterwards**, the only assertion that distinguishes a refusal from a very tidy deletion. The sibling sweep across all thirteen `presets/git/` ops found no second instance of the shape: every other place a raw argument reaches git is confined to a non-ref position, passed after `--`, `rev-parse`-verified, or checked against an allowlist. One residual is filed rather than fixed here — `git-investigate`'s `blame` call omits the `--` its own `git-blame` preset uses, which is read-only and so costs a wrong read, never data.
- **`.env` was on the exclude list for two years and was never once excluded** ([#691](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/691), theme T5). `_DEFAULT_EXCLUDE_PATHS` has carried `.env/`, `.env.local/`, `.env.production/` since [#146](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/146), under a comment stating that the trailing slash catches a *file* of that name as well as a directory — added expressly so `grep`/`glob`/`tree`/`map` could not surface a token into an LLM's context. `_is_excluded` honours that exactly as documented. **Nothing ever called it on a file.** `grep:FAKE:.` over a tree of `.env`, `sub/.env`, `.env.production` and `.netrc` returned all four, contents included, on both engines. This was wiring, not logic: the *directory* entries (`.ssh/`, `.aws/`, `.max/`) worked the whole time, which is most of why it went unseen.

  **Four call sites, four independent misses.** `_grep_candidates` applied the matcher to `dirs[:]` and filtered `files` on extension alone. The rtk-delegated path emitted `grep --exclude-dir=NAME`, which by definition cannot skip a file. `_collect_files` (map) and `op_tree` had the same dirs-only shape. And `_glob_files` disagreed **with itself** — its `glob.glob` half post-filtered files, its `os.walk` half did not, so `glob:.env*` hid `.env` and `glob:**/.env*` listed it. `tests/test_exclude_paths.py` tested directories and only directories, which is the third reason this shipped.

  **The list grew, along a stated boundary.** Added: `.env.*` (replacing the four hand-enumerated variants), `.netrc`, `_netrc`, `.npmrc`, `.pypirc`, `.git-credentials`, `.pgpass`, `.my.cnf`, `.htpasswd`, `.dockercfg`, `id_rsa*`/`id_dsa*`/`id_ecdsa*`/`id_ed25519*`, `*.pem`, `*.key`, `*.p12`, `*.pfx`, `*.jks`, `*.keystore`, `*.ppk`, and supertool's own documented cwd token files `.hashnode-token`, `.devto-token`, `.bluesky-app-password`. A file earns a place only when holding a credential is its entire purpose — an exact name or an unambiguous key-file shape. There are deliberately **no name-fragment heuristics**: `*secret*`, `*token*`, `*password*` hit source and test files constantly, and a search that silently skips your own code is a worse failure than the one the list prevents. `.bluesky-handle` and `.hashnode-publication-id` are public identifiers and stay visible.

  **Exclusion now discloses.** `(2 results in 2 files, scanned 2 files, 10 files hidden by exclude-paths, limit 30)` — on `grep`, `glob`, `map` and `tree`. A hide that leaves no trace is indistinguishable from a file that was not there, which is the same silent-failure shape `scanned N` and the `TRUNCATED` marker were both added to close. It reads zero — and prints nothing — on the ordinary call.

  **Only credential entries are counted, and that distinction is load-bearing.** Build output, caches and VCS metadata are dropped in silence. The first cut of this counted every hidden *file*, using file-versus-directory as a proxy for noise-versus-credential — a proxy that holds only because almost every noise entry happens to be a directory. In a git **worktree** `.git` is a gitfile, so the proxy broke exactly where the agent work happens: `(1 results in 1 files, scanned 575 files, 1 files hidden by exclude-paths, limit 3)` on every search in the tree, against `(2 results in 2 files, scanned 575 files, limit 3)` for the same query in a normal clone. The count is the *entire* justification for hiding a file silently — a `*.pem` in a fixtures directory is survivable because the header says something was dropped — and a number that is never zero is one readers learn to skip, at which point the search that says `2` because a real `.env` was hidden looks like all the others. Nothing is hidden any less: `.git` still never appears in a result. A project's own `exclude-paths` entries always count, since supertool cannot know whether one is noise or a credential and over-disclosure is the safe direction.

  **`_is_excluded` gained glob and negation entries**, which is what makes `*.pem` expressible at all and `.env.*` safe to state. `!.env.example` (plus `.sample`, `.template`, `.dist`, `.defaults`, `.schema`) keeps the committed placeholders greppable — people read those to learn which keys exist, and hiding them is the over-broad direction of this same defect. Negations are evaluated first and are final, so the answer never depends on tuple order. This also fixes a latent silent no-op: a wildcard in a project's `exclude-paths` was normalised to `*.key/`, which fnmatches nothing and no longer looks like a glob either.

  **Both engines are fixed, and proven to agree.** Single-segment entries now reach grep as `--exclude-dir=NAME` **and** `--exclude=NAME`. But the argv is the optimisation, not the guarantee: whatever rtk returns is re-filtered through the same `_is_excluded` the native walker uses, so a grep that ignores `--exclude` — or an rtk release that rewrites the argv — still cannot leak. System grep cannot express a negation, so when the list carries one, wildcard entries are withheld from the argv entirely and left to that filter; if the filter drops anything, the search is redone natively, because the delegated header's count, its `limit + 1` truncation probe and its `?` denominator all describe the unfiltered set. One test asserts the two engines return the identical path set; another drives the real system `grep` with the argv supertool built, so a broken flag cannot pass unnoticed.

  **A path you name yourself is still searched.** `grep:PATTERN:.env` works, as `read:.env` always did. Naming the file is deliberate; gating it would buy nothing and break the case someone meant. The list guards the side effect — a search aimed elsewhere that walks over a credential on the way. `no-exclude` remains the full escape hatch.

- **Two presets echoed a remote error body that could contain our own credential** ([#691](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/691), theme T5). `devto/_rest.py` and `bluesky/_atproto.py` print up to 200 characters of the remote error body so a failure says something useful — but the body is written by the other end, and the other end is free to quote the request back. `bluesky._atproto.create_session` is the sharp case: **its request body is the app password**, so a PDS or proxy that echoes the request replayed the password onto stderr. `hashnode/_graphql.py` already had `_scrub_token`; that helper is now mirrored in both, applied to the credential each function already holds (`app_password`, the session JWTs, `api_key`) and to devto's `bad JSON response` path.

  **hashnode's own scrubbing had an ordering bug.** The caller scrubbed the *formatted* string, by which point the body had already been cut to 200 characters — a token straddling that cut survived as a fragment, because the fragment is not the string `replace()` is looking for. A 190-character body followed by the token leaked its first ten characters. Scrubbing moved inside `_format_http_error`, before truncation, in all three.

  **The existing security tests were structurally blind to this**: every one fed an error body that did not contain the credential, so a formatter with no scrubbing at all passed them. The new tests feed bodies that do.

- **Six more adapters read a non-zero exit as a finding about the file** ([#753](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/753)). The sibling sweep #745 filed rather than half-fixed, now applied with each tool's own diagnostic vocabulary rather than PHP's. `xmllint`, `bash-check`, `node-check`, `gofmt-check`, `terraform-check` and `cargo-check` all turned "the tool exited non-zero" into `code: "xml"`/`"syntax"`/`"formatting"`/`"compile"` at file level, so a path typo, a wrong flag, an unreadable file, a broken `Cargo.toml` and a `terraform` that could not parse its own command line were each published as a defect in a file the tool had not looked at.

  **`node-check` was the worst, and it fabricated a location.** `re.search(r":(\d+)(?::(\d+))?\b", out)` took the first digit-colon sequence *anywhere* in the output. A missing file makes node print `node:internal/modules/cjs/loader:1386` as its very first line, so the verdict was a syntax error at **line 1386** of a three-line source file, with `source_context` rendered for it. The location is now matched against the path node resolved, and node's `node:` module scheme and indented `    at ` stack frames are excluded outright — neither can be a line in the file.

  **`terraform-check` was making a specific claim that was usually false.** `terraform fmt -check` uses *distinct* exit codes and the adapter looked at none of them: `3` means the file needs formatting (with the diff on stdout), `2` means terraform failed — an unparseable `.tf`, a path it could not find, a file it could not read, a flag it did not recognise. All four arrived as `code: "formatting"` with the message "file needs terraform fmt formatting". An HCL syntax error is now a located `code: "syntax"` finding, because telling its author to run `terraform fmt` is advice that cannot work — fmt is what could not parse it.

  **`gofmt-check` did no parsing at all**, and gained a line number rather than only losing a false one: `if r.returncode != 0` emitted one hardcoded error, so `stat <path>: no such file or directory` became a Go syntax error, *and* a genuine `subject.go:3:12: expected ')', found '{'` was reported with `line: null` and no source context. It now reports line, column and context.

  **`ruby-check` is a seventh, and #745's sweep recorded it as CLEAN.** It was the model for the empty-stderr case and modelled the non-empty one not at all, which the sweep noted as a "residual". The residual reproduces on the first thing anyone would try — `ruby -c /nope/x.rb` prints `ruby: No such file or directory -- ... (LoadError)`, which carries no `file:line:` and so fell through to `code: "syntax"`. Fixed here rather than left because a table said clean.

  **The marker, per tool, is a located diagnostic in that tool's own format** — `file:LINE:` for xmllint, `: line N:` for bash, node's resolved-path header, `path:line:col:` for gofmt and cargo's short format, `on <file> line N` inside terraform's box-drawn Error block. Anything else on a non-zero exit routes through `validators/common/refusal.py:tool_fault()`. **Ambiguity still falls towards the file** (#745's rule): a `SyntaxError` banner node's report shape cannot place is still a finding, reported with `line: null` rather than a borrowed number. Nothing is dropped in either direction — every branch is `ok: false`, `count: 1`, rollback-triggering — and because the code is `adapter`, the verdict is also not cached, so a toolchain broken for ten minutes no longer freezes a red keyed on the file's content hash.

  **Each adapter's classifier moved to module level** — `parse_diagnostics` in `xmllint`, `bash-check`, `ruby-check` and `gofmt-check`, `diagnostic_line` in `terraform-check` and `node-check`, alongside `cargo-check`'s existing `_parse_errors`. Behaviour is unchanged; `main()` delegates. The reason is that the rule has to be testable where the fixture cannot run: a fake binary on `PATH` **cannot** intercept these adapters' spawns on Windows, because they pass an extensionless program name to `CreateProcess`, which appends `.exe` and ignores `PATHEXT`. So the rule is driven in process against real captured transcripts on every platform (41 assertions), and only the spawn-and-decode path is POSIX-only — with a per-binary guard asserting the fake was the one that answered, since an unintercepted shim yields a clean verdict or a real finding rather than an error. Written up in `docs/contributing.md` §"A fake binary on `PATH` cannot intercept an adapter's spawn on Windows".

  **`cargo-check`'s cross-file attribution is filed separately** ([#754](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/754)). `cargo check` analyses the whole crate and `_parse_errors` does not filter by `src_file == file`, so a sibling's error is attributed to the file under validation. That is a misattribution rather than a misclassification, and the fix would *remove* errors from a caller's list where every change here only relabels them. Different risk, different review.

- **`php` falling over was indistinguishable from the file being broken** ([#745](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/745)). `phplint` mapped every non-zero exit from `php -l` to `code: "parse"`. But `php -l` exits non-zero for two unrelated reasons: the file does not parse (exit `255`, with `Parse error:` and `Errors parsing <file>`), and PHP could not produce a verdict at all — a fatally failing extension, or a path it could not open, which is exit `1` and the single line `Could not open input file: <path>`. The second was published as a syntax error in a file nothing had read.

  **The invented line number was the concrete half.** `line` came from `re.search(r"on line (\d+)")` across the whole of stdout+stderr, and PHP prints startup warnings *before* any parse error, terminated with `in Unknown on line 0`. So a broken extension plus a genuinely broken file reported the parse error at **line 0**, and rendered `source_context` for a line that does not exist. The line is now read from the diagnostic line itself, so a warning cannot donate its location to a finding.

  **The rule.** php is credited with having spoken about the file when its output carries a lint diagnostic — the linter's own `Errors parsing <file>` verdict line, or a `Parse error:` / `Fatal error:` banner (`php -l` reports compile-time fatals as the latter; some SAPIs prefix both with `PHP `). Anything else on a non-zero exit is `code: "adapter"`, with a message naming the exit code and the raw output — and naming the *absence* of output explicitly, since a silent crash is exactly the input a message template built around the output renders as an empty string.

  **Ambiguity falls towards the file.** A located `in <file> on line N` with no recognised banner still counts as a finding: a PHP whose message shape nobody anticipated must not have its findings relabelled out of a caller's error list. What makes that direction cheap is that neither branch drops anything — an `adapter` result is still `ok: false`, still one error, still rollback-triggering, and its message is fully legible to a reader, so only the label and the invented line move. It is a finding rather than a `skipped`: the binary was found and the process ran, and a fault routed to the third state is a validator quietly reporting clean.

  **`code: "adapter"` is now excluded from the validator cache**, which is what makes the reclassification worth more than a label. A verdict the adapter never obtained is not a function of the file's content, and the cache key is a content hash — so a toolchain broken for ten minutes would freeze a red that replays on every later run until someone edits the file, the shape of the June-2026 2100-poisoned-entries incident. Before this, these exits reached the cache wearing a finding's code and were cached on purpose. `validators/common/refusal.py:tool_fault()` builds the message and is the sibling of `skipped()` on the other side of the same distinction: one analyser declined before running, the other ran and fell over. Documented in `validators/SCHEMA.md` ("`adapter`: the reserved code for 'no verdict was obtained'") and `docs/validators.md` §"Declining instead of guessing".

  **Six sibling adapters carry the same defect and are filed separately** rather than half-fixed here — `xmllint`, `bash-check`, `node-check`, `gofmt-check`, `terraform-check` and `cargo-check` all turn an unrecognised non-zero exit into a file-level `syntax`/`xml`/`formatting`/`compile` code. `ruby-check` already gets it right and was the model. `phpstan`, `pyright`, `psr` and `stylelint` trust parsed output rather than the exit code and were already clean.

- **An adapter assertion recorded none of the verdict it had just received** ([#725](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/725), [#717](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/717)). Two issues, one defect. `assert out["ok"] is True` fires as `assert False is True`, and every adapter under `validators/` had *already stated its reason* — `tool`, `ok`, `count`, `errors`, `duration_ms` — into a payload the assertion discarded. There are roughly a dozen routes to `ok=False` (tool absent, tool present but not executable, the adapter's own internal budget expired, the file genuinely did not parse, no file argument) and that output separates none of them. Both reds were Windows-only, single-occurrence, and never reproduced, so in both cases the one occurrence *was* the entire diagnostic budget and a bare boolean spent it on nothing.

  **`tests/_adapter_verdict.py` is the rendering, once.** `verdict()` replaces `json.loads(r.stdout.strip())`, whose failure mode is a `JSONDecodeError` naming neither the adapter, the exit code, nor the stderr holding the traceback; `assert_ok`/`assert_declined` render the payload into the failure message. The defensive half is the point rather than a nicety: this formats a structure it does not own, so an `errors` that is a string, an entry that is not an object, a missing `errors` key and a crash before any JSON each produce text **naming what could not be read**. A diagnostic that renders blank on a shape it did not anticipate would be this repo's house defect reproduced inside its own fix. Output is bounded at three errors and 200 characters a field, and a capped list says how many it hid ([#719](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/719)'s rule).

  **`duration_ms` is now in the message, and it is the discriminator.** A decline at 4ms is a missing tool; a decline at 30000ms is the adapter's own budget firing, which — after [#716](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/716) made every outer budget exceed the inner one — reads as a verdict rather than as a `TimeoutExpired`. On Windows the phplint outer budget is `(30 + 10) × 3 = 120s` over a 30s inner, so that path is mechanically reachable and was previously indistinguishable from a syntax error.

  **68 call sites across 15 files**, the whole external-tool adapter family, converted in one pass so the lesson is not half-applied again — which is #725's actual complaint: #716 replaced the bare assertion in `test_valid_ruby` and shipped a *new* bare one in a file it created in the same PR. `test_a_file_that_adopts_the_convention_adopts_it_everywhere` walks the AST of every test importing the helper and fails on any message-less `["ok"] is <bool>` left in it. It deliberately does not police files that have not adopted it; the remaining ~78 sites are pure-Python or monkeypatched adapters whose reds reproduce locally, and they are filed separately rather than swept in silence.

  **Neither cause is established, and the issues' own leading candidate is refuted as stated.** #725 proposed that the adapter's tool might be absent from that runner image — but the test is `skipif(not shutil.which("php"))`, so an absent PHP would have *skipped* the leg, not failed it. What remains is unranked and unknowable from one line of output: the adapter's internal 30s budget firing on a leg already observed running at 2.2× ([#658](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/658)); a `which`/exec divergence under Windows process semantics; and one the issue did not list — `phplint.py` maps *any* non-zero exit from `php -l` to `code: "parse"`, so a PHP startup failure is currently indistinguishable from a syntax error in the file. That last one is filed on its own terms. The next occurrence of either red will name itself.
- **A timeout verdict reported `0.0s` elapsed against a 10s budget** ([#727](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/727)). `FAIL (timeout 0.0s > 10s)` was the sole diagnostic on a reproducible CI failure, and its two halves cannot both be true — read literally it says the op was instantaneous, which points at the budget rather than at the process. The issue proposed three mechanisms (unstarted timer, catch-all branch, spawn outside the timed span) and a later comment retracted all three; the real one is narrower. `_elapsed_since` returns a hard `0.0` when `SUPERTOOL_DETERMINISTIC_TIME=1` ([#643](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/643)), and `tests/conftest.py` sets that for the whole suite, inherited by every subprocess a test spawns. Re-derived against master before designing: `sleep 5` under a 1s budget renders `FAIL (timeout 0.0s > 1s)` frozen and `FAIL (timeout 1.0s > 1s)` unfrozen, from one call site.

  **The freeze stays; the absence announces itself.** The other available fix was to exempt this renderer from the freeze, and it was rejected on #643's own reasoning: that fix was placed *at the renderer* "because a call site is a thing to remember", so carving an exemption back out at one call site restores the thing to remember — and puts a varying field back into rendered output, where a future test comparing two renders would go green on the jitter. A timeout render now says `FAIL (timeout after its 10s budget - elapsed frozen, deterministic-time mode)`: the three-state contract applied to a number rather than to a verdict, and still byte-identical across runs. Nothing changes outside the test suite, where the real elapsed is printed exactly as before.

  **A timeout whose elapsed is under its budget is now named as a reporting bug.** `subprocess.run(timeout=T)` raises `TimeoutExpired` only once T has elapsed, so a measured span below the budget is not a near miss or a fast machine — it means the path did not measure the interval that expired. That case renders as a defect in the reporting path rather than as a result, which is the guard that would have caught this whole class at the source.

  **The un-frozen path is still pinned by a test**, via the `monkeypatch.delenv` pattern `test_render_determinism_643.py` already established — so the objection that a diagnostic frozen under test is a diagnostic no test can observe does not hold here. The failure that produced the original message is *not* fixed and was not the defect: `git-status` genuinely exceeded its 10s budget on `windows-latest, 3.12` while 3.9/3.10/3.11 passed the same code in the same run.
- **`git-push` could not make a first push in a repo whose remote is not named `origin`** ([#656](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/656)). On a branch with no upstream there was nothing to read the remote from, and the fallback *was* the assumption: `_split_upstream` returned `origin` and the push itself was built as `-u origin HEAD`. In a repo cloned with `git clone -o gitlab`, or any fork/`upstream`-only layout, that names a remote which does not exist and the push dies on `fatal: 'origin' does not appear to be a git repository` — on the one push where `-u` is the entire point. Reproduced end to end against a real bare remote before anything was changed.

  **The remote is resolved now, down a ladder that starts with git's own.** `branch.<name>.pushRemote`, then `remote.pushDefault`, then `branch.<name>.remote` — the exact keys, in the exact precedence order, that a bare `git push` consults. Honouring them means this op targets whatever plain `git push` would target, and a user who has configured a non-`origin` remote has already answered the question. They are taken verbatim rather than validated against `git remote`, because git accepts a URL there too and its own error for a name that resolves to nothing is clearer than a second-guess of it.

  **Then `origin` if it exists, then the only remote if there is exactly one.** The two rungs can never disagree — with a single remote named `origin` they give the same answer, and with a single remote *not* named `origin` the first does not fire — so the ordering is about the fork layout, where `origin` is your fork and the other remote is the canonical, plausibly public one. Preferring `origin` there is both the safer branch to create and what `git push` already does; being more conservative than the command being wrapped, on the commonest multi-remote layout, would be a second surprise rather than a fix.

  **Two or more remotes, none named `origin`, nothing configured: it refuses.** There is no correct guess available, and guessing wrong on a *first* push does not fail — it succeeds, creating a branch on a remote nobody named and aiming every later push at it via `-u`. That is the one outcome here worse than an error message, so the op declines by name, lists the candidates, and gives both one-line ways out (`git push -u <remote> HEAD`, or `git config branch.<name>.remote <remote>`) — `docs/validators.md` §"Declining instead of guessing". A repo with no remotes at all, and a `git remote` that could not be asked, are separate states with separate sentences; the second never pushes on an answer git did not give.

  **The receipt stopped naming a remote it had not confirmed.** `Upstream: none — setting on first push (origin)` now names the resolved remote and how it was chosen, the upstream-lookup-failed disclosure no longer says "tracks something other than origin", and `_success_receipt`'s fallback line no longer prints `origin/<branch>` at a repo without one. `_split_upstream` and `_success_receipt` both take the resolved remote as a **required** argument — the discipline [#642](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/642) put on `_post_push_advisories`, so a new call site cannot reintroduce the hardcode by omitting it.

  **What #642 already did, and what it left.** It removed the `origin` assumption from the stale-base check by following the branch's *real* upstream and disclosing a skip — but only on the path where an upstream exists to follow. The no-upstream half stayed, which is why the two were filed apart. The three-state vocabulary #642 and [#675](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/675) established is reused verbatim here rather than reinvented.

  **One behaviour change worth knowing.** When the upstream lookup itself does not complete *and* `git config`/`git remote` cannot be asked either, the op now declines instead of pushing to `origin` on the assumption. That is git being unable to answer anything, not a transient; the previous behaviour was a blind `-u origin HEAD` on a branch that may already have tracked something else. Where `git config` still answers — the ordinary case — the resolver reads `branch.<name>.remote` and finds the *real* upstream remote, which is strictly better than the guess it replaces.

  Counted rather than trusted: the issue named two sites, and `push.py` had **five** — the two executable ones plus three user-visible strings asserting `origin` as fact. Nine more live outside this op (`gitlab/mr.py`, `git/checkout.py`, `git/commit.py`, `git/conflicts.py`, `git/status.py`) and are untouched here.

- **`gh-job` blamed the ID for a log GitHub had never written** ([#723](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/723)). GitHub writes a job's log on completion, so a `404` from `actions/jobs/<id>/logs` has four causes and the op rendered all four as `Check the ID. Use gh-run to list jobs first` — both of which the reporter had already done, against an ID the *job* endpoint resolved in full. Reproduced live before touching anything: a `queued` job and an `in_progress` job both return `gh: HTTP 404` with an `<Error><Code>BlobNotFound</Code>` body, identical to each other; a nonexistent job 404s on both endpoints with `gh: Not Found (HTTP 404)`. The premise held, which on this tracker is not the way to bet.

  **The extra API call turned out not to exist.** The design question — pay for the job object on every call, or only after a log 404 — was moot: `main` already fetches it before the log, for the header's name/status/conclusion/run id, and then threw the distinguishing fields away. So all four states separate at **zero additional requests on every path**, including the happy one. `status != completed` says the log is not written yet and to retry; `conclusion` of `cancelled` or `skipped` says no log was ever written and none ever will be, which is the only row whose correct response is to stop looking; a completed job with no log names its conclusion and `completed_at` and calls the log expired or purged; and a job endpoint that 404s keeps the original message, correctly.

  **The fifth row is a decline.** That metadata fetch can itself fail — rate limit, auth, timeout, a torn JSON body — and then the state is genuinely unknowable. It says so and names all three remaining possibilities rather than picking the likeliest (`docs/validators.md` §"Declining instead of guessing"). Guessing "cancelled" there would be this defect with more confidence attached.

  **Every branch stays `ERROR:` and exit 1.** Softening a log that could not be read into an empty log or an ok is the direction a fix like this is most likely to be wrong in, and a test asserts it across three states and `:raw`. Non-404 log failures (401, 403, rate limit) keep their existing messages untouched — `_format_error`'s probe order was preserved verbatim when the classification moved into `_gh_error_kind`.

  **Empty is not absent.** Adjacent and fixed in the same pass: a log fetched successfully that is genuinely 0 bytes used to fall through to the error-pattern search and print the unmatched-failure banner over nothing. It now says `## The log is empty — the fetch succeeded and returned 0 bytes` and names the job state. `:raw` already handled this; no other mode did.

  `gh-run` carries a `_format_error` of the same shape and was checked: it is **not** defective. That op only fetches run metadata, where a 404 does mean the ID is wrong.

- **`gl-mr`'s approvals line had three failure modes and no way to say "I could not tell"** ([#720](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/720)). The issue was filed for the loud one — `.get()` on a parsed `/approvals` response with no type check, `AttributeError` if the payload is a list — and **that half could not be reproduced**. GitLab documents `GET /projects/:id/merge_requests/:iid/approvals` as returning a JSON *object* carrying `approved_by`, on every tier including Free (the array-shaped neighbours are `approval_rules` and `approval_state`, which this op never calls), and the reporter hit it constructing a fixture rather than against a live instance. The guard ships anyway, because it costs one branch and the blast radius of being wrong is the entire render — but it is not what closes this.

  **The reachable half was the quiet one on the same call, and it reproduces in one command.** `glab api` on any failure exits 1 with **empty stdout** — `glab api projects/.../approvals` on an unauthenticated CLI does exactly that, verified here — and the old code only rendered anything at all inside `if returncode == 0`, with `except (TimeoutExpired, JSONDecodeError): pass` under it. So the most ordinary failure available on this call silently deleted the `Approved by:` line, while `Reviewers:` and `Assignees:` two lines above it print `none` specifically so that absence is signal. `Approved by: UNKNOWN — <reason>` now prints in every such state, `glab`'s own stderr included.

  **Declining rather than defaulting, deliberately.** Suppressing the crash into `approved_by = []` would have been a one-word diff and would have printed `Approved by: none` — a wrong answer where there had been a missing one, which is the defect class this tracker is mostly made of (`docs/validators.md` §"Declining instead of guessing").
