For the same reason a payload with **no `approved_by` key at all** declines instead of defaulting: the field is documented as always present, so an object without it is not an approvals response, and `{"message": "404 Not found"}` read as "nobody approved" is precisely the trade being refused.

  **The sweep found the same shape 150 lines below, in the linked-issue block.** `issue_data.get("title", …)` on a non-object payload is the identical `AttributeError`, and its `returncode != 0` path printed nothing at all — an MR whose description names an issue, and no issue section, indistinguishable from an MR that names none. It degrades to `Issue: #N — details unavailable (<reason>)` now, and its `labels`/`assignees` joins no longer assume element types they never checked. `OSError` is caught explicitly at both sites on #507's authority: that issue was filed as one silent decline and the thing found inside it was an unlisted `OSError` that was fatal.

  Remaining, and filed separately rather than folded in: the other seven `json.loads` sites in this file all check the top-level type and then call `.get()` on the *elements* without checking those. Same class, one level down, and no live trigger established for any of them.
- **The session-start hook handed every supertool checkout a wrapper that worked for nothing** ([#711](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/711)). `hooks/session-start.sh` linked `./supertool` at `$CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT/supertool.py` unconditionally. Start a session inside a checkout of this repo — the working convention here is a worktree per issue — and the config, `presets/` and the scripts they point at resolve from the checkout while the core comes from the plugin install. That is precisely the pair `_mixed_tree_pair` exists to catch ([#678](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/678)), so every custom op through that wrapper answered `SKIPPED: ... comes from a different supertool tree` and exited 1. The wrapper was present, looked right, and worked for nothing. #678 is doing its job here — nobody got a wrong answer — but the hook was manufacturing the very case, and before #678 the same setup ran the plugin's core against a branch's presets in silence.

  **The obvious fix was refused.** "Prefer a local `supertool.py` when one is present" walks straight back into [#688](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/688), which hardened this hook specifically so it would not trust or execute whatever sits at that path in the working directory. "There is a file named `supertool.py` here" is not the claim "this is a checkout of this repo", and any marker that closes that gap — a signature grep, a `.supertool.json` key — is still the checkout deciding what the hook links, which is the shape #688 removed.

  **So the hook creates nothing there instead, and says why.** The two designs are not symmetric under a wrong answer, and that is what decided it. A false positive on "do not link here" costs a convenience symlink that would not have worked anyway — the user is told to run `python3 supertool.py`, which is unambiguous, needs no wrapper, and is already what the README recommends first. A false positive on "link the local file" links and later executes a stranger's code. The test is therefore a **refusal test, not a trust test**: the hook asks only whether a wrapper created *here* would be a broken one. Nothing local is read, verified, executed or linked.

  **The check mirrors `_mixed_tree_pair` rather than approximating it**: walk up for the `.supertool.json` that would actually be loaded, then look for a `supertool.py` beside it. The comparison is bash's `-ef` — same device and inode, resolved through symlinks — so the plugin install running a session in its own directory is the same file on both sides and correctly not a mix, including when the plugin root or its binary is itself a symlink, which a `realpath` string compare on directories would have got wrong.

  **`tests/test_session_hook_plugin_path.py:102` did not need to change**, and that is a result rather than an omission. It pins the link target for a project that is *not* a supertool checkout, where the target was never wrong; this change adds a case that file never covered instead of editing an assertion to match new behaviour. The new tests bind the hook to the core rather than to its own source: an ordinary project and the plugin install itself must each end up with a wrapper that then runs a real custom op through the real core to exit 0, so a hook that simply stopped linking anything fails. One control builds by hand the link the hook used to create and shows the core declining it, which keeps the refusal's premise pinned — it goes red if #678's decline is ever removed. And #688 is re-guarded directly: a local `supertool.py` that would write a marker file if executed is neither run nor linked.
- **Three CI guards were green for a reason other than the one they claimed, and one of them was the file held up as the fix for the other two** ([#731](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/731), [#730](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/730)). `.github/workflows/tests.yml` is 183 lines of which roughly two thirds are comments explaining each decision, so a substring match against the file cannot tell the code from the prose about the code. `assert "oven-sh/setup-bun" in text, "nothing installs bun any more"` passed for months because the string survived inside the comment recording that the action had been dropped for `npm i -g bun@1.3.14` — the test kept green by the prose documenting the change it existed to notice. **Two more of the same shape were found in the sweep and neither had been reported.** `assert "--no-cov" in notifiers` matched the comment `--no-cov because pyproject's addopts carry a whole-suite 86% gate`, eleven lines above the flag; and `test_ci_job_timeouts_722.py`'s per-test-timeout guard searched the whole job block, where the comment justifying `--timeout=30` quotes it twelve lines above the flag. Deleting the real `--timeout=30` from the run block left that file **14 passed** — verified by deleting it — in the file [#729](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/729) landed as the structural alternative to exactly this defect.

  **The fix is not a fresher needle.** `"npm i -g bun"` re-arms on the next rename and re-arms immediately on a comment mentioning the old command. `tests/_workflow_parse.py` now parses the workflow into jobs, steps, and each step's `uses:`/`env:`/`run:`, and every guard asserts against those — so a comment can say anything at all and no assertion moves. It extends the indentation parser [#729](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/729) wrote one level deeper rather than growing a second one, and `test_ci_job_timeouts_722.py`, `test_ci_non_python_coverage_557.py` and `test_ci_encoding_546.py` all read through it. PyYAML stays unused for the reason [#729](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/729) gave: CI installs four packages and `yaml` is not among them, so importing it would make every guard skip on all fourteen legs. The parser is fixture-tested, including a fixture whose comments name `oven-sh/setup-bun`, `--no-cov` and `tsc --noEmit` while its one step runs `echo hello`.

  **[#730](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/730)'s second claim was its own finding.** The same guard named two of the five `test_notifiers_claude_channel_*.py` files the job runs, so three could have been dropped from CI without it noticing — while [#605](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/605) and [#609](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/609) had already arrived without being added, and had therefore never executed in CI. Listing names cannot close either direction, so the guard now asserts the set the workflow passes to pytest equals the set `git ls-files` reports; the gate-routing check over those files was widened the same way.

  **Every fix was verified by breaking the product, not by reading.** Delete the bun step, drop a channel file from the step, remove `--no-cov`, unset `SUPERTOOL_REQUIRE_JS`, gut the type-check, add `windows-latest` to the matrix, delete `--timeout=30`, raise it above the job ceiling: each turns the new guard red, and four of them left the old assertion green.

- **Two numeric bounds admitted the value their own test name ruled out** ([#731](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/731)). `test_ops_disclosure_is_one_line_not_a_second_listing` bounded the disclosure at `<= 2` lines, admitting exactly the second line the name forbids; a disclosure that wraps now fails it, and did not before. `test_dot_repeat_dd_same_call` asserted `len(lines) <= 1` after deleting two lines of three, which also accepts nought — an over-deletion, or a `j` that stops advancing the cursor so `.` repeats in place, both passed. It now asserts the surviving line is `beta`, which additionally catches deleting the wrong line. Both verified red against a broken product and green against the old bound.
- **`gh-pr` printed the total comment count and then showed ten of them, with nothing in between saying so** ([#719](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/719)). `## Comments (25)` followed by ten comments is not a partial render a reader can spot: the header hands over a number they have every reason to trust, so the ten read as *the* discussion. They are the ten most recent, which means the fifteen withheld are exactly the ones carrying the original objection or the "do not merge until X". [#698](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/698) is the same defect in character form; this is it in count form.

  **It was not a design problem — it was a call site that never adopted the convention.** `gh-issue`, in the next file over, has printed `N of M shown, K earlier truncated — use :full to fetch all` since [#681](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/681). `gh-pr` did not, the same shape as [#263](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/263): the abstraction was there and one adapter had not taken it up. So nothing new was designed. `presets/_body.py` gains `comments_heading()` and `comment_cut_notice()` next to the character-cap pair it already held, and the wording is byte-identical to what `gh-issue` was already printing.

  **Shared rather than copied, on a count of the sites.** The per-comment cut marker had four (`gh-issue`, `gl-issue`, `gh-pr`, `gl-mr`); the count heading had three (`gh-issue`, `gl-issue`, `gh-pr`). Both clear the bar where a helper stops being premature, and they are two halves of one convention — splitting them across "shared" and "copied" would reintroduce exactly the drift the module exists to prevent. `gl-issue` keeps its own heading: it carries a second dimension (`N human shown, K system skipped`) that does not fit the signature and is not what #719 is about. Its cut marker is shared.

  **One deliberate behaviour change beyond the fix.** An uncut list now prints the bare `## Comments (3)` on both ops; `gh-issue` used to print `## Comments (3 of 3 shown)` on its default path and `## Comments (3)` on `:full` for the same whole list. Saying nothing when nothing was cut is what makes the *absence* of a marker load-bearing — the contract `gl-issue`'s related-MR list already uses ([#635](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/635)).

  **Which ten are shown did not change, and that was a decision.** The issue argues the oldest comments are the ones that matter, and it may be right — but that is a selection argument, not a disclosure one, and changing the selection inside a fix for an invisible cut would answer a question nobody asked while hiding it in a diff about honesty. Filed as [#738](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/738) with both sides written out, plus a third option neither side had — keep the head *and* the tail with the gap marked in the middle, which is structurally what `gl-mr` already does, and which would make the answer "the GitHub ops should render comments the way the GitLab one does" rather than "flip the slice".

- **`gh-pr` also cut each individual comment at 500 chars with no marker anywhere, and `gl-mr` did the same on the `:full` path that is documented as uncapping comments** ([#719](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/719)). Both were found by checking the two open questions #719 attached rather than assuming them, and both are #698's defect on sites #698 did not reach. `gh-pr` never had a per-comment marker and its `:full` — added *by* #698, because the description disclosure had to name a flag that worked — only ever reached `desc_max`. `gl-mr` is the sharper one: its comment *count* disclosure was already correct (a byte-budgeted render with an inline gap naming the hidden count and size), so nothing there needed fixing, but `_render_note` sliced every note at `COMMENT_MAX` unconditionally — including under `:full`, while `docs/presets/gitlab.md` promised `:full` uncapped "the file list and the comments". It uncapped how many notes printed, never how much of each. A `:full` that half-works is the escape-hatch problem rather than a smaller version of it: it stops the reader looking for another way to the text. `_render_note` now takes the cap as an argument and `:full` passes `None`.

  **The tests read rendered output, not helpers.** A helper test passes on a version where `pr.py` never calls it, which is the bug being fixed. All fourteen run through `main()` and assert on stdout, including a cross-op test that pins `gh-pr` and `gh-issue` to the same heading string. Three are guards rather than fixes and say so: the selection guard (the last ten are still the ten), the zero-comment control, and a `gl-mr` regression guard asserting its existing gap-marker convention is *not* replaced by a fourth one. Verified RED first — 11 of 14 failing on the pre-change tree, the three passes being exactly those guards.

- **`_git` existed ten times across `presets/git/`, which is why fixing it once did not fix it** ([#704](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/704)). [#685](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/685) repaired `status.py::_git` so a git call that does not answer stops speaking as though it did. Correct fix, shipped — and it left every other copy untouched, including `conflicts.py`, which then printed `No conflicted files.` and exit 0 over live `<<<<<<<` markers until [#703](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/703). Nothing in that diff, those tests or CI said a word about it. `presets/git/_git_common.py` has held the shared helpers since [#302](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/302); the problem was never that the module did not exist, it was that eight of eleven presets had not adopted it.

  **The issue as filed was wrong about that, and the correction changes the work.** It called for *creating* a shared module and counted four copies. There were ten `_git` definitions plus two raw `subprocess.run(["git", …])` calls inline in `blame.py`, which no count had ever included, against a module that already existed. Two arguments in the issue thread also had to be dropped rather than carried: the security framing (`_git_common._git` not scrubbing `GIT_*`) was retired an hour before this work started by [#714](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/714)/[#732](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/732) scrubbing `os.environ` in `_main`, which covers every child process regardless of route; and the table of which presets had adopted was close to inverted, because every preset imports `use_utf8_stdout` from the module and only two imported `_git`. The reporting and timeout grounds carry the issue on their own.

  **Consolidating meant choosing a contract, because the copies did not agree.** Three budgets — `status.py` 5, six presets 10, `_git_common`/`merge.py`/`commit.py`/`push.py` 30 — none chosen together, none pinned by a test. **10** wins as the shared default: it is what six of the ten already used and the only one that was ever reachable from `SUPERTOOL_GIT_TIMEOUT`. `git-status` keeps 5 as a stated per-preset number, and the four calls that genuinely need longer — the push (300s), its fetch/rebase recovery (120s), the commit that runs a hook chain (30s), the merge that runs merge drivers (30s) — now say so at the call site, where 300 is legible and a module default is not. Two behaviour changes fall out and are documented in `docs/presets/git.md`: the plumbing calls in `git-commit`/`git-push` drop from 30s to 10s, and ten ops gain a `SUPERTOOL_GIT_TIMEOUT` they previously ignored. **The explicit argument now wins over the environment** — `status.py` had the reverse precedence, reachable only through its own default, and keeping it would let `SUPERTOOL_GIT_TIMEOUT=5` cap `git-push`'s 300s and report a push still in flight as failed.

  **`push.py` is where this was not mechanical.** `_git` no longer raising `TimeoutExpired` is not a cosmetic change in a file whose entire design is the [#675](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/675)/[#663](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/663) invariant. Six sites caught the exception; had they been left, a timed-out push would have fallen through to the ordinary `returncode != 0` branch and printed `Status: PUSH REJECTED ✗` about a push that may well have landed — the refactor re-introducing, silently, the exact claim those issues exist to prevent. Each now tests for `TIMEOUT_RC`. Two more would have degraded quietly rather than loudly: `_upstream_ref` and `_remote_sha` would have turned "could not ask" back into "answered nothing", and the stale-base check would have blamed a missing ref and advised a `git fetch` that fixes nothing.

  **The guard is worth more than the refactor, and it is a guard, not an import check.** `test_only_git_common_spawns_a_git_process` is an AST sweep in the shape of `test_every_mutable_global_…`: it fails the build when an eleventh `subprocess.run(["git", …])` is written, at authoring time. A wrapper that *delegates* is explicitly fine — `status.py` still adds each call to its `INCOMPLETE` ledger, `blame.py` still owns its "git not available" wording — because what must happen in one place is the spawn, where the timeout, the encoding and the three-state answer are decided. Alongside it the behaviour test asserts the post-condition rather than the seam: an expired call reports `124` and says so from all eleven presets. Asserting `from _git_common import _git` would have passed on an import nothing called. Both were verified RED against the pre-change tree — 14 failures across 10 presets — before the adoption landed.

  **The shared module stays stateless, by design and by test.** `presets/_env.py` is the precedent for a module every preset imports under one name, and also the warning: its module-level ledger became process-global under pytest and needed a `PRESET_RESET_GLOBALS` entry to stay honest ([#689](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/689)). A third test fails if a list, dict or set ever appears at module level here, because the registration is the part that gets forgotten. One consequence surfaced in the suite and is worth naming: `_list_conflicts` now lives in `_git_common` and therefore calls `_git_common._git`, so a test that rebinds only `resolve._git` no longer intercepts it — `test_git_resolve.py` patches both, and said why.

- **The `GIT_*` scrub covered preset ops only, so a built-in `read` under a leaked `GIT_DIR` described the file using another repository's index — and git exited 0 doing it** ([#714](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/714)). [#692](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/692) is not wrong; it is **narrower than it reads**. `scrub_git_env` had exactly one call site, inside `_resolve_custom_op` — the function that launches *preset* ops. `read` is a built-in and never passes through it, and core spawns git in six of its own places, twelve `subprocess.run` calls, none of which passed an `env=`: `_run_git_ignore_query` (the gitignore pruning behind `glob`, `grep`, `tree`, `map`), `_path_meta_suffix` (the ` m`/` ?`/` !` marker on every `read`), `_branch_probe`, `op_workspace`'s Git section, `op_validate_staged` and `op_format_staged`. Not hypothetical: git exports `GIT_DIR` to every hook it runs, `.githooks/pre-commit` invokes `./supertool`, and `read` is the most-used op in the tool.

  **Why this half is nastier than the half #692 fixed.** There, the op acted on the wrong repo and said nothing. Here git **exits 0** — it is answering correctly, about a different repository — so [#705](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/705)'s `git?` decline has no failure to catch, and the marker is not absent but *wrong*. Measured under `GIT_DIR` pointing at a second repo, all three returning 0: a tracked, locally modified file read ` ?` — untracked — instead of ` m`; `workspace` reported the other repository's branch and `file status: ?? tracked.txt`; and `validate_staged`, the op a pre-commit hook exists to run, printed `no staged files` **with a file staged**. That last one is the worst shape available: a hook that validates nothing and exits 0.

  **The issue's own reproduction did not hold, and the correction is load-bearing for the test.** It relayed an agent's observation that a gitignored file's ` !` became `` — and recorded that the reporter could not reproduce it. It does not reproduce, for a reason that decides the fixture: a bare `GIT_DIR` leaves the **work tree** at the cwd, `.gitignore` is read from the work tree, so the ignore verdict is identical either way. Nothing ignore-derived can discriminate. What flips is anything read out of the **index** — and only when the two repos are **asymmetric**. Two synthetic repos carrying the same paths hide it completely, because the foreign index answers the same thing about the same file. That is the signature of the failed reproduction, and the structural gap stood on its own regardless.

  **The fix moves the boundary up rather than adding a second one.** #692 argued deliberately for one chokepoint, on the grounds that a preset is protected by being *launched* rather than by remembering to opt in. The argument was right and the level was one too low: `_resolve_custom_op` is the launcher for half the op table. `os.environ` is now scrubbed once in `_main`, before argv is even parsed — the process launcher — so presets stay covered (their `dict(os.environ)` copy is taken afterwards and is already clean) and every core spawn is covered without knowing it exists. Guarding each of the twelve spawn sites instead would be exactly the partial adoption [#704](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/704) describes, with spawn thirteen written without the guard. **Still one call site**, and a test pins both that there is one and that it is the launcher.

  **The notice moved with it**: once per call, printed ahead of the op bodies, rather than once per preset op. The scrub is a single event, and six reads should not describe it six times. `docs/presets/git.md` no longer owns the reasoning — it is a call-level property now, so it lives in `docs/operations/meta.md` with a pointer left behind, because a boundary that moves without its map leaves the next reader a stale one.

  **The tests assert the answer, not the call.** A test that checked `scrub_git_env` ran would be a proxy; these check that the metadata describes *this* repo — ` m` and not ` ?`, repoB's branch and not repoA's, `validate_staged` naming the file staged where the caller is standing. A control test asserts raw git really does flip in the same fixture, so a green suite means the scrub acted rather than that the mechanism was never there. One assertion had to be strengthened after the RED run proved it vacuous: `only-in-a.py not in stdout` passed on the broken code, because the broken answer was empty.

- **`gh-pr:N:status` reported nine legs of a fourteen-leg matrix as the whole answer, and the arithmetic check could not see it** ([#724](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/724)). Hit live in the merge path on PR #715: one minute after printing `14 total: 13 passed, 0 failed, 1 pending`, the same call printed `9 total: 8 passed, 0 failed, 1 pending`, while `gh run view --json jobs`, `gh pr checks`, and `gh pr view --json statusCheckRollup` all said fourteen. `8 + 0 + 1 = 9` and the header said `9 total`, so [#454](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/454)'s rule — the states must sum to the leg count — held while the answer was wrong. **Internal consistency cannot detect a missing input.** And the near-miss is the reason this is not cosmetic: had the ninth leg gone green, the next call would have read `9 total: 9 passed, 0 failed, 0 pending` with no marker of any kind, and the merge gate merges on that with five legs invisible rather than pending.

  **The op was not miscounting.** It de-duplicates nothing, paginates nothing, and reads exactly one source — the `statusCheckRollup` list `gh pr view` returns — with no filtering between the fetch and the tally; `github_states()` is length-preserving by construction. Three of the four candidate causes in the issue are ruled out by reading the code. The fourth is the one that survives, and it was **reproduced live** on PR #728: cancel the run, `gh run rerun --failed`, poll. Three cycles, same shape every time —

  ```
  11:28:20  rollup=0   latest_attempt=0   all_attempts_distinct=14
  11:28:24  rollup=11  latest_attempt=11  all_attempts_distinct=14
  11:28:32  rollup=14  latest_attempt=14  all_attempts_distinct=14
  ```

  GitHub withdraws the old attempt's check runs and re-creates them over roughly twelve seconds. Read mid-way, the rollup is genuinely, transiently short. **So the payload was wrong and the tally was faithful** — which is a report about GitHub, not about this repo, and is exactly why the fix is a floor rather than a corrected count.

  **The first floor this PR shipped did not work, and the reproduction is what caught it.** It read `total_count` off `repos/{o}/{r}/actions/runs/{id}/jobs` — the endpoint behind `gh run view --json jobs`, which had reported fourteen while the op said nine. That endpoint defaults to `filter=latest`, and the `latest_attempt` column above is it: **it dips in lockstep with the rollup.** A floor that agrees with the short answer is not a floor; the disclosure would have stayed silent through the entire window it exists for. The issue's four corroborating sources do not contradict this — serial `gh` calls taken over a ten-second window that resolves in twelve are as consistent with "the window had closed" as with "the jobs API led."

  **What actually holds is `filter=all`.** A previous attempt's job rows are history and cannot be withdrawn, so the count of *distinct job names across every attempt* only ever grows — the `all_attempts_distinct` column, flat at 14 through samples where both other sources read 0. That is the floor. It is deliberately the name set and not `total_count`, which under `filter=all` counts every row of every attempt (42 across three attempts of a fourteen-leg matrix) and would declare a complete tally 28 legs short. Both ways it can read low — a matrix computed at runtime that grew between attempts, or more than 100 job records truncating the first page — under-claim a shortfall rather than inventing one.

  **The disclosure.** `⚠ INCOMPLETE — 9 of 14 legs read`, plus a line naming the legs that never arrived (bounded at 5, then `+N more`, per [#605](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/605)). It is a **second** marker beside `⚠ NOT ALL GREEN`, not a rewording of it: "the legs I read were not all green" and "I did not read all the legs" are different claims, and the all-passing shortfall — the dangerous one — carries only the second.

  **An unestablished count declines rather than guessing either way.** No jobs API, or more than `MAX_RECONCILED_RUNS` (4) distinct runs on one PR, renders `⚠ TALLY UNVERIFIED` and says what could not be established. Defaulting to `found` would restore precisely the silence being fixed; defaulting to the larger number would invent legs and trade a loud failure for a quiet one. A rollup naming no Actions run at all — external CI, legacy commit statuses — reconciles silently and at zero cost, and `declared < found` is treated as extra checks rather than as a negative shortfall.

  **The cost is stated because it is real:** one extra `gh api` call per distinct run, roughly doubling `:status` wall time (~1.1s to ~2.0s on this repo). The run ids themselves are free — they ride on the `detailsUrl` [#619](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/619) already parses job ids from. `gh-prs` and `git-status` are deliberately **not** reconciled: paying that per PR on a board is a different trade, and neither line is the one a merge is decided on.

  **The tests pin the difference, not the number.** Every rendering test runs the op twice on an *identical* nine-leg rollup and varies only the declared count, then asserts the two outputs differ — a test asserting `9 total` passes on the broken code, which is exactly what shipped. Also pinned: an all-passing shortfall carries a `⚠`, a reconciled all-green still adds no lines at all, #454's sum-to-total contract survives, the request carries `filter=all` (the half the first attempt got wrong), and a three-attempt payload of 42 rows declares 14 legs rather than 42. One existing #619 test needed a stub rather than an assertion change: unstubbed, its `gh` double answers the new jobs call with a PR payload, and `test_slim_status_all_green_adds_no_extra_lines` would have gone on passing while measuring the length of a decline notice instead of the terseness it names.

- **Every CI job now declares a wall-clock budget, so a hang fails in minutes with its log written instead of reading `pending` for six hours** ([#722](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/722)). `grep -rn "timeout-minutes" .github/workflows/` returned nothing: no job and no step declared one, so GitHub's six-hour default applied to all fourteen legs. `notifiers (bun + TypeScript) (ubuntu-latest)` on [#715](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/715) ran 26 minutes and was still running when it was killed by hand, against 24-37s on every recent master run and a macOS twin that passed in 32s on the same commit — on a PR that touched no TypeScript. It was stuck on step 9 of 18, "Run the channel integration tests for real".

  **The defect fixed here is the reporting, not the hang** — the hang itself is most likely a socket wait and belongs to [#554](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/554), which is about that subsystem having no delivery observability at all. What made an unbounded wait dangerous is that nothing anywhere named it as one. `gh-pr:715:status` rendered `13 passed, 0 failed, 1 pending`, which is byte-identical to a leg that is about to finish; the states still summed to the leg count, so [#454](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/454)'s arithmetic check passed cleanly. It is not a miscount, it is **a wait with no upper bound presented as an ordinary wait**, and because the merge gate requires a full green it silently became a six-hour merge block that only the next person to come looking would notice. There was also nothing to read while it hung: `gh api .../jobs/<id>/logs` answers `BlobNotFound` for an in-progress job, and a job cancelled by hand never writes a log at all. **A job killed by `timeout-minutes` fails with its log written**, which is the half of this worth having — the bound and the artefact arrive together.

  **Two numbers, sized from 70 job observations across five master runs rather than picked.** `pytest` gets **30 minutes** against measured windows legs of 483-574s, macos 128-197s and ubuntu 93-125s — 3.1x the worst leg ever recorded here. `notifiers` gets **10 minutes** against 24-37s. Copying one figure across both is the specific mistake [#702](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/702) exists for: a budget generous enough for a 574s windows leg is ~50x the notifiers job and therefore no guard at all on the one that actually hung. The multiple on `notifiers` is deliberately loose for the opposite reason — its base is 37s and three of its steps are package-manager installs (npm, pip, bun), whose tail latency against a degraded registry is minutes, so a tight multiple would fire on a bad network day. **A budget that reds a genuine green teaches everyone to press re-run, after which it is worse than not having one**; that trade, not the arithmetic, is what decided both numbers. Per-OS budgets inside the pytest matrix were considered and declined: noticing a hung ubuntu leg at 15 minutes instead of 30 is noise against the 360 it replaces, and it is three numbers to keep in step instead of one.

  **The step that hung is bounded by the inner tool, not by a second wall clock.** It now runs `pytest --timeout=30` — pytest-timeout has been in the dev extras and installed by both jobs since it was added, and used by neither. A job ceiling can only ever report "this leg did not finish"; the per-test budget names the test and dumps the stack of the thread stuck in it, which is the one artefact #554 needs and the one a wall-clock kill cannot produce. 30s is ~85x the ~0.35s these ~30 tests average (10-11s for the whole step, measured). A step-level `timeout-minutes` was the alternative and was rejected: it is a third number to keep ordered between the other two and it names no test. **The ordering is [#716](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/716)'s rule one layer out** — an outer budget is a hang-guard on the inner one, so the job ceiling must exceed the per-test budget or the inner guard can never fire — and like `tests/_adapter_budget.py` it is checked by reading both numbers out of the file rather than tabulating them next to it.

  **The `pytest` job deliberately gets no per-test budget, and that is asserted rather than left as an omission.** ~4000 tests under `-n auto` with `slow` included in CI, on the legs this repo has three times measured blowing a hand-written number under contention (#702, #658, [#650](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/650)). There is no per-test timing from a windows leg to size one from, and guessing it against a contended runner is exactly those three incidents one layer up. A test fails if a per-test budget appears there without the measurements behind it.

  **What is pinned and what cannot be, plainly.** No test here can make CI hang, so nothing asserts that a budget is large enough on a loaded runner or that it fires on a real hang — such a test would pass on every machine that does not have the problem, which is the defect rather than the fix. `tests/test_ci_job_timeouts_722.py` pins policy, which holds identically on a fast laptop and a crawling runner: that every job declares a budget (so a fifteenth job cannot arrive without one), that each stays inside a stated band at **both** ends — a budget loosened to 360 minutes spelled out is still 360 minutes — that the two classes do not collapse onto one number, that the step which hung arms the inner guard, and that the job ceiling sits above it. It parses the YAML by indentation rather than with PyYAML, which CI does not install and which would have made the guard skip on all fourteen legs — silence in the file whose subject is a silent wait. Its own parser is fixture-tested, including that a *step* budget is never counted as its job's: the first version of the job-budget regex was missing `re.M`, both fixtures happened to declare the budget on the block's first line, and the parser tests were green while the real workflow read as unbudgeted.

- **A test's timeout on a validator adapter is now derived from what the adapter is allowed to take, instead of being written by hand against the machine that wrote it** ([#702](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/702), [#658](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/658)). Three reds, three hand-written numbers, all Windows-only, all under load: `gofmt-check` at 15s (#702, 1 failed / 5154 passed on that leg), `phplint` twice at 10s (#658, on a leg that ran 258s against ~115s for its siblings), and `git rev-list` at 5s (#650, which aborted a pre-push hook). Raising a fourth number was the one fix explicitly not wanted, because three instances name the pattern rather than the value.

  **The pattern, once: an outer budget is a hang-guard on the adapter, so it has to exceed the adapter's own budget.** Every adapter under `validators/` already wraps the real tool in a `timeout=` and already declines when it blows it. A test spawning the adapter is therefore not waiting on `php` or `gofmt` — it is waiting on the adapter, which owes an answer inside its own budget plus two Python starts. Set the outer budget *below* the inner one and it can never fire for the thing a timeout exists to catch: the adapter declines first and the test gets JSON back, not a `TimeoutExpired`. The only remaining way to trip it is a slow machine. **That is this repo's own benchmark-versus-hang-guard test (#504/#505) failing in the benchmark direction** — multiply the threshold by ten and the assertion catches nothing it did not already catch, so the number *was* the assertion — and a benchmark does not belong in a correctness test. Every reported site was on the wrong side of that line, and so were twenty-eight others nobody had hit yet: phplint 10 < 30, gofmt-check 15 < 30, phpstan/psr/phpmd/prettier likewise, and `cargo-check` at 120 == 120, where a tie is a race.

  **36 call sites across 10 test files now call `_adapter_budget.adapter_budget(ADAPTER)`**, which reads the adapter's internal budget out of its source, adds spawn headroom, and multiplies on Windows — where process spawn is materially slower, antivirus interposes on every temp file under a runner's temp dir, and the runner is shared. Reading rather than tabulating is the load-bearing part: raising an adapter's internal timeout raises every test budget over it automatically, so there is no second number to leave behind. `SUPERTOOL_TEST_ADAPTER_TIMEOUT` overrides it for a run, the same shape as `SUPERTOOL_LINT_TIMEOUT` (#553) and `SUPERTOOL_GIT_TIMEOUT` (#650), and a malformed value is ignored rather than taking collection of the whole suite down (#654).

  **These tests still fail rather than skip when the budget blows, and that is the deliberate half.** #702 asks whether they should decline instead, per `docs/validators.md` §"Declining instead of guessing". They should not, because the fix removes the condition that made a skip tempting: a skip is right for a check that cannot tell "the tool hung" from "the runner was busy", which is exactly what a 10s budget over a 30s adapter could not do. Once the outer budget is derived from the inner one, a blown budget means the adapter did not honour its own timeout — a hang, in the code under test, and it has to stay loud. **What still catches a genuine hang is that assertion, unchanged and un-skipped**; nothing here is marked flaky and nothing is retried.

  **A guard, because the value of doing this once is that a fourth site never gets written.** `test_no_test_spawns_a_validator_adapter_on_a_hardcoded_budget` walks the suite's AST and fails on any literal `timeout=` handed to a `subprocess.run`/`Popen` that spawns an adapter. It is resolved to a fixed point rather than in one pass, and the first version was not: `GOFMT_CHECK = VALIDATORS / "gofmt-check" / "gofmt-check.py"` is two hops from the string `"validators"`, so the single-pass version missed `tests/test_validators_tier2.py` — the exact file #702 was filed about. A guard blind to the incident it was written for is worse than no guard.

  **What this cannot pin, said plainly.** Nothing runnable here can make CI slow, so no test asserts that any budget is large enough on a loaded Windows runner — such a test would pass on every machine that does not have the problem, which is the defect, not the fix. What is pinned is the policy: that budgets come from one function, that its output always exceeds the adapter's own, that the Windows multiplier applies, and that the override parses safely. Those are properties of the code and hold identically on a fast laptop and a crawling runner.

- **An adapter that blows its own timeout now declines instead of killing the op with an empty stdout** ([#702](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/702)). Found by sweeping for the timeout above, and the more serious of the two. `hadolint`, `markdownlint` and `tsc-check` each spawn their tool on a 30s budget and had no handler for it expiring and no top-level `except` either — so a `TimeoutExpired` exits the process on a traceback with **nothing on stdout**, and every caller `json.loads()` that. A slow linter surfaced as a `JSONDecodeError` naming neither the tool nor the timeout: a fact about the machine rendered as a crash in the tool, one layer over the shape #650 fixed for `git`. Their fifteen sibling adapters already caught it. All three now emit the same stated decline — `ok: false`, `code: "adapter"`, and a message naming the budget and saying the file was **NOT** checked, which the siblings' bare `"timeout"` does not (#658 asked for the budget to be observable when it fires). It stays a finding rather than becoming a skip because the binary was found and started, which is where `docs/validators.md` draws that line. `test_every_adapter_that_grants_itself_a_budget_survives_blowing_it` sweeps `validators/` so a sixteenth adapter cannot be added without one, and the per-adapter tests drive the timeout in-process, so they cost milliseconds and — unlike the `/bin/sh` PATH shim #650 used — actually run on Windows, where all three incidents happened.

- **`test_valid_ruby` now says what the adapter found when it claims a clean file is dirty** ([#658](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/658)). It failed on the #658 leg as `assert False is True` and disclosed nothing: triaging it required two unrelated `phplint` tracebacks in the same report to guess at a cause, and **that guess was never confirmed and is not confirmed here**. `ruby-check` is not among the adapters fixed above — it has a top-level handler, so a blown internal budget already reaches stdout as a stated error rather than a crash. The cause of that one failure remains unknown. What changed is that the next occurrence identifies itself: the assertion now carries the adapter's own `errors` list, and `_run` gained the shared budget where it previously had **no** timeout at all, so a genuinely hung adapter is bounded rather than hanging the suite.

### Added

- **`repo:OWNER/NAME` — a leading op naming the repo a call is *about*, which `cwd:` never answered** ([#673](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/673)). Every `gh-*` read op took its repo from the cwd's git remote with no override, so two repos were unreachable through the ops at once: any repo not cloned locally, and any repo whose work happens from a project root that is not a GitHub clone. The reported case was both — `claude-remember` PRs, from a DVSI project root that is a **GitLab** repo, where `gh-*` correctly refuses. `cwd:` into the one GitHub clone available answered about *that* clone instead. No combination of cwd reached the PR.

  **The vocabulary already existed in the family, on the wrong side of it.** `gh-issue-create` has accepted a `repo` key in its payload since it shipped, and the reporter demonstrated it working against a different repo an hour after filing. So this is a write/read asymmetry, not a missing concept: `gh-pr`, `gh-prs`, `gh-issue`, `gh-run` and `gh-job` now accept a target too.

  ```bash
  ./supertool 'repo:Digital-Process-Tools/claude-remember' 'gh-pr:265:status'
  ./supertool 'cwd:~/a-gitlab-project' 'repo:some-org/a-github-repo' 'gh-run:30654362436'
  ```

  First op or immediately after `cwd:`, one per call, `OWNER/NAME` validated before anything runs, scoped to the whole call.

  **The two copy-pasteable `gh api` hints go through the same substitution.** `job.py` prints commands for a human to run — one when a log 404s and the job endpoint could not say why, one when the fetch succeeded and returned zero bytes — and both were built from `gh api repos/{owner}/{repo}/…`, whose placeholders gh expands from the **cwd's** remote. Under a repo target that hands the reader a command pointing at a different repository than the data printed directly above it, which is the exact failure this op exists to close, in the one place a test could not see it: with no target set `_api_repo_path` returns the placeholder string byte-for-byte, so the two spellings are indistinguishable until someone uses a target. Both now route through the helper, pinned by `tests/test_repo_target_job_hints_673.py` on the target case, on the no-target case (which must stay byte-identical), and on the helper itself.

  **Why a leading op rather than the trailing `…:repo=OWNER/NAME` token the issue also proposed.** The suffix grammar in this family is not free. `gh-job:ID:grep:PATTERN` takes an arbitrary regex in exactly that position, so `gh-job:5:grep:repo=x` is a legitimate log search a trailing-token scan would silently steal — and stealing a search term to mean something else is the same class of defect as the one being fixed. `gh-prs` also already spells its filters `key=value` *inside* one comma-separated token (`gh-prs:author=@me,state=open`), so a second, colon-separated `key=` grammar would be two rules for one idea. A leading op lands in one place in the dispatcher instead of five presets' argument parsers, and composes with `cwd:` without either needing to know about the other.

  **A `repo:` that no op in the call can honour is refused, not ignored.** Ops opt in with `"repo_target": true` in the preset manifest (`"payload"` for `gh-issue-create`, which routes it through its own payload), and a call mixing in an op that cannot take one fails before anything runs, naming the op *and* which of the two problems it is — a target to move, or a target to drop. A target silently applying to half a call is the exact shape of the bug this closes; it is not the fix's behaviour either.

### Changed

- **`cwd is not a GitHub repo` no longer describes a wall that has a door** ([#673](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/673)). That sentence was complete and honest while the cwd was the only way to name a repo. With `repo:` shipping it is half the story, so it names the second route as well — and when a target *was* given, the cwd is not blamed at all, because it took no part in the lookup. Three situations, three answers:

  | Situation | Message |
  |---|---|
  | No target, cwd is not a GitHub clone | `cwd is not a GitHub repo and no repo target was given. cd into a GitHub-cloned repo, name one with a leading repo: op …, or run gh directly with --repo OWNER/REPO.` |
  | Target given, `gh` cannot resolve it | `repo target 'owner/name' could not be resolved by gh. Check the spelling and your access: gh repo view owner/name` |
  | Target given, wrong number | `PR #265 not found in owner/name. Check the number, or the repo target (gh repo view owner/name).` |

  The third previously read *not found in this repo … verify you're in the right repo*, which under a target sends the reader to inspect a working directory that had nothing to do with the request.

- **`gh-prs` declines its watch column under a repo target instead of answering it wrong** ([#673](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/673)). Found while building the above, not reported. Watch pollers write `supertool-watch-github-pr__{number}.pid` — keyed by PR number, with no repo in the key — so under a target a live poller for `#12` of the repo you are standing in is indistinguishable from `#12` of the repo the board is about.

  Returning the set anyway marks the wrong rows watched; returning an empty set *asserts* that none are. So `_watched_numbers()` returns `None`, `_board.render_row`'s `watched` becomes three-valued, and rows print `?` — not blank, because blank on this board is the claim "no poller is watching this". The footer drops its ready-to-run `watch:github-pr:N` and says why, since that clause is not decoration: under a target the command it emits would start polling *this* repo's `#N` while the board it came from was about another. An actionable suggestion that does the wrong thing is worse than none, and going quiet would read as "nothing needs watching".

  Callers that know their watch state (`gl-mrs`, `radar`) pass a bool and are unaffected.

- **Read ops (`grep`, `around`, `grep_around`, `between`, `read`) accept the `@file` / `@-` payload the mutating ops already had, and a mis-tokenized read op now names the split instead of just failing** ([#625](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/625)). The report was that a `:` in a search pattern is guesswork, that `grep:Element: <:traces.txt:8:0` tokenizes with `Element` as the pattern and `` ` <` `` as the path, and that unlike a mutating op the failure is silent. **Two of those three did not reproduce.** `_parse_grep_args` and `_parse_around_args` already peel the trailing integers, take the *last* token as the path and rejoin everything before it, so `grep:Element: <:PATH:8:0` and `grep:passed|failed|Error:FILE:15` — the issue's two examples — both parse correctly and always did; and a mis-parse does not return a zero, it returns `path not found`, because the path is checked before the scan. The escape was missing, but the trap was not where it was reported, so the fix is aimed at where it is.

  **Where the colon genuinely defeats the parser is where nothing anchors the right-hand end.** Omit the path and the pattern's own tail is taken for one — `grep:A::CONST` searches for `A:` in a path named `CONST`. And `between:re:START:END:PATH` rejoins **rightward** (`":".join(parts[4:])`), so a `:` in START or END steals from the path rather than from the pattern — the opposite failure to `grep`'s, in the same character.

  **`\:` is not an escape and is not documented as one.** It appears to work because `_split_arg` splits on every `:` regardless and the parsers rejoin the pieces with `:`, so the backslash simply rides into the pattern, where the regex engine reads `\:` and `:` identically. That equivalence ends at `op_grep`'s literal fallback, which searches for the backslash. Documenting an accident as a contract would have been the cheaper fix and the wrong one.

  **So the escape is the payload route, not a new escape character.** `grep:@-` / `grep:@file.toml` take `pattern` / `path` / `limit` / `context` / `count` / `no_auto_read`; `around` and `grep_around` take `pattern` / `path` / `n` (`+ limit`); `between` takes `symbol` **or** `start` + `end`, plus `path`; `read` takes `path` / `offset` / `limit` / `grep` / `full`. It is the shape `edit:@-` already uses, so it is one rule applied consistently rather than a second syntax to remember, and a payload never has to work out where the pattern ends — which is the whole defect. These deliberately do **not** go through `_AT_FILE_BUILTIN_DEFAULTS`: that registry rebuilds a positional parts list and hands it back to the colon parsers, which would re-split the very pattern the payload exists to protect.

  **The `@` gate gives ground rather than taking it.** `grep:@Override:src/` is a real search, and a leading `@` alone must not be read as a payload reference. The route is entered only when the reference actually resolves — `@-`, or a file that exists — so an unresolvable `@pattern` falls through to the search it has always meant. The one behaviour that changes is `grep:@-:PATH`, which now reports that `@-` takes no colon arguments instead of searching for the literal `@-`; loud, self-explaining, and reachable via the payload.

  **The error is the part that carries the fix.** A read op that mis-tokenizes already failed rather than inventing a zero, and a test pins that so it cannot regress; what it did not do was say why. `grep:key: value` now answers `path not found: ' value'` **and** `Read as pattern='key' + path=' value' — i.e. 'key: value' split on ':'`, followed by the `grep:@-` payload that would have worked. The advice is withheld when the leading argument has no `:` and the path still looks like a path, so an ordinary typo (`grep:alpha:nope.txt`) stays unadorned — advice printed on every wrong path is advice nobody reads. `ops`, `help:grep`, `README.md` and `docs/input-forms.md` carry the same route, so it is findable before the error as well as from it.

### Changed

- **Documented what to do in a git worktree, and declined to automate it** ([#671](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/671)). The report is accurate and the papercut is real — a worktree created mid-session has no `./supertool`, and an agent that does not know the incantation loses the tool on its first call. **It is still not supertool's to fix, for the reason [#624](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/624) already gave about the sibling failure.** The wrapper is a gitignored symlink created by the session-start hook in the directory a session *starts* in; `git worktree add` runs after that hook and before the next one, so there is no moment at which any code this repo ships is executing. A `worktree:setup` op would not close the gap either: the agent that needs it is the one standing in a directory with no wrapper, and the agent that could run it is standing in the parent repo and does not know the op exists. In both cases the deliverable is the knowledge, not a mechanism — and [#532](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/532) already owns a config-driven `worktree:setup` if a wider one is ever wanted.

  **The half worth writing down is the half the workaround gets wrong.** The one-liner in circulation — `ln -sf supertool.py <worktree>/supertool` — is correct only for worktrees *of this repo*, and the note accompanying it generalised the rule. For a worktree of any other project there is no local `supertool.py` to point at, and the correct target is the plugin install, i.e. deliberately outside the worktree; that is what the session-start hook does and it is not a mixed tree, because [#678](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/678) fires only when the resolved project root holds a `supertool.py` of its own. Both directions were checked against a running build rather than reasoned about: a plugin-root link from a fixture project with its own custom op answers `PASS`, and the same link from a supertool worktree answers `SKIPPED` and exits 1.

  **The invocation that needs no wrapper is the one the README now leads with** — `python3 .../supertool.py`, which is already what `git-push:watch` falls back to when it finds no wrapper to spawn ([#642](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/642)). Two places in this tool had independently discovered that a worktree has no wrapper; neither said so where a reader would find it first.

### Fixed

- **The other three read ops that sliced a body to a cap now say so, and the four share one cut** ([#698](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/698)). [#681](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/681) fixed `gh-issue:N` and named the three call sites it had found while in there — `gl-issue:N`, `gh-pr:N`, `gl-mr:N` — each doing the same raw `body[:DESCRIPTION_MAX]` with no marker and no count. These are the ops briefs, triage and merge decisions are built from, and a body that ends early with nothing saying so is not a smaller version of the issue: it is a different one, whose last requirement silently does not exist.

  **Each site got the #681 shape, which is two things, and the second only works because of the first.** The cut backs off to the last line break at-or-before the cap instead of a byte offset — a marker cannot survive a cut that keeps landing mid-heading, which in #681's own repro produced `## The` out of a real heading, malformed markdown with a natural-looking ending. Then the withheld amount is stated twice: in the header, before `## Description`, because a footer-only notice is read by nobody in exactly the case it exists for; and again at the point of the cut. Both name the exact char count and both point at `:full`.

  **The caps were checked per site and deliberately left unequal.** An issue description is the brief — the whole reason to run the op — and stays at 3000 (`gh-issue`, `gl-issue`). A PR or MR description is one panel in a render that also carries checks, reviews, unresolved threads, diff stat, a per-file list and comments, and stays at 2000 (`gh-pr`, `gl-mr`): raising it would crowd out the check and review data that is the reason to open that op at all. The disclosure is what should be uniform, not the limit.

  **Two of the escape hatches did not exist, and a stated one that does not work is worse than none** — it stops the reader looking for another way to the text. `gh-pr` had no `:full` flag whatsoever, so it gained one. `gl-mr` had a `:full` documented as uncapping the file list and the comments, and capped the description regardless; it now uncaps the description too. Both were found by writing the test that asserts `:full` returns the whole body, rather than by reading the flag's documentation.

  **The shape moved into `presets/_body.py` rather than becoming a third and fourth copy of itself.** Three new sites plus the two working ones is five hand-maintained copies of one disclosure, which is how a sixth site ships without one — the defect class here being precisely a disclosure that exists at some call sites and not others. `gh-issue` was rewritten onto the helper instead of being left as a separate copy, and that refactor is safe to make inside a bugfix because #681 pinned its exact output in four tests: if the helper renders one character differently, those go red. `presets/github/job.py`, which had the shape first, is left alone — its truncation bounds a line-matched log view by bytes, not a body by a character cap, and folding two different budgets into one helper would be a worse abstraction than two.
- **`git-diff` no longer affirms a file list is clean using a guard that shipped with no rules, and four more checks now separate "could not answer" from "passed"** ([#693](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/693)). Five call sites, one shape — `docs/validators.md` §"Declining instead of guessing" has described the three states since #531 and these had not adopted them. Each is pinned by a test that puts the checker in its can't-answer state and asserts the output **differs** from the passing state; asserting the passing state alone is what let all five ship.

  **The loudest is the review op's own forbidden-path guard.** `forbidden_paths` is project policy, it appears in neither shipped config and in no documentation, and `_json_env` returned `[]` for it — so the shipped state of the guard was *zero rules*, zero rules produce zero hits, and zero hits printed `✓ No red flags, forbidden paths, or missing tests.` A `.env` and an `id_rsa` went through the review op and the commit op with the review affirmatively calling the file list clean.

  **Of the three available answers, defaults were chosen over a refusal.** `radar` refuses to run when unconfigured and that precedent was weighed first, but `radar` is opt-in machinery and `git-diff` is the op you run before every commit: a refusal on every unconfigured repo makes it annoying enough that people stop running it, and an abandoned checker is a worse outcome than the defect. Disclosing "guard not configured" was the third option and it resolves nothing — one line of permanent disclaimer, still flagging no file. `DEFAULT_FORBIDDEN_PATHS` is the only one of the three that makes the *shipped* state a state that can fail, and the shipped state is the one nearly every user is in. It covers secret-shaped names — `.env`, `id_rsa`/`id_ed25519` and friends, `*.pem`/`*.p12`/`*.key`, `.npmrc`/`.netrc`/`.pypirc`, `credentials.json`, service-account keys, `.aws/` — and is deliberately quiet on the files projects commit on purpose: `.env.example` and its siblings are excluded by name and `id_rsa.pub` is a public key, because a default that cries wolf gets configured away, which returns the user to the always-passing state by a longer route. Project `forbidden_paths` still apply, on top rather than instead.

  **A policy that could not be parsed was indistinguishable from a policy nobody wrote.** `_json_env` collapsed absent and malformed into the same `[]`, so a typo in `.supertool.json` disabled a guard silently and the run it disabled still printed the affirmative verdict. It now returns `(rules, why)`: an empty `why` means there was nothing to load, which is an answer, and a non-empty one is a finding printed as `⚠ Policy not loaded`, naming the key and stating that its rules were **not** applied. Declining the project's rules does not decline the shipped ones.

  **And the verdict now names only the checks that ran.** `_check_test_pairing` returns `[]` both for "every added file has its test" and for "there were no rules to run", and the one affirmative line claimed the first on behalf of the second. With no `test_pairing` configured it reads `✓ No red flags or forbidden paths.` — the unchecked leg is not mentioned rather than disclaimed, because a "not checked" line on every run of every unconfigured repo is the permanent disclaimer #621 and #685 both refused.

  **`_sanitize.wrap` says it scanned, and owns its own delimiters.** A clean scan printed nothing, so "the scanner found nothing" and "there was nothing scanning" were one output; it now leads with `[scan] no known injection patterns matched (heuristic — not a guarantee)`, which states the limit of the claim as well as the claim. `safe_short` deliberately does **not** carry it — that renders once per row of every list and browse, and this repo has twice refused a disclaimer on a hot path. Separately, the fence markers were constants, so external content containing `<<END UNTRUSTED CONTENT>>` closed the untrusted region early and continued outside it — while `tests/test_sanitize.py`, which asserts the markers are *present*, stayed green throughout. The fence now carries a per-call nonce the content cannot predict, and fence-shaped runs in the content are neutralised on the way in so a human skimming the block is not fooled either. All three preset copies (`bluesky`, `devto`, `hashnode`) are updated together and a test still pins them byte-identical.

  **`transport.claim_pidfile` returned `0` — "you own this slot, go spawn" — from two paths that created no file.** An `os.open` that failed for anything other than "it already exists" (an unwritable or absent state dir, a path that is a directory: `IsADirectoryError` on POSIX, `PermissionError` on Windows) hit a bare `except OSError: return 0`, and a retry that ran out fell through to the same value. That is the strongest of the three claims made on the evidence of the weakest, and the caller then forked a poller against a slot it did not own — on exactly the machine where two pollers are least wanted. There is now a `CLAIM_UNKNOWN` third value; `start_poller` returns `unclaimable`, `watch` exits 1 naming the pid path and `SUPERTOOL_WATCH_STATE_DIR`, and radar's MR tier counts it as still-uncovered rather than dropping it between "healed" and "failed".

  **Adding this suite to the repo turned 18 unrelated tests red, and the cause was how they reach their module** ([#726](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/726)). `presets/*/` basenames are op names, not module names: twenty of them are claimed by more than one preset directory, and `sys.modules` has one slot each. Four `presets/mcp` suites reached theirs with a bare `import status`, which resolves against a `sys.path` every preset script appends to at import and never trims — so the winner between `presets/mcp/status.py` and `presets/git/status.py` was decided by xdist's work split, and one new test file moved the split. `pytest tests/test_mcp_status_unknown_549.py tests/test_mcp_status_unreadable_config_569.py tests/test_git_status.py` reproduces it with no CI and no platform involved; reversing the argument order makes it pass. The platform labels on the red legs were a coincidence — all four mcp status suites are `skipif(not hasattr(os, "geteuid"))`, so Windows never ran them at all.

  **The four suites now load by absolute path under a preset-qualified name**, via the `tests/_preset_loader.py` that exists for this, joining `test_git_status.py` and `test_status_swallowed_705.py`. Binding the contested name once in `conftest.py` was tried first and rejected: it wins the race rather than removing it, leaves the same one slot two files claim, and would flip back silently on the next conftest edit. `_common` — claimed by `presets/claude-log` and `presets/xml` — had the same shape in two more suites and is fixed with them. **There is no runtime bug here and that was checked rather than assumed**: every preset op is its own subprocess, so two preset directories are never on one process's path in production, and the one place `supertool.py` imports preset code in-process already loads by absolute path under a unique name. `test_preset_basename_collision_726.py` AST-scans `tests/*.py` and fails on the shape, naming the file, the line, and the rival paths, so the next one cannot ship quietly.

  **One of the five did not reproduce.** #693 lists `git-status` collapsing a failed `git stash list` into "there are no stashes". It does not: `status.py` already calls `_note_failed` on the stash result, so the failure reaches #685's `git-status INCOMPLETE` footer with git's own words and the two outputs already differ. What was missing was a test — the only reason a fixed defect comes back quietly — so it gains a regression pin rather than a change.
- **Re-running a batch that applies an edit a SECOND time no longer prints the first run's receipt** ([#701](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/701)). Reproduced on `master` at `b0b65c7` before anything was changed. An edit whose `new` contains its `old` — `old = "def f():"`, `new = "@decorated<newline>def f():"` — keeps its own anchor alive, so the anchor matches again on a second run and the decorator lands twice. Both runs exited `0`; both printed `[result] 1 op run, 1 write`; the only difference anywhere was `edited a.py (line 1-2)` versus `(line 2-3)`, which is not something a reader is watching.

  **Applying it twice is not the defect and has not changed.** `old` was still in the file, find-and-replace found it, and that is what find-and-replace does. An edit that legitimately applies twice exists — appending a second repeated element is byte-for-byte this shape — so refusing would be guessing at intent, which is the line [#680](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/680) already drew. The defect is that **the output could not distinguish "applied" from "applied again"**, and the caller who re-runs a payload is by definition the caller who was already unsure whether it landed. #680 was filed because a batch result was ambiguous enough that the reporter could not tell; the natural response to that is to run the payload again. The two compose: the first makes you doubt, the second punishes checking. An identical receipt reads as "the same thing happened" when what happened is a second mutation.

  **So: disclosure, in the footer's existing vocabulary rather than a third convention.** `[result] N ops run, M writes[, K skipped]` gains `K re-applied`, ending `— an edit already present in the file was applied again`, and the op receipt gains one line under the `edited` line it qualifies (`↳ re-applied: the text this edit produces was already present around the anchor — this is a SECOND application, not a repeat of the first`). It is a separate word from `skipped` deliberately: a skipped op left the disk alone, a re-applied one did not, and collapsing them would degrade `skipped` into "something was odd". It does **not** join `skipped` on the exit code — a re-apply is a legitimate write, and a `&&` chain must not break on a repeated append.

  **The test is positional, not "does the file already contain `new`".** The issue floated the simpler check and it has a real failure mode: `new` may legitimately pre-exist somewhere unrelated (inserting `return None` into a file that already has one in another function), and a signal that fires on a first application is noise — which is exactly how a footer field stops being read. It fires only when the occurrence of `old` about to be replaced is *contained inside* an existing occurrence of `new`, which is the literal statement "this edit's result is already here, around this anchor". Scoped to `edit` (colon CLI and payload route): `replace` is replace-all and already reports occurrence counts, `vim` is scripted rather than anchored.

  This says nothing about whether a batch should be atomic ([#700](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/700)) — it adds a receipt field and no rollback, so both answers remain open.

- **A modified file no longer reads as clean when the check fails, and an MR lookup that could not run no longer reads as "there is no MR"** ([#705](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/705)). Both reproduced before anything was changed, and both live in the ground [#685](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/685) had already cleared: it rewrote `presets/git/status.py` specifically to carry a `git-status INCOMPLETE` footer whenever a call could not answer, and two call sites never reached it. With `glab` and `gh` both refusing for want of a token, `git-status` printed a report identical byte-for-byte to the one it prints for a branch that simply has no MR yet — after spending ten seconds not finding out.

  **The higher-traffic half is the `read` receipt.** `_path_meta_suffix` attaches the `m`/`?`/`!` marker to every `read`, the most-used op in the tool, and dropped it whenever its `git status` failed — a held `index.lock` is enough. A modified file then rendered exactly as a clean one. That inverts the marker's only job: it exists to say the file on disk differs from the index.

  **The third state is a token, not a character, and the field always had room for one.** The suffix looks one character wide because its busiest members are, but it is a space-separated list whose members already include `non-utf8`, `crlf` and `->target broken`. The decline is **`git?`** — it names the check that declined and cannot be read as any of the three answers it replaces, none of which mention git. A spare punctuation mark was the alternative and was rejected on the grounds that made it available: a character no reader has a meaning for yet is the wrong carrier for the token that has to say the most. Absence still means clean, and now means only that.

  **The MR lookup routes into the footer that already existed rather than growing a second convention** — the recurring shape here being an abstraction that is present and a call site that never adopted it. `glab` and `gh` exit `1` both for "no MR on this branch" and for "your token expired", so the exit code cannot separate them and the message does: the phrases that mean a genuine absence stay silent, and everything else is disclosed with the CLI's own words (`exit 1: error: 401 Unauthorized`). Being wrong in that direction costs one footer line a reader dismisses in a second; being wrong the other way is the defect. A CLI that is not *installed* stays silent, by the same reading that keeps a missing `git` binary quiet — a decline that can never resolve is noise on every run.

  **The sweep found two more, so the footer now carries a reason per call.** `git status --porcelain=v1` and `git stash list` have no legitimate non-zero exit inside a repository the branch lookup has already proved exists, and both dropped their whole section on failure with nothing recorded. They are disclosed now; the calls that fail as a matter of course (`rev-parse --verify master` where neither exists, `rev-list @{upstream}` on an untracked branch) stay silent on purpose, because a footer on every run is one nobody reads on the run that needed it. `_UNANSWERED` carries `(command, why)` rather than `(command, budget)`, since "did not answer" alone sends a reader to raise `SUPERTOOL_GIT_TIMEOUT` for what is an expired token.

  **One existing fixture was resting on the defect, and CI found it.** `test_the_incomplete_note_is_absent_when_every_call_answered` — #650's pin for "a permanent disclaimer discloses nothing" — set `PATH` to `os.path.dirname(shutil.which("git"))`, which on a GitHub runner also contains `gh`. Inside a workflow `gh` is unauthenticated and refuses with exit 4 before it ever looks at a remote, so the first honest report of that fact broke a test whose name is a promise that every call answered. **The assertion is untouched; the fixture now builds the premise it was assuming**, a directory holding git alone. Two things argued against the other repair: the test was passing locally only because that machine's `gh` happens to be authenticated — fixture luck either way — and whitelisting an unauthenticated CLI into silence would re-open this issue at precisely the line that closes it, since "an expired token or an unauthenticated CLI" is one of the three cases it names. A missing binary is silent because nothing can ever change that; `gh auth login` changes this one.

- **A leaked `GIT_DIR` no longer redirects an op at another repository, and the two ops that write now say which repository they wrote to** ([#692](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/692)). Reproduced exactly as reported before anything was changed: `git-commit` run with cwd=repoB and `GIT_DIR=repoA/.git` printed `# git-commit on main`, `HEAD before: <repoA's head>`, `HEAD after: … ✓`, and put the commit in **repoA**. Nothing in that receipt named a repository, so nothing in it contradicted the only reasonable reading — that the commit went where the caller was standing.

  **It is not a corner case, because this repo arranges it.** Git exports `GIT_DIR` to every hook it runs, and `.githooks/pre-commit` invokes `./supertool 'git-diff:staged'`. [#416](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/416) learned this lesson once and applied it to the **test runner** — `.githooks/pre-push` has unset five `GIT_*` variables since then, and `tests/conftest.py` does it again before every test. The ops never got the same treatment.

  **The scrub lives at the launcher, not in the git presets, and the adoption survey is why.** The suggested fix was `presets/git/_git_common.py`, on the understanding that five presets import it and six define their own `_git`. The real split is worse: **all twelve git presets import `_git_common`, but only `commit` and `push` import its `_git`** — `checkout`, `conflicts`, `diff`, `diverge`, `investigate`, `merge`, `resolve`, `status` and `trail` each define a local `_git` that shadows it, and `blame` calls `subprocess.run(["git", …])` directly with no helper at all. Fixing `_git_common` would have protected two presets, missed ten, and shipped as a fix for all of them — this repo's signature defect arriving inside the fix for it. `_resolve_custom_op` in `supertool.py` launches *every* preset, so the scrub happens there: a preset is protected by being launched, and preset number thirteen is protected before it is written. A test pins this by asserting `git-status` — which has its own `_git` — acts on the cwd's repo under a leaked `GIT_DIR`.

  **Scrubbed, and said out loud.** Refusing was considered and rejected: the caller this repo creates itself is `pre-commit`, and a commit hook that aborts because supertool declined to run under git's own exported environment is a worse outcome than one that reads the right repo. But a silent scrub makes the tool ignore something the caller may have set deliberately and say nothing — the same "quiet where a loud answer belonged" that made the original bug invisible, pointed the other way. Any op that had variables removed now prints `scrubbed inherited git env: GIT_DIR — this op acted on the repo at <cwd>, not the one those variables named`, on the pass, fail and timeout paths. The wording matches the `pre-push` hook's, which had reached the same conclusion in a shell script three hundred issues earlier.

  **Seven variables, chosen by a rule rather than inherited from the hook.** The membership test is "does this change which repository, index, or refs a git command reads or writes": `GIT_DIR`, `GIT_WORK_TREE`, `GIT_COMMON_DIR`, `GIT_INDEX_FILE`, `GIT_OBJECT_DIRECTORY`, `GIT_ALTERNATE_OBJECT_DIRECTORIES`, `GIT_NAMESPACE`. `GIT_COMMON_DIR` and `GIT_NAMESPACE` were not in #416's five and meet the rule — the first redirects config and refs for a worktree, the second redirects every ref a push writes. `GIT_CEILING_DIRECTORIES` and `GIT_DISCOVERY_ACROSS_FILESYSTEM` were considered and **excluded**: they only restrict discovery, so the worst they can cause is finding no repo rather than the wrong one, and people set them deliberately on slow network mounts — scrubbing them would make supertool disagree with the user's own shell about whether they are in a repo. The list now exists once, in `supertool.py`; `tests/conftest.py` imports it and a test pins the bash hook's `unset` line to it, because two hand-maintained copies of one lesson is how the ops came to be missed.

  **The reporting half, which the issue calls the worse half.** `git-diff` has long stamped a `Repo:` header; `git-commit` and `git-push` did not — so the two ops that *write* were the two that never said where. Both now print `Repo: <toplevel>` from a shared `repo_label()` in `_git_common.py`, `git-commit` before anything is staged so the line survives a hook rejection or a nothing-staged refusal, and `git-push` next to the `Upstream:` line that already said where the commits were going but never where they came from. A bare repo reports its git dir rather than an empty string, and `unknown` when git answers neither — a wrong repo name is the one output worse than no repo name.
