  **Test bar, met the way the issue asked.** Two real repositories, not one; a control test asserts that raw git in the same fixture *does* honour the leak, so a green suite means the scrub acted rather than that the mechanism was never there. The behavioural tests were re-run against the fix with the scrub line disabled and go red again.

- **A path that is not found now names the absolute path it tried, and the project root that has it** ([#624](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/624)). The report bundles two failures under one title, and they belong to different layers. **The first is not fixable in Python.** `./supertool` is a relative path; a shell that has `cd`'d deeper into the repo gets `no such file or directory` and no op is ever parsed — including the `cwd:PATH` that was written as the *first op of that very call*. Reaching the wrapper happens before the process exists, so it is answered in `README.md` (`$PATH` install, absolute `python3 …/supertool.py`, and a warning that piping the call through `grep` turns that error into an empty result that reads like an empty answer).

  **The second — "treat every relative op path as repo-root-relative" — was declined, and most of what it asks for already shipped.** `_auto_cwd_root` (#363) chdirs to the project root when the evidence is unambiguous: an ancestor carries a `.supertool.json`, no path-shaped arg resolves against cwd, and at least one resolves against the root. Making the rule unconditional would make `read:foo.py` mean two different files depending on where you stand, silently pick one, and break every existing relative-path call — trading a loud wrong path for a quiet wrong root, which is the defect this tracker exists for.

  **What was genuinely missing is the issue's second suggestion.** `ERROR: file not found: src/foo.py` cannot distinguish a typo from cwd drift, so the answer was another round-trip: `pwd`. `read`, `grep`, `around`, `around_line`, `map` and `replace` now print `tried: /abs/path (cwd: /abs/cwd)`, and when the file demonstrably exists under the project root above cwd — the ambiguous case auto-recovery declines — they name it and the exact `cwd:<root>` prefix that would reach it. Named, never used: no path resolves anywhere the caller did not point it.

- **`git-checkout`'s three recovery paths fired only for an English git, and the fixtures that covered them could not have noticed** ([#649](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/649)). Filed as a class — "fixtures that pin the current implementation rather than the required behaviour" — off the back of [#641](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/641), with the evidence that **six mocked fixtures in `tests/test_git_push.py` covered the code path and "every one of them passed, before and after"**. The count is right and the diagnosis is right; the evidence sentence is not. Restoring the pre-#641 `tests/test_git_push.py` against today's `push.py` fails all six. They did not pass after the fix — they were rewritten *by* it, in the same commit, to carry `git push --porcelain` status lines. What is true, and is the whole point, is narrower and worse: they passed **before**, against the defect they existed to cover, and then still cost a rewrite when it was fixed. Coverage that catches nothing and bills you on refactor.

  **The fixtures were also not independently wrong.** `_is_non_fast_forward(combined)` took the merged stdout+stderr as its parameter, so *any* fixture written against that signature necessarily fed it a merged stream. Nothing was available for them to encode except the channel the seam exposed. That reframes the remedy: the thing that fixed this class was narrowing the function's input until hook text could not reach it, which #641 did — not loosening assertions downstream of a bad seam. A sweep that only relaxed fixtures would have removed protection and left the class intact.

  **So the audit went looking for the seam, not for the fixtures, and found one still open in `presets/git/checkout.py`.** Three recovery paths there rewrite local state — `fetch --all --prune` drops remote-tracking refs, `checkout -b --track` creates a branch, `checkout -B <ref> FETCH_HEAD` creates *and moves* one — and all three were selected by scanning git's human error message for `pathspec` / `did not match any`. git translates that message. Under `LANGUAGE=fr` it is `le spécificateur de chemin 'x' ne correspond à aucun fichier connu de git`, the substrings never match, and every recovery silently stops firing: a `git-checkout` of a branch pushed after your clone just fails. Same shape as #641 — a state-changing decision taken on a channel the code does not control — with the language git was built to speak in place of a pre-push hook's prose. The decision now reads `git rev-parse --verify --quiet <ref>^{commit}`, whose exit code no locale changes, and which is the question the recoveries actually needed answered.

  **Two ordering details that are not cosmetic.** `checkout -` is `@{-1}`; no fetch can make it resolve, so it is never a recovery case and short-circuits. And when the explicit single-ref fetch *reached* the ref and only the switch was blocked by a dirty tree, the ref is still locally unresolvable but "not found" is the wrong thing to say — [#267](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/267) established that, and a `ref_fetched` flag now vetoes the missing-ref report rather than leaving the ordering to rely on an English string matching first.

  **The four mocked fixtures that covered those paths are replaced, not loosened.** Each handed `_git` the English sentence and asserted the code parsed the English sentence — passing identically in every locale, because there was no real git in them to translate anything, and pinning the exact recovery argv (`["checkout", "-B", "feature", "FETCH_HEAD"]`) besides. `tests/test_git_checkout_recovery_649.py` drives a real git against a real bare remote, asserts where the caller ends up rather than which commands ran, and is parametrised over the locale instead of blind to it — skipping loudly, not silently, where git has no `fr` catalog to reproduce with. Restoring the message predicate under those tests fails four of them under `fr` and none under `C`, which is the defect exactly. The worktree-lock fixture stays a mock on purpose: it picks wording, never an action, and its English dependency is documented where it sits.
- **A call whose core and whose presets come from two different supertool trees now declines instead of printing `PASS`** ([#678](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/678)). Found in the verification path itself: a maintainer smoke-testing a branch ran `python3 ~/st-wt/673/supertool.py 'repo:…/claude-remember' 'gh-pr:282:status'` from a *master* checkout and got a real, well-formed, `PASS`-ing answer about the wrong repository. The `repo:` target under test was parsed by the branch's core and consumed by master's preset, which had never heard of it. One call away from bouncing a correct PR for a defect that did not exist.

  **The reported mechanism was a hypothesis and it holds.** `_load_config()` walks up from **cwd**, and `_find_preset_file` checks `{project_dir}/presets/` before the install dir — so the config, the preset JSONs and the scripts they name all come from wherever the caller is standing, while the core came from the file they invoked. Branch core, master presets, one process, no complaint.

  **The signal is "the resolved project root is itself a different supertool checkout"**, not the cheaper "the invoked `supertool.py` is not under the project root". The cheap version was considered and it is unusable: the documented install is a clone symlinked onto `$PATH` and run from arbitrary project roots, so it would fire on essentially every legitimate invocation and be trained away within a day. A project that merely ships its own `presets/` is not a mix either — overriding a shipped preset is a documented feature. What is detected is two builds of the *same tool* answering one call, which is `realpath` equality on one file and two `stat` calls.

  **Config ops decline; they do not warn.** A warning printed above a `PASS` is the disclosure this repo keeps finding people do not read — #680 landed the same week on exactly that. So a config op under a mix returns `SKIPPED:` naming both trees and the remedy, and registers as a skip, which makes the call exit non-zero. Built-in ops (`read`, `grep`, `edit`) still run: they come from the core that was invoked and are not of unknown provenance. They get a one-line stderr disclosure instead, because the validators and formatters around them are still the other tree's.

  **No legitimate cross-tree invocation was found to protect.** Wanting branch core with another checkout's presets is not a coherent thing to want; wanting the branch's tool *without cd-ing into it* is, and `cwd:<checkout>` as the first op already does exactly that — it chdirs before the config is loaded, so core and presets line up again. That is what the decline points at. `SUPERTOOL_ALLOW_MIXED_TREE=1` exists for anyone who has a case we have not thought of, and even then the verdict line carries the pairing rather than a bare `PASS`.
- **The mutable-global guard no longer implies it covers a codebase it only reads one file of, and `presets/` now has a guard of its own** ([#686](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/686)). `test_every_mutable_global_is_either_reset_or_deliberately_exempt` resolves every name through `getattr(supertool, …)`, so both its snapshot and its restore stop at one module — but nothing in a green run said so, and the name said the opposite. It is now `test_every_mutable_global_in_supertool_py_is_reset_or_deliberately_exempt`, and its failure message names its own boundary and points at the check that holds the rest. **This is not hypothetical and it was found the expensive way, twice.** `presets/_env.py` is imported by 29 presets under one module name, so they share one `_ANNOUNCED` ledger; correct in production, where a preset is a subprocess that reads its knobs and exits, and wrong in a pytest worker that runs many presets' suites in one interpreter. It surfaced as a macOS-only CI red on [#689](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/689) purely because xdist shards by core count and only the smaller runners co-located the two test files that collide. The guard that exists to prevent exactly that was green throughout.

  **The reach is scoped by mutation, not by mutability, and that is the whole design.** `presets/` holds 43 module-level dicts, lists and sets. Thirty-nine of them — `_FLAGS`, `_CHECK_GLYPH`, `_TERMINAL_PR_STATES` — are constant lookup tables that merely happen to be mutable types, and a guard demanding a registry entry for each would have been 91% noise. A guard that is mostly noise gets exempted without being read, which is how a guard stops working; the wide version would have been technically complete and practically dead. `tests/test_preset_global_lifetimes_686.py` asks instead whether a name is *actually mutated from inside a function at run time*. Four are, and each is a real lifetime decision rather than a formality.

  **A declaration is verified, not trusted.** Each of the four must name its mechanism in `tests/conftest.py`: `PRESET_RESET_GLOBALS` for what conftest itself clears (only `_env`, the one preset module it imports — snapshotting all 125 per test is the cost #686 declined), or `PRESET_SELF_CLEARING_GLOBALS` for the `presets/git/status.py` pattern of resetting in `main()`'s own prologue. The second is then re-derived from the source, so a claim that is not true fails the build rather than reading as a considered decision. That distinction earned itself immediately: `presets/mcp/stop.py` writes `_RUNTIME_HINT` inside `main()` as well, but 150 lines in and after a branch, and as the value being *used* — a looser rule of "mutated somewhere in `main`" would have waved it through. It is the fourth global, it had neither mechanism, and it is the pre-existing instance this issue predicted; it now clears itself at the top of `main()` alongside the other two. A third table, `PRESET_RESET_EXEMPT_GLOBALS`, is empty on purpose, and a companion test fails if any of the three ever names something that is no longer state, so the boundary cannot rot in either direction.

- **A mutating op that declined is now named in the footer and fails the call** ([#680](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/680)). Reported as "`batch:` skips a non-matching entry silently, keeps going, and exits 0". **Two thirds of that did not reproduce.** A batch entry whose `old` misses already prints its own `ERROR: old string not found` receipt *with* the nearest-match hint, and the call already exits non-zero — `_body_indicates_failure` matches an `ERROR:` on the line after a sub-op header, and has since `#7`. So the fix the title asks for was already there, and building it again would have been a day spent on a bug that did not exist.

  **What did reproduce is the part the reporter actually experienced.** They were piping to `tail`, and the last line was `[result] 6 ops run, 4 writes` — true, complete, and useless, because it states the failure as a subtraction the reader has to perform while already suspicious. The per-op `ERROR:` was real but sat above two `[validators]` blocks, and `tail` is exactly what a long validators block provokes (which is the whole reason `[result]` exists, #621). The branch went to CI with two `use` imports removed and their users left behind; `phplint` passed, because PHP does not resolve class names at parse time.

  **And one path was genuinely silent, in every context.** `replace` finding nothing returns `(0 occurrences of 'x' found)` — no `ERROR`, so the first-line failure marker never saw it and `./supertool 'replace:…' && git commit` committed. Reachable from a batch directly, and indirectly via `replace_all = true` on an `edit` entry, which promotes it to `replace`. That is the reported defect exactly; it was just not living where the report put it.

  **So: `[result]` gains a third field.** `[result] 3 ops run, 2 writes, 1 skipped`, and the call exits non-zero whenever anything was skipped. A **skip** is an op that ran and deliberately left the disk alone: `edit` whose `old` did not match or matched ambiguously, `replace` with zero occurrences, `vim` whose pattern missed (vim is atomic — none of its actions applied). It is counted where the decline is *decided*, never inferred from `ops - writes`: that subtraction is wrong for a multi-file `replace` (more writes than attempts), for `replace_dry` (a preview writes nothing by design), and for a validator rollback (a write genuinely made, then retracted) — and a skip count that is sometimes wrong is worse than none, being the same absence-read-as-fact it exists to stop. This is the ops-level form of the three states `docs/validators.md` already defines for validators.

  **The behaviour change is `replace`'s exit code.** A `replace` that matches nothing now exits `1`. Chains relying on the old `0` were relying on the defect, but it is a real break and it is the reason this is one line rather than a silent internal tidy.

  **The field is omitted when the count is zero**, against the issue's own suggestion of `0 skipped` on every line. A zero on the green path is the kind of number a reader learns to stop seeing — which is precisely how `4 writes` failed. A word that shows up only when it means something is the stronger signal, and it costs nothing on the overwhelmingly common path.

  **`batch:` is still not atomic, and this does not make it so.** Interdependent edits are the normal case and a half-applied set is rarely wanted, so the argument for an atomic default is real — but flipping it silently changes what every existing caller does, in a tool whose entire value is being predictable, and honest atomicity is not "stop early": it is snapshotting every touched file and restoring it through the deferred formatter and validator queues, which is a design, not a flag. The cheap half already ships as `continue_on_error: false`. What was broken here was the *information*, not the policy — and with the skip named and the exit code honest, a caller who wants all-or-nothing can now see that they need it. Atomicity belongs in its own issue, with its own rollback design.
- **A payload escape that TOML rejects is now rejected on every Python, instead of being silently rewritten below 3.11** ([#684](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/684)). `_load_at_file` parses a TOML payload with stdlib `tomllib` on 3.11+ and with `_mini_toml_loads` below it, and the two did not agree about invalid escapes. `_toml_basic_unescape` defaulted an unrecognised escape to the escaped character — `.get(escape, s[i + 1])` — so `path = "C:\Users\dev\notes.txt"` in a basic string became `C:Usersdevnotes.txt` on 3.9/3.10, and the op reported `path not found` at an address **the parser had manufactured**. On 3.11+ the same payload raised, with the `_toml_delimiter_hint` pointing at the way out. Same input, same tool, and the misleading half ran on the older interpreter. The CI matrix straddles that boundary, which is why [#683](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/683) showed two symptom shapes for one defect.

  **The fallback goes strict, because strict is what agreement costs here.** The constraint worth respecting was that refusing something `tomllib` accepts is worse than the current leniency — it would break payloads that work today. Rejecting invalid escapes does not do that: it rejects exactly what `tomllib` rejects. What *would* have narrowed the language is strictness alone, since the fallback never implemented `\u` / `\U` and had been mangling those the same silent way. So the escapes are decoded rather than refused, along with the line-ending backslash continuation inside a `"""` block, which the fallback used to turn into a literal newline where `tomllib` swallows it. Three divergences, one direction: whatever `tomllib` does.

  **The error names the backslash, since the old failure named nothing.** A rejected escape reports `invalid escape` for the key it appeared in and says to double the backslash or use a literal block. The `\U` branch carries the same advice — `C:\Users` trips the Unicode escape first, and an error about hex digits, while accurate, is not what that reader needs to hear.

  **The test pins agreement, not either parser.** Expectations live in one table; one leg holds `_mini_toml_loads` to it on every interpreter, and a 3.11+-only leg holds the *table* to `tomllib`, so it cannot drift into a transcript of the fallback's habits. A fixture hides `tomllib` from `sys.modules` so a 3.11+ runner also drives the fallback through the real `_load_at_file` seam — otherwise the leg that carried the bug goes untested on the machine most likely to run the suite.
- **`gh-issue:N` no longer silently truncates a long body** ([#681](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/681)). Hit live while triaging another issue for delegation: a body longer than `DESCRIPTION_MAX` (3000 chars) was cut with a raw `body[:desc_max]` slice, no marker anywhere, and the `## Comments (0)` line printed right after gave the output a natural-looking ending — it read as a complete issue rather than a partial one. The cut also landed mid-line, once producing the malformed fragment `## The` out of a real heading.

  **This is the tool's own signature defect** (per `docs/validators.md`, "Declining instead of guessing"): an absence produced by the read op, presented as an absence in the world. Every implementation brief this repo's agents work from starts from `gh-issue:N`, so a silently shortened body is a brief missing its last requirement with nothing saying so.

  The cap itself is right and stays — an unbounded render is a context blowout in the caller, a different bug in the same tool. Only the disclosure changes: the cut now backs off to the last line break at-or-before the cap instead of a raw byte offset, and the withheld amount is stated twice — once in the header, before `## Description`, so a reader who stops at the top still sees it, and once at the point of the cut, matching the existing `## Comments` truncation convention in the same file. Both name the exact char count withheld and say `use :full` to get the rest. `gh-pr`, `gl-issue`, and `gl-mr` bodies go through the identical unguarded `[:DESCRIPTION_MAX]` slice and were not touched here — filed as follow-ups rather than folded into this fix.

- **A git call that does not answer no longer speaks as though it did** ([#650](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/650)). Filed as "two tests flake under xdist load": a pre-push run aborted with `test_branch_reads_the_checked_out_branch` and `test_fresh_commit_on_an_open_pr_says_not_yet` red, both passing in isolation, neither touched by the branch under test. **The load hypothesis did not survive measurement.** On the machine that reported it, worst git latency was 0.30s while the real 11-worker suite ran and 0.76s under 96 CPU burners on 11 cores — 6x inside the 5s budget, at a load no runner will see. So "the machine was slow, raise the budget" would have been a fix aimed at an unmeasured cause, and the cause of that one stall is still unknown.

  **What is first-hand is the traceback, and what it shows is a defect regardless of why git stalled.** `presets/git/status.py::_git` let `TimeoutExpired` escape, so one slow `rev-list` — the courtesy line about divergence from master — replaced the whole report with a stack trace: no branch, no commits, no working tree, no PR. And `supertool._current_branch()` folded a timeout into `""`, the same value that means "there is no branch here"; `_branch_line()` renders `""` as silence, so a mutating op's receipt dropped its branch on exactly the run where the caller was least sure what state the repo was in — and that line exists to catch right-file-wrong-branch. Both are the house defect ([docs/validators.md](docs/validators.md), "Declining instead of guessing"): three states collapsed into two.

  **A stalled call now costs its own line, not the report.** `_git` returns exit code `124` — the shell convention for a timeout — so every call site's existing `returncode == 0` guard skips its section as it would for a git that failed, and `git-status` closes with a `git-status INCOMPLETE` line naming the calls that went unanswered and the budget they were cut off at. The exit code is the load-bearing half: rendering a stall as a success makes an empty `rev-list --left-right --count` print `0 ahead — branch has no own commits!` and an empty `rev-parse --abbrev-ref HEAD` print `Branch:` with nothing after it — a false alarm about the branch manufactured out of a fact about the machine. `_branch_reading()` returns `(branch, why)` and the receipt prints `[branch: UNKNOWN — <what stalled>]`; `_current_branch()` keeps its plain-string contract for every other caller. A **missing** `git` binary stays silent rather than declining — nothing on that machine was ever going to name a branch, and a decline that can never resolve is noise on every receipt.

  **The budget is now `SUPERTOOL_GIT_TIMEOUT` (default 5s, unchanged as shipped and pinned by a test).** The suite raises it to 30s the way [#553](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/553) raises the lint budget — insurance for a 2-core CI runner with a virus scanner on every temp file, where that budget has genuinely blown twice, and explicitly *not* a diagnosis of #650. Neither test is retried, marked flaky, or made quieter to pass: `test_branch_reads_the_checked_out_branch` declines with a stated reason when and only when git itself did not answer, which a companion test proves is unreachable on a healthy read. The new tests drive a real `git` to a real timeout through a `PATH` shim, so the defect reproduces deterministically without needing the load that could not be reproduced at all.

  **The load hypothesis is now disproved on a third and much heavier axis, and the same defect was still live in three more presets.** The 96-CPU-burner measurement above tests the wrong variable: every #650 failure happened while eleven xdist workers were each *spawning* git, and burners load the CPU without spawning anything. Re-measured on the axis that matters — 220 fresh repos built concurrently (nine git spawns each) while two full suites ran, at load average 63 on 11 cores — the worst observed latency for the exact call in the issue's traceback, `rev-list --left-right --count`, was **0.527s against a 5s budget**. A spawn-concurrency probe and a Rosetta-translation probe (this box runs an x86_64 `git` on arm64, so every spawn is translated) peaked at 0.086s. Five consecutive full `-n auto` runs on master were green. The trigger for that one stall remains unknown, and is now unknown at roughly 10x the load that was ruled out before.

  **What the search did find is the same conflation, unfixed, in `git-conflicts`, `git-resolve` and `git-merge`.** `_list_conflicts()` exists in triplicate and every copy returned `[]` for both "git says the tree is clean" and "git did not answer" — the `_current_branch()` defect exactly, in the one preset you only ever run because you are already stopped mid-merge. With a real unresolved file on disk and only the lookup failing, `git-conflicts` printed `State: merge in progress` and `No conflicted files.` in the same breath, then exited `0`: the one sentence that means "go ahead and commit", over live `<<<<<<<` markers. `git-resolve` was as bad by a longer route — its closing `Remaining: 0` is followed by `Next: git-commit …`. An index lock held by a concurrent git is enough to trigger both; no load required, which is how this was reproduced deterministically rather than hunted. All three now return `(paths, why_unavailable)` and print `UNKNOWN` with the reason, and **this changes behaviour**: a conflict lookup that fails or stalls exits non-zero where it used to exit `0`. `presets/git/conflicts.py::_git` also catches `TimeoutExpired` (returning `124`, as `status.py` does) and honours `SUPERTOOL_GIT_TIMEOUT`, so the traceback in the original report is no longer reachable from that preset either.

  **The triplication is left in place and is the obvious follow-up.** Three copies of one function is why fixing `status.py` in #685 did not fix these; consolidating them into `presets/git/_git_common.py` is the real repair, and doing it inside a fix for the behaviour would have made a one-line-per-file diff into a cross-preset refactor with no test to justify it.
- **A numeric env knob set to junk no longer ends the run in a traceback — and is not made silent in exchange** ([#654](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/654)). `SUPERTOOL_MAX_COMMITS=x ./supertool 'git-trail:…'` died in `ValueError: invalid literal for int() with base 10: 'x'`, pointing at `int()` in `presets/git/trail.py`. That names the line that broke but not the variable, not the value, and not what a good one looks like — and the caller who has to work it out is by definition someone who just got the environment wrong.

  **The obvious repair is the wrong one, and the issue was filed saying so.** `try: … except ValueError: return default` stops the crash by making the knob *silently ignored*: a caller who set a cap goes on believing it is in force. A traceback is loud, so this was never the silent-absence class that dominates this tracker — which means the fix had to be careful not to convert it into one. `docs/validators.md`'s three-state contract applies: honour the value, or say plainly it could not be read **and what is being used instead**. Never both silently. One helper (`presets/_env.py::env_int`/`env_float`, twinned in `supertool.py` as `_env_int`/`_env_float`) now does that everywhere: `note: SUPERTOOL_MAX_COMMITS='x' is not a whole number - ignoring it and using 20.`

  **The note goes to stdout, and that is load-bearing rather than stylistic.** `_run_custom_op` returns a preset subprocess's `stdout` on success and appends its `stderr` *only* on a non-zero exit — and falling back to a default **is** success. A warning written to stderr would have been discarded by supertool before reaching the caller, shipping the exact silence the issue forbids. A regression test pins the stream.

  **Out of range is refused, not clamped — a decision, not an inheritance.** `SUPERTOOL_MAX_COMMITS=-5` is announced and falls back to the documented default, on the same rule and in the same message shape as unparseable junk, because `-5` expresses no more usable intent than `x` does. The alternative on offer was the `max(0, …)` / `max(1, …)` clamp already present in `presets/gitlab/mrs.py`, and lifting it wholesale would have carried two silences across with it: that helper swallowed junk without a word *and* clamped without a word, so `SUPERTOOL_ENRICH_WORKERS=0` read identically to `=1`. **This changes behaviour**: an out-of-range value now lands on the default rather than on the floor. The floor is kept as a *validated minimum* — the invariant `ThreadPoolExecutor` needs still holds — and `test_get_config_clamps_workers_floor` in the `gl-mrs` and `gh-prs` suites was rewritten (and renamed) to assert the new contract rather than the old clamp.

  **The sweep is the point, and its boundary is executable.** The issue asked for a sweep rather than a two-site fix, and asked that a bounded one not render as complete coverage. All 49 bare numeric env parses across `presets/` and `supertool.py` were converted — 45 in 29 preset files, 4 in `supertool.py` — and `test_swept_files_have_no_bare_numeric_env_parse` scans the tree so the next one to appear fails a build instead of shipping. Its allowlist of declared exceptions is empty, and a second test fails if the allowlist ever names a file that no longer needs it, so the boundary cannot rot in either direction. **It has already earned that.** [#650](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/650) merged while this was in progress and brought `SUPERTOOL_GIT_TIMEOUT` — two more sites, in `presets/git/status.py` and `supertool.py`, in the silent-tolerate shape — and rebasing turned the scan red immediately rather than leaving them to be found the next time someone typed a timeout wrong. Both are swept here. The scan covers the *syntactic* pattern only; three knobs read in other shapes (`SUPERTOOL_PARALLEL`, the `SUPERTOOL_<OP>_<KEY>` family, and `SUPERTOOL_DEBUG`, which is truthiness-only and not numeric) were checked by hand and are named in a comment beside the allowlist.

  **A notice that repeats is a notice nobody reads.** `_get_op_int` is consulted several times to serve one `read`, and `_git_timeout` once per git call, so a single bad value would have printed the same line six times above the output it was warning about. Each distinct message is emitted at most once per process (`_ENV_ANNOUNCED`, registered as per-run scratch in `conftest.RESET_GLOBALS` per [#397](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/397)). Dedupe is per *message*, not per variable, so a knob that goes wrong a second way still gets its own line.

  **The first cut of these tests had the defect they were testing for.** Three of them proved "the fallback is the value actually used" by pinning commit counts from supertool's own history — `## Timeline (20 commits)` — which passed on a full clone and failed on eight of fourteen CI legs, because `actions/checkout` clones at depth 1 and the repo there has exactly one commit. The assertion was pinning a property of the developer's checkout while claiming to pin a property of the fix. They now build a 25-commit repo they own, guarded by a test that fails if that fixture ever shrinks below the cap under test — otherwise the assertions it feeds would go quietly green rather than red. `fetch-depth: 0` would also have made them pass, by changing CI for every job in the repo to suit one file. `docs/contributing.md` now says so under [Never assume the checkout has history](docs/contributing.md#never-assume-the-checkout-has-history).

  **Two of the sites were worse than the one reported.** `SUPERTOOL_DISPATCH_MAX_DEPTH` is parsed at **module scope** in `supertool.py`, so a bad value raised during *import* and took down every op in the tool, most of which have nothing to do with dispatch depth — the widest blast radius in the class, for the smallest knob. And three sites were already the *silent* half of the same defect rather than the loud one: `_parallel_workers` returned `0` for `SUPERTOOL_PARALLEL=x` (indistinguishable from never setting it, so a request for parallelism quietly got none), `_get_op_int` dropped an unusable `SUPERTOOL_<OP>_<KEY>` override and fell through to config despite the override being documented as taking precedence, and `_lint_timeout` defaulted on `=0` without a word. All three now name the variable, the value, and the number in force.

- **A batch sub-op that ran from an `@payload` is no longer echoed back as a colon string that runs a different op** ([#644](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/644)). The header is sharper than the report. It was filed as a rendering that "either parses as a different op or fails to parse"; it does not fail to parse. `replace` of `time: 10:30` printed `--- replace:time: 10:30:time: 11:45:/tmp/h.txt ---`, and pasting that runs cleanly — as a `replace` of `time` with `10` in a file named `30`. Copy-pasting a header is the natural thing to do when re-running a step or quoting one in a bug report, and on a `replace`/`edit` it pointed at a path nobody named. Same family as [#621](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/621): output presenting itself as a faithful account of an operation and not being one. There the loss was visibility; here it is fidelity.

  **There were two rendering paths and only one of them lied.** Under `_HEADER_ARG_MAX` (160 chars) the arg was echoed verbatim — the flattened lie. Over it, [#384](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/384)'s quoted, elided form (`edit: "What happened instead? Include hook out… (+45 chars)" → …`) took over, which is not a runnable colon string and so was already honest by accident. But that swap is gated on the op having **written**, so a *failing* long payload op fell back to the verbatim lie — at the one moment the header is the only surviving record of what was attempted.

  **The fix labels the route rather than faking a rendering.** A payload-sourced sub-op prints `replace:@payload → src/app/Config.py` — the route and the target, which is what identifies the step — at any length, success or failure. The issue offered rendering the payload route or dropping the header and labelling it; this is the second in the first's clothing, and the reason is that no faithful one-line colon rendering of a payload op exists, so producing something that *looks* like one is the defect rather than the fix. `@payload` is not a resolvable reference, so the header cannot silently misfire; re-dispatching it now answers `'@payload' is a header placeholder, not a reference` instead of a bare `@file not found: payload`, which reads as a missing file and sends the reader looking for one. **The invariant, pinned by a round-trip test rather than a string-shape assertion:** feed a header back to the dispatcher and it must either run the same op on the same target or not be a runnable op at all. A shape assertion passes while the header still lies.

- **A batch sub-op with more than one payload field was dispatched with its fields in alphabetical order** ([#644](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/644)). Found in the same lines and strictly worse than the header defect that led to them: `sorted(_item)` is not any op's argument order, and this was not a rendering — it was the arg that ran. `{"op": "between", "symbol": "…", "path": "…"}` dispatched as `between:<path>:<symbol>`, searching for the file inside the symbol name; `{"op": "read", "path": …, "offset": 5, "limit": 2}` became `read:2:5:<path>`. [#625](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/625) had just shipped an `@payload` route for read ops precisely so a `:` in a pattern would survive, and batch re-serialized it away again on the way in. Every existing test passed because all of them use a single `path` field, where alphabetical order and positional order coincide.

  **Read ops with a payload route now dispatch straight to the op**, exactly as a standalone `grep:@-` does. The rest are placed by declared order (`head`/`tail`, `tree`, `around_line`, `diff`), and an op with several fields and no declared order **declines** — the three-state rule from `docs/validators.md` §"Declining instead of guessing". Guessing an order is how the defect happened; a loud refusal is the one outcome that cannot quietly dispatch something else.

- **A relative `@payload` path now resolves against the directory the call was made from, not against the `cwd:` target** ([#672](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/672)). `./supertool 'cwd:~/other-repo' 'batch:@.max/edits.toml'` reported `@file not found` for a file that was plainly there. `cwd:` had moved the resolution root, so the payload was looked for inside the repo being edited — and the payload is written by the caller, so it lands next to the *call*. Since `cwd:` exists precisely because the target repo has no `./supertool` wrapper, the documented workflow put the payload on one side of the boundary and the lookup on the other every single time. This was the normal path through the feature, not a corner of it.

  **Two kinds of path were conflated and are now separated.** The `@reference` is an argument the caller typed and resolves against the invocation directory; `path = ` *inside* the payload is repo content and still resolves against `cwd:` — that second half is what makes `cwd:` worth having and is unchanged. The same separation covers the auto-resolved project root ([#363](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/363)), which moved the root the same way and was never reported.

  **No fallback, deliberately.** Trying the invocation directory and then quietly retrying under `cwd:` would have fixed the report and made the tool guess: with a same-named payload under both roots it would open one of them and say nothing. A payload missing from the invocation root is an error that names both roots and says which is which — the three-state distinction between "there is no such file" and "I could not look where you meant" (`docs/validators.md` §"Declining instead of guessing").

  **Breaking, narrowly.** A relative `@payload` that lives *inside* the `cwd:` target used to resolve and now does not — the error names the file it found there and prints the absolute path to use instead, so the break is self-repairing in one call. For the read-op payload route (`read:@…`, `grep:@…`) that combination never worked at all: the route is gated on the reference resolving, so it fell through to a literal-path read and failed with `file not found: @…`. Absolute `@paths`, `@-`, and calls without `cwd:` are unaffected.

- **`main()` no longer leaks its working-directory state into a later bare `dispatch()`** ([#672](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/672)). Found while fixing the above, not reported: the invocation-root state is scoped to the call with a `finally`, so a `cwd:` call cannot poison a subsequent `dispatch()` in a long-lived process — which is how the MCP server and the test suite both drive it.
- **Any post-push check could replace the receipt with a traceback, for a push that had already succeeded** ([#675](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/675)). Reproduced before anything was changed, at the layer that matters: with `git` removed from `PATH`, six of the seven helpers `presets/git/push.py` calls *after* the push has landed let an `OSError` straight out of `main()`. The remote had moved; the caller got a stack trace, no `[result]` line, and a `FAIL` from supertool — which reads as "the push blew up", the exact opposite of what happened. Every one of these runs under `_git`'s 30s `subprocess.run` timeout, so a `TimeoutExpired` does the same thing; the reachable cases are `ls-remote` (network) and any call made while another process holds the index.

  **The invariant is about the receipt, not about any one helper, so the fix is at both levels and they have different jobs.** Wrapping each site individually is what produced the state this was filed against — `_live_remote_sha` was the only guarded helper, patched when somebody hit it, and nothing generalised it. So the seven call sites now share one three-state primitive, `_checked_git`, returning `(result, "")` when git answered and `(None, why)` when it could not; and above them sits a guard in `main()` that catches whatever the next unguarded helper raises. The per-site guards are still the better fix per site, because they keep the remaining checks running and name the one that went missing; the structural one is what stops the invariant depending on anyone remembering. [#674](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/674) had established the vocabulary on two helpers (`⚠ … CHECK DID NOT RUN — <why>`) and this applies it to the rest, once.

  **A guard returning `""` would have satisfied the letter and re-introduced the house defect.** An empty string flows into this receipt as silence, and silence here is a positive claim — `_stale_base_advisory` prints nothing when your base is fresh, so a swallowed timeout renders "could not check" exactly like "checked, you are up to date". `None` cannot be mistaken for an answer. The distinction is preserved in the wording too: a non-zero exit where git is *answering* (`@{upstream}` on a branch that has none, a ref this clone does not have) keeps its existing "skipped, `git fetch` fixes it" line, while a call that never completed takes the "DID NOT RUN" vocabulary — because fetching is not the lever when git will not start, and pointing at it would be a confidently wrong cause ([#663](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/663)).

  **The catch-all deliberately does not swallow.** Converting a crash into a silently degraded receipt would trade a loud failure for a quiet one, which is the defect this op keeps being filed for. The exception is printed in full **on stdout** — supertool discards a preset's stderr entirely when the preset exits zero (`supertool.py`, the custom-op runner), and a landed push exits zero, so a crash reported on stderr would be invisible through the op. The verdict is *appended* to the crash, never in place of it. The exit code follows the **push**, not the receipt: a landed push that could not be described is still a landed push, and returning non-zero would tell every caller treating that as failure exactly the wrong thing. The op also states which phase it crashed in, so `NOT PUSHED - no push attempted`, `NOT PUSHED - UNVERIFIED … whether anything landed is UNKNOWN`, and `PUSHED … (RECEIPT INCOMPLETE …)` are three different answers rather than one hedge.

  **Two quieter defects on the same path came out with it, and the second is worse than the one filed.** `_live_remote_sha` — the one pre-existing guard — caught `TimeoutExpired` and not `OSError`, so the helper the issue lists as "guarded" was still a traceback waiting for git to fail to start; it also returned a bare `""`, so every caller reported `remote did not answer ls-remote` whatever actually went wrong. And `_push_verdict` decided its note from `head and live == head`, a conjunction in which a `git rev-parse HEAD` that never answered is indistinguishable from a remote that disagrees with local work — so a failed **local** read printed `verified, but remote != local HEAD`: a positive claim of divergence, manufactured out of an absence, on the one line [#623](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/623) exists to make callers read. Pinned with an unborn `HEAD`, the only mechanism that separates the two halves (`rev-parse HEAD` genuinely fails while `ls-remote` still answers). `_pushed_commit_count` lost its `"?"` fallback for the same reason — `(? commit(s))` is a decline nobody reads as one — and [#674](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/674)'s summary listing that call as already fixed was wrong: it fixed a different `rev-list`, extracted from the same function in the same commit.

  **The first cut of this fix reintroduced [#663](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/663) inside itself.** The mid-push crash verdict advised `git ls-remote origin/feature` — the joined form the receipt prints everywhere else, and not a remote git can resolve, so following the advice returns `fatal: 'origin/feature' does not appear to be a git repository`. Handing someone an UNKNOWN plus a command that errors is worse than handing them nothing. `_RUN` now keeps the remote and the ref apart as well as joined, and the test **executes** the advised command rather than matching it as a string, since a spelling assertion would pass on the broken form the day the two halves are rejoined.

  **Method.** TDD, red before green: **11 failed / 3 passed** against unmodified `master` — the 3 passing are the "silence stays a positive claim" guards, which must pass in both states. GREEN: **5376 passed / 66 skipped** across the suite, coverage 88.97% against a gate of 86% (unchanged, as expected — nothing under `presets/` counts toward it). No `_git` is mocked ([#649](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/649)): `PATH` is scrubbed to an empty directory so `subprocess.run(["git", …])` raises a genuine `FileNotFoundError`, `_CHECK_TIMEOUT` is set to 0 so the checks are really started and really killed, and an unborn `HEAD` separates the verdict's two reads — each asserted to have taken effect before anything is asserted about the receipt. Mutation tooling is **not configured on this repo** (no `mutmut`/`infection` in `pyproject.toml`), so as in [#674](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/674) the mutants are hand-written: **14 run, 14 killed**. The first run had 3 survivors and every one was a real gap — nothing pinned that a helper which could not ask carries a *reason* (the `_upstream_ref` mutant is the one with teeth: an empty upstream is read as "this branch has none" and the op pushes `-u origin HEAD`, **retargeting the branch's upstream** off an answer git never gave), nothing distinguished the stale-base "skipped" wording from "did not run", and nothing stopped the commit count reverting to `"?"`. Fixed by adding three tests, not by deleting mutants.

- **The radar's MR tier sorted MRs it never checked among the green, and disclosed it nowhere** ([#659](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/659)). Reproduced before anything was changed, at the layer radar renders: 45 open MRs, `enrich_cap` 40, the failing one at position 43 — the board came back `45 open | 40 green | 45 watched | feed ok` and `healthy=True`. `presets/watch/tiers/gl_mrs.py` calls the same capped `mrs._enrich` as `gl-mrs`, then sorts by `mrs._sort_key` → `_is_failing` → `_pipeline`, a field that only exists on the MRs enrichment reached; past the cap the predicate answered **`False` — "not failing", not "unknown"**. Worse on a delta board than on a cold one: an unenriched MR is not a standing problem, so after the run that first showed it, it is not on the board at all.

  **This is the [#652](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/652) defect on the surface where it costs more.** You go and *look* at a board; you *trust* a radar — it is the thing glanced at to decide nothing needs attention, so a false green there is acted on. The file had already written the rule it was breaking: `radar_report`'s own docstring says a report "that cannot be trusted to be complete … is the thing radar must never render as green". The tier's existing *capped* vocabulary is about **respawn** caps, a different thing, so a reader fluent in it was if anything more confident the board was complete — which is why the new line deliberately avoids that word.

  **What a radar should do with an incomplete board was established, not assumed.** `healthy` has exactly one consumer: `quiet_when_healthy`, which suppresses a tier's entire board; `radar.main` discards the aggregate and exits on `failures` alone. So `healthy=False` here is a claim about coverage, not an alarm — it cannot produce a red exit nobody can silence, and it is what stops a quiet-configured radar printing *nothing* about a board it could not see the whole of. Since the tier ships `RADAR_QUIET_DEFAULT = False`, that flag alone would be invisible in the default configuration, so the disclosure is also a line on the board: `radar: WARNING — 5 of 45 MRs on this board were not checked …`, plus `5 unchecked` in the footer, which previously counted those rows into a `"none"` bucket it then never printed. A caveat alone was rejected as too weak (the docstring argues past it) and a refusal to report at all as too strong for a channel whose only lever is suppression.

  **The vocabulary already existed and the call site had not taken it up.** [#657](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/657) gave `_enrich` an `_enriched = bool(detail)` marker and `_unchecked_count()`; the tier now reads both rather than inventing a parallel mechanism, and `presets/gitlab/mrs.py` is **unchanged** — no helper needed exporting. `bool(detail)` is why a detail lookup that timed out inside the cap is also disclosed, and why the notice names `SUPERTOOL_ENRICH_CAP` only when `total > cap`: on a three-MR board the cap never applied, and naming it would be a confidently wrong cause.

  **The cap is not lifted.** [#657](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/657) measured it — at shipped defaults it saves 20 calls out of 100, nearly inert, but `per_page` / `SUPERTOOL_PER_PAGE` are unbounded and a `per=200` board would fan out 400 calls with no ceiling. A budget with a real reason to exist is not fixed by deleting it.

  **Silence is pinned as hard as the disclosure.** `tests/test_watch_radar_unchecked_659.py` drives `radar_report()` — and, for the suppression case, `radar.main()` — over the two boundaries the tier actually reads (`glab mr list -F json`, and the per-MR detail/approvals API, faked because `glab` is unauthenticated in CI), so `live_open_mrs` and the cap inside `_enrich` genuinely run; a test that handed the tier a pre-enriched list would have passed on the broken code. Every case asserts the fixture really hit the cap (`len(fetched) == CAP`, and the past-cap iid absent from it) before asserting anything about the output. One test asserts a fully-checked board prints **no** marker and stays healthy — that is the half that rots. Nine mutants were run against the suite (disclosure never/always prints, always/never blame the cap, `healthy` ignores unchecked, footer drops the token, board drops the line, count re-derived as `total - cap`, and an off-by-one in the guard); all nine were killed. The `_mr` fixture in `tests/test_watch_radar.py` gained `_enriched: True`, since it stands for an MR that carries a `_pipeline` — i.e. one whose status was read.

- **`gl-mrs:failed` could not show a failing MR past the enrichment cap, and the one notice that would have hinted at it was disabled by the line that caused it** ([#652](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/652)). Both defects reproduced before anything was changed. `_enrich` reaches `enrich_cap` (40) MRs; `_is_failing` reads `_pipeline`, which only exists on the MRs it reached; so past the cap the predicate returned **`False` — not "unknown", `False`** — and `mrs = [m for m in mrs if _is_failing(m)]` dropped them. With the defaults listing 50 and enriching 40, the failing-MR board could not contain anything past position 40. The second defect is on the next line but one: that filter **rebinds `mrs`**, and the cap notice then tested `len(mrs) > enrich_cap` against the *filtered* list, which under `failed` is almost always a handful of rows — so the notice could not fire in the only mode where the truncation leaves no trace in the table.

  **The compounding is what makes this worse than the usual instance.** `:failed` exists to be the short list you check before walking away, so an absence there is read as a positive claim of health. Same shape as [#486](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/486), except the mechanism that would disclose the narrowing was switched off by the line that caused it. This also corrects the record from [#635](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/635), which examined this exact cap and left it alone on the grounds that "the board discloses it downstream". That was true of the default board and false of `:failed`, which is the mode where it mattered.

  **The decision on the unknowns was disclosure, not removal, and it was established rather than assumed.** Enriching the full set when `failed` is requested was measured first: enrichment is two `glab api` calls per MR, so at the shipped defaults the cap saves 20 calls out of 100 — nearly inert — but `per_page` and `SUPERTOOL_PER_PAGE` are unbounded knobs, and a `per=200` board would fan out 400 calls with no ceiling. A budget with a real reason to exist is not fixed by deleting it, so the cap stays and the board now states its own edges: `(5 of 45 MRs not checked — pipeline status unavailable, so a failing MR among them cannot appear on this board. Enrichment cap is 40; raise SUPERTOOL_ENRICH_CAP=N)`, printed **above** the table (header position — a footer is lost by exactly the consumer that truncates, [#633](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/633)/[#635](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/635)) and repeated in the footer as `N unchecked` beside the failing count it qualifies. The default board keeps its existing sentence and gains the same count and knob; its unchecked MRs are still listed, as `? none`, so its wording is deliberately weaker than `:failed`'s.

  **The unknown state is data, not arithmetic.** `_enrich` marks every MR it reaches with `_enriched`, and `_unchecked_count` counts the MRs lacking it. Recomputing `total - cfg["enrich_cap"]` would have produced the same number today and drifted silently the first time `_enrich` changed which rows it reaches — a disclosure that can go quietly wrong is the defect being fixed, one layer up. A test pins the seam by shrinking the cap inside `_enrich` and asserting the notice follows the work actually done.

  **The cap turned out not to be the only way an MR goes unchecked, and the first cut of this fix was certifying the other one as healthy.** `_fetch_mr_detail` returns `{}` on a timeout, a 5xx or an unparseable body, which lands as `_pipeline = ""` — indistinguishable to `_is_failing` from an MR past the cap, and well inside it. Reproduced the same way as the headline defect: five MRs, one broken lookup, `:failed` rendered a bare `No MRs match.` So `_enriched` is `bool(detail)` rather than `True`: the property that matters is "we read this MR's status", not "the loop reached it". **And the notice names the cap only when the cap is what cut** (`total > cap`) — on a five-MR board the unchecked MR is a failed lookup, and pointing at a limit that never applied would be a confidently wrong cause offering an escape that cannot work, which is the mistake [#635](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/635) spent a paragraph on. The mutation that always blames the cap survived the first version of that test, which asserted a substring the new wording never contained — a test that would have passed on anything, caught by mutating rather than by reading it.

  **`failed,iids` discloses on stderr.** That feed pipes into the `watch` supervisor, so an MR dropped there is one no poller ever starts on and nobody looks at again — the longest-reach version of this omission. stdout stays ids and nothing else; the notice goes to stderr. Losing it on both streams was the one option not available.

  **Silence is pinned as hard as the disclosure.** Four tests assert that a fully-checked board prints *nothing* extra on either stream — no marker, no knob name, empty stderr — including the all-clear case where `:failed` renders `No MRs match.`, because that unqualified sentence is the whole product. `nopipe` is pinned too: it claims nothing about pipelines, so a cap notice there would be noise. `tests/test_gitlab_mrs_failed_cap_652.py` drives `main()` over the two boundaries the op actually reads — the `glab mr list -F json` payload and the per-MR detail/approvals API, faked because `glab` is unauthenticated in CI — and asserts only on rendered stdout/stderr, never on a predicate's return value. Every case asserts the fixture really hit the cap (`len(fetched) == CAP`) before asserting anything about the output, because a fixture that quietly enriched everything would make the file green while testing nothing ([#641](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/641) twice over). Five mutants were run against the suite — dropping the `_enriched` marker, moving the notice back below the filter, re-deriving the count from the configured cap, deleting the stderr disclosure, and letting the notice fire on a clean board — and all five were killed.

  **Not affected, checked rather than assumed:** `presets/github/prs.py`. Its `failed` filter reads `_checks`, set by `_annotate` from the `statusCheckRollup` field that ships in the `gh pr list` JSON — free, uncapped, and applied to every PR. The enrichment cap there governs review threads, which the filter does not read.
- **`git-push` reported a force-push over an existing branch as `(branch created)` — the opposite of what happened** ([#661](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/661)). Reproduced end to end against a real bare remote before anything was changed: a colleague's commit destroyed, and the receipt reading `Remote now at da11f14 (branch created)` two lines under its own `Force discarded 1 remote commit(s)`. `(branch created)` was inferred from `remote_before` being empty, and `remote_before` is empty whenever `@{upstream}` does not resolve — which needs `branch.<name>.remote`/`.merge`, while `--force-with-lease` leases against the remote-tracking **ref**. Unset only the config, as `git branch --unset-upstream` or a worktree checked out without tracking does, and the lease still passes, the push still overwrites the remote, and the op announces a creation. Not a cosmetic mislabel: it fires on the one operation here that destroys work irrecoverably, and tells the caller the least alarming story available — nothing was there, so nothing was lost. Someone who force-pushed over a colleague's branch reads that and stops looking.

  **The answer comes from git, not from a local absence.** `_push_outcome` reads the `--porcelain` per-ref summary — the machine-readable channel [#641](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/641) established — where the four outcomes cannot be confused: `[new branch]`, `<old>..<new>` (two dots), `<old>...<new> (forced update)` (three dots, `+` flag), `[up to date]`. So a genuine creation still says `(branch created)`, an overwritten branch says `Remote <old> → <new> (force-updated — the branch already existed on the remote and was overwritten)` **naming the SHA git says it destroyed**, a fast-forward says the branch already existed, and an up-to-date ref stops being reported as a push at all. `_forced_update_old_sha` ([#655](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/655)) is now a thin reading of the same primitive rather than a second parser of the same bytes.

  **The fourth state is a decline, and it reaches the verdict.** When git reported no per-ref line, or a summary this grammar does not cover, the receipt says `⚠ Remote now at <sha> — what it pointed at BEFORE this push is UNKNOWN`, says explicitly that this is *not* evidence of a creation, names `git reflog show <remote>/<branch>`, and carries `PRE-PUSH REMOTE STATE UNKNOWN` onto the `[result]` line — a receipt is read from the bottom ([#623](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/623)) and a body line several rows up is lost to `| tail`. Three states, not two (`docs/validators.md`, "Declining instead of guessing"). The non-fast-forward recovery path also pushes with `--porcelain` now, because it fed the same receipt with no evidence to read.

  **The variant with nothing else to catch it was the worse one.** The reported case is a `:force-with-lease` push, where [#660](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/660)'s discard check at least contradicts the false line. An *ordinary* push over an existing branch with no upstream configured has no force machinery at all: `Remote now at 70a90ec (branch created)` was the receipt's only statement about the remote, and the branch had been there all along. Both are pinned.

- **Two more receipt checks in `git-push` swallowed a non-zero git exit and printed nothing** ([#662](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/662)). Both reachable, both reproduced with real failures. The ahead/behind block guarded on `if ab.returncode == 0:` with no `else`; an in-sync push legitimately prints nothing extra there, so a `rev-list --left-right --count HEAD...@{upstream}` that failed was indistinguishable from one that ran and found agreement. And `_uncommitted_leftovers` never read the return code at all — a failed `git status --porcelain` yields empty stdout, which became an empty list, which renders as silence, which in this receipt means *nothing was left behind*. So the warning that exists to catch work you forgot to commit went quiet on precisely the run where git was not answering and you were least sure what state you were in.

  **Same three-state contract, deliberately without a verdict suffix.** `⚠ vs upstream: UNKNOWN — <command> exited N — <why>` and `⚠ UNCOMMITTED-CHANGES CHECK DID NOT RUN — <why>`, the latter stating in plain words that it is not claiming a clean tree and naming `./supertool 'git-status:full'`. Neither adds a suffix to `[result]`, and that is a decision rather than an omission: the verdict already carries the verified remote SHA against local HEAD, which is the load-bearing half of the ahead/behind block, and the leftovers warning is a body line in its *working* case too — suffixing only the failure would put the check in a channel it otherwise never uses. A `[result]` line that grows a clause for every soft check stops being readable, which is the one property [#623](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/623) bought. [#661](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/661)'s decline does take the suffix, because there the doubt *is* the push outcome.

  **A fatal bug was hiding inside the quiet one.** `_git` runs every call under a 30-second `subprocess.run` timeout, and these two run **after** the push has landed. A `TimeoutExpired` or `OSError` there is an unhandled traceback that costs the caller the entire receipt of a push that succeeded — the [#399](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/399)/[#640](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/640) failure shape, arriving as a stack trace instead of a verdict. Both call sites now decline instead of raising. The other post-push `_git` calls in the receipt still have this exposure and are out of this change's blast radius.

- **`git-push`'s timeout advice named a knob that cannot lengthen the budget that expired** ([#663](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/663)). `_report_push_timeout` told the caller to raise `ops.git-push.timeout` in `.supertool.json`. The push subprocess runs under `_PUSH_TIMEOUT = 300`, a bare constant read from no config, so the advice is followed, nothing changes, and the caller is left believing they already tried the fix — then goes looking for a slow network or a broken remote, because the budget "already moved". Naming a knob that does not govern the thing that cut is a confidently wrong disclosure and worse than silence, because silence does not lie ([#633](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/633)).

  **The advice was corrected rather than the constant promoted, and that was the judgment call.** Making `_PUSH_TIMEOUT` configurable would make the original sentence true, but the constant is not arbitrary — the module docstring states the invariant it exists for: this budget must stay **strictly under** the op-level cap in `presets/git.json` (300 vs 420) so that *this script*, not supertool's outer cap, owns the timeout and can ask `ls-remote` what landed before reporting. A process killed by the outer cap verifies nothing and leaves the caller acting on a bare `FAIL (timeout …)` for a push that succeeded ([#399](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/399)). Exposing it as a knob means enforcing that ordering, and both enforcements are worse than the problem: clamping silently gives the user a value they did not ask for — trading a loud failure for a quiet one, the exact move this repo keeps filing bugs about — and refusing means a config typo blocks a push. `_report_recovery_timeout` had already settled the same question the same way one function below, naming `_RECOVER_TIMEOUT in presets/git/push.py` and adding that raising the op-level cap alone will not move it; two different answers to one question in one file would have been worse than either.

  **"Edit the source" is not advice on its own, so the receipt also names the lever that works.** On this path the push **landed** — the branch is reached only when `ls-remote` says the remote already matches HEAD — and the only cost of the timeout is a truncated receipt. Re-running `git-push` prints it in full and reports already-up-to-date, with no configuration change at all. That was invisible in the old sentence, which offered a config edit that could not work in place of a re-run that always would.

  **Tested against a real timeout.** The suite pushes once so the remote matches HEAD, then sets `_PUSH_TIMEOUT` to `0` and pushes again: `subprocess.run`'s own clock kills the real push subprocess and the real timeout path executes. Only the constant is substituted — no `_git` is mocked ([#649](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/649)).

- **A force-push that destroyed someone else's commits could report destroying nothing** ([#655](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/655)). `_discarded_by_force` had three return paths and two of them were a bare `[]` — no pre-push remote SHA, and a `git log` that exited non-zero — which the caller could not tell apart from "the check ran and nothing was discarded". So a check that *could not run* rendered byte-for-byte like a clean force-push. This is the one check in `git-push` whose subject is **other people's commits**, on the one operation in the op that destroys work irrecoverably from the remote's point of view, and the failure direction was the harmful one: the receipt reassured precisely where it should have warned. `--force-with-lease` does not cover it — the lease can be perfectly current and the caller still be discarding commits that were never listed.

  **Three states, not two** (`docs/validators.md`, "Declining instead of guessing"). Checked-and-found lists the commits *and* now carries `FORCE-DISCARDED N remote commit(s)` on the `[result]` line, because a receipt is read from the bottom ([#623](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/623)) and the listing sits several lines above it. Checked-and-empty prints **nothing**, so the absence of a warning stays a positive claim. Could-not-check prints `⚠ DISCARD CHECK DID NOT RUN — <why>`, says in plain words that whether commits were destroyed is UNKNOWN, and names the one command that settles it — `git log <old> --not HEAD` when the SHA is known, `git reflog show <remote>/<branch>` when it is not.

  **How loud, and what it deliberately does not do.** It does not block, abort or undo the push: the caller asked to force and that decision stands. It does not fire on ordinary pushes — the whole path is behind `:force-with-lease` — and it does not default to a frightening message when the check simply had nothing to find, which is the silent case. The one thing it refuses is letting "could not check" render as "checked, clean" on a destructive operation, in the body *and* on the verdict line.

  **The empty-SHA case was reachable, and it was worse than the reported one.** `--force-with-lease` leases against the **remote-tracking ref**, while the op's pre-push SHA comes from `@{upstream}`, which needs `branch.<name>.remote`/`.merge`. Remove only the latter — `git branch --unset-upstream`, a worktree checked out without tracking — and the lease still passes, the push still overwrites the remote, and the old call-site guard `if "force-with-lease" in flags and remote_before:` skipped the check **without a word**. Reproduced end to end: a colleague's commit erased, receipt `PUSHED … (verified, 1 commit(s))`. The SHA now falls back to the one git itself reports on the `--porcelain` channel (`+ <old>...<new> (forced update)`) — the same machine-readable source [#641](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/641) established for the non-fast-forward decision, never free text. That flag is also what proves the *silent* case: when git says this push did not force-update the ref, nothing could have been discarded, so the silence is measured rather than assumed.

  **The tests use real repositories and real failures, not mocked ones** (`tests/test_git_push_force_discard_655.py`). A mocked failure pins the implementation rather than the contract ([#649](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/649)), so the `git log` is broken by an invalid `log.date` in the repo config — which breaks `git log` and, verified in the fixture, nothing else, so the push still lands and only the discard check is unable to answer — and the missing upstream is produced by unsetting the real config keys. The load-bearing case is the one where **the check fails while commits genuinely were discarded**, and it asserts the output does not read as a clean push; a suite covering only the happy path passes on the broken version. Every fixture asserts it actually took effect (the push forced, the commit really left the branch, `git log` really broke) before anything is asserted about the receipt, and silence is pinned as hard as disclosure. All twelve hand-written mutants of the new logic are killed.

- **`git-trail` and `gl-issue` cut their lists without saying so; the third reported site was already disclosing and was left alone** ([#635](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/635)). Three sites were filed off [#622](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/622)'s sweep. Each was checked before being touched, and **one of the three needed no fix at all**: the `mrs[:cap]` / `prs[:cap]` enrichment cap in `presets/gitlab/mrs.py` and `presets/github/prs.py` is disclosed downstream by the board itself — `(pipeline enrichment capped at 40 MRs)` and `(review-thread enrichment capped at 40 PRs)`, both printed *above* the table, which is already the header position [#633](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/633) had to learn the hard way. Nothing was added there. A redundant second marker would have made the diff look complete at the cost of making the output worse.

  **The `gl-issue` site was the half-silent kind, and that is the most misleading shape.** `Related MRs: 47` printed a correct total directly above a list cut to ten, with nothing marking the cut — so the failure mode is not "the reader misses ten of forty-seven", it is "the reader concludes the tool's own totals disagree with its own output" and stops trusting both. It now reads `Related MRs: 10 of 47 shown (37 not listed — count limit of 10; use :full for all)`, repeated under the list. The escape is `:full`, which already meant "stop truncating" for the description and the comments, so the cap is discoverable from the grammar the op already has rather than from a new env knob nobody would find.

  **The `git-trail` site was reported as fully silent and is not — it was footer-only, which is the specific mistake [#633](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/633) made first.** `(4 more commits — showing first 10 only)` has been printed since the op was written; what was missing is the header. That distinction is the whole point: a footer is lost by exactly the consumer that cuts the output, so the disclosure is absent for the one reader it exists for. `## Details` now carries `[CAPPED: 10 of 47 commits shown by count — raise SUPERTOOL_TRAIL_DETAIL_CAP=N]`, planned before the first byte goes out, and the footer names the same knob. The cap became a real env knob in the process, because a disclosure that names no way to raise it is a dead end.

  **A twelfth site turned up while eyeballing the fix, and it was making the fix itself dishonest.** `## Timeline (20 commits)` is bounded by `SUPERTOOL_MAX_COMMITS`, silently — `git log -20` returning exactly 20 rows is indistinguishable from a repository that has exactly 20 matching commits. So the new detail marker would have printed `10 of 20` with `20` presented as a measured total when it was a ceiling: a confidently wrong number, which this repo rates as strictly worse than silence. The pickaxe now asks for `max_commits + 1` and shows `max_commits`, so the overshoot is what converts the ceiling into a statable one, at a cost of one extra log row. The timeline header says `[CAPPED: newest 20 by count, more exist — raise SUPERTOOL_MAX_COMMITS=N]` and **claims no total**, because none was measured; the detail denominator then renders `10 of 20+` rather than inventing one.

  **Every marker names a *count*, because a count is what cut** — none of these is a byte budget, so [#633](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/633)'s wording ("limited to N bytes by size") was deliberately not copied across. Pointing at a knob that does not govern the cut is the failure that rule exists to prevent.

  **Silence is pinned as hard as disclosure.** Half the tests assert that an uncut result prints *nothing* extra — bare `## Details`, bare `Related MRs: 3`, no knob name anywhere in the output — so the absence of a marker stays a positive claim rather than an ambiguity, and cannot be quietly broken by a later change that decides to always print a status line. `tests/test_git_trail_635.py` runs `trail.py` as a subprocess against throwaway repos built with real commits and a real `git log -S`: the cut is produced by git, not by a mock, so the test cannot pass by pinning the implementation, and each case asserts the fixture actually ran (`## Timeline (14 commits)`) before asserting anything about the marker. `tests/test_gitlab_issue_related_mrs_635.py` fakes the glab API response — the boundary the op reads — and asserts only on rendered stdout.
- **`git-push`'s rebase recovery threw a raw traceback on a slow fetch or rebase, leaving a half-rebased worktree with no receipt** ([#640](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/640)). `_recover_by_rebase` wrapped its two `push` calls in `try/except subprocess.TimeoutExpired` but not `_git(["fetch", …], timeout=120)` or `_git(["rebase", …], timeout=120)`, both of which reach `subprocess.run(timeout=…)` and raise. **Confirmed reachable, and reproduced rather than argued**: a `git rebase` stalled by a custom low-level merge driver expires *after* git has created `.git/rebase-merge`, so the exception fired on a tree git had already paused — the caller got a stack trace and a paused rebase nobody told them about. Same class as [#623](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/623): the op mutates state and then fails to say what it did.

  **The fix is a receipt, not a suppression.** Catching the timeout and returning quietly would be strictly worse than the traceback — a traceback at least says something broke. The op still fails (`return 1`); what changes is that the failure names the worktree state. `_rebase_state()` reads `rebase-merge` / `rebase-apply` through `rev-parse --git-path` (correct in a worktree, where `.git` is a file) and answers with **three** states, not two: `in-progress` gets both exits spelled out (`git rebase --continue` / `git rebase --abort`) plus a `git-conflicts` pointer; `not-started` states the working tree is unchanged; and when git itself does not answer, the verdict says the rebase state is **UNKNOWN** and to run `git status` first. Defaulting that third case to "not-started" would tell a caller whose worktree is paused that nothing happened, which is the worst thing this receipt could claim. The `120s` budget is now the named `_RECOVER_TIMEOUT`, and a backstop around the whole recovery call catches the path's smaller git calls too.

  **The tests use real timeouts, not mocked exceptions** (`tests/test_git_push_hazards_640_642_647.py`). The fetch is routed through a sleeping `ext::` transport helper with `remote.<name>.pushurl` left on the real bare repo, so the non-fast-forward rejection that opens the recovery path is a genuine rejected push and only the fetch stalls; the rebase is stalled by a sleeping merge driver mid-rebase. Both assert a sentinel proving the helper actually spawned — a fixture that cannot spawn otherwise makes the test pass while testing nothing ([#649](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/649)).

- **`git-push` degraded silently twice: the stale-base check skipped on a non-`origin` remote, and `:watch` could no-op inside a worktree** ([#642](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/642)). Both are the same shape — a check that *could not run* rendering identically to one that ran and found nothing.

  **(1) `_post_push_advisories` counted `HEAD..origin/{target}` regardless of the branch's actual upstream.** On a fork/upstream layout the ref does not resolve, the count exits non-zero, and the warning simply did not print: **a genuinely stale base rendered exactly like a fresh one**, and the caller pushed onto an out-of-date base believing the op had checked. The remote is now resolved from the upstream via the `_split_upstream` the file already had, and passed to `_post_push_advisories` as a **required** parameter so a new call site cannot quietly reintroduce the constant.
