A ref that still does not resolve prints an explicit `⚠ stale-base check skipped — <ref> does not resolve locally, so how far behind the target you are is UNKNOWN`, with the `git fetch` that enables it. Silence from that check now means exactly one thing: it ran, and the base is fresh.

  **(2) `_spawn_watch` resolved `supertool` as the repo-root wrapper only.** That wrapper is a gitignored symlink, absent in every git worktree — which is the environment agents work in — so `Popen` failed, the `OSError` was swallowed, and `:watch` degraded to the manual hint with no sign a spawn had been attempted. It now falls back to `sys.executable supertool.py`, which is the invocation that works there, and returns `(started, how)` instead of a bare bool so a failure can be *named*: `⚠ :watch requested but the watcher could not be started — <why>`, followed by the command to run by hand. `:watch` with no open MR/PR says so rather than printing nothing.

- **`git-push:watch` was advertised, accepted, and did nothing — and every unknown flag vanished the same way** ([#647](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/647)). `presets/git.json` advertised `git-push[:force-with-lease][:no-verify][:watch]` while `_KNOWN_FLAGS` held only the first two, and the parser was `if t in _KNOWN_FLAGS: flags.add(t)` with **no else**. So `"watch" in flags` was permanently `False`, `_spawn_watch` was unreachable dead code, and the caller who typed `:watch` got the ordinary "run this yourself" hint with no indication the flag had been discarded.

  **`:watch` is implemented, not withdrawn**, because `_spawn_watch` was complete — this was a wiring bug, not an unbuilt feature — and because the worktree spawn failure above is fixed in the same change. Shipping the wiring alone would have delivered a flag that works only in the live clone.

  **The larger defect is the missing `else`, and an unknown flag is now refused rather than dropped.** `git-push:no-verifyy` used to run an ordinary *verified* push while the caller believed the pre-push hook had been skipped; `git-push:force` did the same for a force they thought they had asked for. Refusal is chosen over warn-and-push because nothing has changed yet at that point in `main()`: the cost of stopping is a retype, the cost of continuing is a push whose behaviour is not the one requested. The receipt names the flag, lists what *is* accepted, states that nothing was pushed, and exits `2`. **Blast radius, stated plainly:** a script carrying a typo'd flag that used to push silently now fails instead — which is the point, but it is a behaviour change, not a pure bug fix.

- **`git-push` rewrote local history off a substring: a pre-push hook printing `fetch first` triggered an unrequested fetch + rebase** ([#641](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/641)). `_is_non_fast_forward` decided "the remote rejected this push as a divergence" by scanning the **merged** stdout+stderr of the push subprocess for `fetch first` / `non-fast-forward` / `tip of your current branch is behind`. A pre-push hook writes to exactly those two streams, so a policy hook, a linter, or a test harness echoing git's own hint could put any of those phrases in front of the predicate. The consequence is not a misleading message: `_recover_by_rebase` runs `git fetch` and then **`git rebase` on the caller's branch**, so the op rewrote local history on the strength of a substring appearing in text it did not produce — and a clean rebase looks exactly like a successful push afterwards.

  **Reproduced end to end before anything was changed**, against a hermetic bare remote and a real hook: with the remote one commit ahead and a hook that blocks the push while printing `fetch first`, HEAD came back a different SHA and the receipt read `NOT PUSHED - REJECTED after a clean rebase`. The push never reached the remote at all — the hook stopped it — so there was nothing to recover from and the rebase was pure damage. One correction to the issue's framing: the predicate sits behind `if result.returncode != 0`, so a hook printing the phrase on a push git **accepts** was never able to reach it. The reachable shape is a hook that *blocks*, which is also the shape where a hook has most reason to be printing divergence advice.

  **The channel is `git push --porcelain`, and the reason is that it separates the streams at the source rather than parsing them apart.** Porcelain puts git's per-ref status on **stdout** in a fixed three-field grammar — `<flag> TAB <from>:<to> TAB <summary>` — and the decision now reads only a line carrying the `!` flag *and* naming the ref we pushed. The two rejected alternatives: git's **exit code** cannot distinguish a remote rejection from a hook refusal (both are 1) and is the very ambiguity the module already documents working around; **stderr in isolation** is not a clean channel either, because in default mode git's own `! [rejected] … (fetch first)` and the hook's output are *both* on stderr — measured, not assumed. Porcelain's decisive property is what an empty result means: a hook-blocked push produces **no status line at all**, because git never contacted the remote. Hook output would have to be a tab-separated line with the `!` flag and our exact ref to be seen — not something a hook does by accident.

  **All three markers were fixed as a class, and so were the hints that had the same exposure.** The `stale info` lease hint and the `rejected` / `declined` server-side-rule hint also scanned the merged stream, so a hook's wording could pick which advice the caller was shown; both now read the same porcelain ref status. `[remote rejected]` (protected branch, pre-receive hook) is explicitly excluded from the divergence path — it is a server-side rule, and rebasing does not help. The dead disjunct in `"stale info" in low or "stale" in low` went with it.

  **The undetermined state fails loudly rather than falling through.** `docs/validators.md` ("Declining instead of guessing") is the contract, and the worst available option here is silently entering a history-rewriting recovery path on a state the tool could not determine. When git reports no ref status, the op stops and says exactly that — naming the likely cause (local pre-push hook or transport), stating that a rebase would not help, and pointing at `git-push:no-verify` — then dumps the hook's own output and ends on the `[result]` verdict line that [#623](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/623)/[#639](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/639) require. The genuine non-fast-forward still detects and still recovers; that path has its own hermetic test asserting the local commit survives, the remote tip is the replayed commit, and the verdict is `PUSHED`.

  **The deliverable is the test, not the predicate.** `tests/test_git_push_hook_text_nff_641.py` drives `push.main()` against a bare remote with a real `pre-push` hook, parametrized over all three markers, and asserts the things a rebase would disturb rather than an internal return value: `.git/FETCH_HEAD` never appears (neither `clone` nor `ls-remote` writes it, so its presence *is* the unrequested fetch), and HEAD is byte-identical afterwards. Every hook-dependent test asserts the hook actually ran via a sentinel file — a fixture that cannot spawn would otherwise make the whole file pass while testing nothing, which has happened on this repo twice — and the hook is a `#!/bin/sh` shim exec'ing `sys.executable` on a Python file, avoiding the three Windows traps (no `/bin/sh` semantics for `echo`, the bare-name `python3` App Execution Alias, and `str(Path)` eaten by POSIX-mode `shlex.split`).
- **A test comparing two rendered blocks could pass because the two differed only in a duration supertool measured itself — and it failed in the direction that reports a defect fixed** ([#643](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/643)). Surfaced by [#621](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/621)'s own agent, in its own RED run, unprompted: the new headline invariant test **passed under xdist and failed serially**, on the same code, with the bug fully present. The `[validators]` block renders its own elapsed time, so two tails identical in every way that mattered compared unequal. That assertion exists to say *"a no-match and a real edit are indistinguishable — that is the bug"*; jitter made them distinguishable, so the test went green and **reported the defect fixed while nothing had been fixed.** Green when it should be red, non-deterministically, depending on scheduling and on whether xdist is in play — invisible precisely when it matters, and this repo's signature defect one layer in: a difference produced by the instrument, read as a difference in the world. [#639](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/639) normalised durations out of that one test. This is about every other one.

  **The audit was the deliverable, and the finding is that the class is one.** Every test in `tests/` that compares two rendered outputs to each other was swept — direct `==`/`!=` between two captured outputs, helper-wrapped comparisons, `in`/`not in` between two renders, `set`/`sorted`/`splitlines` forms — and for each candidate the origin of the output was traced: mutating `dispatch` (can carry `[validators]`), custom-op dispatch (carries the `PASS (0.02s)` header), or a direct call to a preset function or `op_*` (carries neither). **Exactly one has a varying field reaching the comparison, and it is the one [#639](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/639) already fixed.** The rest compare preset functions invoked directly, `capsys` output that never passes through supertool's render path, or values that are not text at all. Of the assertions pinning a literal that contains a *real* rather than mocked time-derived field, six exist, and **none of them can flake**: three assert only shape (`FAIL (timeout `, `"s)"`, `\d+\.\d+s`), and three pin the `read` file-age suffix (`10d`, `8w`, `mo`) against an mtime the test itself planted with `os.utime` — deterministic by construction, not measured. The remainder assert on a `duration_ms` or `elapsed_s` the test supplied as a constant, which is case (c): already tolerant, and left alone. So the honest verdict is that the incident was real and isolated, and the worth of this issue is prospective rather than remedial: nothing stopped the next one, and the next one is silent when it goes wrong.

  **Fixed at the renderer, not at each call site, because a call site is a thing to remember.** `SUPERTOOL_DETERMINISTIC_TIME=1` makes every duration supertool *measured* render as a frozen `0.0s`, and `tests/conftest.py` sets it beside the seven `SUPERTOOL_*` switches already there for determinism — so a test author gets the property without knowing this issue exists, which is the point. One seam carries it: `_elapsed_since(t0)` replaces all ten `time.monotonic() - t0` expressions that feed rendered output — the custom-op `PASS`/`FAIL`/timeout header, `_validator_run_one`'s `elapsed_s` on its normal, timeout, unspawnable and unparseable paths, the builtin syntax backstop, and the syntax floor's own `duration_ms`. Normalising in each test would have left the next test unprotected, which is the failure mode being fixed rather than a fix for it.

  **The switch is test-only, and pinned as such.** Nothing in shipped code, presets, validators, formatters, hooks or CI sets it, and a test fails if that ever changes — a real run must still report the real time, because that number is how a human sees which validator is slow. A second test deletes the variable and asserts a 0.30s op still reports at least 0.25s, so the mode cannot quietly become the default and freeze a field real users read.

  **Durations supertool was *given* are deliberately not frozen.** An adapter's `duration_ms` is a value out of the reply, not a measurement, and tests supply it as a constant — so freezing it at render would have destroyed working assertions (`assert "(12ms)" in row`) to fix a defect that is not there. The residual is named rather than left implicit: the ~30 bundled adapters under `validators/` and `formatters/` time themselves, so a test comparing two blocks rendered from *real* adapter runs would still see jitter. No such test exists today, and closing it means 30 files each honouring the switch, bought against no defect.

  **`tests/_render.py::stable_render` covers what the environment cannot reach** — a recorded fixture, a subprocess with its own environment, a test that deliberately unsets the switch to exercise the real formatting path. It rewrites duration-shaped tokens and nothing else, because **a normaliser that strips too much makes its tests pass on anything, which is a worse version of this bug.** Eight tests pin that: counts, verdicts, tool names, line numbers, diff metrics and [#621](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/621)'s own `[result]` footer all survive normalisation, and one more asserts that a normalised comparison still goes red when a validator genuinely starts failing.

  **Found, not fixed, and reachable in real output:** the `read` / `workspace` file-age suffix (`10d`, `8w`, `3mo`) recomputed from `time.time()` against the file's mtime; `mcp:status`'s PID, UPTIME and IDLE columns — whose existing tests already dodge them with substring assertions, which is a tell that this has bitten someone before; `gl-mr`'s `running 3m` and `gl-runners`' `seen 12m`, both computed from now against an API timestamp; and the `%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S` stamp in the call log. None is compared block-to-block by any test today. Rendered *ordering* was checked as well and is not a source: every thread-pool fan-out reassembles in declared or submission order before rendering.

- **`git-push` ended on its untracked-file list instead of its verdict, so `| tail -6` never said whether the push landed** ([#623](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/623)). Reproduced at the layer a caller reads — the last N lines of rendered output — with the reported working-tree shape (23 generated `placeholder-*.zip` files, no open MR): the tail was six `??` lines and nothing else, and the author had to run `git fetch` + `git log FETCH_HEAD` twice in one session to learn what the op was run to tell him.

  **The fix is ordering, not formatting, and the report's own remedy would not have been enough.** The issue proposed *leading* with `[push] BRANCH -> origin SHA`. Leading does not survive `tail`, which takes the end; and `Status: pushed ✓` was already the fourth line, so the verdict was never missing — it was outrun. Every return path from `main()` now ends on a single `[result]` line, and it is the **last** line: after the MR line, after the advisories, after the `--- git output ---` dump. `[result] PUSHED  feat -> origin/feat @ f1627b7  (verified, 2 commit(s))`.

  **The failure cases are the ones that had to survive truncation, not the success case.** This repo's signature defect is an absence produced by the tool being read as an absence in the world, so a push that did not happen must not render like one that did: `NOT PUSHED - already up to date`, `- REJECTED`, `- REBASE PAUSED (conflict in N file(s))`, `- UNVERIFIED` (timed out, remote does not match HEAD), and `- no push attempted` for the two guards that return before `git push` is ever invoked (not a repo, detached HEAD). A 40-line pre-push hook dump and a `fatal: Could not resolve host` now both end on a line naming the verdict; each state has a test asserting on the last three lines only.

  **The sha is now read back off the remote, which removes the round-trip the report is actually about.** `_remote_sha()` reads the local remote-tracking ref — true, but derived from the push's own report, which is what made the author fetch anyway. The `[result]` line asks `ls-remote` and marks the sha `verified` when it matches HEAD. When the remote does not answer, it says `unverified - remote did not answer ls-remote` and falls back to the tracking sha, labelled: **a sha we did not read is never printed as if we had**, per `docs/validators.md` "Declining instead of guessing", and per the older scar of an unconditional `echo "pushed"` that printed success while the branch never left.

  **Untracked files were relocated, not suppressed.** They are worth mentioning on a push — you may have forgotten to stage something — so the count stays (`⚠ 23 change(s) NOT in this push (uncommitted)`) and the listing moves behind a named escape hatch (`./supertool 'git-status:full'`). The count is the signal; the eight-line listing was the thing occupying the tail. No error is suppressed, no stderr swallowed, and no state defaulted to make the output tidy.
- **A mutating call now ends with `[result] N ops run, M writes`, so `| tail` can no longer read a call that changed nothing as one that did** ([#621](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/621)). Reproduced exactly as filed: `replace:::NOPE_NOT_THERE:::x:::f` and `replace:::alpha:::gamma:::f` — one wrote a file, one did not — produce **byte-identical `tail -4`**, because the receipt that distinguishes them sits above the `[validators]` block and the last line on screen is `git-status : ok`. That line describes the validators; it reads as though it described the edit. Twice now it has been believed: an 11-file `replace` reported to a teammate as having matched nothing, and an agent on [#634](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/634) reporting a no-matched `batch` edit as landed, which cost a 14-leg CI run. Another instance of this repo's most-filed defect class, with the polarity that hurts most: the tool was not silent, it was *legible as the opposite of the truth*.

  **A footer, not a move.** The issue offered both. Moving the receipt below `[validators]` would change the ordering every existing test asserts and break anything reading output positionally, and in a batch it would tear each op's receipt away from the op it describes — for a benefit a repeated line already delivers. So the receipt stays exactly where it was (a test pins that), and one honest line is repeated below the noise. The duplication on a short call is the price, and it is one line.

  **The invariant pinned is stronger than "print the summary last":** *an op which changed nothing must not end with output that looks like an op which did.* The tests assert on the rendered tail — what a reader piping to `tail -4` actually sees — not on any function's return value, and the headline test compares the tails of a no-match call and a real one and requires them to differ. It fails on `master`. Validator durations are normalised out of that comparison, because `0.1s` vs `0.2s` would otherwise let it pass while the bug was fully present.

  **`N` and `M` are counters, never a re-read of the prose.** `N` is `_MUTATION_ATTEMPTS` (bumped when a mutating op runs, before its outcome is known); `M` is `_WRITE_COUNT` (bumped at `_atomic_write`, decremented by `_retract_write`), so **a validator rollback reports `0 writes`** — the fix must not trade a loud failure for a quiet one, and a reverted edit claiming a write would be exactly that. In a batch the pair is "requested" vs "applied": `3 ops run, 2 writes` exposes one no-match among two successes, which is the single line that would have caught the [#634](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/634) incident on its own. For the issue's original report it would have read `1 op run, 11 writes`.

  **Where it can't tell, it declines.** `N == 0` means no mutating op was accounted for — the call never reached the chokepoint. Printing `0 ops run, 0 writes` there would be an invented verdict, so no line is printed at all, per `docs/validators.md`'s three-state contract. Nothing is clamped or defaulted to tidy the output.

  **Scope.** One footer per call, never one per sub-op — a nested batch emits exactly one, tested. `[branch: X]` remains the final line, since [#381](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/381)'s tests assert `endswith` and the branch is the cheaper thing to keep last; `[result]` sits directly above it, and is emitted **even outside a repo** where there is no branch line — that is precisely the case where output would otherwise end on `[validators]`. Preview ops (`replace_dry`) and read-only ops get no footer: a read op's own count line is already the last thing printed, so nothing intervenes between the summary and end-of-output, and a footer on the highest-frequency path would be noise bought with no defect to fix.

- **Every `.json` edit reported an error that was not there: the validator rendered its own unreadable adapter reply as a finding about the user's file** ([#634](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/634)). `validate:presets/gitlab.json` printed `jsonlint : 1 err (0ms)` with `orchestrator  adapter bad json: Expecting value: line 1 column 1 (char 0)` against a file `json.load()` reads happily. That text is what `json.loads("")` raises, so it was never about the file — and the `0ms` was the second tell that no checker had run. This is this repo's most-filed defect class arriving inside a validator, but **inverted**: [#263](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/263) turned a silence into a false clean, this turned one into a false *finding*. A missed error costs one bug; an invented error costs the credibility of every error the validator prints, and this one fired on every `.json` edit — which is exactly how the first genuinely malformed file gets read as the usual noise and waved through.

  **The root cause was configuration, not the adapter.** This repo's own `.supertool.json` had `"cmd": "{python} -m json.tool {file}"` while the bundled, SCHEMA.md-conformant `validators/jsonlint/jsonlint.py` sat unused beside it — `docs/validators.md` had shown the correct entry all along. `json.tool` is a pretty-printer: it exits 0 and echoes the reformatted file, so the last stdout line is `}`, and the orchestrator parses the last line as a receipt. `json.loads("}")` raises precisely the reported error. The checker was never missing and never failing; it was never a checker.

  **The orchestrator no longer converts its own confusion into a verdict.** Four exits in `_validator_run_one` now take the third state (`docs/validators.md`, "Declining instead of guessing") instead of a synthetic `1 err`: empty stdout, stdout that is not JSON, a reply that parses but carries neither `ok` nor `skipped` — found while running the failing tests, and the same absence with the *other* polarity, since a bare `{}` renders as a pass invented out of a reply with no verdict — and a `cmd` that could not be spawned at all. That last one is judgment call 1 from the report: if the checker genuinely is not installed, the honest output is `skipped` naming the binary, not `1 err` claiming the file is broken. Skips omit `ok`/`count`/`errors` ([#515](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/515)), never enter the before/after delta, and never roll back an edit.

  **The missing-binary message names the program on every platform.** The first cut read the name out of the `OSError`, which works on POSIX and names nothing on Windows (`[WinError 2] The system cannot find the file specified`) — caught by this PR's own Windows legs, and the same hole as [#627](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/627). The name now comes from the spec's `cmd` and only `e.strerror` is quoted, so POSIX no longer repeats it either. A test injects the Windows-shaped error on every platform, since the contract should not be observable only on the runner that happened to break.

  **A timeout is deliberately left loud.** The binary exists and was invoked; a tool that hangs is a validator failure, and guessing towards silence there is how a broken validator starts looking clean.

  **Not suppression, and the message now says whose JSON was bad.** Muting the row would have traded a loud false positive for a quiet absence of coverage — the failure nobody notices. The row still prints on every run: `jsonlint : skipped — jsonlint adapter replied with something that is not JSON — … — this file was not checked`. It names the adapter, attributes the failure to the adapter's own reply rather than to the file, and states that the file was not checked. `adapter bad json` read as "your JSON is bad" to everyone who had not read `supertool.py:10840`.

  **Tests pin the trade, not just the fix.** A valid file reports a clean pass; the literal `presets/gitlab.json` repro passes; an empty reply, an unparseable reply and a missing binary are each a skip that cannot regress; the reason names the adapter and never the file under test — and, most importantly, **a genuinely malformed `.json` still reports an error with a line number**, so the fix cannot have disarmed the validator. Two existing tests in `tests/test_validators.py` asserted the old "no output / bad json → `ok: False`" contract; they were updated rather than deleted, since that contract is the defect. The other two validators configured here (`lsp-diag`, `git-status`) were checked and are wired to real adapters — a new test asserts every configured `cmd` points at one. The two `_example-*` entries in `.supertool.example.json` (`phpcs …`, `pytest -q …`) are the same wrong shape, but exist to illustrate `opt_in` and `resolve`; they are now documented as not usable as adapter commands.

- **`gl-job:...:grep:` / `gh-job:...:grep:` emitted an unbounded dump that the consumer then cut in silence, so a partial match list was indistinguishable from a complete one** ([#622](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/622)). `gl-job:6990710:grep:Owner component` was read as 13 affected components; the three sibling jobs returned 1, 1, 1; the blast radius was judged small and the triage moved on. It was not small — going around supertool entirely (`glab api .../trace | grep`) returned the full set. This is the eleventh-ish filing of this repo's most-filed defect class, an absence produced by the tool read as an absence in the world, and the disclosure the report asked for (`47 more matches not shown (raise :LIMIT, current 20)`) would have been a *wrong* one: **the op has no `:LIMIT` and was not truncating at all.** It printed every match. A reproduction on a 22,333-line trace with the incident's shape — each match a whole assertion failure with rendered HTML — emitted **277 KB** and reported its 61 matches honestly. What cut was the consumer, at its own cap, with no marker anywhere. Unbounded-then-cut-downstream is the same silence as a limit that never announces itself, one layer further out, and the layer that can still see the true total is this one.

  **So the bound moves into the op, where the total is already known.** Emission is budgeted by `GL_JOB_GREP_MAX_BYTES` / `GH_JOB_GREP_MAX_BYTES` (default 65536), and the three states stay distinguishable: a view that fits prints nothing extra, and that silence is a positive claim that the list is whole; a view that hit the budget states `... (14 of 61 matching lines shown — ...)` with both numbers real, because `match_count` is computed over the whole trace before the first byte goes out — this is not the streaming case where only "there was more" is knowable, and it was not weakened to one; and the note is a single bounded line ([#605](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/605)), never one line per dropped match, which would re-spend the budget the bound just saved.

  **The note names size, never a count.** `raise :LIMIT, current 20` on an op with no match limit is a confidently wrong disclosure, and wrong is worse than silent: the reason the cut fires far earlier than any line count would suggest is precisely that the lines are enormous, so a count-shaped explanation sends the reader to a knob that does not exist. The wording is `output capped at 65536 bytes by size, not by a match count limit; raise GL_JOB_GREP_MAX_BYTES=N or narrow the pattern`, and a test asserts the byte figure and the env var appear in it while `:LIMIT` does not.

  **The warning is in the header as well as the footer,** because a trailing note is lost to the very truncation it exists to report — a 64 KB view handed to a consumer that cuts at a few tens of KB loses its footer with the tail, which would have re-created the silence one layer out. Emission is planned before printing so the header can carry `[CAPPED: 14 shown, output limited to 65536 bytes by size — raise GL_JOB_GREP_MAX_BYTES=N]`. The header's own match count stays the **true** total, not the shown one: bounding the view must not corrupt the number the view is being measured against. The first match is always emitted whole however fat it is — a bound that can return zero matches for a pattern that matched would invent an absence, which is the disease itself.

  **Scoped to the two `:grep:` paths.** A sweep of every cut site in `presets/` found the rest already disclosing — `gl-mr`'s `… +N more`, `gl-issue`'s `N earlier truncated — use :full`, `git-diff`'s `… N more`, `git-status`/`git-commit`'s `… (N more)`, `gl-job:raw`'s `[CAPPED at N]`, the PHPUnit block budget's `N of M ... not shown in full`, `gl-runners`' throughput flag. Three silent ones are left for separate filings rather than smuggled in here: `gl-issue`'s related-MR list (`mrs[:10]`, total stated, cut unmarked), `git-trail`'s per-commit detail (`commit_hashes[:10]`, no marker), and `gl-mrs`/`gh-prs`' enrichment cap. `presets/_checks.py`, `presets/gitlab/mr.py` and `presets/github/pr.py` were left untouched — an open PR is on them.

- **`gh-issue-create` / `gl-issue-create` leaked a raw traceback on a bad `body_file` / `description_file`, the guard #620/#627 gave the payload read never having reached the second read ten lines later** ([#630](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/630)). `{"title": "x", "body_file": "typo.md"}` handed the user a `FileNotFoundError` traceback; the GitHub script's `Path(body_file).read_text()` sat inside a `try:` whose only clause was a `finally:` for temp-file cleanup, so nothing ever caught it, and the GitLab script's `Path(description_file).read_text()` was not wrapped in anything at all. Both now reuse #627's `is_dir()`-before-read shape — the directory verdict comes from `Path.is_dir()`, not from catching whichever `OSError` subtype a platform happens to raise on read (`IsADirectoryError` on POSIX, `PermissionError` on Windows) — and report `ERROR: body_file not found: PATH` / `ERROR: body_file is a directory, not a file: PATH` / `ERROR: permission denied reading body_file: PATH — ...` (and the `description_file` equivalents), naming the field so a `body_file` error is never mistaken for a payload-file error. The guard is duplicated per script rather than extracted into a shared helper — the two scripts share no code today (that drift is [#628](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/628)), and #627 already established the precedent of duplicating this exact shape across both files rather than introducing the first cross-script dependency as a side effect of a bugfix.

- **`gh-issue-create` / `gl-issue-create` with no `@FILE` argument crashed with a five-frame `IsADirectoryError` traceback instead of saying what was missing** ([#620](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/620)). Supertool's `{arg}` substitution never omits the placeholder — an omitted `@FILE` arrives at the script as an empty string, not a missing `sys.argv` slot, so the existing `len(sys.argv) < 2` guard never fired. The empty path then reached `Path("").read_text()`, which resolves to `Path(".")`, and the resulting `IsADirectoryError` propagated as a raw traceback — the same failure `help` handles correctly for its own missing argument. Both scripts now validate the effective path (after stripping a leading `@`) before reading it and report `ERROR: gh-issue-create needs a payload — gh-issue-create:@FILE (...)` (and the `gl-issue-create` equivalent); a path that resolves to an actual directory now reports that distinctly (`payload path is a directory, not a file: PATH`) instead of leaking the same exception; and an unparseable payload's error now names the expected shape (`JSON or TOML with title/body` / `title/description`) rather than just echoing the parser's message.

  **The directory check was itself POSIX-only and stayed broken on Windows through the first fix.** `except IsADirectoryError` never fires there — opening a directory for read on Windows raises `PermissionError` instead, so the original traceback survived on every Windows CI leg while every other platform went green. Catching `PermissionError` alongside it would have been a *worse* defect: a genuinely locked or wrong-ownership file also raises `PermissionError`, and reporting that as "is a directory" is a confidently false statement, not just an unhelpful one. Both scripts now decide with an explicit `Path(path).is_dir()` check before attempting the read at all, so the verdict no longer depends on which errno a given OS happens to pick; a real `PermissionError` on an actual file now gets its own distinct message (`permission denied reading payload: PATH — ...`).

- **`runner_silent` fired forever on a runner that had been replaced, because "jobs match its tags" is not the same fact as "jobs are stuck behind it"** ([#613](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/613)). Runner 29 `dptools-runner-7`, paused and six days silent, sat beside runner 32 `dptools-runner-7 V2`, online and carrying the same tags. Both halves of the gate were literally true and the conclusion drawn from them was false: the pending job ran on 32. Nothing about that state clears on its own, so the event re-fired on every poll where any job carrying those tags was queued — a permanent false alarm, which is the failure mode this source's own docs name as the reason a real starvation goes unread.

  **The abstraction already existed and the call site had not adopted it.** `gl-runners:queue` answers the same question correctly (`[dptools-runner-7] 1 job(s) -> 1 live runner(s)`) by filtering candidates through `_is_responsive`; the watcher correlated a silent runner with jobs matching *its* tags and stopped there. That filter is now `runners_op.stranded_for`, shared by op and watcher for the reason `starved_tags` already is — a table reading STARVED while the watcher stays quiet is two right answers to one question.

  **Coverage is judged per job, never per tag,** because GitLab routes on *one runner carries all of a job's tags*. Checking the silent runner's own tag set is wrong in both directions at once: a tag no queued job asks for keeps the alarm ringing on work that was never affected, and two live runners splitting `docker` and `gpu` between them read as coverage for a job needing both that can start on neither — which would silence a genuine starvation, the one failure the source exists to prevent. Both directions are pinned by a test.

  **No `runner_superseded` event, though the issue proposed one.** The watcher emits on transitions and is quiet on its first tick by design, so such an event would fire at the moment of replacement — the moment nobody is watching for — and say nothing during the weeks the stale record actually sits there. It would have missed the reported case outright, runner 29 having been dead six days before anyone looked. A deletable runner record is housekeeping, not an incident, and it is already visible as housekeeping: the fleet table lists it with its heartbeat age, `runner_paused` fires on the pause. The watcher keeps the fact in state instead — a `superseded` list beside `silent` — so "why is this runner not firing?" is answerable on demand without being pushed at anybody.

  **Silence is not traded for silence.** Suppression is never inferred from an absence: it needs a named runner that passes `_is_responsive` and may take the job, so a successor that is itself dead still reports the wedge, and an unannotated fleet raises `UnannotatedFleetError` rather than reading as covered — the [#533](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/533) refusal applied to the new question rather than routed around. A failed pending read still suppresses `runner_silent` and `runner_recovered` alike through `queue_known`. Tests drive `poll()` with the GitLab API stubbed rather than the helper mocked, so a fix that lands in `stranded_for` without being adopted at the call site still fails; the payload now carries `stranded_for_it` beside `pending_for_it`, and the notification headlines the number that is actually stuck.
- **The dispatcher reported a project-scoped op as `unknown operation`, which says the op does not exist when the truth is that it exists and is unavailable from this cwd** ([#614](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/614)). One binary, one invocation, two answers about what it can do: `gl-mr` from the project resolved, `gl-mr` from a sibling clone returned `ERROR: unknown operation: gl-mr` under a `Valid operations:` list that silently omitted every preset op. This repo's most-filed defect class — an absence the tool produced, read as an absence in the world — arriving in the tool's own front door. It cost its filer twice in one evening: `unknown operation: gh-job` was read as "the installed build predates that op", and several turns went into hand-rolled `gh api` calls. The fallback worked, so nothing looked broken.

  **Three states, not two.** `docs/validators.md`'s "Declining instead of guessing" one layer out: `available`, `unknown`, and `unavailable here` with the reason. The vocabulary already existed for validators; the dispatcher had simply never adopted it.

  **The only absence it can name honestly is a shipped preset op.** `_shipped_preset_ops()` indexes `presets/*.json` from beside `supertool.py`, so a hit is a fact about *this binary* rather than a guess about somebody else's config. A custom op in a project that was never opened stays genuinely unknowable, and is not guessed at. **A typo still reads as a typo** — hedging every miss into "maybe you need a project root" would trade a correct message for a vaguer one, which is the same defect pointed the other way; four tests pin that boundary from both sides.

  **The two failing situations are separated, because they have different fixes.** The loader always knew which `.supertool.json` it used and threw the path away; it is now kept as `_CONFIG_PATH`. No config found anywhere above cwd → the message names the cwd and points at `cwd:<project-path>`. A config that exists but does not list the preset → the message names that file and the `"presets"` key to add it to. "Run it from somewhere else" and "edit this line" are not the same sentence.

  **`ops` had the same hole and gets the same treatment.** From a non-project directory it listed the built-ins and stopped, which reads as the tool's whole capability. It now carries one line naming the presets that are *not* loaded and their op count — presets, never their ops: enumerating them would roughly double the listing and be eaten from the tail by the SessionStart cap. Placement follows which absence it describes: leading outside a project, where the listing actively misleads and the cap truncates from the bottom; trailing inside one, where the gap is that project's own choice and a permanent banner on the most-read output would just be noise.

  **Two of this fix's own guards were passing on an error message.** The tests proving an available op *still runs* drove it with `{python} -c print('MR-OK')`. Custom ops run argv-form through `shlex.split(posix=True)`, which strips unescaped quotes, so Python received `print(MR-OK)` and raised `NameError`. On Python >= 3.13, whose tracebacks echo the offending `-c` source line back, the string `MR-OK` appears *inside that traceback* — so the assertion matched its own malformed input reflected out of a failure. Green on 3.14 locally, red on all five CI legs (3.9-3.12) whose tracebacks do not echo the source. The sibling guard using `LOCAL` was worse: `NameError: name 'LOCAL' is not defined` contains its token, so it passed on every platform, by luck, while proving nothing. Both now use the quoting idiom the rest of the suite already used, and both assert a **sentinel file** the probe touches — a filesystem side effect no error message can fake — plus the absence of `Traceback`/`FAIL` in the receipt. The same defect this entry is about, in the tests written to prove it fixed: an absence (the op never ran) read as a presence (its name was on screen).

  **The `Valid operations:` list was itself under-reporting.** Hand-maintained, it had rotted to 18 names while the dispatcher accepted 41 — `edit`, `vim`, `batch`, `between`, `append`, `paste` and `cwd` were all missing, the same defect one layer further in and reachable from any typo. It is now derived from the sets dispatch actually uses, so it cannot drift again, and a test probes every advertised read-only name back through `dispatch` to catch rot in the other direction. Two names surfaced on the way: `vi` sits in `_BUILTIN_OPS` from before the op was renamed `vim` and no branch has handled it since, and `cwd` is valid from the CLI but never reaches `dispatch()` because `main()` strips it — it is listed, with the exemption written down rather than inferred.
- **`notifiers/claude-channel`'s `asAttr` refused to coerce an object, array, `null`, or non-finite number — correctly — and the payload key it belonged to vanished with no trace anywhere** ([#612](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/612)). Not in the attributes, not in the body, not on stderr: the producer sent a field and the session never saw it, with nothing recording that a decision was made. Measured against the six live `presets/watch` pollers before deciding this was worth fixing: five build scalar-only payloads, but `gl-runners`'s `runner_added`/`runner_silent` events send `payload.tags` as a `list[str]` — a real instance, not a guard against a mistake nobody makes.

  `buildMeta` now collects every payload key `asAttr` refuses into a new `unsendable` disclosure, alongside #605's `clamped` (dropped for size) and #609's `collided` (dropped for colliding with a reserved name) — the same event from a poller author's perspective ("I sent a field and it did not arrive"), now uniformly told apart from silence. The disclosure names the key and its *shape* (`object`, `array`, `null`, `undefined`, `NaN`, `Infinity`) — never the contents, since quoting is exactly what `asAttr` exists to refuse — bounded by `WITHHELD_NAMED_MAX` and `ATTR_MAX_CHARS` the same way `clamped`/`collided` are. `unsendable` is reserved (a payload key cannot forge it) and applied after `clampMeta`, uncounted against the clamp, so a clamp can never withhold the disclosure about a value it never touched.

  **Tests.** `tests/test_notifiers_claude_channel_612.py`, written red first: 4 of 5 failed (the fifth, the over-reach guard, passes unchanged before and after by design). Green: 25 passed across `_612`, `_609`, `_605`, `_554`.

- **`_runtime_base()` accepted any `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` that was a directory, so anyone who could write it could replace the runtime dir supertool creates, owns and validates** ([#607](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/607)). [#568](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/568), [#583](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/583) and [#598](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/598) each noticed the unchecked ancestry and each parked it as a metadata leak on the reasoning that the leaf is `0700` so a stranger cannot enter it. Locally correct, globally wrong: a stranger does not need to *enter* a directory to replace it, because POSIX permits `rename()` over any entry in a writable, non-sticky directory regardless of that entry's own owner or mode. The leaf's `0700` was never the gate — the parent's write bit and sticky bit were, and neither was looked at.

  **The premise was reproduced before anything was designed**, since it arrived second-hand: with `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` at mode `0777` and `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/supertool` pre-created `0777`, `runtime_dir()` on `master` returns the leaf, exit `0`, no output on either stream. Cross-uid reachable, as filed.

  **The check.** `_verify_ancestry` walks from the validated directory up to the filesystem root, climbing with `os.open("..", dir_fd=…)` so every component it judges is reached through the descriptor chain anchored at the directory already validated rather than by re-resolving parent paths. Each component must be owned by **you or `root`** (`/`, `/run`, `/home`, `/Users` are root-owned on every machine there is) and must not be **group- or world-writable unless sticky** — `ssh`'s `StrictModes` question, with the exception that keeps `/tmp` (`1777`) usable, since `S_ISVTX` means only an entry's owner may rename it. The two rules are independent rather than alternatives: a `1777` directory owned by *someone else* is still theirs to empty, and a mutant that made sticky excuse foreign ownership is one of the nine killed below. Mount points are crossed, not treated as a boundary: every process that later re-resolves the returned path traverses those components too.

  **It refuses; it does not relocate.** Falling back to a private directory supertool creates for itself is the defensible-sounding option that would move every warm daemon out from under the clients still connecting to the old socket path — a quiet failure swapped in for a loud one, which is the trade this module exists to refuse. The refusal names the offending component, its mode or its owner, and the `chmod` that fixes it, on the same terms as the ownership refusal (#544) and the mode refusal (#568) beside it.

  **"Could not check" is a different sentence from "check failed."** Three declines, in their own words, none of them reported as a clean result: a platform without `openat` cannot make the walk (`_ANCESTRY_DIR_FD`, probed at import for the same reason as its siblings); a runtime dir with no search bit for its owner has no starting point for it; an ancestor that will not open leaves the question unanswered. No new vocabulary was designed for this — `_require_dir_fd` and `require_relative_ops` already had the shape one call site up, which is [#263](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/263)'s lesson applied rather than re-learned.

  **The common case had to stay silent or the check would be worth nothing**, since the first person to hit a false alarm turns it off. `systemd`'s `/run/user/<uid>` (`0700`, yours), `~/Library/Caches`, `~/.cache` and a `/tmp`-shaped sticky ancestor all pass without a word, and three tests assert *no output on either stream* rather than merely no exception.

  **One behaviour change beyond the attack.** A `0o600` runtime dir was accepted by #568 — owner-only, nothing exposed, and its test said so — and is now refused, because `..` cannot be opened from a directory you have no search permission on. It is reachable only where the tightening `fchmod` is a no-op (exFAT/SMB), the daemon could not have opened anything inside it either, and it gets its own sentence: "your runtime dir is missing `x`" and "something above your runtime dir is world-writable" send an operator to two different directories.

  **Found while reading the same function, not briefed.** `_runtime_base()` applied no absoluteness check to either variable, so a relative `SUPERTOOL_RUNTIME_DIR` or `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` was resolved against the current working directory — a different runtime dir per project, inside the project, with the project's ancestry. The freedesktop spec requires `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` to be absolute for exactly this reason. Both now refuse rather than guessing what the path was meant to be relative to.

  **A second find, in the test layer, that had nothing to do with #607 and everything to do with trusting it.** `_paths.py` probes its platform capabilities at *import* precisely so a test double over `os.chmod`/`os.open`/`os.listdir` is never read as a missing syscall — but that only holds if the import wins the race against the first double, and every suite here imports `_paths` from inside a test body. A worker whose first import landed inside `test_mcp_runtime_dir_mode_568.py`'s `no_chmod` fixture recorded the double and permanently believed `os.chmod(dir_fd=)` was unavailable, failing three unrelated #598 tests with a platform verdict invented by another test. Latent on `master`; adding tests changed the xdist split and set it off. `tests/conftest.py` now imports `_paths` at collection time, which is the condition the probes' own comments assume.

  **Tests.** `tests/test_mcp_runtime_dir_ancestry_607.py`, 14 tests, written and run red first: **10 failed, 3 passed** — the three that passed are the silent-case tests, which pass against the unfixed code by construction and exist to stay green. Every loud test builds a real world-writable or foreign-owned ancestor on disk and asserts a refusal naming *that component*; "would this pass if the check did nothing?" is answerable no for each. **Green: 14 passed.** Full suite **5073 passed, 47 skipped**, coverage 88.89% (gate 86%).

  **Nine mutants, all killed — but only after one survivor exposed a weak assertion.** `sticky excuses a stranger-owned ancestor` survived the first run: the tests asserted `str(component) in message`, and since every refusal also names the runtime dir it protects, and every ancestor is a *prefix* of that path, the substring check passed while the walk blamed the wrong directory. The assertion now pins which component the sentence opens with. The others: the check never called, `0o022` narrowed to `0o002`, the sticky exception dropped, "owner or root" narrowed to "owner", the walk stopped at the immediate parent, the `dir_fd` decline turned into a silent pass, an unwalkable ancestor treated as clean, and a relative runtime dir accepted.

  **The #598 test that documented this as a finding was inverted, not deleted.** `TestTheAncestryOfTheRuntimeDirIsUnchecked` asserted the acceptance and the `rename` mechanic; the class is now `…IsCheckedSince607`, the first test asserts the refusal, and the mechanic demonstration stays where it was found so the provenance survives.

- **The daemon opened its socket, pidfile and logs by path, so everything #583 validated could be swapped out from under it before a single byte was written** ([#598](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/598), completing [#583](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/583)). `presets/mcp/daemon.py` is a separate process that received `sock_path` / `pid_path` as strings and re-resolved every one of them — `server.bind`, `os.chmod`, the `O_EXCL` pidfile claim, `f"{sock_path}.stderr"`, `f"{sock_path}.log"`, and the `unlink`s in `_spawn.cleanup` and `stop.py`. `O_NOFOLLOW` on those opens guards the *leaf file*; it says nothing about the directory component, so replacing the directory between validation and use redirected all of them at once with every ownership and mode check still passing.

  `daemon.py` now calls `_paths.open_runtime_dir()` itself and holds that descriptor for the daemon's whole life. The pidfile, logs, fingerprint and unlinks are `dir_fd`-relative; `stop.py` removes pidfiles through the same descriptor that listed them. `socket.bind()` takes no `dir_fd` and `socket` offers no descriptor-relative form, so the socket is bound by **basename** inside a two-call `os.fchdir` window — placed before `subprocess.Popen` and before the log-drain thread exists, so the MCP server child inherits your cwd and no other thread can observe the change. A forked child holding the bind was the alternative and buys nothing when the window is already single-threaded and childless.

  **The premise was checked before it was fixed, and it was not the one the issues state.** Both #583 and #598 describe the residual window as needing "write access to a directory supertool owns" — which, for the default locations, means the attacker is already you and the whole thing is not worth building. That is true of `~/Library/Caches` and `~/.cache`, whose parents are inside `$HOME`. It is not true of `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR`, which `_runtime_base()` accepts on the sole evidence that it `is_dir()`. Someone who pre-creates `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/supertool` mode `0777` owns the *parent* of the leaf supertool then creates, owns and validates — and POSIX grants `rename()` over any entry in a writable, non-sticky directory regardless of that entry's owner or mode. So `0700` on the leaf is not what gates this, the swap is reachable by another uid, and the fix earns its keep. Both facts are asserted in `TestTheAncestryOfTheRuntimeDirIsUnchecked` and documented in `docs/mcp-integration.md`; tightening the ancestry is a separate change.

  **A relative bind also repairs a regression #583 introduced.** `sockaddr_un.sun_path` caps at 104 bytes on macOS (measured: 103 usable) and 108 on Linux. #583 resolves the configured path before use, and resolving can only lengthen it, so a short symlink deliberately pointed at a deep runtime dir stopped binding — the daemon failed with a bare `OSError: AF_UNIX path too long`. This was not hypothetical: the new tests could not bind under pytest's own `tmp_path` until the bind went relative. Binding the basename spends none of the budget on the directory.

  **Found while reading the call sites, not briefed — a fatal `OSError` hiding inside the quiet bug.** `_safe_open` is the #148 defence against a `.stderr` pre-created as a symlink. It runs *after* `subprocess.Popen` and its `OSError` was caught nowhere: it unwound past a `try` it had never entered, so `proc.terminate()` in that `finally` never ran. The symlink guard, on the one occasion it fired, left the heavy MCP server child running with no parent — precisely the stray daemon [#451](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/451) and `_spawn.py`'s docstring exist to prevent. The `Popen` is now inside the reaping `try`. A loud failure that leaks a process is worse than the silent fallback it is usually contrasted with, because nothing survives to notice it.

  **Two smaller things at the same call sites.** `daemon.main` read the pidfile with a bare `open()` + `int()` — following symlinks and accepting `0`, `+0` and `-1`, the values `_paths.read_pid` exists to reject ([#569](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/569)) — in a file whose docstring promises one reader and no drift between surfaces. There were three; `claim_pidfile`'s use of `_spawn.read_pid` was the other, and it collapsed "unreadable" and "mid-write empty" into `0`, i.e. *no owner*, and unlinked a live daemon's pidfile. Both now go through `_paths.read_pid`. And `_spawn.write_fingerprint` left its `<name>.<pid>.tmp` behind forever if the `os.replace` failed, one per crashed boot, in a directory nothing ever sweeps.

  **The three-state contract holds.** `dir_fd=` and `os.fchdir` are POSIX-only, so `_paths.require_relative_ops` declines with a sentence rather than falling back to path-based opens and reporting success — the failure this repo keeps filing. It is written independently of `_require_dir_fd` even though that one refuses first on every platform CPython ships, on the same rule as #583: a guard correct only because another guard runs earlier is a comment pretending to be code. The capability set is probed at **import**, because `os.supports_dir_fd` holds the original function objects and asking after a test has wrapped `os.open` would turn a test double into a platform verdict.

  **Tests.** `tests/test_mcp_daemon_dirfd_598.py`, 11 tests, written and run red first: **5 failed, 2 passed**. They are swap tests, not symlink tests — the fixture patches `_paths._verify_runtime_dir` (a seam that exists in the unfixed code, and the last thing that runs before validation returns) to perform a real `rename` swap the instant every check has passed, then asserts the decoy is never touched. Red evidence: `no pidfile in the validated directory — it went to ['supertool-mcp-3bcba188b101.pid'] instead, a directory nothing inspected`, and `OSError: AF_UNIX path too long` ×3. **Green: 11 passed.** Six mutants, all killed: absolute bind, path-based pidfile claim, path-based log opens, the log opens hoisted back outside the reaping `try`, the decline turned into a silent pass, and `stop.py`'s unlink back to path-based — the last of which survived the first run and is why the `stop.py` test exists.

  **Four existing fixtures were keyed on a seam this moves, and were re-keyed rather than relaxed.** `test_mcp_stop_outcome_547.py` and `test_mcp_stop_crash_574.py` both patch "the first thing `main` reaches on the named-server path" to inject a refusal or a crash; that was `socket_pid_paths`, and it is now `open_runtime_dir` (`socket_pid_names` precedes it but is a pure hash that touches no filesystem and cannot fail). What they pin — a stated refusal keeps its own exit code, a bare numeric exit propagates untouched, a traceback exits `1` and is never read as `ok` — is unchanged. Full suite: **5054 passed, 47 skipped**.
- **Nothing capped the size of a watch event, so one well-formed event could spend the whole context window** ([#605](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/605)). Measured through a real socket at a real `claude-channel` server on `4da713f`: an event carrying two 400 KB strings arrived as `title` 400,000 chars, `url` 400,000 and an 800,034-char body — **1,600,261 bytes in one notification**, roughly twice an entire 200K-token session. The server stayed healthy and every value was a well-formed string, so nothing in the design considered it a fault. This is [#554](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/554)'s class by a different mechanism: the transport now survives anything, and this is the event that costs the user the thing the transport exists to protect. Now 501 bytes.

  **The cap is per event, because per attribute is not sufficient.** 2,000 well-formed 200-char attributes — every one of them far under any per-value limit — measured 425,123 bytes delivered; that shape is only reachable by a limit on the event as a whole. Both are enforced (`SUPERTOOL_CHANNEL_ATTR_MAX` 2,048, `SUPERTOOL_CHANNEL_EVENT_MAX` 8,192, both overridable), and when an event is over budget its attributes are withheld largest-first, so the fewest attributes go and the reader keeps the most distinct facts. The numbers are set from measurement, not taste: across ten live watchers the largest complete payload was **488 characters** and its longest single value **117**, so each cap is ~17x anything real and the per-event budget is ~1% of a context window.

  **Oversized attributes are withheld whole, never truncated — and the event still arrives.** Silently shortening is the trap, not the fix: the first 2,048 characters of a title read downstream exactly like the title, which converts "your event was too big" into "the tool quietly showed you something else". No prefix is delivered, so nothing can be misread as complete — the rule `asAttr` already applies to structure (`String({})` has no honest string form), one axis over. Dropping the event was the other option and it loses the routing signal, *that MR 33173 went red*, over bytes that were never the point — the silent-gap failure #554 was filed about. So the event is delivered with a `clamped` attribute **and** a line in the body naming each withheld attribute and its real size, bounded with a `+N more` marker the way `gitlab-mr`'s `observed_failed_jobs` already bounds itself. Both places, because the attribute is machine-readable and the body is what Claude reads as prose; a clamped event is distinguishable from a complete one from the event itself. An event that fits says nothing at all. The one case with no good half — a *routing* key over the cap — goes through the existing loud `drop()` path, since withholding `event` would deliver a notification that says nothing about what happened.

  **`buildContent` was reading the payload a second time**, so the body reproduced the same 400 KB the attributes had just withheld. It now reads the clamped `meta`, which is what the file already claimed ("the body and the attributes cannot disagree about what this event is") — extended to what it still contains.

  **A second, louder defect on the same six lines: the NDJSON line buffer was unbounded.** The read loop accumulated bytes until a newline arrived, with no limit and no log. 50 MB with no newline took a real server from 74 MB to **770 MB RSS**, super-linearly (1.0s → 13.7s for equal increments, because every chunk rescans the whole buffer), delivering nothing and staying green from every angle a session can check — #554's invisible failure with a memory leak attached, and a poller killed mid-write reaches it by accident. Past `SUPERTOOL_CHANNEL_LINE_MAX` (1 MB) the line is refused on stderr and the connection **resyncs** to the next newline, so the following real event still arrives: wedging the connection shut would trade a memory leak for a delivery gap. The same 50 MB now costs 122 MB RSS and one loud line. The parse budget is deliberately far above the delivery budget — an oversized-but-parseable event has to reach `buildMeta` to be clamped and disclosed, and refusing to parse at 8 KB would turn every disclosure into a silent loss.

  **A cap override that is not a positive integer exits (code `4`) rather than falling back.** Quietly substituting the default would leave an operator who typed `2O48` believing a limit is in force that isn't — the same disease one layer up.

  **Tests fire real oversized events at a real server and assert on what the consumer receives**, not on what a helper returns (`tests/test_notifiers_claude_channel_605.py`, reusing #554's live-server harness). Seven tests, six red before the fix; the seventh — a normal 500-char event must be delivered whole and report *no* clamp — passes in both states on purpose, so a patch that clamped everything would fail here instead of reading as progress. Size and disclosure are asserted separately, because a cap that shrinks an event without saying so would satisfy the first and be a worse bug than the one it fixed.

  **Not fixed, stated instead: a burst is not capped.** Forty 40 KB events cost the window what one 1.6 MB event does, and no per-event cap addresses that shape. Dropping event #41 for the sins of #1–40 would refuse an event on grounds unrelated to its own content, and a rate limit that silently eats a `pipeline_failed` is a worse radar than a chatty one. The per-event cap does bound the realistic burst — forty oversized events measured 3,209,660 bytes before and 18,020 after — but forty *legal* 8 KB events still cost 320 KB, and that remains open.

- **A watch payload could rename the event it was attached to: `buildMeta` set the routing fields, then merged the poller's `payload` over them, guarding only `source`** ([#609](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/609)). Reproduced before it was designed for, against a real `claude-channel` server on `235b377`: an event announced as `gitlab-mr 33173: pipeline_failed` was delivered as **`not-gitlab 11111: pipeline_succeeded`** — `watcher_source`, `id`, `event`, `ts` and `first_tick` all replaced by payload keys of the same name, the body and the attributes in perfect agreement, nothing on stderr. A session sees a green pipeline for an MR it was never told about and acts on it. This is not a checker reporting a false absence, it is the transport reporting a false **identity**, and it is the [#608](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/608) agent's incidental observation, filed and then verified rather than taken on trust.

  **Guarded, not namespaced — and the guard is derived from `ROUTING_KEYS` rather than re-listed.** Nesting the payload under one key makes collisions structurally impossible, and the argument for it is that a hand-maintained guard must be remembered the next time a routing field is added — #608 had just added `watcher_source` and `first_tick`, so "next time" was not hypothetical. Spreading the same constant `clampMeta` already iterates removes that objection: the edit that adds a routing field guards it in the same breath, and under this shape #608's two fields would have been protected by the commit that introduced them. What namespacing would have cost is the product itself — the flat `title="…" url="…"` attributes are what a session reads, `buildContent` reads `meta.title`, and the instructions string, both READMEs and every downstream consumer name them; a wire-format change to every event, paid forever, to close a collision on nine names.

  **A losing key is disclosed, not swallowed**, in #608's existing vocabulary rather than a second one: a `collided` attribute **and** a body line naming each key, its real size and the reserved set, bounded with the same `+N more` marker and the same `ATTR_MAX` fallback as `clamped`. The reserved set is named because the producer's fix is to rename the field, which is not guessable from the key alone. An event with no collision says nothing at all.

  **The disclosure surface was itself forgeable — found in the same repro, not briefed.** `clamped` was an ordinary payload key, so an event that lost nothing could arrive announcing that two attributes had been withheld: the one attribute #605 built to be believed, writable by the party it reports on. `clamped` and `collided` are both reserved now.

  **And the collision could destroy the event outright, loudly, for a false reason.** A `payload.id` of 400,000 chars overwrote the routing id; `clampMeta` then found a routing key over the per-attribute cap and refused the whole event through `drop()` with the reason `routing key over 2048 chars — nothing routable to deliver`, while the actual routing id was `66666`. An ordinary `pipeline_failed` never arrived and the stderr line explaining why was untrue. A loud failure carrying a false reason is not the safe side of that trade.

  **`__proto__` was a silent loss one layer down.** `meta["__proto__"] = "x"` on an object literal runs the inherited setter and creates no own property, so a payload key by that name left no trace on the wire and no line anywhere. It is reserved too, which converts an invisible discard into a stated one.

  **`buildContent` no longer takes the raw event at all.** It read `first_tick` from the raw event while the attribute came from `meta`, so the two surfaces could disagree about the same fact — the same second read that put the withheld 400 KB back into the body before #605. `buildMeta` now returns `{ meta, collided }` and the body is built from `meta` alone, so there is nothing left in scope to reach for.

  **Tests fire real colliding events at a real server and assert on what the consumer receives** (`tests/test_notifiers_claude_channel_609.py`, reusing #554's live-server harness). Seven tests, written and run red first: **6 failed, 1 passed** — the pass is the over-reach guard, an ordinary event that must be delivered untouched and report *no* collision, so a patch that reserved too much fails there instead of reading as progress. Guard and disclosure are asserted in separate tests, so a version that protects the routing key without saying anything fails on its own.

- **The three Hashnode write ops documented the opposite of what they do: "a warning is printed and publish proceeds", in files that have aborted since they were written** ([#603](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/603)). `presets/hashnode/{publish,comment,react}.py` each carried that sentence in the module docstring — the first thing a reader consults to find out what the op does with an unverifiable check — while `main` writes `ABORT — pre-flight lookup failed …` and exits 1. Hashnode is the preset [#599](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/599) and [#601](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/601) copied the fail-closed shape *from*, by reading the code; the prose had been wrong for the whole life of the files. Corrected to match, in the phrasing the two PRs left in `devto/publish.py` and `bluesky/{like,follow,publish}.py`.

  **`react.py`'s docstring was wrong twice, and contradicted its own second paragraph.** It claimed the op "queries the post's `myTotalReactions` field and aborts if >0" — `preflight_react` is a one-line `return None, 0` that makes no API call at all — and then, four lines below the "warning is printed and the like proceeds" sentence, the AUTO-FORCE section correctly explained that the op "aborts every time without `|force`". Both halves of the same docstring, one true.

  **The sweep found no other stale preset prose, and two more names of the [#602](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/602) shape.** `warning is printed` appears in exactly three files, all fixed; every other `PRE-FLIGHT` docstring in `presets/` (`devto/{publish,comment}.py`, `bluesky/{publish,like,follow}.py`) already says ABORTS, and `docs/presets/{devto,bluesky}.md` already document the third outcome. What the sweep did turn up was in `tests/test_hashnode.py`: `test_comment_preflight_api_error_degrades` asserted `already is None` — the fail-closed value — under a name claiming degradation, the exact pair #602 renamed one function away in the same file; and `test_react_main_aborts_on_dupe` names a branch `hashnode_react` has no way to reach, since `preflight_react` can never return `True`. Renamed to `..._api_error_is_unknown` and `..._aborts_when_unverifiable`.

  **One test was deleted rather than renamed.** `test_react_main_api_error_degrades` neither exercised an API error nor degraded: it passed `|force`, mocked the mutation and asserted `"liked"` — a strict subset of `test_react_main_force_bypasses_preflight` three functions above it, which asserts the same thing plus that no pre-flight query was issued. It is the fourth survivor of the group #601 collapsed for asserting one contract under several names.

  **Docstrings cannot be unit-tested, so the contract they describe now is.** `test_publish_main_aborts_when_preflight_cannot_run` and `test_comment_main_aborts_when_preflight_cannot_run` drive `main` with `gql_safe` returning `None` and a `gql` that raises if called — pinning that `publishPost`/`addComment` never runs after an unverifiable check, which is precisely the sentence the old docstrings got backwards. The existing tests stopped at what `preflight_*` *returns*; `react` already had the `main`-level coverage, under the wrong name. Both were shown red by deleting the two `sys.exit(1)` lines — reproducing the documented warn-and-proceed behaviour — and the failure was the mutation firing, not a message assertion. No docs linter: three files and a phrase are not a tooling problem.

  **`docs/presets/hashnode.md` gained the "When the duplicate check cannot run" section** its devto and bluesky siblings got in #599/#601. It was silent rather than wrong, which is why the sweep counts it as the gap it was.

- **Four more pre-flight checks answered "no duplicate" when they meant "could not check", so a platform hiccup authorised the write each one was guarding** ([#601](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/601)). The class [#562](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/562) belongs to, swept rather than assumed: `presets/devto/publish.py::preflight_publish`, `presets/bluesky/{follow,like,publish}.py::preflight_*`. Each returned the *negative answer* on its own failure path, and each had a companion `test_*_api_error_degrades` asserting that value — a live copy of the pair [#599](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/599) pulled apart. All four now return `bool | None` with a fail-closed caller: `False` means *we looked and there is nothing*, `None` means *we did not look*.

  **The argument was re-made per op rather than copied.** #599's case for declining loudly turned on an asymmetry — one keystroke of `|force` against a public comment this preset cannot undo — and it does not transfer for free. `devto_publish` keeps it intact: a duplicate article is public, there is no `devto_unpublish` op in this preset (the issue's claim that one exists does not hold), and `hashnode_publish` has declined on `None` since it was written. `bluesky_publish` keeps it too — a duplicate reply is a public artifact on somebody else's thread.

  **`bluesky_like` and `bluesky_follow` did not inherit that argument, and got there another way.** A duplicate like or follow is close to free, so the #599 asymmetry genuinely does not apply, and "leave them degrading" was the live option. What settled it was reading `_atproto.xrpc`: it calls `sys.exit(1)` on every HTTP and network error, so the *common* failure already reached `except Exception` as a `SystemExit`, escaped it, and killed the op with a bare `ERROR:` and no hint. These ops were already declining on the realistic failure — just badly, and only by accident. Making the unknown explicit preserves that outcome and adds the `|force` bypass to the message; the alternative, warning and proceeding, would have *loosened* a live safety behaviour under cover of a bug fix.

  **The `SystemExit` catch in `bluesky/publish.py` turned out to be doing something other than what the issue said.** It does not swallow a deliberate `sys.exit` from `_get_root_uri` — that helper catches `SystemExit` itself and returns `None`, so none ever escapes to be swallowed. What the catch actually absorbs is `xrpc`'s own exit on the `getAuthorFeed` call: a transport accident, the same class as the other three, not a refusal being overridden. The genuine loss of a deliberate decision was one line up: `_get_root_uri` returning `None` — *the root is unknown* — was read as `False`, *nobody replied to this root*. That is now `None`.

  **Tests pin the distinction rather than the value.** `tests/test_preflight_unknown_601.py` asserts, for every op, that the checked outcome and the unchecked outcome **differ** — the one assertion the old code could not satisfy, since `False` was both. Eight of its 22 tests pass before the fix as well as after: they guard against over-reach, so a patch that turned every outcome into "unknown" would fail instead of reading as progress. The four `test_*_api_error_degrades` companions were renamed to `..._is_unknown` and now assert `None`, and `test_security_bluesky.py::test_preflight_exception_returns_false` became `..._returns_unknown`.

  **The three `test_react_preflight_*` tests in `tests/test_hashnode.py` collapsed into one.** They asserted the same hardcoded `(None, 0)` under three names — `already_reacted`, `not_reacted`, `api_error_degrades` — for a function that is a one-line `return None, 0` and makes no API call. Three literals are not three cases. The replacement names the actual contract (Hashnode exposes no per-user reaction field, so `react` always declines) and monkeypatches `gql` to raise if called, so giving that function a real but broken body fails here instead of continuing to pass.

- **`devto_comment`'s duplicate check answered "no duplicate" whether it had looked or not, so a swallowed `ImportError` posted an unchecked comment** ([#562](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/562)). `presets/devto/comment.py::preflight_comment` wrapped its own sibling imports — `from _auth import get_api_key`, `from _rest import request` — inside the same `except Exception` as the HTTP call, and returned `(False, [], "")` from both. That tuple is *also* the answer for **the check ran and the article is clean**, and it is the value the only caller reads to decide whether to post. So a missing shim was not a traceback naming the module; it was a plausible-looking go-ahead.

  **Demonstrated before anything was touched**, because the issue was filed from an incidental finding and the causal chain needed proving. `tests/_preset_loader.py` pops every shim from `sys.modules` before each preset load (line 77) and — since [#555](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/555)/[#561](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/561) — restores `sys.path` afterwards, so any test module loading a *second* preset leaves devto's `comment` module holding function-level imports that resolve to nothing:

  ```
  load_preset_module("devto", "comment")     # devto's shims cached
  load_preset_module("hashnode", "comment")  # pops _auth/_rest, restores sys.path
  comment.preflight_comment(999, "max-ai-dev")
  -> (False, [], '')          # identical to a clean article
  from _rest import request
  -> ImportError: No module named '_rest'    # what actually happened
  ```

  **#555 is genuinely the commit that made it reachable, not merely adjacent.** `git show 0cd3ac9^:tests/test_devto.py` — the pre-#555 loader inserted `PRESET_DIR` into `sys.path` and never removed it, so the shim eviction re-resolved from disk and the `except Exception` never fired. The restore is correct and stays; the conflation it exposed is the bug.

  **The tests were green while asserting nothing, literally.** `test_comment_preflight_not_commented` and `test_comment_preflight_api_error_degrades` asserted the *same tuple* under two names, so neither could distinguish a checked article from an unreachable module — [#403](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/403)'s shape inside a test fixture. The new `test_lookup_failure_is_not_no_duplicate` asserts the two outcomes **differ**, which is the one assertion the old code could not satisfy.

  **Fixed in production, not only made visible to tests, and the abstraction already existed.** `presets/hashnode/comment.py` has returned `bool | None` with a fail-closed caller since it was written — [#263](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/263)'s lesson, the sibling had the answer and the call site had not adopted it. devto now says the same thing: `None` means *we did not look*, `False` means *we looked and there is nothing*, and `main` declines with `ABORT — pre-flight lookup failed for article N (cannot verify whether already commented). Use |force as 4th field to bypass and post anyway.`

  **Which failure was chosen.** Declining to post is the loud direction, and it is the right one *here specifically*: the decline is recoverable in one keystroke (`|force`, already documented and already the bypass for a real duplicate), while a duplicate comment on somebody else's article is public and not undoable by this preset. The same block's `WARNING: pre-flight check failed … — proceeding anyway.` became an `ABORT` too: announced, but announced in a way that scrolls past while the comment goes out unchecked either way.
