- **`gl-runners` liveness refuses to judge records nobody gathered evidence for, instead of quietly degrading to the fleet-wide-false-alarm version.** `_is_responsive` ranks evidence in descending strength — completed work, then `job_execution_status`, then `contacted_at` — and its top two rungs only exist if `annotate_recent_work` / `annotate_live_jobs` have run. Without them the judgement falls through to `contacted_at` alone, the field GitLab throttles, which is precisely the version that fired `runner_silent` on **6 of 6 online runners** in [#527](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/527)'s first hour. The precondition was documented and held by convention; nothing pinned it. Each annotator now leaves a mark, and an un-annotated record raises `UnannotatedFleetError`. Closes [#533](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/533).

  **The un-annotated caller was not hypothetical — it was already in the tree.** `radar_report` deliberately skips the history scan when the queue is empty (a registered tier should stay cheap), then computed `live = [r for r in runners if _is_responsive(r)]` from the records that skip left un-annotated and printed `fleet ok — N/M runners live`. A ratio that reads as measured and was inferred from the throttled field. It now reports `fleet ok — M runners, 0 pending, none blocked` in that case and judges liveness only on the annotated path, keeping the cost saving the comment argues for.

  **Refusing is the choice between two failures, not the removal of one.** Judging anyway re-ships the false alarm. Defaulting an unknown runner to responsive returns an empty starvation list for a fleet nobody looked at — a **false all-clear**, strictly worse in a monitoring tool, and `test_the_refusal_is_never_downgraded_into_an_all_clear` pins `starved_tags` declining rather than answering `{}`. Raising is not a crash: the watch dispatcher already records a failed poll as `last_error` and emits no events, and radar already renders a raised tier as `WARNING — tier failed` with the board not green. The three-state vocabulary from [#406](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/406) / `docs/validators.md` — `ok`, a finding, **cannot tell** — was reused at the layer above the call site, in the [#263](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/263) shape, rather than a parallel tri-state being invented inside the op.

  **The refusal is unconditional, checked ahead of the paused/offline disqualifiers.** Those would answer correctly without annotation, and a guard that lets some un-annotated calls through is a guard a new caller passes in review, passes in tests, and still ships the alarm with.

  Red first: 6 of 7 new tests failed against the unmodified tree — five on `AttributeError: module has no attribute 'UnannotatedFleetError'`, and the sixth on the live radar output, `assert 'runners live' not in 'radar: fleet ok — 1/1 runners live, 0 pending, none blocked'`. The evidence ordering, the 30-minute heartbeat threshold and the throughput window are untouched; every pre-existing test in the file passes with no assertion weakened, the fixture gaining only the two keys the annotators leave behind.

- **Test suite spawns validator/preset adapters via `sys.executable` instead of the literal `"python3"`.** On Windows, PATH resolution of `"python3"` can hit the App Execution Alias stub, which blocks rather than errors, surfacing as an unrelated `subprocess.TimeoutExpired` flake (observed on PR #527's Windows/3.10 leg). 27 genuine `subprocess.run` spawn sites across 9 test files converted; fixture argv data describing fake process-table rows (`test_watch_pid_set_511.py`, `test_watch_death_supervision_513.py`) deliberately left untouched. Guarded by `tests/test_no_bare_python3_spawn.py`, an AST-scoped check over real `subprocess.*` call sites. Closes [#529](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/529).

## [0.22.0] - 2026-07-29

### Added

- **A syntax floor guard that runs a real old interpreter, because no amount of AST reasoning on a new one can do it.** `_syntax_floor_check(paths)` compiles every given `.py` under an interpreter older than the one running the suite, and `tests/test_syntax_floor_478.py` runs it over the repo. Closes [#478](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/478).

  **The check it replaces was wrong in the most expensive direction.** `ast.parse(src, feature_version=(3, 9))` was prescribed as the verification bar for [#473](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/473) and reports **clean** on a 3.12+ host for the exact source that broke nine of twelve CI legs: `feature_version` gates *grammar productions* — walrus, `match`, `except*` — and never touches the tokenizer change PEP 701 made, so nested same-type quotes inside an f-string replacement field parse fine before and after the fix. `test_feature_version_is_blind_to_pep701` pins that gap directly, so nobody re-derives the false clean and concludes it is fine. Verified here on 3.14: `ast.parse(feature_version=(3, 9))` clean, the guard `f-string: unmatched '['` at 1:12.

  **Where the interpreter comes from, in order.** `$PYTHON39` — the explicit escape hatch, **verified rather than trusted**: pointed at anything no older than the running interpreter it is rejected outright rather than falling through, because a declaration that silently buys nothing restores exactly the false clean it was set to prevent. Then the running interpreter when it is itself at or below the floor — the 3.9 CI leg, where the check runs for real with nothing installed. Then the lowest `pythonX.Y` on `PATH` between the floor and the current version; the binary is *asked* for its version, the filename is never believed, so a `python3.9` shim that is really the host is refused (pinned by test).

  **What it does not cover, said out loud rather than left to be discovered.** With nothing older than the host it returns a `skipped` result naming the escape hatch and does **not** report a pass — the absence-as-evidence failure of [#454](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/454) / [#469](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/469) / [#477](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/477). With an interpreter above the floor the result carries a `partial` note: it catches PEP 701 and anything newer than that interpreter, not syntax legal on 3.11 and illegal on 3.9. A skip that is universal is indistinguishable from a pass, so `test_ci_matrix_covers_the_syntax_floor` fails if the 3.9 leg ever leaves the matrix or `requires-python` drifts from `SYNTAX_FLOOR` — the one place the guard is *guaranteed* to run.

  Red first: 7 of 10 new tests failed against the unmodified tree (`AttributeError: module 'supertool' has no attribute '_syntax_floor_interpreter'`), and the guard was proven on a real regression rather than a mock — a PEP 701 file that `ast.parse(feature_version=(3, 9))` calls clean is reported `ok: false, count: 1` by the check.

- **`PHPSTAN_MCP_PATHS` — skip an out-of-scope file without the 9.2s daemon round trip.** Set it to the analysis roots (`os.pathsep`- or comma-separated) and `phpstan-mcp` answers `skipped` locally for a target outside every root, never opening the socket to `mcp-phpstan-warm`. Measured in the session that produced [#406](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/406): 9.2s per edited file, spent to be told phpstan would not look at it. In a multi-file batch that is 9.2s per unique path. Closes [#412](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/412).

  **Re-measured before building, against the real daemon**, since the filed figure predated a lot of work: an out-of-scope DVSI test file on an already-warm `mcp-phpstan-warm` cost **4.8s** (`duration_ms: 4804`), returning the refusal verbatim — `analyse: path is outside the configured --paths allowlist.`, still thrown by `PhpstanRunner::assertPathAllowed()`. So 9.2s is a cold/loaded reading and 4.8s a warm one; the same run with `PHPSTAN_MCP_PATHS` set returns `duration_ms: 0`.

  **Opt-in, and the daemon stays authoritative when unset — because the two failure directions are not symmetric.** Skipping a file that IS in scope loses the analysis silently: the file looks handled and is not. Analysing a file that is NOT in scope wastes a round trip and still returns the right answer. So the scope is never inferred, never read out of `phpstan.neon`, and never defaulted on; unset, behaviour is byte-identical to before, and a blank or whitespace-only value is treated as unset rather than as an empty allowlist that would skip the entire repo. The var is the repo stating that it knows the scope.

  **The skip reason names the env var, not phpstan** — `path outside PHPSTAN_MCP_PATHS allowlist`, not `path outside --paths allowlist`. A wrong skip is caused by this configuration; pointing at a tool that never saw the file sends the reader to the wrong file to fix it. The receipt is the ordinary third state (`validators/common/refusal.py`, `docs/validators.md`), so the core's existing no-rollback / no-delta / no-cache handling applies with no second code path.

  **Matching is `os.path.abspath` with an `os.sep` boundary**, not a prefix compare — `/srcbad/Foo.php` is not inside `/src`. Empty entries between separators are dropped rather than `abspath()`ed into the working directory, where one stray separator would silently widen the allowlist to everything; relative roots resolve against the working directory, like every other path in a committed `.supertool.json`.

  **The helper is generic; the second env var is not shipped.** `refusal.outside_roots(file_path, env_var)` takes the variable name as an argument, so `phpmd-mcp` or any future MCP adapter is one call away — but nothing declares `PHPMD_MCP_PATHS` until a second adapter actually asks for it. An opt-in nobody requested is an opt-in nobody can be surprised by.

  Red first: 10 of 15 new tests failed against the unmodified adapter, and the load-bearing one failed by *reporting that the daemon had been contacted*. Both directions are pinned by monkeypatching `ensure_daemon`/`ndjson_call` — to raise, so an out-of-scope path that still dials the daemon fails; and to record, so an in-scope path that silently stops reaching it fails too. A third test pins the unset case against the same recorder.

### Changed

- **README: `gc` section removed, `Watch` cut to framing plus links (docs-only).** `gc` runs itself hourly on a stamp file and nobody needs to learn it to use supertool, so it is now one line under `## Operations` pointing at [docs/operations/meta.md](docs/operations/meta.md#gc--cache-retention) and [docs/configuration.md](docs/configuration.md#gc--cache-retention) — 17 lines to 1, with no fact lost (all of them were already in those two pages). `## Watch` dropped its `### Quickstart` and `### Reference` subsections, which restated `docs/presets/watch.md` — 627 lines and substantially extended since — keeping what a reader needs to decide whether to click: what it is, the two-layers framing, and why `radar` exists. 42 lines to 14. README 506 → 465 lines.

### Fixed

- **The skipped-result contract had two published definitions; a skip now omits `ok`/`count`/`errors` everywhere.** `docs/validators.md` told adapter authors to omit the verdict keys, `validators/common/refusal.py:skipped()` padded them and argued for padding in its own docstring, and `validators/SCHEMA.md` — the normative document — was a third statement siding with the helper. An author following the prose produced results that differed from every `refusal.skipped()` caller, and nothing caught it: the tests pinned the implementation, which is why the drift went unnoticed. Closes [#515](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/515).

  **Omitting won on evidence, not taste.** The case for padding is that a uniform shape lets consumers read `result["ok"]` without a branch. That is not true of this codebase: every core consumer already tests `"skipped" in result` *before* touching a verdict key — `_validator_regressed` (the one that decides rollback, #406), `_validator_result_is_cacheable`, `_validator_render_row`, `_validator_render_diff` — and cannot do otherwise, because the reason string exists only on a skip. Nothing consumes `ok` on a skipped result, so padding bought no consumer anything. Meanwhile the core's own built-in skips (`_builtin_syntax_run`, unresolved targets, `warm_unsafe`) already emitted the omitting shape from four sites, making the shared helper the odd one out rather than the standard. And `ok: true` on a receipt meaning *never looked at* is precisely the absence-read-as-a-pass failure the three-state contract exists to end — a contract now backing [#477](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/477), [#482](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/482), [#345](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/345), [#263](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/263) and [#412](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/412).

  `tool`, `file` and `duration_ms` stay — they describe the attempt, not a verdict about the file. `SCHEMA.md` marks the three verdict keys as absent on a skip and now points authors at the helper instead of at a hand-rolled dict.

  **Pinned against the documentation this time.** `tests/test_skipped_shape_contract_515.py` asserts the *documented* shape of `refusal.skipped()`, asserts the core's built-in skips and the shared helper publish the same key set, and reads both `SCHEMA.md` and `docs/validators.md` so the prose cannot quietly drift back. A fourth test feeds a hand-written documented skip through all four core consumers, proving none of them needs `ok`. Red first: 3 of 6 failed, the shape test reporting `padded verdict keys the documentation tells adapter authors to omit: ['ok', 'count', 'errors']`.
- **`watches` unlinked a dead poller's PID file and dropped the row, so a slot that *lost* coverage rendered byte-identically to one that never had any.** The worst member of this repository's recurring "an absence the tool produced, read as an absence in the world" family, because the thing going quiet is the monitoring surface: a validator that declines costs one check, a `radar` that lost a watcher costs *every* event on that MR while the board keeps rendering as though coverage were complete. In the session behind [#511](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/511) there were two tracked-but-dead PIDs and the board was silently blind on both MRs for its duration. [#512](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/512) made `unwatch` say it — but only if you happened to run `unwatch`. Closes [#513](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/513).

  **The distinguisher is not a heuristic, it is the artifact each exit leaves behind.** A terminal exit (`merged`/`closed`) releases the PID file *and* clears the state file; a deliberate `unwatch` releases the PID file; only a poller that never ran its shutdown path — SIGKILL, crash, OOM, reboot — leaves a PID file naming a dead process. So that, and nothing else, is a death. The ledger is kept **inside the state file**, which is the design rather than a convenience: a terminal exit deletes that file, so a legitimate exit cannot leave behind a record for anything to misread as a loss. The invariant holds by construction instead of by a check that could drift out of step with the poll loop.

  **Deaths are derived from the PID file only — never from the process scan.** A poller spawned before #512's argv labelling is invisible to the scan while being perfectly alive, so treating scan-invisibility as evidence of death would have reported the maintainer's entire running fleet as lost on the first run after this landed. Pinned by a test that puts an unlabelled poller in the fixture process table and asserts the row reads alive.

  **`radar` heals it, and the healing is bounded — that bound is the substance.** `heal` reaps the stale PID file first, so the death is recorded before the new claim overwrites the evidence, then respawns. Past `transport.DEATH_RESPAWN_LIMIT` (3) recorded deaths the slot is **refused** rather than respawned, and the refusal is loud: `radar: WARNING — !33161 has lost its poller 3 times; NOT respawning`, naming the exact `watch:` op that re-arms it. Respawning forever would keep the board green while a watcher failed over and over — a visible failure converted into an invisible loop, which is the same bug one level up. A refused slot is counted as **uncovered**, because it is.

  **A healed loss is reported once; a lost slot is reported until it is resolved.** The asymmetry is deliberate. `radar` prints `NOTE — !33161 lost its poller (PID 42520 died without being unwatched); respawned.` on the run that healed it and then goes quiet, and a live slot only keeps a `flapping — N deaths recorded` note above one death. A permanent warning on a slot that is now covered is what trains a reader to skim the board, and skimming is how a real red gets missed — the failure #511 opens with. For the same reason a deliberate `unwatch` clears the ledger outright: withdrawing coverage on purpose is not losing it. Nothing automatic clears a record; the two doors out are both explicit operator actions, `unwatch` (seen, stopping) and `watch` (seen, re-arming).

  **`list_active_pids` still drops a dead row and radar's coverage is unchanged** — the split #512 was careful to make. The death is written down as that function passes, but the row it returns is still absent, because radar derives coverage from it and a dead PID is not coverage. Only `list_watchers`, which nothing but the `watches` renderer reads, grows the LOST rows.

  Red first: 18 tests, 16 failing against the unmodified preset. They pin all four directions the issue asked for — a death is reported and keeps being reported on a second read, a deliberate `unwatch` is not a death, a terminal exit leaves nothing behind (driven through the *real* poll loop, not asserted about it), and a repeatedly-dying watcher stops being respawned *and says why*. Nothing spawns or signals a real process: the process table is a fixture and `os.kill` is recorded, following #512's pattern, so the maintainer's live radar session was never touched.

- **A watcher slot tracked one PID, so a second poller on it was not merely untracked — it was unreachable.** Over a five-hour radar session `watches` showed one watcher while several emitted; one `mr_opened` arrived 3×, then 9×, then 13× in four seconds, each copy injected into a model's context window. `unwatch:gitlab-mr-feed:@me` answered `Stopped watcher … (PID 92379)`, the events continued, and the next `unwatch` answered `No active watcher` while the state file was still being rewritten every tick. The only recovery was `pkill`. Part of [#511](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/511).

  **The `ps` labelling is the load-bearing fix, not the bonus item it was filed as.** A poller is forked, so it wore the argv of whatever spawned it: every per-MR watcher displayed the *feed's* command line. Three such rows were read as duplicate feed pollers and two were killed — they were the watchers for two different MRs, one of them the MR that most needed watching. That same missing label is why nothing could enumerate the extras either, so it had to be fixed first: after the fork the grandchild `exec`s into `dispatcher.py poll SOURCE ID`, which is not a label *describing* the process but the process itself, and is the same argv every reader matches on — so `ps` and `watches` cannot drift apart. `exec` rather than `setproctitle`: no new dependency, and the PID is unchanged by exec, so [#484](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/484)'s claim-before-fork ordering and the PID reported up the pipe both stay valid. A failed `exec` falls through and polls unlabelled — a working poller that is hard to see beats no poller.

  **A slot is now read as a set, from the PID file and a `ps` scan, and the two answer different questions.** The PID file knows who *claimed* the slot; the scan knows every live poller that *names* it. `watches` renders the union: an id with more than one poller shows the count and every PID, and a poller whose PID file was deleted is listed as `no pidfile` rather than vanishing. `unwatch` acts on the union — it names every PID with its provenance before signalling anything, stops each, names any it could not stop instead of aborting the rest, and exits `1` if any refused.

  **The multi-kill is a deliberate inversion of the failure mode, and it is bounded by evidence rather than by a pattern.** The bug it replaces is a survivor nobody can reach; the risk it takes on is stopping a process someone wanted. So every PID acted on belongs to a process whose own argv names this exact source and id *as whole argv tokens* — `33248` cannot match `332480`, and an id appearing inside another command's arguments is not a match. The one real over-reach left is a poller from a different checkout of supertool, which shares the same `/tmp` slot and so genuinely is the same watcher. `SIGKILL` is now `getattr(signal, "SIGKILL", signal.SIGTERM)`, and `ProcessLookupError` is separated from other `OSError`s: previously one un-signallable PID raised out of the op and left the rest running, which is the reported bug with extra steps.

  **Three absences that were one sentence are now three, because they call for different actions:** nothing running (verified both ways), a *tracked* PID that is dead — the session had two of those, and the board was silently blind on both MRs — and a scan that could not run, which is never rendered as "no watcher". `list_active_pids` was deliberately left alone and the scan added in a new `list_watchers`: radar derives coverage from the former, so a row there that no PID file backs would change which MRs it believes are watched.

  **Part 1 of the issue (respawn without dedup) was verified as already fixed rather than rebuilt.** All three spawn tiers — the `watch` op, `radar.ensure_feed` and `radar.heal` — reach `dispatcher.start_poller`, which claims the pidfile under `O_CREAT|O_EXCL` *before* forking, and `_spawn_poller` has no other caller. #484 and [#490](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/490) hold. The duplication observed came from pollers spawned earlier in the session, running pre-fix code.

  **Known limit, stated in the docs rather than papered over:** a poller started before this landed still wears its parent's argv, so neither the scan nor `unwatch` can reach it and nothing can tell it apart from the process that spawned it. Clearing those is a one-time `pkill -f 'presets/watch/'` plus a fresh `radar`. `docs/presets/watch.md` now says plainly that `ps` output cannot be used to identify a watcher, and that when `ps` and `watches` disagree, `watches` is right.

  Red first: 25 tests, all failing on the absent API, pinning the exact observed sequence — two live pollers on one id → `watches` shows both → `unwatch` reaches both → a second `unwatch` reports honestly. Nothing in them spawns or signals a real process; the process table is a fixture.
- **A second agent opening a worktree failed six innocent tests in another agent's run.** The repo-state guard ([#319](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/319)/[#428](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/428)) fingerprints the suite repo before and after every test and blames the test that was running for any change. `config` was in the set it treated as unattributable-to-siblings, on the stated grounds that no sibling worktree can touch it. That is false — `config` lives in the **common** git dir, and `git worktree add -b x <path> origin/master` sets up tracking, writing `[branch "x"] remote/merge` into the file every xdist worker is watching. The [#501](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/501) sweep's first push aborted on six teardown `AssertionError`s across `test_dispatch`, `test_append_op_383`, `test_edit_ops` and others, all passing individually and on re-run. Closes [#505](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/505).

  **Verified before it was fixed, because the hypothesis in the issue was a correlation.** A probe repo, a sibling `git worktree add -b feat/x ../wt1 origin/master`, and the shared `.git/config` gains a `[branch "feat/x"]` block it did not have a second earlier. The end-to-end test that now pins this reproduces the reported failure verbatim: `test mutated the suite repo's git state (config, refs/heads/third-branch) — build git fixtures in tmp_path…`, raised against a test whose only crime was being in teardown.

  **Attributed by key rather than declined wholesale.** `docs/validators.md`'s "declining instead of guessing" would have said `inconclusive` for any config change beside a live sibling — correct, and it would have thrown away the `core.bare=true` tripwire #319 exists for. Instead `_parse_git_config` splits both blobs into `{(section, subsection, key): value}` and `_config_change_owner` attributes the diff, which is the move [#428](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/428) already made for refs: tracking config for a branch checked out **elsewhere** is that worktree's, exactly as its ref is. Everything else — `core.*`, `user.*`, `remote.*`, and tracking config for the branch *this* worktree holds — is still ours, siblings present or not. **CI is byte-for-byte unchanged:** with no sibling worktrees, `other_branches` is empty, every branch key lands in the unattributable bucket, and the verdict is `mutated` exactly as before.

  **The parser declines rather than guesses, in both directions.** An absent config, non-UTF-8 bytes, or a line it does not recognise returns `None`, and `None` means *ours* — undecidable is not innocent. A byte change that moves no key (a reformat, a comment) is also ours: bytes moved, and nothing about that says a sibling did it. Keys are tuples, not dotted strings, because branch names contain dots and `branch.feat.x.remote` cannot be split back without a guess that could read `branch.<ours>` as somebody else's.

  Red first: 11 new tests, 6 failing — the three parser tests, the two new attribution buckets, and the end-to-end sibling-`worktree add` reproduction. The nine pre-existing attribution tests, including `test_classify_blames_this_test_for_a_config_flip_even_beside_siblings`, were untouched and stayed green throughout.

- **The timing audit [#504](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/504) scoped to one file was finished across the suite, and it moved nothing.** Every `time.time()`/`perf_counter()`/`monotonic()` delta in `tests/` was enumerated and classified. The five files #505 named — plus `test_security_mcp_daemon.py`, which it did not — hold **16 timing assertions and every one is a hang-guard**, not a benchmark. `pytest -m 'not benchmark' --collect-only` counted 4426 before and 4437 after (the +11 are this PR's own tests); `pytest -m benchmark -n0` collects and passes the same 4, all in `test_xml.py`.

  **That is the finding, not a failure to find one.** The five flagged files are security suites, and a security suite's timing assertion bounds a *defect* — ReDoS, a symlink loop, unguarded recursion, a DoS cap that must reject before it executes — at 100x the real cost, so the test fails instead of hanging the suite. `test_notifiers.py` asserts a 5s notifier does not block its caller; `test_mcp_client.py` asserts a 0.5s connect budget is honoured instead of the 60s default. Marking any of them `benchmark` would delete exactly the coverage they exist for, which is the reasoning #504 applied to `test_edge_cases_vim.py`. No thresholds were widened either: none of the 16 has been observed to flake, and loosening a bound on suspicion is churn.

  **The marker comment now carries a mechanical test, since "reads a clock" catches both classes and decides nothing.** Multiply the threshold by ten: if the assertion still catches the bug it was written for, it is a hang-guard — keep it, and widen rather than move it if it flakes; if 10x makes it vacuous, the number *is* the assertion and it is a benchmark. Equivalently, ask what a failure would tell you: "the machine was busy" versus "something did not terminate". The comment also records that the security suites sit wholly on the hang-guard side, so the next audit can recognise the family instead of re-deriving it per test.

- **`gl-mr`'s conflict hunk preview vanished with no note when `git merge-tree` timed out.** `_get_conflict_hunks` returned a bare `{}` on `TimeoutExpired`, and the caller rendered the `## Conflicts` section with the file list and nothing else — output indistinguishable from a conflict whose hunks were genuinely empty. Twelfth filing of the same class in this repo: an absence produced by the tool, read as an absence in the world. Closes [#507](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/507).

  **Three outcomes were collapsed into one, so all three are now separated** — and all three are pinned by tests, not just the new path. Hunks computed and present renders as before, with no note. Hunks computed and genuinely none (add/add, delete/modify, rename conflicts have no inline hunks) says `No hunk preview for N file(s): …`. Hunks *not* computed says `Hunk preview unavailable: <cause>` and names the cause. This mirrors the three-state vocabulary `docs/validators.md` defines for validators (`ok` / a finding / `skipped`) and the shape [#487](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/487) settled on for an out-of-range `gl-job raw` range: answer, and say what you did.

  **The note is careful about what it does *not* undermine.** The conflicted **file list** comes from a different call — `git merge-tree --write-tree --name-only`, which carries no blob content and so cannot hit this timeout ([#501](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/501)) — so a hunk failure never means the file list is wrong. The note says that explicitly rather than leaving the whole section looking unreliable, and the resolve commands print unchanged in all three cases; they never needed the hunks.

  **`TimeoutExpired` was not the only failure the `{}` was swallowing, and fixing one would have earned a thirteenth filing.** A non-zero `git merge-tree` exit (bad object, unfetched ref, a git too old for the syntax) fell through to `if not result.stdout: return {}` and rendered as "no hunks" — the identical defect, never filed. A `merge-base` that finds no common ancestor did the same. Both now return a named cause. **`OSError` was worse than silent:** only `FileNotFoundError` was caught, so a `PermissionError` or a fork failure propagated out of `_get_conflict_hunks` and took the entire `gl-mr` render down — a crash, not an absence. Both calls now catch `OSError` (which subsumes `FileNotFoundError`) and report it.

  **The 15s timeout was reviewed rather than kept by default, and it is now a floor rather than a budget.** Wall time here is git's merge computation, not the pipe write, so it scales with how many files git has to merge: `max(15s, 5s × conflicted files)`, capped at 60s so a pathological repo still returns a bounded dashboard. The caller already knows the file count from the list it just printed, so this costs one argument and no extra call. 15s is generous for one conflicted text file and thin for a dozen files or a cold object cache — which is exactly where a human most needs the preview.

  **Return contract change, deliberately not the small version.** `_get_conflict_hunks` now returns `(hunks, skip_reason)`; `skip_reason` is `None` when git answered, *including* when the honest answer was "no hunks". Both agents who met this (fixing [#498](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/498) and [#501](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/501)) declined to absorb it on the grounds that signalling the timeout means changing the helper *and* its caller's rendering. That judgment was right, and this is that change.

  Red first: the five existing helper tests asserted `== {}` — the collapse itself — and were rewritten to the tuple contract; ten new tests (five driving `main()` end to end with a stubbed `subprocess.run`, so a fix to the helper that never reaches the rendering still fails) opened red, 15 failures in all. Verified live against a genuinely conflicted MR with four conflicted files.

- **A `benchmark`-shaped test escaped the marker #495 introduced to hold it.** #495 fixed [#485](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/485) by replacing an absolute wall-clock threshold with a scaling ratio and excluding the new `benchmark` marker from both the default run and the pre-push hook. `TestParseScaling::test_xml_count_time_scales_linearly_with_input` was never marked, so it kept running in the hook — and a ratio of two elapsed measurements is still timing-derived, so it aborted three real pushes in an hour, on diffs that never touched the code timed. Closes [#503](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/503).

  **The classification gap wasn't limited to the filed test.** `TestPerformance::test_parse_1000_file_elements_under_2s` (`tests/test_xml.py`) asserts `elapsed < 2.0` with zero ratio protection — an older, plainer violation of the same rule, predating #495 entirely. Both tests now carry `@pytest.mark.benchmark`.

  **Two other timing reads were audited and left alone, deliberately.** `test_security_mcp_daemon.py`'s stale-sock-file test measures `ensure_daemon` with `subprocess.Popen`, `os.path.exists` and `time.sleep` all monkeypatched — the 2s budget sits over near-zero real work, so it is a guard against a real block/retry loop being reintroduced, not a performance measurement (it is also currently `@pytest.mark.skip`'d for unrelated scaffolding reasons, so it isn't a live flakiness source either way). `test_edge_cases_vim.py`'s four subprocess-timing assertions are explicitly framed by their own docstrings as hang guards ("no infinite loop", "must not OOM or hang") with generous margins (2-10s) — moving them to `benchmark` would remove real infinite-loop coverage from the default run and the hook to fix a flakiness problem they don't actually have.

  **The `benchmark` marker's comment in `pyproject.toml` now names that distinction.** It previously defined `benchmark` only by contrast with `slow`; it now also contrasts it with a hang-guard — a wall-clock read whose margin is wide enough that it only ever fires for the non-termination it exists to catch, not for scheduler noise — so the next person auditing a timing assertion has a rule to apply instead of re-deriving one per incident.

  **Deterministic scaling — replacing the ratio with a work-done count (AST/element/call counts instead of elapsed time) — was costed out, not built.** `xml_count` (`presets/xml/count.py`) parses via a custom expat-driven `_LineTrackingBuilder` (`presets/xml/_common.py`) and then calls `root.findall(xpath)` for the plain, non-`contains()` queries `TestParseScaling` exercises. Counting expat callback invocations would only reproduce the already-known input size (expat's per-character contract is linear by construction, so its count carries no information about `xml_count`'s own behaviour); the actual linear-vs-quadratic risk lives inside `ElementTree.findall`'s C accelerator, which exposes no instrumentable node-visit counter. Getting a real work-done metric would mean re-implementing `findall`'s traversal in instrumented Python — diverging from the code path actually shipped, which defeats the point of the test. Sizeable, and a different shape of test than a marker fix; filed as a follow-up rather than folded into this PR.
- **99 `subprocess` calls decoded their child's output with the locale codec and a strict error handler.** [#498](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/498) was the first one to actually fire — a conflicted PNG put a `0x89` on `git merge-tree`'s stdout and `gl-mr` died mid-render — and it was fixed as a single call. This is the class. Closes [#501](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/501).

  **It was never runtime-only detection, and that framing is what kept it unfixed.** [#461](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/461) listed the class as "~270 sites, runtime-only", so it was filed as unenumerable and left. `text=True` (or `universal_newlines=True`, or any `encoding=`) with no `errors=` is a purely syntactic property of the call site: the same AST walk that already backs the read half of `tests/test_encoding_seam.py` enumerates every one without executing anything.

  **The count was enumerated before anything was swept, and it was not ~270.** Shipped code — `supertool.py`, `presets/`, `hooks/`, `validators/`, `formatters/`, `notifiers/` — holds **99**. The rest of the ~270 are in `tests/` (125), deliberately out of scope: a test that mis-decodes its own fixture output fails loudly on a runner, which is not the failure this rule exists to stop. Enumerating first is house practice for a reason — [#429](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/429) was filed as two instances and was seven, [#418](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/418) was filed as four and was sixty-nine.

  **Two bugs share the line, so both halves are pinned.** The strict handler is the crash. The missing `encoding=` is quieter and arguably worse: `subprocess._text_encoding()` takes the codec from the locale, so a `C`-locale runner mangles every accented path, branch name and commit message without anything crashing — #418's defect at a seam #418's scan never covered. All 99 now carry `encoding="utf-8", errors="replace"`.

  **The "content vs git porcelain" split was proposed and does not survive contact.** `status`, `diff --name-status`, `diff --stat` and `log --format` all embed paths, branch names, author names and commit messages; latin-1 commit messages are ordinary in older repositories. Only hashes, counts and `--version` are genuinely safe, and pinning those costs nothing, so the sweep is uniform rather than triaged.

  **`errors="replace"` is silent, and at two seams silence is the wrong trade.** It renders a decode failure as mojibake indistinguishable from content — fine where the output is only displayed, not fine where the decoded text *becomes something else*, because there it converts a crash into a wrong answer, the trade this repository has rejected nine times ([#414](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/414)/[#445](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/445)/[#454](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/454)/[#459](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/459)/[#477](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/477)/[#482](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/482)/[#345](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/345)/[#487](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/487)/[#263](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/263)). Seven sites are therefore not merely swept. The **vim shell verbs** (`:!`, `:%!`, `:r !`) push the child's stdout back into the user's file — `errors="replace"` there would overwrite their bytes with U+FFFD and report success — and now refuse, naming the offset, leaving the file byte-identical. **`git diff --cached -z --name-only`** feeds `validate_staged` / `format_staged`: `-z` turns off the octal quoting that keeps porcelain ASCII, so a latin-1 filename comes back holding U+FFFD, fails `os.path.isfile`, and vanishes from the list — a pre-commit gate silently declining to check a file being committed, and returning `no staged files` when it was the only one. Those paths are now named in the output.

  **Capturing bytes and decoding by hand was considered and rejected** at those seams: the detection it buys is exactly what U+FFFD already provides after a `replace` decode, at the cost of restructuring `input=`/`stdout` handling through the vim op. #498 had already settled the shape with `_is_binary_hunk` — decode leniently, then detect and label — and this reuses it as `_undecodable_at()`.

  **The scan declines instead of guessing**, per `docs/validators.md`. Three states, not two: a call that provably decodes and leaves either kwarg to the default is a violation; a call whose kwargs are not literals the parser can read (`**opts` forwarding, `text=some_flag`) is *undecidable* and is enumerated separately rather than counted clean. Both lists are empty today, so a new `**kwargs` call site turns the suite red and has to be answered. Blind spots are named in the docstring rather than implied away: aliased imports, `getattr`-built calls, and `tests/`.

  Red first throughout: the rule's fixture test pins all three states with line numbers and fails against a no-op implementation, the enumerator opened red on all 99, and seven of the ten guard tests fail with the U+FFFD check disabled.

  **Not absorbed, and not silently:** `_get_conflict_hunks` still returns `{}` on `TimeoutExpired`, so the hunk preview vanishes with no note. Same omission family, different trigger, and not a one-line fix — signalling it means changing the helper's return contract and its caller's rendering. It wants its own issue.

  **Windows CI caught a second, unrelated defect in the same test file.** The mojibake-guard tests build a `:!` shell command by interpolating `sys.executable` and a temp script path straight into a vim mini-language SCRIPT string. The mini-language's own `\e` -> ESC convention (`_op_vim_impl`) is a blind text substitution over the whole script, and a Windows path segment starting with a lowercase `e` right after a backslash — `\emit.py`, guaranteed by this fixture's own filename — collides with it: the ex-command capture for `:!` gets cut off mid-string on every Windows run, before the mojibake guard (or the shell-out itself) ever executes. All four Windows legs failed on that collision, not on the guard logic. Two fixes: `_op_vim_impl` now recognises an escaped-backslash form (`\\e`, matching the `\\` -> literal `\` sentinel convention `_decode_escapes` already uses) so a literal `\e` can survive inside TEXT/ex-command content instead of always being consumed as ESC; and the test's `_emitter()` normalizes both interpolated paths with `Path.as_posix()` before building the command string — the same fix #472 used for the identical shape of bug in a TOML basic string, and the safe way to build a `:!` command from a Windows path at all, since Windows accepts `/` in paths passed to `CreateProcess`.

- **`phpstan` reported CLEAN on files PHPStan had refused to analyse.** Reported as an inheritance-scope gap: `phpstan:<file>` said CLEAN on a framework property that the DVSI pre-push hook then blocked at the same level with `property.extraNativeType`, so a typing pass across dozens of framework classes only discovered the incompatibilities at `git push`. Part of [#263](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/263).

  **The issue's stated cause does not reproduce, and that had to be established before anything was built.** #263 attributes the miss to single-file scope — "inheritance-compat rules require loading the parent class, which analysing one file in isolation does not do". PHPStan does load it. Given a parent declaring `$label` untyped and a child typing it `string`, analysing **the child file alone** under the project config reports `property.extraNativeType` on the child; verified both hermetically and against DVSI's own `phpstan-fwk.neon` with the real `Shared\Components\ComponentGroupBase` as parent. Parents are resolved through the config's `paths` / `scanDirectories`, not through the analysed file list. Where a parent genuinely is unreachable, PHPStan says so loudly — `class.notFound`, "Child extends unknown class Parent" — rather than going quiet. So both horns the issue offered are answers to a question that was not being asked: widening scope buys nothing PHPStan was not already doing, and documenting "the op cannot gate inheritance-compat" would have documented a limitation that does not exist. A regression test now pins the real behaviour in both directions so it cannot be lost.

  **The symptom was real; the mechanism sat one layer down, and it is this repository's recurring shape.** When PHPStan declines to analyse a file — the path sits under `excludePaths.analyse`, matches nothing, or the run dies — it writes to stderr, exits non-zero, and puts **nothing** on stdout. The adapter read empty stdout as `{"totals": {"file_errors": 0}}` and emitted `ok: true, count: 0, errors: []`. A green meaning *"I analysed nothing"* was byte-identical to one meaning *"I analysed it and it is fine"*, and no caller could tell them apart. The same applies to any run producing no stdout: a fatal, an OOM, an unreadable config all rendered as a pass. This is the eighth filing of *a tool's inability to answer rendering identically to an answer* ([#414](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/414)/[#445](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/445)/[#454](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/454)/[#477](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/477)/[#482](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/482)/[#345](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/345)), and it needed no new vocabulary — the three-state contract and `validators/common/refusal.py` already existed, `phpstan-mcp` and `phpmd-mcp` already used them, and the cold `phpstan` adapter simply did not.

  **A run with no parseable result now resolves into one of three states, never silently into the fourth.** A refusal the adapter recognises returns `skipped` — no `✗`, no rollback, never cached, never in the before/after delta. An exit it cannot explain stays an error naming the exit code and the tool's own first stderr line, because swallowing an unknown failure is the same category mistake pointing the other way. Only a genuinely quiet success (empty stdout, exit 0) keeps the clean verdict. The skip reason quotes **the line that actually refused**, not whatever printed first: real runs open with `Note: Using configuration file ...`, and reporting that back names the config file while saying nothing about why the file went unanalysed. `PHPSTAN_SKIP_PATTERNS` teaches the adapter a house-specific refusal without a release, matching `phpstan-mcp`'s existing knob.

  **Nothing about a real result changed.** Findings still report as findings when PHPStan exits non-zero — which it does on every run that finds anything — and a clean parse is still clean. Nine tests, four red before the fix, cover both directions including the exact stdout/stderr/exit shape of the real excluded-path run.
- **`gl-mr` crashed with `UnicodeDecodeError` on a conflicted MR containing a binary file — after printing half the answer.** `git merge-tree` writes conflicting blob *content* to stdout, so one conflicted PNG puts a `0x89` on the stream; `subprocess.run(..., text=True)` decoded it strict-UTF-8 and the op died at `_get_conflict_hunks`, having already rendered the conflict count and the first two filenames. A partial answer followed by a traceback, on the exact op someone runs *because* the MR is conflicted. Closes [#498](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/498).

  **The option the issue leaned toward is not available from this command.** "Ask `git merge-tree` for less — `--name-only`, then fetch hunks per-file for text files only" assumes the hunk-producing form accepts a pathspec. It does not: `git merge-tree [--trivial-merge] <base-tree> <branch1> <branch2>` takes three tree-ish arguments and nothing else, and `--name-only` is only meaningful in the `--write-tree` form, which emits no hunks at all. There is no per-file call to make. (A `--write-tree` + `git show <tree>:<path>` route does exist, but it returns whole merged files rather than hunks and is a different feature, not a fix.) The file list, meanwhile, *already* comes from `--write-tree --name-only` — a separate call that never carries blob content — which is why the conflicting binary was never at risk of vanishing from `## Conflicts` and why the crash was confined to the hunk preview.

  **So: decode deliberately, and label rather than drop.** The call now pins `encoding="utf-8", errors="replace"`. `errors=` alone would have left the codec at whatever `subprocess._text_encoding()` picks from the locale, which is its own latent bug — git writes UTF-8 no matter what `LANG` says, so a `C`-locale runner would mangle every accented path in a French repo. The hunk parser wants line structure, not exact bytes, so a text conflict is now byte-identical to before and a binary blob decodes to replacement characters instead of an exception.

  **Mojibake is not an answer either, and neither is silence.** Forty lines of U+FFFD would satisfy "does not crash" while telling the reader nothing, and quietly skipping the block would be this repo's house bug — *an absence read as an absence* ([#414](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/414)/[#445](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/445)/[#454](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/454)/[#477](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/477)/[#482](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/482)/[#345](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/345)) — traded for a loud one. A block containing U+FFFD or a NUL is detected by `_is_binary_hunk` and rendered as its `### path` heading plus `(binary file — conflict hunks not shown; resolve by picking a version)`. The file keeps its place in the list, keeps its heading, and says what it is; only the unreadable bytes are withheld.

  **The file-list call got the same encoding treatment**, for the same reason one step earlier: it prints paths, and a path is bytes, so a non-UTF-8 filename would have taken down the conflict list itself — the one output that must survive.

  Two tests, both red first: a helper-level one and an end-to-end `main()` run, each stubbing `subprocess.run` with a *real* subprocess that writes the fixture bytes using the production kwargs, so the decode under test is the one `subprocess` actually performs rather than a mock of it. The end-to-end test reproduces the reported shape exactly — conflict list printed, then `UnicodeDecodeError` — and now asserts the binary file is named, the text hunk is intact, and the `To resolve:` block that used to be lost still prints.
- **`radar`'s board could silently narrow between invocations, and nothing on it said so.** The author/state filter lives for one invocation only, so a session that deliberately widened to `author=@me,author=modular.system` and later ran a bare `radar` got the default single-author population back — rendering a perfectly healthy board that omitted a whole category of MRs. In the session that produced the report, the wide filter is what surfaced a week-old conflicted dependency bump on a payment path nobody was tracking. Closes [#486](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/486).

  **Another filing of the defect this repo keeps hitting: an omission the tool produced, read as an absence in the world.** A board that narrowed looks exactly like a board with nothing to report — the shape of [#414](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/414)/[#445](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/445)/[#454](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/454)/[#459](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/459)/[#477](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/477)/[#482](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/482)/[#345](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/345). The fix is the same one those arrived at: state the thing rather than imply it. **Every board now names the scope it was built from, the default one included** — `scope author=@me,state=opened (default) | 7 open | …`. The `(default)` token is the distinction that was missing: an unlabelled footer used to spell both "this is the default population" and "nobody said which population this is", and the old code only labelled *non*-default boards, which is precisely the case that never needed labelling.

  **The split-scope half is reported, not resolved.** Changing the filter respawns the feed watcher without retiring the old one, so effective scope ends up divided between what the last invocation passed and what a still-running watcher was started with — confirmed live on the maintainer's machine, where `watches` shows feed pollers for both `@me` and `author=@me,author=modular.system,state=opened`. A live feed on any other scope now prints a `radar: NOTE` naming it, the fact that its MRs are not on this board, and the exact re-run that would show them. Read-only: `other_feed_scopes` reads pid files and spawns nothing. **Killing the older poller was considered and rejected** — `prune_terminal` already documents that a second radar over another population is a legitimate arrangement, so auto-retiring would break the working case to tidy the confusing one. Only the reader knows which population they meant.

  **What this does not do, stated plainly: it gives visibility, not continuity.** A bare `radar` after a wide one still covers the default, and re-widening still means re-typing the filter. That is the deliberate half of the trade. Persisting the filter would mean a board whose population comes from a file nobody in the session chose — the same hidden state that produced the split scope in the first place, one level up. **Breaking, cosmetic:** the footer gained a leading `scope …` token, so anything matching the footer from its start needs updating.

- **`gl-job:ID:raw:START:END` declined to answer using a bound it had just printed.** A START past the end of the log returned `## Raw — start (3830) > total (3674); nothing to show`, two lines below a header reading `Log: 3674 lines total`. Because `:raw` is reached as a fallback when `:fail` was unhelpful, START is always a guess, and an overshooting guess cost a full round-trip to re-read a number already on screen — three times in one session on the same job. Closes [#487](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/487).

  **Out-of-range now returns the tail of the width requested, and says it is not what was asked for.** Silently clamping is the same disease one level down: a caller who asked for 3830–3870 and is handed 3634–3674 without being told has received different data than they requested. So the response carries both facts — `## Raw — requested 3830-3870 is past end of log (3674 lines); showing the last 41 lines instead`, followed by the ordinary `## Raw lines 3634-3674 of 3674` header that every raw response already prints. With no END, the fallback width is the `lines` config (default 80).

  **`:raw:-N` is the ergonomic half, and it is the half the issue actually identified.** The interesting lines are found by walking backwards from the end, so `raw` is nearly always used to read the tail — which previously required knowing the total first, i.e. spending the call that returns it. `gl-job:6933325:raw:-40` is now the last 40 lines with no prior knowledge. It refuses an END (`raw:-40:50` has two contradictory anchors and guessing which wins is how a caller silently gets lines it never asked for) and clamps to the log length. A negative START used to be coerced to `1` and dump the whole log, so this replaces a silent wrong answer as well as adding a form.

  **Two adjacent silences found while fixing this one, and closed with it.** An empty log is now distinguished from a request the op could not serve — `## Raw — the log is empty (0 lines)`, which is the only case where "nothing to show" was ever the honest answer. And an inverted range (`raw:10:5`) used to slice to nothing under a header reading `## Raw lines 10-9 of 20`, an empty body that reads as an empty stretch of log rather than as a range the op declined; it now exits 1 and says which bound is backwards. Ten tests across both surfaces, red before the fix. **`gh-job` carried a byte-identical copy of the bug** and is fixed in the same commit rather than waiting to be re-filed against the other preset.

- **A warm validator reported failures that the cold runner could not reproduce, and there was no way for it to say "I cannot judge this file".** `phpunit-mcp` returned 2 failures on a DVSI test extending `SiControllerTestCase`; the real `phpunit:` op on the same file, same commit, same `phpunit.xml`, passed 3/3 with 173 assertions. A validator that cries wolf erodes trust in its greens too — after that receipt the only way to know the truth was to re-run the real runner, which defeats the point of an inline validator. Closes [#345](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/345).

  **The reds are fabricated, not pre-existing, and the two need opposite fixes.** The issue does not distinguish them and the distinction decides everything, so it was established before anything was built. `mcp-phpunit-warm` prewarms by running the project's `phpunit.xml` bootstrap in the long-lived **parent** process — that is the whole point of prewarm, and it is on by default (`PhpunitRunner::prewarm()`, `bin/mcp-phpunit-warm`) — then forks a child per call so user classes are parsed fresh from disk. Correct for staleness ([#265](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/265)), but every resource that bootstrap opened — a DB handle, a session, a platform singleton — is now shared by the parent and by every child that will ever fork from it. The resulting failure depends on warm-process state, not on the file, which is exactly why a cold run cannot reproduce it. Same family as #265 and rector's `ClassReflection` ([#273](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/273)); the fifth filing of the shape this repo keeps hitting ([#414](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/414)/[#445](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/445)/[#454](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/454)/[#477](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/477)/[#482](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/482)) — *a tool's inability to answer rendering identically to an answer*.

  **This kills the option the issue listed first.** "Give the MCP runner the same env (test DB, autoload cache) as the standalone ops" assumes the env differs. It does not. Both routes run from the same repo root against the same `--config=phpunit.xml`; `.claude/scripts/phpunit.sh` adds nothing but output caps, and the daemon's cmd adds nothing but the binary. There is no missing environment variable to supply — the difference is process *topology* (a fork inheriting its parent's bootstrapped handles), which lives in `mcp-phpunit-warm`, not here. Nothing in supertool can set it.

  **And it kills the third, for the reason the issue suspected.** "Suppress results the runner self-labels pre-existing" is wrong twice over. A pre-existing failure is a real failure, and suppressing it is how a genuinely broken file starts looking clean. It is also unnecessary: regression-only rollback ([#406](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/406)) already compares against a pre-edit baseline and refuses to roll back a red that was already there — which is precisely why these were labelled "pre-existing" in the first place, since the baseline pass fabricates them too. The framework layer was already correct. The bug is upstream of it: the red is not a fact about the file at all.

  **So the fix is #482's shape — decline rather than guess.** A new per-validator spec field, `warm_unsafe`, takes a regex or list of regexes matched against the **resolved** target's content (the test file `resolve` picks, not the edited source). A hit short-circuits `_validator_run_one` before the adapter is spawned at all and returns `skipped`, which the framework has treated as an absence of information since #406: never a ✗, never a rollback, never cached. The receipt names the pattern that matched rather than printing a bare `skipped` that sends the reader back to the config to guess. Opt-in and vendor-neutral: supertool cannot work out which of a project's tests touch shared bootstrap state, the project can, and absent config nothing changes.

  **The honest cost, stated plainly: a warm-unsafe test is no longer validated inline at all.** A green from an untrustworthy runner is worth no more than its red, so both are declined — the alternative is keeping a verdict we have just finished proving is unreliable. Those files need the cold op (`phpunit:PATH`) or CI. This is the same trade #482 made and the same one #414/#445/#454 keep arriving at: a visible gap beats an invisible wrong answer.

  **Config typos bias towards running, never towards silence.** An unreadable target, a non-string pattern, a pattern that does not compile — all ignored, and one bad pattern does not disarm the good ones beside it, because the failure mode of a muting feature is a validator that has silently stopped working. Reads are capped at 256 KB, enough for any class declaration. Seventeen tests, ten of them red before the fix, pin both directions: a fabricated red is never reported as a failure (skipped, no ✗, no rollback, adapter never invoked, nothing rendered from its output), and a genuine failure on a non-matching target still reports `2 err` and still regresses.

  **The rector half of #345 was already fixed** — `toMutatingScope() on null` has been dropped at the source by the `rector-mcp` adapter since the `engine_glitches` work, which is why that is a suppression and this is not: a PHP fatal inside rector's engine is not a finding about the file, whereas a phpunit failure is exactly the shape of a real one and cannot be told apart from it by reading the output.

- **`radar` and `gl-mrs` rendered `[conflict]` from a field that does not mean conflict.** `has_conflicts` is a straight alias for `cannot_be_merged?`, and GitLab annotates the exposure itself in `lib/api/entities/merge_request_basic.rb`: it "is generally indicative of conflicts … However, it can also indicate that either `#has_no_commits?` or `#branch_missing?` are true." So an MR with no diff rendered `[conflict]` on the triage board and in radar's snapshot — [#465](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/465)'s false positive on two more surfaces. Closes [#471](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/471).

  **The issue's blocking objection turned out not to apply, and that is the whole fix.** #471 declined to port the poller's `_has_no_diff` guard because the list endpoint does not return `diff_refs`, degrading the check from three signals to two. Live inspection confirms the premise — every `glab mr list` row carries `detailed_merge_status` and `sha`, none carries `diff_refs` — but not the conclusion. Neither surface reads the list payload alone: `mrs._enrich` already fetches `projects/:id/merge_requests/{iid}` for every MR on the board to get pipeline status and diff size, and `radar.live_open_mrs` routes through the same function. That is the *identical endpoint the poller polls*, and it was fetching `diff_refs` and discarding it. The full three-signal guard was available at zero additional requests; it just needed one line in `_enrich` to stop throwing the field away.

  **Where the signal genuinely is unavailable, the guard degrades to two of three and never to a false negative.** Rows past `enrich_cap` and the `nopipe` flag skip enrichment, so `_diff_refs` is simply absent there. Absent fields are never evidence — the same bias as the poller and as [#463](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/463) — so a row the guard cannot see keeps `has_conflicts` trusted. The check can only ever fail to *downgrade* a conflict, never invent silence about one.

  **A blocked, empty MR is relabelled `[empty]`, not suppressed, and `[conflict]` is not renamed.** Suppression was the obvious port from the poller and is wrong on this surface: the poller writes to an event stream where silence is a correct answer, but a triage board that prints nothing for an unmergeable MR is a silent omission, which this repository rates as strictly worse than a mislabel ([#445](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/445)/[#454](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/454)/[#414](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/414)). Renaming the column to `[blocked]` — the option #471 raised — was considered and rejected on two counts: it merges "you have a real conflict to resolve" with "you forgot to push" into one word, discarding exactly the severity distinction a triage board exists to draw, and it is a breaking output change for anything grepping `[conflict]`. Adding a value costs neither. For the same reason the empty MR stays in radar's standing-problem set, printing as `empty` / `failed+empty` on the exclusion line.

  **`detailed_merge_status == "conflict"` is still only a widener, never a gate.** It reports the *first* failing mergeability check, and in `MergeRequest.all_mergeability_checks` the draft check runs second while conflict runs dead last, so a genuinely conflicted draft reports `draft_status`. Gating on it would silently stop reporting real conflicts on every draft and every MR with unresolved threads — refuted on #465 and not re-attempted here.

  **One shared predicate, four call sites.** `mrs._conflict_label` now backs `mrs._flags`, `radar._snap_entry` and `radar._problem_label`, which each carried their own copy of the same wrong expression. Seven tests, red before the fix, driven over stubbed `glab mr list` stdout and stubbed detail responses rather than against the helper, so a half-implementation that never wires `diff_refs` through enrichment fails. Both directions pinned: the two observed empty-MR shapes plus the enrichment-only `head_sha == base_sha` case render `[empty]`, while a real conflict — and a real conflict whose detail fetch failed — still renders `[conflict]`. **Breaking, minor:** radar's snapshot `conflict` key was a bool and is now `"conflict"` / `"empty"` / `""`, so the first run after upgrading prints one full board.
- **Three gates that could fail for a reason the author could not act on, fixed as one job.** A wall-clock benchmark, a socket race and a tracked build artifact have nothing in common mechanically; they had one thing in common that matters more, which is that each of them made a red run mean something other than "this diff is wrong". `master` went genuinely red one morning from a merge-order race and was caught in minutes **because a red `master` still meant something** — and both of the flakes here land as **pre-push refusals**, ~110s in, on diffs that could not have caused them. Every failure that does not mean "this diff is wrong" spends the credibility of every failure that does. Closes [#485](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/485), [#491](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/491), [#489](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/489).

  **#485 — the benchmark was measuring the machine, and it was only ever pointed at one machine.** `test_xml_count_real_clover_fast` asserted `elapsed < 5.0` over a real 25 MB clover file, and reported `5.72 < 5.0` and `5.02 < 5.0` — the second is a 0.4% miss, which is a busy scheduler, not a regression. It aborted three pushes in one night across two work streams, on branches touching neither `test_xml.py` nor anything it calls. The detail that decides the fix is that `REAL_CLOVER` is an **absolute path inside one maintainer's home directory**: CI has never run this test, so the pre-push hook's `-m ''` — written to *mirror* CI — was in fact running something CI cannot. A hook can only mirror CI for tests whose inputs are also mirrored. The two wall-clock members of that class are now `@pytest.mark.benchmark`, excluded from the default run, from the hook and from CI, and run deliberately with `pytest -m benchmark -n0`; the `tracemalloc` member is deliberately **not** marked, because it measures the process rather than the box and is as deterministic on a loaded runner as an idle one.

  **The number was not widened; the question was changed, and the replacement is a test that fails against a quadratic parser.** `TestParseScaling` asks "did `xml_count` stay linear in the size of the document" using a ratio between two input sizes measured in the same process, so machine speed cancels out of it. Getting that to be a guard rather than the next flake took three measured corrections: a naive 4x step swung **5x to 8.7x on an idle machine** against a quadratic expectation of 16x — no threshold fits in that gap. Disabling the GC around each sample (its cost tracks total heap, not this document), stepping the input 8x, and warming up once to pay the cold-process cost off the clock produce **8.4-8.5x across six idle runs, 1% spread**, and **7.7x / 10.8x / 13.7x with twelve CPU hogs saturating the machine**. A deliberately quadratic `safe_xpath` measures **61.8x**. The gate at 24 sits 1.75x above the worst loaded reading and 2.6x below the regression — against the 0.4% of margin that #485 reported on.

  **Two metrics, because peak allocation is exact and insufficient.** Allocation repeats to within 0.1% and catches a parse that goes quadratic in *space*, but it is blind to one that re-walks a tree it has already built — verified, not assumed: the simulated quadratic above passes the allocation test and fails the timing one. Timing catches that and cannot be exact. Both ship, and what neither can gate is stated in the test's own docstring rather than left for the next person to rediscover.

  **#491 — the fixture was waiting for the wrong thing, and the client is deliberately unforgiving.** Two different tests in `tests/test_mcp_client.py` failed on macOS within one hour, one on `master` and one on a PR, both `[Errno 61] Connection refused ... daemon never wrote a stderr log`, both clean on re-run. The mechanism is a two-line window: `bind()` publishes the socket path and `listen()` is what makes a connect succeed, so **between the two the file exists and every connect is refused** — reproduced directly, `ConnectionRefusedError(61)` against a path `os.path.exists` reports as present. Every mock fixture polled for that file. On the other side, `MCPClient` given an explicit `socket_path` connects **exactly once** and raises, which is correct and intentional: nobody else is going to bind a test path, so polling it burns the budget for nothing. That is not a regression from [#488](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/488) — checked, because #488 touches the same branch: the diff shows `if not self._auto_spawn:` becoming `if not self._auto_spawn or not _mcp_autospawn_allowed():`, so the single-shot exit for explicit test paths was already there and #488 only widened the same exit to provenance-suppressed callers. The window is sub-millisecond idle and unbounded on a loaded runner under `-n auto`, which is why this reads as hourly rather than never.

  **Readiness is now a connect, which is the question the client actually asks.** `tests/_mcp_mock.py` replaces four hand-rolled copies of the same fixture — three `mock_uds` definitions plus two inline spawns — with one spawn helper that probes by connecting, and reports a server that died during startup immediately instead of after a ten-second wait. The test that pins it uses a new `MOCK_MCP_LISTEN_DELAY` in the mock server to hold the bind→listen window open for a second, so the race is a fact rather than a coin flip: against the previous existence-based probe it fails with the exact error from the issue, byte for byte. No test was pinned to a worker, grouped, or serialised — `docs/contributing.md` already says that hides a dependency rather than fixing one, and this was a real dependency.

  **#489 — a second tracked copy of the whole tool, which nothing kept in sync and nothing read.** `build/lib/supertool.py` was committed *and* regenerated by the suite, because the console-entry-point test ([#442](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/442)) runs `pip install .` against the repo root. It surfaced four times in one session across three people: blocking a `git pull` with an unstaged-change error, and dirtying two unrelated diffs. It was confirmed dead before deletion rather than assumed — nothing in the tree references `build/lib`, `build.lib` or the directory in any import or packaging path, and `pyproject.toml` declares `py-modules = ["supertool"]` from the root — so the fix is untracking plus a `build/` (and `dist/`) ignore, not a divergence check. The standing bar: a clean checkout that runs the test suite is still a clean checkout.

- **`gl-mr`, the fourth surface, printed `Conflicts: YES — cannot merge` on an MR with no diff — and it already held the disproof.** [#492](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/492) fixed the same `has_conflicts`/`cannot_be_merged?` false positive on the two board surfaces and named this single-MR dashboard as a fourth, deliberately left out of that PR's scope. It is the worse of the four: `_get_conflicting_files` runs, comes back empty because there is no diff, and the line prints `YES` anyway rather than reading its own result. Closes [#494](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/494).

  **The fix reuses `mrs._conflict_label` rather than a fifth copy of the predicate.** `gl-mr` fetches the MR via `glab mr view --output json`, which is the same full resource shape as the detail endpoint the board enriches through — `detailed_merge_status`, `sha`, and `diff_refs` all arrive in the one call this surface already makes, no second request needed. The only wrinkle: `main()` already binds a local named `mrs` (the branch-lookup result list), which would shadow a module-level `import mrs` for the rest of the function, so the fix imports the function directly (`from mrs import _conflict_label`) instead.

  **The empty case gets a sentence, not a word.** The board's `[empty]` is sized for a table column; this view has a full line and a reader who navigated here specifically to find out what's wrong, so it now reads `Conflicts: NO — cannot merge: source branch has no commits, so there is nothing to merge` — naming the real blocker instead of a generic label, and kept as a stated problem rather than silently omitted (the omission class this repo already rates as worse than a mislabel: #445/#454/#414/#477/#482). A real conflict is unaffected and still prints `Conflicts: YES — cannot merge`, including on a draft and including with unresolved review threads — `detailed_merge_status` reports only the *first* failing mergeability check, and conflict runs last, so gating on it (rejected on #465) would have silently stopped reporting real conflicts on exactly those two shapes.

- **`radar`'s heal tier spawned pollers around the singleton, not through it — and it is the tier where that costs the most.** [#484](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/484) closed the test-then-fork race for the two spawn sites it found, `cmd_watch` and `radar.ensure_feed`, and `start_poller`'s own docstring names them as "every tier". There is a third. `radar.heal` called `dispatcher._spawn_poller` directly, below the claim, so the one caller that spawns *per open MR* was the one caller not covered by the fix. Live inspection on the maintainer's machine found six per-MR pollers all wearing `radar.py`'s argv — the heal tier is where the fleet actually comes from, and none of it was going through the door. Part of [#417](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/417).

  **The multiplier is what makes this different from #476 rather than a footnote to it.** `heal` derives its gaps from a `watched` set built by reading pidfiles, and a pidfile is published by the grandchild after a fork, an interpreter start and a detach — the same several-hundred-millisecond window. What changed is the population on either side of it: item 1 of #417 moved the watched set from "the MRs that are already failing" to **every open MR**, and radar runs on a five-minute loop from more than one place plus a SessionStart hook. Two runs overlapping in that window therefore duplicate the entire fleet — seven pollers for an active dev, not one feed. The fix is not new machinery: `heal` now calls `dispatcher.start_poller`, which claims the slot with `O_CREAT|O_EXCL` before any side effect, so losing the race costs nothing.

  **A slot already held is neither healed nor uncovered, and that third state is the point.** `start_poller` answers `alive` when another radar owns the slot, and both of the obvious places to file that answer are wrong. Counted as healed, radar reports an action it did not take. Counted as uncovered, radar prints `[UNWATCHED]` against an MR that has a live watcher — and that warning is the single thing on this board that has to be trusted absolutely, because the entire op exists to make "nothing is watching" distinguishable from "nothing is wrong". Poisoning it to describe a won-by-someone-else race would trade the defect for a worse one.

  **No cap was added, deliberately.** "One poller per open MR" is bounded by the number of MRs a person has open, and the resource question that made a cap look attractive was really the duplication question — a race, not a population size. A cap answers it by silently declining to watch an MR, which is precisely the failure #417 was filed about: a radar that is quiet because it is not looking renders identically to a radar that is quiet because everything is green. Deduplicating is free; dropping is not, so the tie goes to watching. Four tests pin it, three of which fail against the previous implementation: no stacking across two heals, the claim precedes the fork, the already-held slot is reported in neither list, and a failed spawn gives the slot back rather than wedging that iid shut forever.
- **`lsp-diag` stops returning a clean verdict about a file it cannot see.** Called on a `.py` file containing an unterminated string literal it returned `{"ok": true, "count": 0}` — not a skip, not a crash, a confident wrong answer.
