- **A failed job could report `## No error patterns matched` — a crashed job rendering as green.** [#445](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/445) and [#444](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/444) are two readings of one defect: `gl-job:<id>:fail` reported the wreckage and not the reason. In #445 a `test_unit_dpt` job whose paratest worker took a SIGSEGV printed *nothing at all* under a header saying `Status: failed`, and was triaged as "no information, retry" rather than "a worker segfaulted in `UserModularForm02Test`" — the cause was sitting at line 1616 of the log, simply unmatched. In #444 the window did match, but anchored on the stack trace, and a stack trace is the *consequence*: the `Caused by` / `PDOException: SQLSTATE[23000] … foreign key constraint fails` that explained it was 23 lines above the first frame and never shown, so recovering it meant falling back to `:raw` with guessed line ranges — 8 extra round-trips across one session, one of which overshot a 3674-line log and had to be guessed again. The invariant the fix is written against: **a failed job must never produce output a reader can mistake for "nothing wrong", and where a cause exists in the log the output must contain the line that states it, not merely the wreckage around it.**

  **The unmatched case is where the real fix lives, and it is not a pattern.** Adding a `WorkerCrashedException` pattern closes #445 and leaves the defect: the next unmatched exception class produces the same silent green, and the set of things that can kill a CI job is not enumerable. So zero matches on a job GitLab calls `failed` now prints `## FAILED — no error pattern matched`, says in words that supertool could not classify it and that this is a missing pattern here rather than a clean log, lists the patterns it tried, names the last `section_start:` the runner entered (the failing step), dumps the last 40 lines, and points at `:raw`/`:grep`. `## No error patterns matched` survives **only** for jobs that did not fail, where it is true; a green job with a quiet log is not accused of anything. The same banner replaced the *default* view's silent fallback, which was worse than #445's: it printed a bare tail with no statement at all, so the reader had to notice the status line six lines up and infer the rest. The pre-existing `test_errors_mode_no_matches` pinned the old wording on a fixture whose status is `failed` — it was pinning the lie, and now pins the banner.

  **Anchoring moved onto the cause rather than widening the window.** More lines is not more signal, and this op exists so nobody has to read a raw trace. Instead a small set of always-on `_CAUSE_MARKERS` matches lines that *state a reason* — `Caused by`, an FQCN-shaped `…Exception: `/`…Error: ` message, `SQLSTATE[`, symfony's `In <File>.php line N:`, `Exit Code: N`, `Fatal error:`, `Segmentation fault/violation`, `Allowed memory size … exhausted`, `…CrashedException` — with context leaning *downward* (2 lines above, `error_context` below), because a cause is followed by its message body and preceded by whatever happened to run before it. A blind lookback above matched stack frames was considered and rejected: it adds lines in proportion to the surrounding noise without knowing whether any of them explain anything, whereas a marker is a positive assertion that this line is a reason. The cost is that an exception shape nobody has a marker for still needs one added — a one-line, testable change, and the unmatched banner catches the case where it matches nothing at all.

  **This interacts with `job_patterns` deliberately.** Cause markers are matched *in addition to* the selected pattern set, not merged into it, so a per-job entry can go on narrowing the noise it was written to narrow without being able to hide the reason — a table tuned for phpstan identifiers would otherwise suppress a segfault outright. `GL_JOB_CAUSE_MARKERS=0` turns them off, `GL_JOB_CAUSE_CONTEXT_BEFORE` and `GL_JOB_UNMATCHED_TAIL_LINES` tune the rest; `docs/presets/gitlab.md` documents the new unmatched-case contract, since anyone relying on the old wording is relying on a lie.

  Worth recording that **#444's own shape turned out to be already fixed.** Written faithfully — a PHPUnit `N) Class::method` listing with a nested `Caused by` above its frames — the reproduction passes on unmodified master, because [#404](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/404)'s block expansion already holds a touched PHPUnit failure whole. The shape that genuinely reproduces is the same cause-above-trace defect *outside* a PHPUnit block: a bare exception dump anchored only by the runner's trailing `ERROR: Job failed`, which is what the fixture now uses, and it is red against master. That is a narrower bug than #444 describes and it is the one that was there. Closes [#444](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/444) and [#445](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/445).

- **`gh-job:<id>:fail` had the same defect one preset over — it never got #444/#445's fix because both were filed from GitLab usage.** `presets/github/job.py` is a separate, duplicated implementation, and fixing `gl-job` did nothing for `gh-job`. Zero matches on a job GitHub calls `failure` now prints the same `## FAILED — no error pattern matched` banner — status, an explicit "a pattern is missing here, not a clean log", the patterns tried, and a 40-line tail — in both the default view and `:errors`/`:fail`. `## No error patterns matched` survives only for jobs that did not fail. The pre-existing `test_fail_mode_no_matches` had the same shape as `gl-job`'s pinned lie: its fixture's conclusion was `failure`, and it asserted the old wording.

  **The cause-markers half was not ported, and this is checked rather than assumed.** Fifteen real failed Actions job logs were pulled from this repo's own CI history (`gh run list --status failure`, all `pytest` legs) — every one of them already matches the default `error_patterns` (`FAILED`, `ERROR`, `##[error]`), because pytest's own `short test summary info` line puts the exception type and message on the same `FAILED test::name - SomeError: msg` line that already trips the flat pattern set; none of the 320-400 line logs pulled contained a bare `Traceback` at all, since this suite's pytest config doesn't dump multi-frame tracebacks on assertion failure. The PHP-shaped markers ported for `gl-job` (`SQLSTATE[`, `Fatal error:`, symfony's `In <File>.php line N:`) have no Python analogue and the general "cause precedes trace" shape they encode was not observed once in real material — GitHub's own `##[error]` annotation and pytest's inline exception message already do the job the markers exist to do on the GitLab side. Porting them here would be inventing coverage for a gap this repo's CI does not have. The banner (the actual safety net for a job that fails outside pytest's summary — a setup/timeout/OOM failure with no `FAILED`/`ERROR` text at all) is kept, since it is a defense against the unenumerable case regardless of platform. `docs/presets/github.md` documents the new unmatched-case contract. Closes [#453](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/453).

- **`TestDispatchEndToEnd` invoked a file that no fresh clone or worktree has.** `_supertool()` resolved to `<repo>/supertool`, which `.gitignore:18` excludes — it only exists on CI (a staged copy) and on the maintainer's machine (a `supertool -> supertool.py` symlink from an unrelated change). `git worktree add` does not materialize ignored files, and every agent working this repo works in a worktree, so those three tests were red for a reason unrelated to whatever anyone was actually changing — one contributor bypassed the pre-push hook over it, which is exactly the precedent that lets a real red get shipped past next time. Fixed by pointing `_supertool()` at `supertool.py` directly: it is tracked, so it is present everywhere, and the equivalence was verified rather than assumed — every invocation in this test class runs `[sys.executable, str(path)]`, never the exec bit or the pip console-script shim, so the copy CI staged was always a byte-identical duplicate of `supertool.py` running through the identical dispatch path. The now-redundant CI staging step (copy + chmod) is removed along with it. Verified green with the maintainer's `supertool` symlink moved aside and separately in a scratch `git worktree add` with no install step — both exercise real subprocess dispatch, nothing here is skipped. Closes [#438](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/438).
- **A notifier test waited for its marker file to *exist*, then read it before the write had landed.** `test_empty_placeholders_preserve_positional_argv` took the macOS/3.9 leg red on master with `json.decoder.JSONDecodeError: Expecting value: line 1 column 1 (char 0)` — a message that is the entire diagnosis, since that is precisely what `json.loads("")` raises. Not malformed JSON and not truncated JSON, which reports `Unterminated string starting at: line 1 column 2 (char 1)`: character zero of an empty string. The fixture writes the marker with `open(path, "w").write(...)`, and `open(..., "w")` creates and truncates in one syscall while the content arrives in a later one, so between them the file exists and holds nothing. `exists()` goes True at the `open()`, which means the poll was exiting at the *start* of the write instead of the end and the 2-second budget was never spent — raising the timeout would have fixed nothing. The window is normally sub-millisecond and only widens when the writing process is descheduled between the two syscalls, which needs more runnable processes than cores: three consecutive full runs at 14 cores are green, and it is a 3-4 core CI runner that hits it. **`-n auto` becoming the default in [#440](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/440) is what moved this from theoretical to observed** — the notifier child now competes with N-1 workers' subprocesses rather than having the box to itself — and as with [#437](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/437) the parallel switch exposed the fragility rather than introducing it. The fix is one shared `conftest.read_when_ready(path, parse)` that polls for content the reader can actually *use*, with the rule that waiting for existence is not waiting for content written into its docstring rather than into this file, since the next person to write a marker-and-poll test will be reading the helper and not the changelog. Two failures that `assert marker.exists()` could not distinguish are now reported apart, because they have different suspects: *never appeared* means the writer never ran, *appeared and never became parseable* means it ran and its write never landed. Three sibling sites moved onto it — the two `Path.write_text` markers had the same shape and failed as a baffling `assert "".startswith("edit ")` rather than a traceback. **Reader-side polling was chosen over making the fixtures write atomically**, and the reasoning is worth keeping: supertool spawns notifiers and never writes the marker itself, so an `os.replace` in the fixture would only prove that a well-behaved fixture is well-behaved, while every real consumer of a user-authored notifier that does `open().write()` would still read it mid-write. `_run_notifiers` is not implicated and already does the right thing for the one file it does own — `before_file` is closed before the `Popen` loop, so the child receives a complete path. The `time.sleep(0.2)` in the three debug-log tests looked like the same class and is not: `_notifier_log` writes in-process, before `_run_notifiers` returns, so those sleeps are dead weight and their removal makes the tests *stronger* by pinning that the dispatch line is on disk on return. The pin reproduces the failure deterministically with no dependence on CI load — a writer that holds the marker open and empty until the reader has provably started — and the shipped reader shape fails it with the CI traceback verbatim on a 14-core laptop. Closes [#443](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/443).

- **A test passed serially and failed under `-n 4` because the absolute path of `tmp_path` was part of what it measured.** `test_short_edit_keeps_its_verbatim_header` asserts that a *cheap* op header is echoed verbatim rather than compacted, and the whole decision is `len(arg) > _HEADER_ARG_MAX` — 160. The arg was built by interpolating the absolute fixture path, which on macOS is ~130 characters of `/private/var/folders/…/pytest-of-<user>/pytest-NNNN/`, so the test was passing at **exactly 160**: one byte of margin, on a threshold nothing in the test mentions, decided by the length of the username running it. xdist adds a `popen-gwN/` segment to give each worker its own tmp root, that segment is 10 characters, and the test went red — reproducibly, on unmodified master, with no diff to blame ([#437](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/437)). It cost real time during the review of [#436](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/436), where a red result in a 53-file change reads as "the change broke something" until you check out master and prove otherwise. The fix removes the dependency rather than the parallelism: the test `chdir`s into `tmp_path` and uses the relative `x.py`, so the arg is a fixed 27-char literal whose length is a property of the test instead of the machine. A precondition assertion in front of the dispatch makes the reintroduction loud — restoring the interpolation now fails with "it is 161 chars against a 160 threshold — it would be asserting the opposite of its name" instead of an opaque `startswith` mismatch. Worth naming that this was **not** the shape the issue predicted, and the prediction was the reasonable one: shared module-level state, a sibling test's leftovers, an env var. It was environment-shaped, not order-shaped, which is why the same test also fails serially for anyone whose home directory is one character longer than the author's — the parallel run did not introduce the fragility, it exposed it. Five consecutive full `-n auto` runs (~20,000 test executions) are green with no other failure, and the `test_git_status.py`/`test_edge_cases_git_commit.py` errors #433 recorded did not recur — those were the pre-#428 guard blaming a sibling worktree, and were already fixed. Closes [#437](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/437).

- **Every MR lifecycle transition was reported twice, once by each watch tier.** `gitlab-mr-feed` and the per-MR `gitlab-mr` pollers are two independent layers over one fact, so a single merge arrived as `merged` from the poller and `mr_merged` from the feed, ~28s apart under different event keys. The radar renders one line per event, and the cost is not the noise: duplicates train the reader to skim the board, which is the exact failure mode that makes a real red get missed, and each duplicate either burns a redundant `gl-mr:<iid>:status` confirmation round-trip (procedure is "never report red off an event alone") or gets silently dropped — and *silently dropped* is how a radar stops reporting without anyone noticing. Cost scaled with the open-MR count: N open MRs, N duplicated terminal transitions per merge wave. [#434](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/434) listed four possible shapes and declined to pick one; three of them are lossy or leave the defect. Splitting responsibilities (feed = discovery only) and feed auto-unwatch both make the feed's terminal events disappear, which is only safe if a per-MR poller is guaranteed to exist — and "feed with nothing behind it" is a supported configuration, so both trade a visible duplicate for a silent coverage hole. Documenting it leaves the skim-training in place. **So the feed now suppresses its own `mr_merged`/`mr_closed`, but only when a per-MR poller for that iid announces that transition itself** — with pollers, one line; without, the feed is still the sole and unsuppressed reporter. Both configurations stay correct by construction rather than by assumption.

  **The load-bearing part is that liveness at the moment of emission cannot answer "will anyone else report this", in either direction** — the obvious implementation of that rule is wrong, and wrong toward silence. Reporting `merged` is precisely what makes a per-MR poller terminal: it emits, `is_terminal` fires, the loop breaks, and the `finally` unlinks the PID file *and* clears the state file. So by the time the feed's 300s tick notices the iid has left the population, the reporter that produced the duplicate is already gone without a trace, and "reported and exited" is indistinguishable from "never existed" — conditioning on a live poller would have suppressed nothing at all in the observed case. The mirror is worse. A poller that *is* alive at that moment is precisely the one that has **not** spoken yet, and `_departure` was calling `stop_watcher` on it — SIGTERM then SIGKILL — before emitting, so suppressing there would kill the canonical reporter and turn the duplicate into no report at all. And a live poller can be permanently incapable of reporting the merge anyway: `only=pipeline_failed` is a healthy, running, watched poller that will never say a word about a terminal state, so its mere existence is not evidence of anything. Coverage is therefore **recorded per iid while the MR is still in the population**, from the `only` filter each poller now publishes into its own state file, and read back at departure from the entry the previous tick wrote. The terminal `stop_watcher` moved onto the branch where the feed does the reporting, which is the stale-PID cleanup it was always meant to be; a poller that still owes us an event is left alone to deliver it.

  Three calls worth naming. (1) **`only` had to become published state.** It lived solely in the poller process's memory, so no other tier could distinguish a poller that will announce a merge from one filtered away from saying so — a question that is now asked in a place where guessing wrong means a transition nobody reports. It is written once at loop start and survives the per-tick read-modify-write, and a test pins that: a filter that gets clobbered on tick two answers correctly exactly once. (2) **`None` and `[]` are different answers and must not resolve the same way.** `[]` is the recorded fact "this poller emits everything"; the key being absent means nobody could tell us, which includes the state file being unreadable, the poller predating this field, and a `known` entry written by a feed upgraded mid-session. (3) **Every unanswerable case resolves to *not covered*, so the feed reports.** This is the bias `presets/_proc.py` already encodes when an unanswerable liveness question resolves to *not alive*, carried through to its consequence here: the fallback is a duplicate — visible, cheap, and self-evidently a duplicate — never silence. The pins were written before the fix and the defect one fails as `assert ['mr_merged', 'mr_merged'] == ['mr_merged']` against the old code, but the pin that matters most is the one that passes both before and after: feed event, no poller covering that iid, event still reported. That is the whole difference between this fix and a silent regression, and the same shape guards a live-but-filtered poller, an unreadable filter, a pre-upgrade state file, and two MRs merging in one wave where only one is covered. Closes [#434](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/434).

- **Supertool could not start under a C locale, and presets still died printing their own success line on a Windows console.** [#418](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/418) collected four defects that came out of one seam over an afternoon; [#415](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/415) fixed the two that were blocking its own CI and left the rest deliberately. They were only ever found one at a time because nothing in this suite had run supertool as a **real subprocess under a non-UTF-8 environment** — every test calls `main()` in-process under pytest's UTF-8 capture, where all four are invisible by construction, and the suite was green through every one of them. So the detection came first, as its own commit: a static AST scan enumerating every text read/write in shipped code that leaves the codec to `locale.getpreferredencoding()`, plus five live pins that drive the real binary under `-X warn_default_encoding` with `EncodingWarning` promoted to an error ([#431](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/pull/431)'s technique, generalised), under `PYTHONIOENCODING=cp1252`, and under a C locale with PEP 538/540 coercion disabled so it stays ASCII. All seven fail against the pre-fix code on Linux and macOS — no Windows runner needed, which matters because every member of this family so far was found by a platform the author was not sitting on. The scan named **69** call sites, against the handful the issue listed: `.supertool.json` and every preset JSON, but also the validator cache, the notifier debug log, `op_diff`, `op_around_line`, the tree-sitter fallback extractor, and 38 preset scripts reading the markdown bodies and auth tokens users hand them. That gap between what we knew and what was there is the argument for building the enumerator before fixing the known list.
- **An unreadable config took the whole tool down instead of the ops that needed it.** `_load_config` and `_merge_presets` caught `JSONDecodeError` and `OSError`; `UnicodeDecodeError` is a `ValueError`, so it went straight past both as a startup traceback — for every op, including the ones that never touched the config. `presets/git.json` holds `—` and `✓`, so under a C locale the file that could not be decoded was one we ship. It is now caught and the file skipped, which is what the loader has always done with unusable state — it already walked past malformed JSON to the parent directory. But it did so in silence, and `_preset_warnings` had been collected since it was introduced and never printed by anyone, so a skipped preset meant the user's ops were simply absent with nothing on screen connecting that to a file. Both now report on stderr, once, before any op output. The except clauses name `UnicodeDecodeError` rather than widening to `Exception`: the same `try` covers `_merge_presets` and the MCP block, and a `TypeError` out of either is a bug in this file that must not be filed under "bad config".
- **`PYTHONIOENCODING=utf-8` is now set for every child process supertool spawns.** The reader was pinned to UTF-8 in #415; the writer was left to the console. A preset is a separate process and inherits none of supertool's stream reconfiguration, so on a cp1252 console it dies with `UnicodeEncodeError` while printing its own `✓` — the worst failure mode available to a tool that mutates state, because the work lands and the receipt says it crashed, which invites the operator to do it again. Setting it once at the top of `main()` covers all four spawn sites and every preset, rather than depending on each of ~50 scripts to remember a call; an explicit `VAR=… cmd` prefix on an op still overrides it. All twelve `presets/git/*.py` entry points additionally adopt `use_utf8_stdout()`, since those get run straight from a shell mid-conflict with no supertool in front of them, and `diff.py`'s inline copy from #308 collapses onto the shared helper — a duplicate is what lets two implementations drift, and it was the reason the other eleven looked like they had a licence not to bother. The non-git presets print more non-ASCII than the git ones do, not less (`gitlab/mrs.py` alone prints `·»Δ—…→⊘◌●✋✓✗`), which is why the fix for them is at the spawn point rather than 50 copies of a line.

- **The test suite's git-state guard blamed a sibling worktree's commit on whichever test was in teardown.** `_guard_repo_git_state` exists to catch a test that runs git against the real repository — the [#416](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/416) damage, where a leaked `GIT_DIR` put fixture commits on master and flipped `core.bare`. It fingerprinted `config`, `HEAD` and every ref under `refs/heads/`, and `refs/heads/` lives in the **common** git dir that every linked worktree shares. So an agent working in `st-wt/424` committing at 16:41 failed the suite running in `st-wt/422`, at the teardown of a test that had touched nothing — the `st-wt/NNN` convention exists precisely so several changes can be in flight, so this is normal usage, not abuse. A guard that reports a violation nobody committed is the defect class this repo has been clearing: the first few false positives teach you to skip its output, and it is the real ones you then miss. Worse here than usual, because the failure lands on an *innocent* test and the message therefore points away from the cause. The guard now asks "did **this test** mutate the repo", not "did the repo change". The fingerprint is a mapping rather than a hash — a hash can only answer the second question — and a detected change is *attributed* before it is reported. `config`, this worktree's `HEAD`, and the branch `HEAD` points at are what a test running git against the ambient repo moves, and what no sibling can touch, since a checked-out branch is exclusive to one worktree: any of them moving is this test's doing, siblings present or not, so the #319 tripwire keeps its teeth. Refs checked out in other worktrees, read from `git worktree list --porcelain`, are theirs by definition. Three calls worth naming. (1) **Anything left over is reported as `inconclusive`, not as a failure** — a stray branch or a rewritten `packed-refs` could be this test or could be a sibling's `git branch`/`gc`, and an honest "cannot tell, re-run with no other worktree busy" beats a confident wrong accusation; the *only* thing that buys silence is the presence of a sibling, so **in CI, and in any single clone, the guard is bit-for-bit as strict as before** — an unattributable change with no sibling to blame is still a failure. (2) **Attribution is on the slow path only.** The before/after mappings are compared first and `git worktree list` runs solely when they differ, so the ~7k per-test teardowns cost no subprocess; a per-test `worktree list` would have added minutes to the run to answer a question that is almost always "nothing changed". (3) **Narrowing the fingerprint to exclude sibling refs was rejected as the whole fix** — it needs the worktree list to be current, and `git worktree add -b` creates a worktree *and* its ref mid-run, so a list captured earlier would silently mis-attribute exactly the case that motivated the issue; classifying at teardown reads the list after the fact instead. The failure message now names the keys that moved (`refs/heads/master`, `config`) rather than the generic "config/HEAD/refs changed". Proven from both ends by three subprocess tests that run the real guard, from a real conftest copy, over a real repo with a real linked worktree: a sibling committing during a test leaves it green, a test committing into its own repo still fails, and an unowned branch beside a live sibling warns instead of accusing — the first two both go red against the pre-fix guard. **Identifying our own worktree by path is Windows-fragile**, so two things guard it. `_same_path` compares through `os.path.normcase`, because NTFS is case-insensitive and `C:\\Repo` versus `c:\\repo` would otherwise make this checkout a sibling of *itself* — flipping `has_siblings` true on a lone Windows clone and softening the unattributable bucket to a warning where it must still fail. And ownership in `_classify_git_state_change` is derived from the `HEAD` bytes and tested **before** the sibling set, so even a completely mis-parsed sibling list cannot excuse a commit on the checked-out branch; that ordering is the invariant, and a test pins it by passing our own branch inside `other_branches` and requiring `mutated` anyway. Both are pinned from any runner rather than left to a Windows leg: the CRLF case feeds `\\r\\n` porcelain directly (a `split("\\n")` would leave a trailing `\\r` on every branch name), and the case-folding case forces `ntpath.normcase` under `monkeypatch`. Closes [#428](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/428).
- **A read-only status query could terminate the process it was asked about.** `os.kill(pid, 0)` is the POSIX idiom for "does this PID exist" — the null signal is delivered to nothing and only the error tells you the answer. On Windows there is no signal delivery, and Python routes any signal other than `CTRL_C_EVENT`/`CTRL_BREAK_EVENT` to `TerminateProcess`, so the idiom does not ask whether a process is alive: **it kills it**. [#422](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/422) replaced the probe in `presets/watch/transport.py` with a read-only `OpenProcess(PROCESS_QUERY_LIMITED_INFORMATION)` + `GetExitCodeProcess`, but deliberately scoped itself to one file — and the idiom lived in **seven** call sites across six others (`gitlab/mrs.py`, `github/prs.py`, `mcp/status.py`, `mcp/daemon.py`, and twice in `mcp/stop.py`). `radar` reaches `mrs._watched_iids()` on every run. Reachability is bounded today — it needs a live PID in a `supertool-watch-*.pid` file on Windows, and `_spawn_poller` needs `os.fork` — but the guard rails are incidental rather than designed: the helper scans `/tmp` for *any* such file with no check that this tool wrote it, PIDs get recycled by the OS, and "we cannot spawn on Windows" is a limitation someone will reasonably lift one day without suspecting it arms a process killer inside a status command. The probe now lives in **one** place, `presets/_proc.py`, alongside the existing presets-root helpers `_board.py` and `_publish_safety.py`; `transport.py` became a consumer too rather than keeping the copy that would have started the next divergence. Three copies of the same six lines is what produced both this bug and the `WinError 87` escape #422 fixed — two copies had the `except OSError` and the third did not — so the shape of the fix matters as much as its content. Kept from #422: the probe asks for `PROCESS_QUERY_LIMITED_INFORMATION` rather than `PROCESS_ALL_ACCESS`, so it never holds a handle powerful enough to terminate what it inspects; the `kernel32` calls sit behind a seam whose test stub raises on any attribute other than the three read-only ones, so a probe that ever reaches for `TerminateProcess` fails loudly in tests rather than quietly in production; and an unanswerable question resolves to **not alive**, because a duplicate poller is visible and cheap while a phantom one is silent blindness. The regression is pinned by post-condition, not proxy — a test asserting only "returns False for a bad pid" passes on the destructive implementation, so the pin probes a **real live subprocess** under simulated Windows semantics and asserts it is still running afterwards (it fails with `returncode -9` on the old code). A further test walks the preset tree with `ast` and fails on any `os.kill(x, 0)` outside `_proc.py`, so a future copy is a red test rather than a silent reappearance. Closes [#429](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/429).

- **`radar` was hardcoded to `author=@me`, so the MRs least likely to have a human watching them were the ones it could not see.** `radar.py` built its query inline as `{"author": "@me", "state": "opened"}`: `main(argv)` ignored `argv` entirely, and the filter bypassed `defaults.py` — the module whose stated purpose is that `radar` and `watch-mine.sh` "cannot drift apart". Editing `DEFAULT_FEED` to follow a second author moved the shell supervisor and silently did not move `radar`, so the anti-drift mechanism had a hole in precisely the field a user would want to change. The motivating case is an automated agent opening Draft MRs under `modular.system`: bulk changes that appear with nobody sitting at a terminal, and unlike stale discovery, re-running does not help — another author's MRs are permanently out of scope, not stale. `radar` now takes the `gl-mrs` filter vocabulary (`radar:author=modular.system`, `radar:reviewer=@me`), inherited by reusing that parser rather than growing a second one, so `gl-mrs:X` and `radar:X` describe the same population by construction. **The load-bearing part is that one filter drives all three views.** Filtering only the watcher fleet while `live_open_mrs()` kept its hardcoded author would satisfy the feature request and still be broken — that is already reachable today by spawning watchers directly through `watch-mine.sh`, and it produces a session receiving `pipeline_failed` for an MR the board does not list, while the board still renders as a complete picture of the world. Same failure class the module docstring names for a different cause: silent incompleteness, arriving through another door. So `live_open_mrs()`, `heal()` and the feed scope all read one resolved filter, and the default comes from a new `defaults.DEFAULT_FILTER` that `DEFAULT_FEED` is now built from, closing the drift hole rather than papering over it. Three calls worth naming: (1) **Multi-author is a repeated key, not a plural `authors=`.** GitLab takes one `author_username`, so several authors must fan out to N `glab` calls unioned by iid; `author=@me,author=modular.system` reuses the existing comma tokenizer as-is, whereas `authors=a,b` would need a second value separator inside a syntax whose separator is already the comma. `_parse_multi` is now the single tokenizer and `_parse_args` is its scalar view (last value wins), so both readings come from one place and a single-valued filter produces byte-identical argv to before. One failing query in a fan-out fails the whole board: a partial union is a board quietly missing rows, which is the exact thing `RadarError` exists to prevent. (2) **The snapshot is now keyed by a hash of the normalised filter.** It was one fixed path — safe only while exactly one population could exist. The moment `radar` takes args, `radar` and `radar:author=modular.system` compare against each other's snapshot: every MR in one reads as new, every MR in the other as gone, and the delta column actively lies. The key ignores key and value order (the same population, written two ways, is one snapshot) but not identity. (3) **The feed poller was retrofitted to the same plural filter.** #422's `gitlab-mr-feed` already accepted a literal `gl-mrs` filter as its scope, so the board's filter reaches the discovery tier unchanged and only `fetch_population` needed the fan-out; a filter that expands to one of the feed's own aliases resolves back to the alias, because the scope is a PID filename and `@me` versus its expansion would otherwise be two pollers over one population, i.e. two copies of every `mr_opened`. A non-default board now prints its filter in the footer, since two radars in one window otherwise render as one board printed twice. Closes [#425](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/425).

- **Discovery ran once, so a radar session went stale the moment a new MR appeared.** `radar` conflated two tiers: *reconcile*, which runs once when a human types it, and *watch*, which runs continuously but polls **one known iid**. Nothing in the running system could therefore discover an MR that did not exist at spawn time, which makes an idle radar session structurally blind to every new MR until someone re-runs the command. Observed in the watcher start times: !33176 opened between a 16:38 radar run and a 16:44 one, and only the second found it. In between, the board was not visibly wrong — it was confidently complete and missing an MR, which renders identically to all-green. That indistinguishability is the whole failure class this subsystem exists to remove. The fix is a new first-class watch source, `gitlab-mr-feed`, fitting the existing `sources/<name>/{events.json,poller.py}` contract rather than adding a subsystem: its state is the population returned by a `gl-mrs` filter, a new iid spawns `watch:gitlab-mr:<iid>` and emits `mr_opened`, and a vanished iid is reported as what actually happened to it. `radar` now reconciles once **and** ensures exactly one feed poller is alive. The ID is a *scope* — `@me` and `@reviewer` alias `gl-mrs` filters, anything else is a literal filter — so a team radar can cover review requests, which `author=@me` never returns. Interval is 300s (MRs appear on human timescales; the per-MR pollers already carry the 30s traffic) and each poll is one `glab mr list` with **no** pipeline enrichment, because the feed's job is "which MRs exist" and the per-MR watcher's is "what is happening to this one". Four calls decided explicitly: (1) **`is_terminal` is never** — a population has no final state, and a feed that stopped itself would restore the blindness silently, so it ends only on `unwatch`, SIGTERM or the machine going away; idempotence is enforced by short-circuiting on a live PID, since N radar runs stacking N feeds means N copies of every `mr_opened`. (2) **`mr_opened` is on by default** via a separate `DEFAULT_FEED_ONLY`, and deliberately *not* added to `DEFAULT_ONLY` — that filter belongs to the `gitlab-mr` source, which cannot emit `mr_opened`, and listing an event beside a source that never emits it is simply untrue. (3) **Vanished is not merged.** An iid leaving `author=@me,state=opened` could have merged, closed, been reassigned, or the filter could have changed; guessing `mr_merged` is right most of the time and confidently wrong the rest, so the feed spends one `glab api` lookup on the truth (departures are rare, so the call is too). Only a confirmed terminal state stops the watcher, and `mr_merged`/`mr_closed` are emitted without a desktop notification because the per-MR watcher reaches the same conclusion within 30s and owns that ping. A still-open MR gets `mr_left_feed` carrying the real state and **keeps** its watcher — following an MR the feed no longer returns is legitimate, the same distinction `radar`'s prune already makes. (4) **A dead feed is reported, not silently absent** — the footer always carries `feed ok` / `feed respawned` / `feed DOWN`, and a down feed (or one alive but erroring every tick, which `watches` renders as perfectly healthy) prints an explicit `WARNING` line that survives delta suppression. A first poll records its baseline without emitting, because announcing every MR that was already open is a notification storm rather than a discovery, and a failed fetch returns no events and keeps the known set, because an unreachable GitLab must never read as "everything vanished". Two supporting fixes: the dispatcher now clears `last_error` after a successful poll so a reported error is current rather than a scar, and `SOURCE`/`ID` reject `/` alongside `__` now that a feed id can be a filter string interpolated into a `/tmp` path. Closes [#422](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/422).

- **`watch-mine.sh` watched only MRs that were *already* failing, so it could never see one go red.** The feed default was `gl-mrs:author=@me,failed,iids`, which means an MR that is green or running when watchers are spawned is never watched at all — the exact event the tool exists to catch is the one it is structurally blind to. Observed: !33172 was `running` when watchers were spawned and went green 30 minutes later, never appearing in `watches` and never firing an event. The default is now `state=opened` — watch everything open and let `only=` filter at the event level, which is where filtering belongs. Cost is one poller per open MR (~7 for an active dev), each a 30s `glab api` poll. The `only=` default had the mirror-image problem: `pipeline_failed,merged` means you only ever hear bad news, so the loop red → fix → push → *?* never closes and the user goes back to polling by hand to learn the fix worked — the behaviour being eliminated. `pipeline_succeeded` was already emitted by the poller and merely filtered out at the default; it is also the only signal that an automated fix actually worked. The default is now `pipeline_failed,pipeline_succeeded,merged,closed,conflicts_appeared`: `closed` because it is terminal and stops the poller, `conflicts_appeared` because it is actionable and otherwise entirely silent (observed live on !19509). `pipeline_running` stays out (you just pushed; it carries no information) and `comment_added` stays out because GitLab's `user_notes_count` counts *system* notes, so every pipeline transition would fire twice. Both defaults moved into `presets/watch/defaults.py` and are read from there by the shell script, because `radar` must heal with the identical filter and two literals in two languages drift silently — with a symptom of a watcher that exists but never notifies. Part of [#417](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/417).

- **A watcher that reached a terminal state left its state file behind forever.** `watches` sweeps stale *PID* files but never touched `/tmp/supertool-watch-{source}__{id}.state.json`, so a poller that exited correctly on `merged` still left a file on disk reading `mr_state: "merged"` — observed for !33136 and !33137 a full day after they merged. Any consumer that globs the state files therefore reports long-merged MRs as live watches, which is precisely what the new `radar` op does. The file is now deleted by the dispatcher when the poll loop breaks on `is_terminal`, alongside the PID file it already removed: the poller is gone, so the file is not a record of anything live. Retention was considered and rejected — keeping it would push the filtering onto every present and future consumer, and a consumer that forgets is silently wrong rather than loudly broken. `radar` prunes terminal files independently as part of its reconcile, since the dispatcher's cleanup only covers the case where the process lived long enough to run it; a poller killed by a reboot leaves its file behind whatever the happy path does. Files belonging to a *live* poller are never pruned by either path — the owner clears its own. Part of [#417](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/417).

- **`paths = [...]` in an `@payload` did not parse on Python 3.9 or 3.10.** `_mini_toml_loads` — the fallback that runs wherever stdlib `tomllib` is absent — was written with "no inline arrays" as a deliberate limit, which was true right up until [#340](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/340) gave payload fields a *variadic* form. A variadic field is written as a list, so `git-commit:@-` with `paths = ["src/Foo", "src/Bar"]` — the shape the op's own description documents — died with `ERROR: @file TOML parse error: unknown value type for 'paths'` on a third of the supported matrix (`requires-python = ">=3.9"`). It parsed fine on 3.11+, so nothing local caught it and the documentation described a form that did not work. The value parser is now one `_toml_parse_value` function that inline arrays recurse into, so elements get every scalar type the parser already had and arrays nest; whitespace, newlines, comments between elements and a trailing comma are all accepted, per TOML. A malformed array raises rather than returning a short list — a silently truncated `paths` would commit the wrong set of files, which is worse than the parse error it replaces. The new cases call `_mini_toml_loads` directly rather than going through a payload, so they run on **every** interpreter: routed through the `@file` loader they would only execute on 3.9/3.10, and the 3.11+ jobs that stayed green throughout would keep proving nothing.
- **`git-commit` crashed on a non-UTF-8 console, reporting a crash for a commit it had already made.** `supertool.py` reconfigures its own stdout to UTF-8, but every preset runs as a *separate* process and inherits none of that. `commit.py` prints `✓`/`✗` in its receipt, so on a Windows cp1252 console the first success line raised `UnicodeEncodeError: 'charmap' codec can't encode character '✓'` — after the commit had landed. That is the bad failure mode: the work is done, the receipt says traceback, and the obvious next move is to redo something that already happened. No existing test could see it, because they call `commit.main()` in-process where pytest's capture is already UTF-8; it surfaced only once the op was driven as a real subprocess. `diff.py` had carried this guard inline since [#308](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/308) — it now lives in `_git_common.use_utf8_stdout()` and `commit.py` calls it, so the remaining git presets printing the same glyphs (`push.py`, `resolve.py`, `checkout.py`, `merge.py`) have one call to adopt rather than a copy to paste. The regression pin forces `PYTHONIOENCODING=cp1252`, which reproduces the Windows failure on any OS.
- **An op's receipt then rendered as mojibake on that same console.** The other half of the same journey, and only visible once the crash above was fixed: `commit.py` correctly wrote `✓` as UTF-8, and supertool read it back through `subprocess.run(..., text=True)` with no explicit encoding — which decodes using `locale.getpreferredencoding()`. On a cp1252 console the three UTF-8 bytes `E2 9C 93` came back as the three characters `âœ“`, so `HEAD after: bc63422 âœ“` was the receipt for a commit that had worked perfectly. Nothing about this is specific to `git-commit`: it is the one call every preset and custom op returns through, so any op printing a glyph was affected. The decode is pinned to UTF-8 with `errors="replace"`, so a command emitting genuinely undecodable bytes degrades to a replacement character rather than taking the run down with it. The regression pin deliberately does not use the git preset — it declares a one-line custom op in an ASCII-only config and forces a C locale, which turns the mis-decode into a hard error and so goes red on Linux and macOS instead of relying on the Windows jobs to notice.

- **A validator that declined to analyse a path reported it as one new error.** Every edit under a DVSI `tests/` directory came back `phpstan-mcp : 0 → 1 (+1) ✗` with `phpstan.exit analyse: path is outside the configured --paths allowlist.` — which is not a finding about the file, it is phpstan declining to look at it. `format_response` in the phpstan-mcp adapter had one fallback for a non-zero exit with no findings, and it manufactured a single synthetic error out of whatever the daemon said, refusals included. The rendering is the smaller half of the harm. `ok` has two values and the world has three — clean, broken, and never looked at — so folding a refusal into "broken" makes an unmeasured file indistinguishable from a measured one that is genuinely wrong, and the delta arithmetic then inherits a `+1` that no edit caused. For a validator carrying `rollback_on_fail`, that phantom `+1` is enough to revert a good edit on the strength of a scope-config mismatch, and once the counts are known to lie the rollback signal stops being trusted at all — which is the mechanism that catches real breakage. `skipped` is now a first-class third state end to end: the adapter recognises phpstan's own refusal messages (`--paths` allowlist, "no files found to analyse"; extend with `PHPSTAN_MCP_SKIP_PATTERNS`) and emits a clean-shaped result carrying a `skipped` reason, the row renders as `skipped (path outside --paths allowlist)` instead of a red delta, the result is excluded from the cache because a skip is decided by config while the cache key is a content hash, and it can never roll back an edit. `phpmd-mcp` turns its daemon's error string into one error through the same shape and gets the same treatment; `rector-mcp` and `phpunit-mcp` discard the daemon's message and synthesise their own, so there is nothing there to classify but a string this repo writes itself. Unrecognised non-zero exits still report as errors — a refusal we cannot explain is not a refusal we should swallow. Separately and in the same code path: the rollback decision was made by scanning *rendered* rows for a `✗` on a line starting with the validator's name, so `phpstan` was rolled back whenever its neighbour `phpstan-mcp` went red, and no amount of correct skip-reporting could have been read reliably by a string match anyway. Both the marker and the revert now read from one `_validator_regressed` predicate over the result dicts, so the red the caller sees and the revert it triggers cannot disagree. Closes [#406](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/406).

- **`gl-mr:NUM:status` omitted the source branch, so the cheap mode couldn't answer the cheapest question.** Getting one string — the branch of MR !33161, to check it out and apply a fix — meant escalating to `:full`, which printed the description, the metadata, and every one of the MR's 200 changed files. That is the compact mode failing at the one thing it exists for: `:status` is the call you make so you *don't* pay for `:full`, and a field that is identity rather than detail belongs in it. The irony was that `:full` opened with `You are on: X ⚠ MISMATCH — switch with: ./supertool 'git-checkout:<branch>'`, so the tool already knew the branch and already treated it as the actionable next step; `:status` just didn't print it. `source_branch`/`target_branch` were already in the `glab mr view --output json` payload slim mode parses, so this costs **no extra API call** — one line, ~45 bytes, and the escalation disappears. Both branches earn the line: the source is what you check out, and the target is what tells a `release/v19.0.x` MR apart from a `master` one, which changes what you rebase onto. Nothing else was added — every field in a compact mode is paid for by every caller on every call, so the tests pin what `:status` must still omit (`## Files`, `Labels:`, `## Description`) alongside the branch value it must now contain. `gh-pr:NUM:status` had the identical gap with `headRefName`/`baseRefName` already in hand, and got the identical line. Closes [#405](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/405).

- **`gl-job:ID:fail` no longer guts a PHPUnit failure and hides that it did.** The failure view keeps every line matching an `error_patterns` entry plus `error_context` lines around it, which is the right shape for a stack trace — the middle frames are noise — and exactly the wrong shape for an assertion failure, where the middle *is* the evidence and the head and tail are boilerplate. On a real DVSI job the only matches inside a 33-line block were `Failed asserting` at its top and `FAILURES!` below its bottom, so the two ±8 windows never met: the op returned the opening `<tr …>` and the closing `' [ASCII](length: 2170) does not contain "5</"` with a bare `...` between them. The dropped middle held `<td class="num-days"><span>2.5</span></td>` — the whole diagnosis, since `2.5</span>` is what matched the needle `"5</"` — and the output did not look truncated, so the failure read as a mystery until a second `gl-job:ID:raw:14015:14047` call went and fetched it. A view whose job is to carry evidence must not decide the evidence is context. A PHPUnit failure is now an atomic unit: `_phpunit_blocks` bounds it from its `N) Class::method` header to the last non-blank line before the next header or the run summary (the trailing `/path/File.php:LINE` frames), and a match landing anywhere inside pulls in the whole block. The region is bounded by construction, so the budget can be generous — `GL_JOB_PHPUNIT_BLOCK_MAX_LINES`, default 500 — and a block past it keeps its head and tail rather than being dropped whole. Expansion is a union with what the pattern windows already selected, so no call returns less than it did before. Per-block generosity needs an aggregate ceiling, though, because the moment `:fail` earns its keep is the one broken shared component that failed thirty tests at once — twenty expanded blocks would be ten thousand lines where the op exists to return a small readable payload. `GL_JOB_PHPUNIT_TOTAL_MAX_LINES` (default 2000: four maximal blocks, or every failure of a realistic 30-to-80-line suite breakage, and well under the 5000 lines `raw` already allows for a deliberate "give me the log" call) budgets expansion across the whole trace, and past it whole failures are dropped in file order rather than gutted — which is the invariant the issue is actually about, since a gutted failure is a mystery and a missing one is a known unknown. A dropped failure still falls back to its ordinary pattern window, so union-only holds there too, and the view ends with `... (3 of 21 PHPUnit failures not shown in full — raise GL_JOB_PHPUNIT_TOTAL_MAX_LINES=N)`. Separately, the gap marker stopped being a shrug: `...` now reads `... (44 lines elided)`, and a run that drops nothing prints no marker at all, where the old code emitted a leading `...` even when it started at line 1. The silent part was the real defect — a truncated block is recoverable, not knowing it was truncated is not. Closes [#404](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/404).

- **A `git-push` that timed out reported failure for a push that had already landed.** A repo whose pre-push hook runs static analysis over every changed file took a 36-file push past the op's 120s cap, and the receipt was a bare `FAIL (timeout 120.0s > 120s)` — while the remote ref had in fact been updated. Acting on that verdict means re-pushing, and the second push came back `remote: error: cannot lock ref …: is at b7826b93 but expected a32c1323`, which reads like a concurrent-write conflict rather than a duplicate of your own successful push; `git fetch` then showed local and remote at the same sha all along. A clock expiring is not evidence of failure for an op that mutates remote state, and push is the one class of error where a wrong retry is expensive. Two things made it unrecoverable. `push.py` budgeted its `git push` subprocess at 120s while the op's own cap was *also* 120s, so supertool's outer cap always fired first and killed the script before its verification logic could run — the inner budget was unreachable by construction. And the outer timeout handler discarded the killed command's stdout, so the receipt lines the script had already printed never reached the caller. The push budget is now `_PUSH_TIMEOUT = 300` against an op cap of 420, so the script owns its own timeout, and on expiry it asks `ls-remote` what actually landed: a remote ref matching the (possibly hook-rewritten) local HEAD reports `Status: pushed ✓ (push timed out locally; remote ref matches HEAD)` and exits 0, which is the same "trust the remote sha over the exit code" rule the hook-amend path already used for [#297](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/297). A remote that genuinely did not move is reported as `PUSH TIMED OUT ✗` with both shas and an explicit *fetch before retrying, do not force-push on a timeout alone* — unverified, not rejected, because the push may still be in flight server-side. The `ls-remote` probe itself is now guarded against timing out, so the verification cannot become the crash it exists to prevent. Independently, `_resolve_custom_op` appends whatever the killed command printed under `--- partial output before timeout ---`, so any timing-out op keeps its evidence instead of returning a verdict with nothing behind it. Closes [#399](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/399).

- **A post-edit lint that times out no longer reads as a file that linted clean.** `_vim_render_lint` ran its linter with a hardcoded `timeout=5` and caught `TimeoutExpired` alongside the missing-binary cases, returning `""` for all of them — and `""` is also what a clean file returns. The two are opposite outcomes sharing one representation: "the file was checked and is fine" and "the file was never checked". It surfaced as a single red `pytest (windows-latest, 3.9)` job asserting `POST-EDIT LINT FAILED` in a receipt that carried nothing, while the same commit passed 3.10/3.11/3.12 and every other platform. `xmllint` availability is OS-level, not Python-version-level, so a genuine regression would have had to take all four Windows jobs down; one job meant a slow process spawn — antivirus scanning a freshly written temp file is the usual suspect there — crossing 5s. The cost was not the flake but its silence: it presents as "the lint said nothing", which reads as a code defect, and it took a diagnostic pass to establish that a merged commit was *not* at fault. A timeout now returns its own marker, `--- POST-EDIT LINT TIMED OUT — <tool> (<N>s) ---`, saying explicitly that the file was not checked; `FileNotFoundError`/`OSError` keep returning `""`, because there genuinely is no lint to report. The budget is configurable with `SUPERTOOL_LINT_TIMEOUT=<seconds>` so a slow runner is given room without a code change, falling back to 5s on a value that is unparseable or not positive. Silence now means clean, and only clean. Closes [#396](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/396).

- **The autouse test fixture resets every module-level cache, not the two someone remembered.** `_disable_rtk_and_config` restored RTK, config, tree-sitter and ctags state between tests, but `_REPO_ROOT_WALK_CACHE` (the repo-root walk memo behind the [#393](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/393) formatter gate) and `_MUTATION_ATTEMPTS` (the counter behind the `[branch: X]` footer) were module-level and survived across tests in the same process. The walk cache is keyed by directory and `tmp_path` is unique per test, so it mostly got away with it — and the six tests that did care had each written `supertool._REPO_ROOT_WALK_CACHE.clear()` by hand, a workaround repeated six times being the fixture saying where it should have lived. The failure this sets up is the expensive kind: a test that passes alone and fails in suite order with nothing in its body to suggest why. Rather than add two names to a hand-maintained list — every future cache is another chance to forget, and the forgetting is silent — the reset is now driven by an explicit `conftest.RESET_GLOBALS` tuple restored in place from an import-time deep copy (in place, because supertool holds direct references to these objects), with a companion test that enumerates supertool's module-level dicts, lists and sets and fails when one appears in neither `RESET_GLOBALS` nor `RESET_EXEMPT_GLOBALS`. Nine further caches joined the reset; the exemptions are documented with their reasons, and writing that list is what found `_AT_FILE_REGISTRY` — built once behind an `_AT_FILE_REGISTRY_BUILT` guard and *rebound* rather than mutated, so emptying it in place is permanent and every `@file` route afterwards fails with `ERROR: empty path`. The six manual `.clear()` calls are gone. Closes [#397](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/397).

- **A custom op's argument no longer expands placeholders found inside itself.** Substitution ran as a chain of `str.replace` calls, so each pass rescanned the text the previous pass had just inserted. `git-commit`'s template ends in `{args}`, which means a commit message is inserted — correctly `shlex.quote`d — one line *before* `{argjoin}` is substituted; a message that happened to mention `{argjoin}` therefore had that token expanded inside its own quoted value, splicing quote characters into the middle of the string. `shlex.split` then shattered the message into separate argv entries and `git add` received the subject's third word as a pathspec (`fatal: pathspec 'custom-op' did not match any files`). The trigger was writing documentation *about* the placeholders — the one message guaranteed to contain them. Substitution is now a single regex pass (`_substitute_placeholders`), so inserted text is never rescanned and a value reaches the receiving script byte-for-byte as one argv entry; unknown names such as `{path}` are left untouched, since preset templates have already had theirs resolved by `_resolve_preset_cmd` at config-load time. `_resolve_alias` had the same chained shape and got the same treatment. Beyond the formatting damage this was argv injection — content flowing through a write op (`git-commit`, `mr`) could append arguments to the command being run. Closes [#377](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/377).

- **Every remaining placeholder site substitutes in one pass, so ordering stops being load-bearing.** [#377](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/377) fixed `_resolve_custom_op` and `_resolve_alias`; the other five chained-`str.replace` sites were left alone because each happened to put its caller-controlled value last, which made them safe *by accident of ordering* — a property nothing enforced, so any future reorder silently reintroduced argv injection. `_run_notifiers` was already past that line: `{file}` was substituted third, and the four `.replace` calls after it rescanned the path just inserted, so a file literally named `{line}.txt` had that token expanded inside its own `shlex.quote`d value and `shlex.split` then shattered one path into several argv entries. The notifier subprocess discards stdout and stderr, so the corrupted invocation failed silently — nothing would ever have reported it. The advice-message builder had a second variant of the same bug: `{path}` was interpolated before `"{target}" in message` was tested, so a path containing the literal text `{target}` picked the interpolate branch over the append branch and the resolver's target was spliced into the middle of the path (`touched src/{target}.py` → `touched src/src/other.py.py`). The `{target}` test now reads the template rather than the substituted text. `_validator_resolve`, `_validator_run_one`, `_formatter_run_one` and `_advice_resolve` are converted too — not because they were reachable today, but so correctness no longer depends on the order somebody writes the dict in. Closes [#379](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/379).

- **`bash-check : ok (unchanged)` said the opposite of what it meant.** The column reports the delta in the *validator's own result*, but "(unchanged)" reads as "the file is unchanged" — the one thing that definitely is not true just after a mutating op. It now reads `(no new errors)`, matching the sibling `(pre-existing — not from this edit)` it sits next to. Part of [#380](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/380).
- **A batch nested inside a batch reported no branch at all.** The `[branch: X]` footer is emitted once per call: sub-ops are suppressed by dispatch depth, and the batch itself carries the single line when one of its sub-ops mutated something. That test was a flag set in the batch loop when the sub-op appeared in `_OP_TARGETS` — and `"batch"` is not in `_OP_TARGETS`, so an outer batch whose only sub-op was an inner batch stayed `False` while the inner one was silenced by depth. An edit landed and **zero** footers printed, which is the wrong-branch miss [#381](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/381) exists to close, in the one shape where nothing warns. The signal moved to the mutating-op chokepoint: the footer now fires when `_MUTATION_ATTEMPTS` grew during the call, a counter bumped in `_run_with_validators` before the op runs, which every mutating op passes through however deeply it is nested. Nesting depth no longer decides whether a write gets reported. Attempts, not landed bytes: review caught a first cut keyed on the *write* counter, which restored the zero-footer bug for a batch whose sub-op failed its anchor or was rolled back by a regressing validator — the two moments a wrong-branch hypothesis is most worth having, and both leave the write count exactly where it started. Closes [#392](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/392).

- **Formatters no longer rewrite files in repos that never format.** A one-line `edit` to a `CHANGELOG.md` in a repo with no prettier config — no `.prettierrc`, no `prettier` in any manifest, no prettier in its CI — came back with 126 lines reformatted across two files, because the policy came from the shell's cwd rather than from the repo holding the file. The diff is the deliverable, and a 2-line change arriving as 128 changed lines is unreviewable; where the markdown tables are hand-aligned it is also just wrong. A formatter now runs only where the repo shows evidence it wants one: the tool's own config file (searched from the *edited file's* directory up to **its** repo root, so a parent repo's config never opts a nested one in), a manifest naming the tool, or an `env` entry in the spec carrying the rules — DVSI runs `phpcbf` with `PHPCBF_STANDARD=PSR12` and no `phpcs.xml`, and that is a legitimate opt-in. A tool supertool has no marker table entry for is never gated: absence of knowledge is not evidence of opt-out. `"requires_config": false` forces always-run, a list of globs names custom markers, and `SUPERTOOL_FORMAT_WITHOUT_CONFIG=1` restores the old behaviour wholesale. The gate covers the post-edit hook and `format_staged` — a sweep over files nobody named, usually from a pre-commit hook — but deliberately not `format:PATH`, where the caller named the file and declining would be the wrong answer. Symlinks are resolved before the walk, for the same reason `_atomic_write` resolves them: a file reached through a linked directory has its real repo elsewhere, and walking the link's own location climbs past the config that governs it. Closes [#393](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/393).
  - **Upgrade note.** This changes behaviour for anyone running a formatter on the tool's own defaults with no config file in the repo — `prettier`, `black` and `rustfmt` on a bare project are the realistic cases. Those files are now validated and left alone, and the receipt says `[formatters] skipped: <name>` rather than skipping silently. Add the tool's config file (which the tool itself would read anyway), or set `"requires_config": false` on that spec to keep the old behaviour for it. A formatter whose spec carries its own rules through `env` — `PHPCBF_STANDARD`, `PHPCSFIXER_CONFIG` and friends — is unaffected, and so is any tool supertool has no marker table entry for.

- **A TOML payload whose content contains `'''` now says so.** The `@-` / `@file` route uses `'''literal'''` blocks precisely so code goes in unescaped, but content that itself holds `'''` — Python source that inspects Python source, which is any test reasoning about docstrings — closes the block early. The failure was `Expected newline or end of document after a statement (at line 9, column 53)`, pointing at where the block closed rather than at what closed it, and the documented advice ("preserves backslashes/quotes/newlines") reads as though nothing ever needs escaping. There are two ways out and both already worked; neither was findable from the error. It now names them: a `"""basic"""` block (escapes apply, so backslashes double) or the JSON payload form, which needs no delimiter at all. The hint fires on an odd number of `'''` runs — every literal block opens and closes, so a stray one means the content carried its own — and stays silent otherwise, where a delimiter lecture would be noise on an ordinary TOML error. The `"""` route is covered by the `<3.11` fallback parser too, so the advice holds on the platforms without stdlib `tomllib`. Closes [#394](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/394).

- **`path not found` now names the op that fixes it.** Working in a second checkout, every call needs an absolute path or it fails; `cwd:PATH` solves that, but the error said nothing about it, so it stayed undiscovered through a long session. The message ends with `Prefix the call with cwd:PATH to run it from elsewhere.` Part of [#380](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/380).

### Added

- **`radar` — a reconcile that makes "nothing is wrong" distinguishable from "nothing is watching".** `watches` reports which pollers are alive; it cannot report what is true, and the two diverge routinely. Observed live on !33173: `last_event: pipeline_failed on pipeline 154177` against `source_state: running on pipeline 154180` — a board built on the event calls that MR broken while it is mid-retry on a newer pipeline. **`source_state` is truth; `last_event` is history**, and the divergence is surfaced as an explicit `[drift: 154177→154180]` mark rather than silently resolved in favour of one of them. The deeper problem is that events cannot survive a session boundary at all: the transport is fire-and-forget, so an event emitted while no channel consumer is bound is gone permanently; pollers are processes and die with the machine or a cleared `/tmp`; and pollers stop themselves on terminal state by design. At the start of a session an event-driven view therefore knows *nothing* — and "knows nothing" renders identically to "everything is green", which is the failure that makes a monitoring tool untrustworthy, because it is quiet either way and the user cannot tell which. So `radar` is an idempotent reconcile rather than a printer, safe to run on every session start: **one** `gl-mrs` query for open MRs is the authoritative input (state files are cache and may be absent or hours stale, so they never decide what is open or what is red), terminal state files are pruned, drift is flagged, and — the step that makes coverage survivable — a watcher is respawned for every open MR that has no live poller, covering reboot, crash, a cleared `/tmp`, and MRs that were green when watchers were last spawned, in one action. Rows render through the same `_row` helper as `gl-mrs` so there is one definition of the board format, with `[healed]` and `[unwatched]` marks appended for the two signals only radar can report. Output is the full board on cold start and delta-only afterwards, where *nothing moved* means the set of open MRs is unchanged, no MR changed pipeline status, pipeline id, draft or conflict flag, and radar itself pruned, healed and drifted nothing. Two deliberate exceptions to delta suppression: a standing failure or conflict is re-printed even when unchanged, because an unfixed red is a current fact rather than history; and a run where nothing moved still prints one summary line (`radar: no change | 7 open | 7 watched`) instead of nothing, because total silence is indistinguishable from a radar that failed to run — the exact defect the op exists to remove. For the same reason an unreachable or unauthenticated GitLab is a hard error with no board printed and nothing pruned or healed, never an empty green board. Part of [#417](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/417).

- End-to-end tests `test_git_commit_payload_route` (8 cases) pinning the bytes `git-commit` actually commits, on both of its routes. [#400](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/400) reported the payload route as missing and multi-line messages as mangled; the route has existed since [#340](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/340), and the mangling came from `"...$(printf '\n\n')..."` — command substitution strips *all* trailing newlines, so bash handed the op a single-line string and the op committed exactly what it was given. What was genuinely missing is coverage: the existing `@file` tests stub `_at_file_specs`, so a regression in the syntax-derived field registry, in preset argv quoting, or in `commit.py`'s message handling would leave them green while no correct message ever reached git. The new cases run `supertool.py` as a subprocess against a throwaway repo and assert `git log -1 --pretty=format:%B` byte-for-byte — colon in the subject, blank line, multi-line body, and the appended `Co-Authored-By:` trailer — across `@-` stdin, `@file` TOML, `@file` JSON, a payload that already carries its own trailer (not duplicated), and the variadic `paths` list. Three more pin the colon CLI unchanged, including a genuine newline surviving in the argument, and one reproduces the issue's shell mangling to show where the newlines are lost. Verified as a regression pin, not decoration: run against `#340^`, the five payload cases fail with the issue's own `missing required field 'msg['` while all three colon-route cases pass.
- **A content-heavy mutating op no longer echoes its arguments twice.** Every mutating op prints its full arguments in the section header and then the diff underneath, so a 20-line `edit` put its old and new strings on screen twice — the header first, before the diff that actually shows the change. On a 6-op `batch` carrying code blocks, the headers alone were a large fraction of the response, and the cost scales with exactly the usage pattern supertool encourages. Above 160 characters the header is rebuilt from the parsed fields, keeping whatever identifies the *target* and dropping the content the diff is about to show: `--- edit: "    staging_contents: dict[str, str] = {}… (+609 chars)" → pipeline/shell.py ---`. `replace_lines` keeps its line range, `paste`/`append` keep the path and a character count, `vim` keeps its script. The elided count is reported rather than trailing off, since a silent truncation reads as "that was the whole argument", and a multi-line anchor is folded to one line. Anything shorter keeps its verbatim header, so ordinary short ops are untouched — and so does **any op that did not write**: no diff renders when nothing changed, which makes the verbatim header the only surviving copy of what the caller sent, and eliding it would take the reproduction material away at the one moment it is needed. The test is a write counter bumped past the rename in `_atomic_write`, not an `ERROR` prefix on the receipt — `replace`'s zero-match returns `(0 occurrences of 'x' found)`, a failure that never says so, and a `replace_dry` preview never writes at all. Closes [#384](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/384).
- **A `\\` at the end of a line in a shell script is flagged.** The trap is that getting the escaping wrong does not always fail to match — sometimes it writes. In bash, `FOO=$(cmd \\` followed by a newline is an escaped backslash and then a new command, not a line continuation: it is syntactically valid, passes `bash -n`, passes the `bash-check` validator, and does something entirely different from what was meant. No syntax validator can see it; only the byte pattern can. The check runs at `_atomic_write`, the one place every mutating op passes through, and is scoped to `.sh`/`.bash`/`.zsh`/`.ksh` — a trailing `\\` is ordinary in Python, C or JSON. Bash consumes trailing backslashes pairwise from the left, so **parity** decides: an even run ends the line, while an odd run leaves one over to continue it. Only even runs are flagged, which means a single trailing backslash *and* a run of three — one escaped backslash plus a genuine continuation — both stay quiet. A backslash followed by trailing spaces or tabs gets its own warning at any parity: the escape applies to the space, so the line ends there whatever was intended, and the difference is invisible in a diff. The queue is keyed by path, so a validator rollback retracts the warning for the content it just reverted — a warning about bytes no longer on disk would be worse than none — and it un-counts the write with it, since a change that did not stick leaves no diff to read the arguments from. In practice it fires on the `@payload` route, where TOML literal strings preserve backslashes verbatim; the colon CLI decodes escapes before the write, so the pattern never reaches the file. Part of [#380](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/380).
- Unit tests for the `git-push` timeout verdict (6 cases): a push that outlasts its budget with the remote ref at HEAD reports pushed, the same after a hook amended HEAD, a remote that did not move reports `PUSH TIMED OUT` with both shas, an unreadable remote reports `unknown` rather than guessing, the `ls-remote` probe surviving its own timeout, and a guard asserting the push budget stays strictly below the op cap. Plus two in `test_custom_ops` for partial output surviving a timeout, and staying absent when the op printed nothing.
- Unit tests `test_state_reset_and_lint_timeout` (9 cases): a lint timeout is reported rather than swallowed and is not conflated with a lint failure, `SUPERTOOL_LINT_TIMEOUT` reaches the subprocess and appears in the marker, an unparseable value falls back to the default, a missing binary keeps its silence, a clean lint stays clean, a polluting test followed by one asserting the fixture cleaned up after it, and the guard that fails when a new module-level mutable global is accounted for in neither conftest tuple.
- Unit tests `test_compact_header_384` (15 cases): short and read-op headers stay verbatim, a long `edit`/`paste`/`replace_lines` header keeps its target and drops its content, the elided count is reported, a multi-line anchor becomes one line, the batch sub-op case from the issue, the shell-backslash warning firing plus its four negatives, the per-dispatch drain, and the `cwd:` mention in `path not found`.
- **`_count_lines` was defined twice at module scope, and the wrong one won.** Python binds the last definition, so every call — including the glob/grep auto-read line cap the *first* one was written for — reached the second. Their error paths are opposites: the first returns `MAX_AUTOREAD_LINES + 1` so a file it cannot measure is treated as over-cap and left unread, and its docstring says exactly that; the second returns `0`, so an unreadable file counted as *under* the cap and was auto-read. The comment describing the cap was authoritative-looking and had been wrong for months. There is one definition now, and because the callers genuinely want opposite answers the error value is an `on_error` parameter rather than a constant somebody has to guess right: the auto-read cap passes the sentinel and fails closed, while `map` and the workspace summary keep the truthful `0` a listing needs. Closes [#388](https://github.com/Digital-Process-Tools/claude-supertool/issues/388).


- Unit tests `test_count_lines_388` (8 cases): counting with and without a trailing newline, the empty file, a count spanning the 64KB chunk boundary, both error values, permission-denied as a second unreadable case, and a guard asserting only one module-scope definition survives.
- **Every mutating op reports the branch it wrote on.** Two near-misses in one session were the same shape — right file, wrong branch. One was caught only by running `git status` before committing, which is the difference between "unrelated docs edits swept into an open PR" and not; the other surfaced as `ERROR: old string not found`, a confusing symptom for "you are on the wrong branch", where the first hypothesis is a bad anchor and the next move is a `read` round-trip to check it. Neither is supertool's fault, but supertool is the thing that knows. `edit`, `replace`, `replace_lines`, `paste` and `vim` now close with `[branch: main]` — on failure as well as success, since a failed edit is exactly when a wrong-branch hypothesis should already be on the table.
